Orientation — Scope, Estimation and Method

0.1 Scope and audience0.2 How to read this guide0.3 Estimation discipline0.4 The workload model0.5 Vocabulary and notation0.6 Review checklist

This opening section fixes the scope, states the reader, and calibrates the method used throughout. It argues that system design is an exercise in explicit estimation under uncertainty rather than in pattern recall, and that the dominant cause of production failure is a mis-stated requirement rather than a mis-chosen pattern. The vocabulary defined here is used without redefinition in all eighteen remaining chapters.

0.1 Scope and audience

This subsection states what the guide covers, what it excludes, and the level of expertise it assumes.

The guide addresses online, request-serving systems: multi-tenant services, content platforms, transactional back offices, and the data planes that support them. It assumes a reader who has operated production systems and wants reference precision — the mechanism named, the parameter range over which it behaves as advertised, and the failure mode that makes it fail. Three areas are excluded: build and release engineering, except where it changes a runtime topology such as blue/green cutover or schema-compatible deployment; single-node storage internals beyond what is needed to reason about I/O behaviour; and proprietary managed services, which are never prescribed. Where a closed offering is the only viable answer in some organisational context, the underlying mechanism is described so the reader can recognise an equivalent anywhere.

# Version baseline assumed across this guide
postgres  18.x   mysql  9.x     redis   8.x    memcached 1.6.x
nginx     1.28.x haproxy 3.0.x  envoy   1.34.x kubernetes 1.33+
node      24.x   go     1.25.x  rust    1.90.x dotnet 10.x
python    3.14.x php    8.5.x   vue     3.5.x  cassandra 5.0.x
kafka     4.x    etcd   3.6.x
# Not ceilings: thresholds changed default behaviour in specific
# minor releases (PG18 async I/O, Redis 8 hash expansion, Envoy
# 1.34 QUIC). Re-verify against the deployed build.

Version pinning is a liability if treated as a ceiling. Several mechanisms discussed changed default behaviour in specific minor releases: hash-join spilling thresholds in PostgreSQL 18, hash field expansion in Redis 8 cluster mode, QUIC transport in Envoy 1.34. Any threshold quoted in this guide should be re-verified against the deployed version before it is adopted as a design input.

0.2 How to read this guide

This subsection establishes the reading contract: chapter order, per-subsection structure, and the dependency each chapter assumes.

Chapters 1 through 6 form the irreducible core: requirements and service levels, capacity estimation, storage engine selection, caching, request routing, and database distribution. Chapters 7 onward build on that core in dependency order. The dependency is real rather than editorial: caching cannot be designed before service levels exist, because a cache without a stated staleness budget is an unbounded correctness risk; sharding cannot be designed before estimation, because a shard count chosen without a storage model is a guess.

# Reading order, with the dependency each chapter assumes
ch01 requirements     -> none
ch02 estimation       -> ch01 (service levels bound headroom)
ch03 storage engines  -> ch01 (access-pattern mix)
ch04 caching          -> ch01 (staleness), ch02 (working set)
ch05 routing          -> ch02 (rps per instance)
ch06 databases@scale  -> ch02 (volume), ch03 (engine)
ch07 replication/queue-> ch05 (routing), ch06 (replication)
ch08 streaming        -> ch07 (durability semantics)
ch09 consistency      -> ch06, ch07
ch10 coordination     -> ch05, ch09
ch11 partitioning      -> ch02, ch06
ch12 reliability      -> all above
ch13 observability    -> ch01 (SLIs)
ch14 deploy/config    -> ch05, ch12
ch15 security         -> ch01 (data classification)
ch16 batch/stream     -> ch08
ch17 data platform    -> ch02, ch08
ch18 decisions        -> everything

Each subsection follows a fixed structure: the mechanism, the parameter range over which it behaves as advertised, the failure modes observed in production, and a configuration or diagnostic artefact. Subsections comparing options carry a table; subsections introducing a formula carry the derivation; subsections describing an operation carry a config excerpt. Exercises close every section and are design tasks with a written deliverable, never comprehension checks.

0.3 Estimation discipline

This subsection argues that design decisions must be traceable to a written number, and shows how to produce one under uncertainty.

A decision that cannot be traced to a number cannot be reviewed, and a decision that cannot be reviewed cannot be reversed cheaply. The discipline is not to produce exact figures — nobody can, twelve months out — but to produce a range, name the assumption the range is dominated by, and state the measurement that would collapse it. A design that survives when its dominant assumption is wrong by an order of magnitude is a resilient design. One that only survives when the assumption holds is a bet, and bets are acceptable only where they are cheap to reverse.

# Worked: photo sharing, 12 M MAU
# S1. Per-user activity: 2.0 uploads/MAU/mo, 4.0 views/MAU/mo
# S2. Derive per-second FIRST, then scale
#   uploads/s = 2.0*12e6/(30*86400) = 9.26 w/s
#   views/s   = 4.0*12e6/2.592e6   = 18.5 r/s
#   peak 3.0x mean -> 27.8 w/s, 55.4 r/s, 83 rps combined
#   THIS is the load-balancer sizing number.
# S3. Payload: original 2.5 MiB, derived 320 KiB, meta 1.2 KiB
# S4. Monthly volume
#   24e6*2.5 MiB = 58.6 TiB originals
#   48e6*320 KiB = 15.0 TiB derivatives
#                   = 73.6 TiB/month
# S5. 24-month horizon at 18% MoM growth
#   73.6*(1.18^24 - 1)/0.18 = 28 760 TiB = 28.1 PiB
# Object storage cost binds. Compute is not the constraint.

Anchor the planning number at the top of the plausible band. Real systems carry unlisted surface area: retries, replication writes, secondary indexes, log shipping, backups, and per-tenant growth asymmetries. Anchoring at the median of a derived range under-provisions by 1.5–2.5×. Re-derive quarterly and diff against the previous version; a parameter that has not moved in four quarters is usually one nobody is measuring.

# Turning an estimate into a testable hypothesis
claim:   "83 rps peak now, 1.2 M rps at 18 months"
dominant:"views per MAU per month holds at 4.0"
sensitivity: "1.0 -> 2.0 views/MAU doubles egress and read rps"
confidence: low
measure:
  - { name: views_per_user_per_month, src: clickstream 30d,
      by: product }
  - { name: derived_bytes_per_view, src: X-Upstream-Bytes,
      by: edge }
collapse_trigger: "views/MAU outside [3.2, 4.8] for 2 weeks"
review_date: 2026-12-01
# collapse_trigger is the field that matters: it is the
# condition that reopens the decision, and it is checked
# on a schedule rather than hoped for.

0.4 The workload model

This subsection defines the workload archetypes that determine where a system is hard, and the measurements that select a design.

Traffic shape decides where the difficulty lives, and is described by three axes: read-to-write ratio, key-distribution skew, and correlation between response size and request rate. A 100:1 read/write system with uniform keys is a caching problem with a database underneath. A 1:1 system where one key carries 3 000× the mean is a partitioning problem, and caching will not help because the key is hot precisely because it is one row. A system where the largest 1 % of tenants produce 70 % of traffic is a multi-tenancy problem. These axes must be measured before design, not assumed afterwards.

Workload archetypeRead:writeKey skewDominant difficultyPrimary technique
OLTP, uniform access5:1 – 20:1max/median < 5Tail latency under lock contentionPrimary/replica split, read replicas
Hot key / celebrity200:1+1 000:1 – 100 000:1Single-shard saturationKey replication, request coalescing
Catalog / browse500:1 – 5 000:1Zipfian on categoryHit rate and invalidationCDN, two-tier cache, versioned keys
Ledger / financial3:1UniformCorrectness, durable orderingSerialisability, single writer, quorum
IoT / telemetry ingest200:1Extreme device skewWrite amplification, back pressureBuffered log, compaction, rollup
Multi-tenant SaaS10:1Tenant skew 100:1Noisy neighbour, quotasPer-tenant rate limit, key prefixing
Graph traversal100:1Degree skew, power lawFan-out, supernodesAdjacency sharding, closure precompute
 
# The three measurements that decide the architecture
# 1. read:write ratio per route and tenant
SELECT route, tenant_id,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE kind='read')::float
     / nullif(count(*) FILTER (WHERE kind='write'),0) AS rw
FROM request_log WHERE ts > now() - interval '7 days'
GROUP BY 1,2 ORDER BY 3 DESC LIMIT 20;

# 2. skew: max/median, and share held by the top 1% of keys
WITH k AS (SELECT user_id, count(*) c,
       row_number() OVER (ORDER BY count(*) DESC) rn,
       count(*) OVER () n FROM events GROUP BY 1)
SELECT max(c)::float / NULLIF(percentile_cont(0.5)
         WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY c),0) AS max_over_median,
       sum(c) FILTER (WHERE rn <= n*0.01)::float
         / sum(c) AS top_1pct_share FROM k;

# 3. size-rate correlation; cv > 1 means the mean lies
SELECT route, avg(size_bytes) mean, count(*) n,
       stddev(size_bytes)/nullif(avg(size_bytes),0) AS cv
FROM request_log GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY n DESC;
# 4. concurrent users, to size pools via Little's law (ch2.1)
SELECT date_trunc('minute', ts) AS m, count(DISTINCT session_id)
FROM request_log WHERE ts > now() - interval '1 day'
GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 1;
# Interpretation: cv below 0.5 means the mean response size
# is representative; above 1.5 the capacity number must be
# derived from the p99, and a 2.4x p99-to-mean ratio is
# common for routes that return an image half the time.

A 3× peak-to-mean ratio is an assumption, not a constant. Consumer products routinely show 8–20× peak-to-mean in evening hours, and scheduled batch windows can produce 50×. Derive the multiplier from the product’s own traffic curve where one exists; where it does not, plan for 3× and make the headroom decision reversible, because retrofitting 3× headroom costs an order of magnitude more than reserving it at design time.

0.5 Vocabulary and notation

This subsection fixes the terms the guide uses throughout, including the ones engineers routinely conflate.

Terminology drift causes incidents, because two engineers who mean different things by "eventually consistent" ship different systems and both are surprised. Where a term has a competing industry definition it is named rather than silently overridden. The most consequential distinction is between a latency target and a latency measurement: network transit is frequently 30–60 % of an end-to-end figure, and a budget that ignores it is not a budget.

TermDefinition used hereCommon confusion
LatencyRequest emitted to last response byte receivedConfused with server-side duration
SLIThe measured quantity, e.g. 1 − bad/valid over 28 dTreated as the target rather than the measurement
SLOThe target value of an SLI, e.g. 99.9 % of requests succeedConflated with SLA, which carries a commercial remedy
Error budget1 − SLO as permitted unreliability, e.g. 40.3 min per 28 dReported as 0.1 % with no time equivalent
RTOMaximum tolerable outage before the business declares failureConfused with detection time, which adds to it
RPOMaximum tolerable data loss, measured as a time windowConfused with "replication is on", which bounds neither
ConsistencyWhether a read may observe a state in no serial orderConfused with freshness, which is a latency property
Working setHot bytes reachable inside the latency targetConfused with dataset size, often 100× larger
HeadroomProvisioned ÷ peak demand − 1, as a fractionApplied to the mean rather than to the peak
AmplificationBytes written or ops issued per logical operationExcluded from capacity models by default
 
# Notation used in every worked calculation in this guide
#   rps  requests/sec at PEAK unless stated
#   wps  write events/sec BEFORE replication or indexes
#   p50/p99  percentiles over a rolling 5-minute window
#   GiB/TiB binary units; 1 GiB = 1 073 741 824 B
#   N nodes, f replication factor, W/R amplification factors
#   A availability of one node (decimal), S skew factor

# Identities that recur throughout the guide
#   Little's law    concurrency = throughput * latency
#                   1000 rps * 0.050 s = 50 requests in flight
#   Availability    A_sys = 1 - (1 - A_node)^(N / f)
#   Scatter-gather  p_total = 1 - (1 - p_single)^N
#                   5 calls at p99 30 ms -> total p99 107 ms;
#                   serialised instead -> 150 ms
#   Zipf share      share(N) = H(N,s) / H(K,s)
#   Compounding     1.45^2 = 2.10, not 1.90

Percentiles do not add, and means do not describe a tail. A p99 of 50 ms made of a 48 ms query and 2 ms of serialisation is not a 50 ms query with overhead. The p99 of an N-way parallel fan-out approaches the Nth percentile of N draws, not their sum. Always state whether a percentile is computed before or after fan-out aggregation, and never report a mean as a service level.

0.6 Review checklist

This subsection provides the review gate applied to every design in the remaining chapters.

A design is ready for review when it can answer the following without its author present. These are not conversational questions; each is a document section that must exist. The architecture decision record carries them, and a design that cannot be expressed in one ADR page plus one capacity worksheet is not yet a design — it is an intention. The gate exists because the most expensive defects are not the ones that are hard to see; they are the ones nobody wrote down, which surface as production surprises eighteen months later.

# architecture-decision-record.md -- required sections
#  1. Context       one paragraph, no solution language
#  2. Decision      the mechanism, named specifically
#  3. Drivers       the three numbers the decision satisfies
#  4. Options       all credible alternatives, incl. do-nothing
#  5. Trade-offs    what is lost, in the same units as #3
#  6. Numbers       the capacity worksheet; ranges, not points
#  7. Failure modes per domain: detect, isolate, recover
#  8. Reversal cost in engineer-weeks; one-way door or not
#  9. SLO impact    which SLI moves, and in which direction
# 10. Review trigger the measurement that reopens this decision
# Worked entry for #7:
#  AZ loss       RTO 90 s   RPO 0    cross-zone NLB, 2 of 3
#  primary loss  RTO 30-60s RPO 2 s   async repl, auto-failover
#  cache loss    RTO 0     RPO n/a   stale-while-revalidate
#  bad deploy    RTO 5 min RPO 0     5% canary, auto-rollback
# A design is not a diagram, it is a decision plus its numbers
# ------------------------------------------------------------
# A diagram shows a topology. A design states which topology,
# for which measured traffic, at which cost, and what breaks
# first as traffic grows. The three artefacts a design needs:
#   1. the capacity worksheet (ch2.6) -- inputs, derivations,
#      falsification schedule
#   2. the architecture decision record (template above)
#   3. the failure-mode table, one row per failure domain
#
# The failure-mode table is the one that gets skipped and the
# one that matters most in an incident, because it is the only
# artefact that is read under time pressure. Required columns:
#   domain     process, node, rack, zone, region, dependency,
#              deploy, induced load, correlated software
#   detect     how, by what mechanism, and how long it takes
#   isolate    what stops accepting traffic, and in what order
#   recover    the action, whether automated, and its duration
#   RTO / RPO  the numbers the design promised
#   verify     the last time this row was exercised, and where
#
# A row whose "verify" column is empty is a claim, not a
# design, regardless of how the rest of the document reads.
# The four numbers every design in this guide must produce
# ------------------------------------------------------------
# 1. peak requests per second, at p99 of the 1-second rate
#    derived as  users * actions / period * peak_to_mean
#    used for:   node counts, connection pools, load balancers
# 2. bytes per second, split into origin egress and total egress
#    derived as  resp_size * read_rps * miss_ratio
#    used for:   NIC sizing, CDN cost, storage IOPS
# 3. resident working set, in gigabytes
#    derived as  hot_keys_for_hit_rate * value_size * overhead
#    used for:   cache RAM, buffer pool size, index fit
# 4. maximum tolerable staleness, per read path
#    derived from the requirement, NOT from the technology
#    used for:   every TTL, every grace window, every RPO
#
# Numbers 1-3 are capacity. Number 4 is correctness, and it
# is the one most often left implicit. A design that states
# 1-3 and omits 4 has a capacity model and a guess.
#
# A worked sketch, per the media service used throughout:
#   1. peak_rps        1 860
#   2. origin_egress   136 MB/s
#   3. cache_ram       8.1 GiB per node
#   4. product price   60 s      product detail   60 s
#      order status    0         balance          2 s
#      search results  5 min     analytics rows  60 s
#   Note that only one of the four read paths is permitted
#   any staleness at all, and that it is the one a customer
#   is most likely to notice.

Write the reversal cost before the design, not after. Any decision costing more than one engineer-week to undo is a one-way door and deserves a prototype, a load test, or a spike. A decision reversible by configuration change can be made quickly and revised in production. Naming the class explicitly in the ADR stops the two being argued about with identical rigour, and it is the cheapest risk classification available.

Exercises 1

  1. Produce a workload profile for a real or hypothetical service: read:write ratio, top-1 % key share, peak-to-mean from a 24-hour curve, mean and coefficient of variation of response size per route, and per-tenant skew. Classify it against the archetype table and justify the classification in 300 words, naming which axis dominates.
  2. Run a full back-of-envelope calculation for 5 M DAU, 3 items published per user per month, 20 reads per item, a 900 KiB mean original and a 90 KiB derivative set. State the result as a range, identify the dominant assumption, then repeat with that assumption at its 10th and 90th percentile and state which decision reverses between them.
  3. Compute availability at 99.9 %, 99.95 % and 99.99 % for three topologies: a single node; three nodes at replication factor 3; three nodes at factor 2 across three failure domains. Assume A_node = 0.997, one independent failure per domain per year, and a 0.05 correlated-failure probability within a domain. State which topology meets which target and the cheapest control for the level not met.
  4. Derive a staleness budget for a price shown on a detail page, at checkout, and on a cached marketing landing page. For each, state the maximum acceptable age, the enforcing mechanism (TTL, versioned key, event-driven invalidation), and the behaviour when the invalidation channel is down. Justify the divergence in the three answers.
  5. Draft a one-page ADR for a real decision made in the last twelve months using the ten-section template. Have it reviewed by someone who was not present when the decision was made, and record every question they could not answer from the document alone — those gaps are the documentation debt the team carries.

1 · Requirements, Constraints and Non-Functional Targets

1.1 Functional requirements as a contract1.2 SLOs, SLIs and error budgets1.3 Scale parameters and their derivation1.4 Latency budgets and percentiles1.5 Availability targets and failure domains1.6 Consistency, staleness and ordering requirements

This section converts a product ambition into the numbers that constrain every subsequent design decision. It argues that the requirements document is the highest-leverage artefact in a system: an unstated latency target silently forces synchronous fan-out, an unstated staleness bound silently forces a cache miss storm, and an unstated durability requirement silently forces either data loss or an unrecoverable latency spike. Requirements must be expressed as ranges over named distributions, because a single scalar cannot distinguish a system that is fast for the median user from one that is fast for the median request and pathological at p99.

1.1 Functional requirements as a contract

This subsection establishes that functional requirements define the system boundary and must arbitrate conflicts.

A functional requirement set exists to arbitrate conflicts, not to describe the product. A system has a finite budget of latency, consistency, and cost, and when two requirements collide only the requirements document can say which one yields. Requirements written as narratives cannot arbitrate; requirements written as testable predicates can. The working form is a triple — trigger, guarantee, and boundary of the guarantee. A requirement stated as "search results are returned" is unimplementable, because it omits both the guarantee ("within 200 ms at p95 over a 10 M document corpus") and the boundary ("excluding cross-tenant results"). Where a requirement is phrased non-functionally, as in "the checkout page is fast", it must be rejected at specification stage and returned to its owner as a request for a number.

# FR-014  Cart merge
#  trigger:   POST /carts/{id}/merge with a source cart id
#  guarantee: deterministic union of both carts, serialised per
#             user_id; identical input yields identical output on
#             any replica
#  boundary:  excludes checked_out carts; idempotent for 72 h via
#             operation_id dedupe
#  failure:   either cart locked > 5 s -> 409 + Retry-After,
#             never a partial merge
# FR-027  Feed rendering
#  trigger:   GET /feeds/home?cursor=...
#  guarantee: 40 items ordered by (score desc, post_id desc), no
#             duplicates per page, no gaps in pages 1-2 unless a
#             source is down
#  boundary:  fan-out capped at 12 sources; a timeout is skipped
#             and recorded, never blocking
#  failure:   > 3 of 12 fail -> serve the previous cursor page
#             rather than a short feed

Never let a requirement smuggle in an implementation. "The system shall use a relational database" is an unexamined design decision written into the contract, and it forecloses the sharding and polyglot options that chapter 6 is otherwise free to consider. Requirements state observable behaviour; mechanisms belong in the design document. If a requirement cannot be falsified by an external test against the running system, it is not a requirement.

1.2 SLOs, SLIs and error budgets

This subsection derives the SLO from a business tolerance and shows how the error budget becomes a release policy.

An SLO is derived, not chosen. The derivation runs backwards from consequence: if a 15-minute read-path outage costs £40 000 in lost margin at 2 %, the monthly tolerable loss against £2 M revenue is 0.16 %, giving 99.84 %. The team then picks a number at or slightly better than that, and — critically — decides which requests count as bad. Counting client-generated 4xx is the most common SLO defect, because one client bug can then consume an entire quarterly budget. The error budget is the operational consequence: a 14.4× burn over 1 h and 2 h windows exhausts 2 % of a monthly budget, meaning a continuation of a few hours consumes the month, which converts an abstract percentage into a runbook.

# Multi-window multi-burn-rate alerting (Sloth / OpenSLO form)
- service: checkout
  slo:
    objective: 99.9            # 0.1% budget per 28 d
    timeWindow: 28d
    indicator:
      ratio:
        good:  rate(http_requests_total{service="checkout",
                        code!~"5..", code!~"429"}[{{.window}}])
        total: rate(http_requests_total{service="checkout"}
                        [{{.window}}])
        # 4xx EXCLUDED, except 429 which is our own shedding
  burnRates:
    - { severity: page,   short: 5m,  long: 1h, factor: 14.4, pct: 2 }
    - { severity: page,   short: 30m, long: 6h, factor: 6,    pct: 5 }
    - { severity: ticket, short: 2h,  long: 3d, factor: 3,    pct: 10 }
# 14.4 = (30d/1h) * (0.1% / 2%): 2% of budget gone within 1 h
# Release gate bound to the same number:
#   budget >= 50%   normal cadence, 1 deploy/day
#   10-50%           weekly, 25% canary held 30 min
#   < 10%           freeze, reliability work only
ObjectiveUnreliabilityBudget / 28 dAt 14.4× burnBlast radius
99 %1 %6.72 h~3.0 minInternal tool, batch job
99.5 %0.5 %3.36 h~1.5 minReporting, exports
99.9 %0.1 %40.3 min~8.6 sRead-mostly consumer product
99.95 %0.05 %20.2 min~4.3 sCheckout, authentication
99.99 %0.01 %4.03 min~51 sPayments, ledger writes
99.999 %0.001 %24.2 s~4.2 sOrder-of-nines; needs quorum, not redundancy

Achievable availability is a function of component count, not component quality. Ten components each at 99.99 % compose to 0.9990^10 = 99.90 %. A service with forty network-dependent hops cannot offer a 99.99 % SLO however well each hop is built; the only route is to remove hops through collocation, batching, or by accepting the objective. Count the synchronous dependencies on the critical path before committing to a number.

def burn_rate(bad, good):
    total = bad + good
    if total == 0:
        return 0.0
    return (bad / total) / (1 - SLO_TARGET)

def page(short_s=300, long_s=3600):
    """Page only if BOTH windows burn. A fast spike and a
    slow trend together are the only actionable signal."""
    b_s = burn_rate(bad_events(short_s), good_events(short_s))
    b_l = burn_rate(bad_events(long_s), good_events(long_s))
    return b_s >= 14.4 and b_l >= 14.4

# Why two windows: a 5-minute window alone fires on a deploy
# and on nothing else; a 1-hour window alone takes an hour to
# fill, by which time the budget is spent. Requiring both
# removes 80-90% of pages while holding detection at ~5 min,
# because the short window fills instantly and the long
# window only needs to be ALREADY burning.

1.3 Scale parameters and their derivation

This subsection derives the five scale parameters that drive every capacity and topology decision.

Five parameters determine the architecture of almost every online system: peak request rate; data volume at the 24-month horizon; the working set, being the fraction of that volume touched within the latency window; write rate including index maintenance, replication, and compaction; and entity cardinality with its skew. Each must be a range with a named dominant assumption, expressed in the unit the consuming decision requires. The most common error is stating a per-day quantity where a per-second one is required, or the reverse — a 2 TB/day ingest figure and a 2 TB/s read figure differ by eight orders of magnitude, and the error surfaces as a baffling choice of instance class rather than as a recognisable capacity failure.

# Five scale parameters, event ingest service
# INPUTS (dated, attributable, not "assumed")
#   devices = 4 000 000   events_per_device_hour = 180 (measured)
#   bytes_per_event = 240 B p50 / 1 800 B p99   growth 35%/yr
# 1. RATE -- per second first
ev_s = 4e6 * 180 / 3600 = 200 000 events/s (mean)
peak = 200 000 * 2.5     = 500 000 events/s   <-- routing #
# 2. VOLUME -- multiply per-second by 86 400, do not re-guess
daily   = 200 000 * 86 400 * 240 = 3.861 TiB/day
y1      = 3.861 * 365 = 1 409 TiB
horizon = 1 409 * 1.35**2 = 2 567 TiB = 2.5 PiB
# 3. WORKING SET -- assume 4% of a partition touched per 24 h
ws = 3.861 * 0.04 * 1024 = 158 GiB, must be RAM/L3 resident
# 4. WRITE RATE -- with amplification, never raw
wps_raw = 200 000
wps_tot = wps_raw * (1 + idx*2 + repl*2 + compact*2.4) = 7.4x
# 5. CARDINALITY -- 1 device = 21 600 events/day; hot devices
#    reach 400x p50. Shard count follows WRITE throughput
#    per shard, never volume alone.

Re-derive quarterly and diff the result. A parameter that has not moved in four quarters is usually one nobody is measuring. Store each derivation as a dated artefact and diff it against its predecessor; a parameter that changed by more than 2× with no corresponding product change is almost always a deploy, a crawler, or a retry loop rather than a real shift in demand.

# The per-second vs per-day error, in both directions
WRONG: "4 TB/day is a storage problem, we need a distributed store"
  4 TB/day = 4e12 / 86 400 = 46.3 MB/s = 0.7% of one NVMe
  => the problem is cost, not throughput. "We must
     distribute" is unsupported by the arithmetic.
WRONG: "200 000 rps" with no qualifier
  200 000 * 86 400 = 1.73e10/day, which is neither the peak
  nor the day; a 2.5x diurnal peak means 500 000 rps peak
  and 2.4e10 requests/day. The two errors partly cancel and
  the figure looks conservatively safe.
RULE: 1. reduce to per-second immediately
      2. multiply out only for storage, quota, or billing
      3. label every figure with unit AND percentile
      4. an unlabelled number is not a number, it is a feeling

1.4 Latency budgets and percentiles

This subsection decomposes an end-to-end latency target into per-component budgets and shows why a fan-out’s p99 is a max, not a sum.

A latency target is not a constraint until it is decomposed, and it must be decomposed at p99 for every component. The sum of component p99s sits above the target because independent latencies combine as a distribution rather than a stack: five components with a p99 of 40 ms each give a total p99 nearer 90–110 ms, not 200 ms, when correlations are near zero. The corollary is that adding a synchronous hop is never a small change. A 40 ms dependency does not add 40 ms to the p99; it multiplies the probability of a slow response across the whole request and perturbs every downstream timeout, retry count, and connection-pool budget. The count of synchronous dependencies on the critical path is therefore itself a design metric.

# Latency budget, p99 per component (ms)
component                     p99  cum   note
DNS (cached resolver)          2     2   TTL 60 s
TLS 1.3 resumed handshake      8    10   tickets on, 0-RTT off
Edge/CDN to origin            25    35   cross-region: the big one
LB to app, same zone           1    36
AuthN, JWKS cached            2    38   1 h TTL, no network call
AuthZ, OPA local bundle        3    41   policy decision point
App overhead, Go 1.25, 2 vCPU  4    45
Fan-out of 3, PARALLEL        35    80   p99 of max(12,14,9), not 35
  serialising the same 3       80    80   sum of p99s
DB primary, index-only        28   108   2 rows, 0 heap fetches
Redis hit (63% hit rate)       1   109
Serialise + gzip              11   120
Return network                 8   128
-------------------------------------
total p99 = 128   target 200   slack 72 ms (36%)
p99.9 of the SERIALISED variant = 268 ms -> OVER TARGET
Parallelism is worth ~93 ms here: it is the entire budget.
import numpy as np

def tail_of_max(p99s, corr=0.0, n=200_000, seed=7):
    """p99 of a parallel fan-out over near-independent draws.
    Lognormal, not normal: the normal right tail is too thin
    to be credible for network services."""
    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
    z = rng.standard_normal((n, len(p99s)))
    cols = []
    for i, p99 in enumerate(p99s):
        sigma = np.log(p99 / 0.5) / 2.326   # p50 ~ p99/2.1
        mu    = np.log(0.5)
        s = np.sqrt(1 - corr) * z[:, i] + np.sqrt(corr) * z[:, 0]
        cols.append(np.exp(mu + sigma * s))
    total = np.max(np.column_stack(cols), axis=1)
    return {q: float(np.percentile(total, q)) for q in
            (50, 95, 99, 99.9)}

print(tail_of_max([12, 14, 9], corr=0.25))
# p50 14.9  p95 22.6  p99 27.1  p99.9 33.4
# Serialising the same three: p99 44.8, p99.9 61.0.
# A sum of p99s overstates; a max understates. Real behaviour
# sits between, closer to the max.

Percentiles computed after aggregation understate the tail. Averaging p99s across instances, sampling before computing, or re-bucketing an already-aggregated histogram all lose precisely the sub-second bursts that constitute the tail. The SLI must be evaluated over the raw event stream with histogram resolution concentrated in the 90–99.9th percentile range; a one-minute rate counter resampled to a p99 discards the tail it claims to measure.

1.5 Availability targets and failure domains

This subsection decomposes an availability target into the failure domains it must survive and sets RTO and RPO per domain.

An availability target is a claim about surviving a specific list of failures; a target without a failure list is unfalsifiable. The list is the failure domain enumeration, and only the first four domains are survivable by redundancy. The remainder require mechanisms: automatic rollback, rate limiting and load shedding, circuit breakers. Availability also degrades with time, not only with component count, because a second correlated failure during the recovery window is not independent. The failover path must be exercised while the first failure is still in progress, since that is the state in which it will actually be used.

Failure domainDetectRTORPOMechanismBy redundancy
Process crash / OOM< 5 s5 – 30 s0Restart, readiness probe, circuit break✓ Yes
Node loss15 – 60 s30 – 120 s0Drain, replace, load-balancer eviction✓ Yes
Rack loss30 – 90 s2 – 5 min0Anti-affinity, 3 racks, quorum✓ Yes
Zone loss60 – 180 s5 – 20 min0 – 30 sMulti-zone, cross-zone routing, quorum✓ Yes
Region loss2 – 10 min30 min – 4 h0 – 60 sActive-active or warm standby, async repl✓ Yes
Dependency outage10 – 60 sDegraded0 if queuedCircuit breaker, bulkhead, queue, degrade✗ No
Bad deploy< 60 s1 – 5 min0Canary, progressive rollout, auto-rollback✗ No
Retry storm / induced load30 – 300 s5 – 30 min0 if queuedRate limit, load shed, retry budget + jitter✗ No
Correlated software fault5 – 60 minHoursVariesFleet canary, version quorum, feature flags✗ No
 
# Availability arithmetic
A_proc, A_node, A_rack, A_az = 0.9999, 0.997, 0.995, 0.99

def avail_n_way(a, N, f):
    """N nodes, replication factor f, independent failure."""
    return 1 - (1 - a) ** (N / f)

# 6 nodes, f=3, across 3 zones -> 1 - 0.003^2   = 0.999991
# 3 nodes, f=2, across 3 zones -> 1 - 0.003^1.5 = 0.9952
#  ^ the second topology is ONE zone-loss event from a quorum
#    split, because quorum is 2 of 3. See section 6.5.

# Correlated-term correction. A 5-minute node recovery is a
# WINDOW during which a same-rack second failure is NOT
# independent:
#   P(two) ~= 0.15 * P(one)^2   for same-rack
# Effective availability sits 0.4-1.2 points below the naive
# product. That gap is invisible in a spreadsheet and is why
# "we have three replicas" and "we have 99.99%" are
# different claims.

RPO and RTO are asymmetric and only one is usually binding. RPO is a data property set by replication mode: synchronous to one peer gives RPO 0 at the price of a cross-node round trip on every write; asynchronous gives RPO equal to observed lag, which is 50 ms at p50 and 2–30 s at p99 under load. RTO is an operational property set by how fast a procedure can act. Read paths are usually RTO-bound; ledgers are RPO-bound; both together mean a synchronous commit on every write.

# tier      write_mode        RPO     RTO    price paid
# cart      async, 1 replica  <= 5 s  0 s   +0.4 ms p50
# profile   async, 2 replica  <= 5 s  0 s   3x storage
# checkout  sync, 1 replica   0       60 s  +0.6-2.0 ms p50-p99
# ledger    sync quorum 2/3   0       30 s  +1.2-6.0 ms, +33% space
# media     async, 3 replica  <= 60 s 0 s   4x storage (object)
# config    etcd quorum 3/5   0       15 s  p99 fsync 8-15 ms
# PostgreSQL 18: synchronous_commit = on | local | off
#   off is only safe with a replicated log in front of it.
# Measure the ACTUAL RPO rather than the configured one:
SELECT application_name, state, write_lag, flush_lag, replay_lag,
       pg_wal_lsn_diff(sent_lsn, replay_lsn) AS bytes_behind
FROM pg_stat_replication ORDER BY bytes_behind DESC;
-- potential RPO in seconds = bytes_behind / wal_rate_per_sec

1.6 Consistency, staleness and ordering requirements

This subsection converts freshness and ordering rules into named consistency models and the repairs each implies.

Consistency is a property of a read or a transaction, not a system-wide setting, and the highest-value act in requirements gathering is to classify each read by how stale it may be. The classification is small — strong, session, bounded staleness, eventual, read-your-write — and maps mechanically onto mechanisms. Stating the bound rather than the model name, as in "the balance may be up to 2 seconds old but never older", makes the requirement testable and the mechanism choice automatic. Two anomalies need explicit treatment because they are the ones users notice: read-your-write is violated by any fan-out mixing a strong read with a cached one, and monotonic reads are violated by round-robin across replicas with independent lag.

# Consistency requirement per read path -- classify, don't guess
path              model            bound     mechanism
----------------------------------------------------------------
balance display   bounded stale    <= 2 s   replica + lag alarm
balance mutation  read-your-write  immediate session token pin
inventory reserve strong           0         serialisable, quorum
search results    eventual         <= 5 min indexer round trip
product price     bounded stale    <= 60 s  versioned key + TTL
order status      read-your-write  immediate write then read master
feed order        monotonic        n/a       cursor carries (score,id);
                                            server rejects regressions
comment list      causal           n/a       parent revision + MVCC
notifications     bounded stale    <= 30 s  read-your-writes, vector
                                            clock per user
audit log         strict order     0         one writer partition per
                                            stream key
class ReplicaSet:
    """Read from any replica, but never move backwards."""
    def read(self, key, min_rev, max_lag_s=2.0):
        best = None
        for r in self.healthy_replicas():          # any order
            rev, rows = r.query(
                "SELECT rev FROM t WHERE k=$1 AND rev > $2 "
                "ORDER BY rev LIMIT 1", key, min_rev)
            if rev is None:
                continue                     # behind the floor
            if r.lag_s() > max_lag_s:
                continue                     # too far behind
            if best is None or rev > best.rev:
                best = Result(rev, rows, r)
        if best is None:
            # Either read the primary, or return 409 with the
            # current floor. (b) protects the read-path SLO;
            # (a) protects correctness. Choose deliberately.
            return self.primary.read(key, min_rev)
        return best
# Three replicas at 0.3 s, 1.9 s, 0.7 s lag, round-robin: a
# user paging with cursor 4471 gets 4471, 4468, 4473. The bug
# is intermittent and load-correlated, so it survives for
# months before anyone reproduces it.

Make staleness observable before making it tolerable. Every cached or replicated read path should emit its age — an X-Data-Age-Ms response header, a data_age_seconds histogram, and a log field — before any argument about whether the bound is acceptable. A staleness requirement that is not measured is enforced only by the caching layer’s configuration, which changes the moment someone redeploys the cache with a longer default TTL.

Exercises 2

  1. Convert a real feature specification into twenty requirements in the trigger/guarantee/boundary form and identify the three pairs in direct conflict. For each, state which requirement yields, the compensating mechanism, and the notification sent to the losing requirement’s owner. Deliverable is a two-page document with the conflicts marked.
  2. Derive an SLO for a service with £1.2 M monthly revenue at 4 % contribution margin, assuming a 45-minute read-path outage and a 20-minute checkout outage are the credible incidents. Compute the tolerated unreliability for each, pick a blended SLO, then write the four-tier burn-rate alerting configuration stating for each tier the action it authorises and who may take it.
  3. Build a complete latency budget for a page making eleven backend calls, four of which can run in parallel. Give every component a p50 and p99, compute the total under correlated and uncorrelated assumptions, and identify the two components that must be removed to reach 150 ms. Then set a per-call timeout so the sum cannot exceed 150 ms plus one retry.
  4. Produce a failure domain table for a system you have operated, covering all nine domains with measured rather than assumed detection and recovery times where available and explicitly marked estimates otherwise. Compute availability under the independent-failure model, then quantify the correlated correction for the two domains where it is largest.
  5. Classify a real system’s read paths into the six consistency models above and write the enforcement mechanism for every non-strong path as a code-level specification. Then deliberately violate one mechanism in a test environment and record the exact user-visible symptom and the time from violation to detection.

2 · Capacity Estimation and Back-of-Envelope Maths

2.1 Traffic, request rate and Little’s law2.2 Storage volume and growth2.3 Bandwidth, egress and payload sizing2.4 Memory, working set and cache sizing2.5 Headroom, peaks and failure margins2.6 Building and falsifying the capacity model

This section is the arithmetic layer of the guide. It converts the scale parameters of chapter 1 into concrete numbers — requests per second per instance, bytes per second of egress, gigabytes of resident working set, and shard counts — using a fixed order of operations that eliminates the two dominant estimation errors: mixing per-second with per-day units, and quoting means where percentiles are required. Capacity is not a number but a chain of derived numbers in which each link can be independently wrong, so the chain is written down, labelled, and re-derived against measurement at a stated cadence.

2.1 Traffic, request rate and Little’s law

This subsection derives peak request rate from user counts and converts rate to concurrency using Little’s law.

Throughput is derived, never assumed. The shape is always users × actions per user per period ÷ seconds per period, giving a mean, multiplied by the peak-to-mean ratio. The output that matters is the p99 of the one-second rate rather than the mean, because a five-minute aggregate hides exactly the bursts that provisioning exists for. Concurrency then follows from Little’s law — requests in flight equals arrival rate times mean time in system — which is an identity rather than a heuristic and is the correct basis for pool sizing. Sizing a pool from throughput alone under-provisions whenever latency rises, and a pool that under-provisions during a latency event converts a slow dependency into a total outage, because the queue moves inside the application and exhausts memory rather than absorbing load.

# Peak request rate, step by step
#  DAU = 1 200 000, sessions/user/day = 1.8, req/session = 24
#  peak_to_mean = 3.1 (measured), mean service 85 ms,
#  p99 service 310 ms
# 1. REQUESTS PER DAY -- label it, do not consume it downstream
rpd = 1_200_000 * 1.8 * 24 = 51 840 000 / day
# 2. MEAN per second
mean_rps = 51_840_000 / 86_400 = 600 req/s
# 3. PEAK per second -- the provisioning number
peak_rps = 600 * 3.1 = 1 860 req/s
# 4. CONCURRENCY -- Little's law, L = lambda * W
conc_p50 = 1_860 * 0.085 = 158 in flight
conc_p99 = 1_860 * 0.310 = 577 in flight
# 5. POOL per instance, 2x burst allowance, 24 instances
pool = ceil(577 * 2 / 24) = 49  ->  size at 64
# NEVER size from mean latency. A 3x latency excursion
# consumes 3x the concurrency, not 3%.
Per-instance capacity figureTypical valueSet byCheck first when missed
rps per vCPU, Go 1.25 net/http8 000 – 25 000Handler CPU costIs TLS terminated in-process? ~2 500 if so
rps per vCPU, Node 242 000 – 6 000Event loop, JSON costIs the work off the main thread?
rps per vCPU, PHP 8.5 FPM800 – 2 500OPcache hit ratioopcache.validate_timestamps=0 set?
queries/s per PostgreSQL 18 connection200 – 3 000Round trip, heap fetchesIs the plan index-only?
Redis 8 ops/s per core80 000 – 150 000Value size, pipeliningValues > 512 B? batch with MGET
MB/s per 10 GbE NIC at 70 %~900IRQ affinity, TSOsoftnet_stat drops non-zero?
IOPS per NVMe at queue depth 32120 000 – 350 000Block size, coalescing4 KiB random is 5-10× worse
 
# Little's law verified against the event stream, not a fit
# 1860 rps, p50 = 85 ms, p99 = 310 ms, lognormal assumption:
#   sigma = ln(310/85)/2.326 = 0.556 ;  mu = ln(0.085) = -2.465
#   mean = exp(mu + sigma^2/2) = 0.0992 s
#   L    = 1860 * 0.0992 = 184.5 concurrent
SELECT histogram_quantile(0.50, le) AS p50,
       histogram_quantile(0.99, le) AS p99,
       sum_le * 1.0 / sum(count)    AS mean_events,
       avg_le                       AS mean_bucket
FROM http_request_duration_seconds_bucket
WHERE route = '/checkout';
# mean_events, NOT avg_le. A bucket average is biased low by
# 15-25% on skewed traffic, which silently under-provisions
# the pool and converts the p99 into a 503 storm.

Little’s law also explains why retries destroy capacity. When a dependency slows from 80 ms to 800 ms, in-flight requests grow 10× at constant arrival rate. If the pool was sized for the healthy case, the excess queues inside the client, memory grows, and the failure propagates upstream as a timeout storm even as the dependency recovers. A client with bounded concurrency and a short timeout is a circuit breaker in disguise; a client with unbounded concurrency is a fan-out amplifier.

2.2 Storage volume and growth

This subsection converts entity count and payload size into a storage forecast and accounts for engine write amplification.

Storage planning fails in two directions. Under-provisioning is a procurement problem and is visible. Ignoring write amplification is the more common failure, because amplification is invisible in the entity count and appears only in bytes written. A relational engine with six indexes writes roughly three to eight times the logical row size; a log-structured engine writes ten to thirty times before compaction, settling to 1.5–4× in steady state. Those factors belong in the storage calculation, not in the tiering calculation. The forecast is made at three horizons, because they select different technologies: under roughly 100 GB of hot data a single node with a hot replica is correct and a distributed store is pure overhead; between 100 GB and 5 TB, vertical scaling with read replicas and partitioning remains correct; beyond 5–10 TB per shard of related data, or 20–50 k writes per second, distribution becomes unavoidable.

# Storage forecast, media service
# users_y1 = 12e6, growth 45%/yr, 24 photos/user/yr
# original 2.5 MiB, derivatives 0.32 MiB, meta 1.2 KiB
# row_overhead = 2.6 (B-tree + MVCC + 0.7 page fill)
photos_y1 = 12e6 * 24 = 288e6
orig_y1   = 288e6 * 2.5 MiB  = 703 TiB  x3 repl = 2 109 TiB
deriv_y1  = 288e6 * 0.32 MiB =  90 TiB  x3 repl =   270 TiB
meta_log  = 288e6 * 1.2 KiB  = 337 GiB
meta_pg   = 337 GiB * 2.6    = 876 GiB -> primary + 2 repl
#          = 2.6 TiB, plus WAL at ~4% of logical per hour
#            retained 7 days = 337 GiB * 0.04 * 168 = 2.2 TiB
# YEAR 3: 1.45^2 = 2.10, cumulative, not additive
photos_y3  = 288e6 * 2.10 = 605e6
object_y3  = (703 + 90) * 2.10 = 1 667 TiB x3 = 5 001 TiB
meta_pg_y3 = 876 GiB * 2.10 = 1.8 TiB
# DECISION: 1.8 TiB relational at 2x the write rate fits two
# instances of 64 vCPU / 512 GiB. Do NOT shard yet; add a read
# replica per 3-4x read amplification first.
EngineWrite amp, steadyRead ampSingle-node ceilingFirst exhaustion symptom
PostgreSQL 18, B-tree, 3 indexes3 – 8×1× (index-only)20 – 60 k wps, 5 – 15 TBCheckpoint storms, autovacuum lag
MySQL 9.x InnoDB, clustered, 5 idx4 – 10×1 – 3×15 – 40 k wps, 2 – 8 TBHistory list grows, purge stalls
Redis 8, string/hash, AOF everysec1 – 2×1×150 k ops/s, 256 GBevicted_keys rises, hit rate falls
Object store, 3 repl, erasure cold1× + 1× intra-AZ1×Unbounded, ~5 000 PUT/sRequest rate limits, throttling
Wide-column LSM, 3 replicas1.5 – 4× post-compact1×15 – 50 k wps/shardCompactions lag, read repair
Search index, inverted1.2 – 2× segment merge1×1 – 2 M docs/shardMerge queue backlog, segment count

The growth multiplier compounds before it adds. A service growing 45 % year over year holds 2.10× its year-one volume in year two and 3.05× in year three, not 1.45× and 1.90×. Cost plans built by adding the annual increment understate year-three storage by roughly 1.6×, and the shortfall arrives as a mid-year procurement emergency resolvable only with more expensive capacity. Above 30 % annual growth, compounding must be computed rather than estimated.

2.3 Bandwidth, egress and payload sizing

This subsection sizes network egress, separates internal from internet-facing bandwidth, and shows how payload and miss ratio combine into a bill.

Bandwidth is the most reliably mis-estimated quantity in design, because it is the only one where a plausible-looking estimate is off by an order of magnitude. The calculation is mean response size times read rate times cache miss ratio, and it goes wrong in three places: the response size, sampled at the wrong percentile or measured compressed at the client rather than at the origin; the miss ratio, assumed rather than measured; and the omission of non-customer traffic, which includes replication, write-ahead logging, backups, and log shipping. Internal and internet bandwidth must be sized separately. Internal bytes are free but contend for NIC capacity; internet egress is billed per byte and is usually the largest single line item for a content business. An origin shipping a 2.5 MiB original to an edge holding a 320 KiB derivative pays eight times the egress its users see.

# Egress, media service
# peak_read_rps 1860, edge hit 0.94, api fanout 0.9 MiB,
# derivative 0.32 MiB, cacheable fraction 0.88
# 1. Only misses reach the origin
miss_rps = 1_860 * (1 - 0.94) = 111.6 rps
# 2. ORIGIN EGRESS
origin = 111.6 * (0.32 + 0.9) MiB/s
       = 136.2 MiB/s = 1.14 Gbps
#       10 GbE at 70% = 7 Gbps usable -> 6.3x headroom
# 3. USER-FACING EGRESS, which the CDN absorbs
user = 1_860 * (0.32*0.88 + 1.9*0.12) MiB/s
      = 886 MiB/s = 7.43 Gbps
#      billed at 0.06 list from CDN vs 0.09 from origin
# 4. INTERNAL TRAFFIC -- the item always forgotten
replication = 8 400 wps * 1.2 KiB * 2   = 20.2 MB/s
wal_stream  = 1 240 MB/s   (uncompressed WAL volume)
backups     = 5 001 TiB/7 per day * 0.35 = 848 MB/s
log_ship    = 2 400 pods * 4 MiB/s      = 9.6 GB/s  <<<
# Container log shipping alone can exceed all customer traffic.
# Always include it; it is frequently the largest line item.
# Nginx 1.28 -- egress instrumentation and proxying
worker_processes auto;
worker_rlimit_nofile 65535;
events { worker_connections 32768; use epoll; multi_accept on; }
http {
    log_format bytes '$remote_addr "$request" $status '
                   'req=$request_length sent=$body_bytes_sent '
                   'cache=$upstream_cache_status '
                   'up=$upstream_response_time';
    access_log /var/log/nginx/access.log bytes buffer=256k flush=1s;
    gzip on; gzip_vary on; gzip_comp_level 5; gzip_min_length 1024;
    sendfile on; tcp_nopush on; tcp_nodelay on; aio threads;
    upstream origin {
        least_conn;
        server 10.0.1.11:8443 max_fails=3 fail_timeout=15s;
        server 10.0.2.12:8443 max_fails=3 fail_timeout=15s;
        server 10.0.3.13:8443 max_fails=3 fail_timeout=15s;
        keepalive 512; keepalive_timeout 60s;  # > upstream p99.1
    }
    server {
        listen 443 ssl http2 reuseport backlog=65535;
        proxy_http_version 1.1; proxy_set_header Connection "";
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
        proxy_connect_timeout 2s; proxy_read_timeout 5s;
        proxy_next_upstream error timeout http_502 http_503;
        proxy_next_upstream_tries 2;  # ONE retry: retries turn a
                                     # partial outage into a total one
        proxy_buffering on; proxy_buffers 32 32k;
    }
}

Measure response size on the wire, split by cache outcome. The capacity-relevant figure is bytes leaving the origin, which is the compressed size at the origin, not at the edge — compression ratios differ by 15–40 % depending on whether the CDN re-encodes. Instrument a histogram of bytes sent per route split by cache outcome and re-derive egress monthly; a single 4 KB header added at 1 860 rps is 7.2 MB/s, invisible on a dashboard and visible on an invoice.

2.4 Memory, working set and cache sizing

This subsection defines the working set and computes the RAM a cache needs for a target hit rate.

Cache sizing is not "how much data do I have" but "how much data is touched while the cache is filling". The working set is the intersection of the hot set and the access window. A naive TTL-window calculation for a 300-second TTL at 1 860 rps gives 558 000 entries, but with a Zipf exponent near 1.1 the top 4 % of keys take 80 % of requests and the RAM achieving a 95 % hit rate is 5–15× smaller. The correct method is to size from a measured hit-rate curve rather than a distribution assumption: load a representative key sample into a simulated cache, sweep the memory size, and read the size at the target hit rate with 20 % margin. Object overhead is the second half of the question and is frequently underestimated, since a 1 KB Redis 8 string with its object header, hash-table entry, and allocator rounding costs 1.2–1.4 KB resident.

import numpy as np
K, S = 41_000_000, 1.08

def zipf_share(top_n, K, s):
    """H(N,s)/H(K,s), H the generalised harmonic number."""
    i = np.arange(1, K + 1, dtype=np.float64)
    return np.cumsum(i ** -s)[top_n - 1] / np.sum(i ** -s)

for n in (50_000, 200_000, 800_000, 2_000_000):
    print(n, round(zipf_share(n, K, S), 4))
# 50 000 0.5418 | 200 000 0.7916 | 800 000 0.9229 | 2M 0.9603
# ~2 M keys gives ~96% share, i.e. ~95% hit rate.
# RAM = 2e6 * 1200 B * 1.35 overhead = 3.02 GiB
#     + tail streamed in, 60% resident      = 1.52 GiB
#     = 4.54 GiB data + 3.5 GiB other      = 8.04 GiB
#     -> 16 GiB node at 60% utilisation.
# SENSITIVITY: at s = 0.9 (a newly launched, flatter product)
#   2M keys  -> 0.751 share, ONLY a 75% hit rate
#  10M keys  -> 0.953 share, needing 16 GiB of data alone
# The design is sensitive to a DISTRIBUTION parameter, not to
# data volume. Monitor the exponent quarterly.
# Redis 8.x -- the knobs that make the sizing achievable
maxmemory 12gb
# allkeys-lru  : correct for a pure cache
# volatile-lru : only keys with a TTL; right when a few config
#               keys must never be evicted
# noeviction   : WRONG for a cache; turns memory pressure into
#               503s that look like an availability incident
maxmemory-policy allkeys-lru
maxmemory-samples 10     # 5-10 is right; 3 undersamples and
                         # evicts hot keys far more often
lazyfree-lazy-eviction yes   # background free, not inline
lazyfree-lazy-expire yes
repl-backlog-size 64mb
appendonly no               # a cache must not fsync
save ""
activedefrag yes            # if RSS >> used_memory for > 1 h
io-threads 4                # read path only
io-threads-do-reads yes
enable-protected-configs yes
# Verify the hypothesis directly
redis-cli INFO memory | egrep 'used_memory|maxmemory'
redis-cli INFO stats | egrep 'keyspace_(hits|misses|evictions)'
# A rising evicted_keys rate is a capacity SIGNAL, not a cache
# statistic: it is an admission that the model is falsified.

Eviction is a capacity signal, not a behaviour to be tuned away. A rising evicted_keys counter means the working-set estimate was wrong, and the visible symptom is a hit rate that falls in proportion to load — which reads as a performance regression and gets "fixed" by adding CPU. Monitor hit rate against the memory watermark directly and alert when evictions exceed a small fraction of requests; sustained eviction falsifies the capacity model, and the correct response is to re-derive the working set rather than to add cache nodes.

2.5 Headroom, peaks and failure margins

This subsection distinguishes ordinary headroom from failure headroom and shows why the second decides outcomes.

There are two headrooms and conflating them is expensive. Ordinary headroom is provisioned capacity divided by peak demand, and absorbs forecast error: growth, launches, seasonality. Failure headroom is the capacity remaining when a failure domain is lost, and absorbs redistribution onto survivors. A system with 40 % ordinary headroom and no failure headroom tolerates a forecast miss and fails instantly on a zone event — the exact inverse of what the capacity plan intended. The 2× rule is the shorthand for failure headroom in a horizontally scaled tier: N+1 tolerates one node loss, 2N tolerates a rack loss. Where a tier is not horizontally scalable, a single writer database or a single partition, headroom is expressed instead as a write rate and the control is a limiter rather than a machine count.

# Headroom, both kinds, for the media service
peak_rps, nodes, per_node_cap = 1_860, 24, 142

# --- ORDINARY HEADROOM ---
provisioned = 24 * 142 = 3 408 rps
ordinary    = 3_408 / 1_860 - 1 = 0.83   (83%)
# 83% buys 24 months at 45% YoY with no capacity ticket.

# --- FAILURE HEADROOM: the number that decides outcomes ---
# 1 node down -> 23 nodes
load1 = 1_860 * 24 / 23 = 1 941 rps -> 84.4/node = 59%  OK
# 1 AZ down (8 of 24) -> 16 nodes
loadAZ = 1_860 * 24 / 16 = 2 790 rps -> 174.4/node
        = 123% of capacity  <-- AT 2.5x MONTHLY PEAK THIS
#        IS AN OUTAGE, not a degradation.
# REQUIRED >= 32 nodes (11/11/10) so 16 survivors carry
# 2 790 rps at 80% of 142. Cost +33%; the zone event
# becomes survivable.

# --- SINGLE-WRITER TIER: headroom as a rate, not a node count
db_peak, db_measured = 8_400, 6_100   # 27% headroom
# Adding read replicas does NOT raise writer headroom. The
# only levers are: shed write load (429 + Retry-After),
# batch writes into fewer larger transactions, or move a
# class of writes to another store.
admission_ceiling = 6_100 * 0.85 = 5_185 wps
# at 8 400 peak, 3 215 wps (38%) is rejected then queued.
# Kubernetes 1.33 -- failure headroom, declared
apiVersion: autoscaling/v2
kind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler
metadata: { name: media-api }
spec:
  scaleTargetRef: { apiVersion: apps/v1, kind: Deployment,
                    name: media-api }
  minReplicas: 32          # NOT 24: survives one zone loss
  maxReplicas: 220
  metrics:
    - type: Resource
      resource:
        name: cpu
        target: { type: Utilization, averageUtilization: 55 }
        # 55% not 70%: at 70% the HPA waits 5 min to react,
        # longer than a zone event takes to move traffic.
  behavior:
    scaleUp:
      stabilizationWindowSeconds: 30
      policies: [{ type: Pods, value: 8, periodSeconds: 30 }]
    scaleDown:
      stabilizationWindowSeconds: 600
# Plus a PodDisruptionBudget with minAvailable 24 (may drain
# 1 of 32), and topology spread in the Deployment: maxSkew 1
# on topology.kubernetes.io/zone, DoNotSchedule. Without the
# spread constraint the scheduler packs and the arithmetic
# above is fiction.

Scale-up time belongs in the headroom budget. A pod that takes 45 seconds from image pull to ready cannot help a two-minute event, and a 30-second initial readiness delay means it cannot help at all. Pre-pulled images on every node, a five-second readiness probe, and a short termination grace are what make autoscaling an availability mechanism rather than a cost mechanism. Confirm the arrival time before believing the headroom.

2.6 Building and falsifying the capacity model

This subsection specifies the capacity model as a maintained artefact with named assumptions, owners, and a falsification schedule.

A capacity model that lives in a slide deck is dead. The model with value is a versioned artefact naming every input, its unit, its source, the sensitivity of each output to it, and the measurement that would falsify the conclusion. The requirement is that a reader who did not attend the workshop can identify, without asking a question, which number would have to change for the recommendation to reverse. The falsification schedule converts the estimate into engineering: each derived number is paired with a measurement, an owner, and a review date, and when the measurement returns the model is diffed. The pattern of which parameters drift, and in which direction, is far more informative than any single value.

# capacity-model.yaml -- the model as a maintained artefact
apiVersion: capacity/v1
kind: CapacityModel
metadata: { name: media-service, owner: platform,
            last_reviewed: 2026-08-14 }
inputs:
  - { id: dau, value: 1200000, unit: users, confidence: high,
      source: analytics 28d rolling,
      sensitivity: "every other input is linear in this" }
  - { id: peak_to_mean, value: 3.1, unit: ratio,
      confidence: medium, source: edge log 90d p99 of 1s rate,
      sensitivity: drives node count linearly }
  - { id: payload_p50, value: 1200, unit: bytes,
      confidence: high, source: body_bytes_sent histogram }
  - { id: cache_hit_rate, value: 0.94, unit: fraction,
      confidence: medium, source: redis keyspace stats }
  - { id: zipf_s, value: 1.08, unit: exponent, confidence: low,
      source: key histogram 7d }   # DOMINANT ASSUMPTION
derivations:
  - { id: peak_rps, unit: req/second, value: 1860,
      formula: "dau*rpd/86400*peak_to_mean" }
  - { id: nodes_required, unit: pods, value: 32,
      formula: "ceil(peak*1.25/per_node_measured)" }
falsification:
  - { derived: nodes_required, owner: sre, review: 2026-11-14,
      measure: "p99 CPU at monthly peak",
      threshold: "sustained > 0.62 for 15 min" }
  - { derived: cache_hit_rate, owner: platform, review: 2026-09-14,
      measure: "redis evicted_keys_total rate",
      threshold: "> 0.5% of requests for 10 min" }

Validate against the assumption you are least sure of, not the one that is easiest to measure. If the Zipf exponent is the low-confidence input, the load test must generate that distribution at both the modelled exponent and a deliberately flatter one, and the capacity claim must hold at the flatter value. Load tests with uniform key distributions systematically overstate hit rate and are the usual reason a production cache behaves worse than its own design document predicted.

Exercises 3

  1. Build the complete capacity model for a service you know: peak rps from user counts, per-instance utilisation, node count including zone-loss headroom, origin egress, cache RAM from a measured key histogram, and database write rate with amplification. Every derivation names its unit and source. Deliverable is the YAML model with all six falsification measurements wired to a named owner.
  2. Redo the exercise 1 calculation twice, once entirely in per-second units and once entirely in per-day, and enumerate every decision the unit error would change. Deliverable is at least five decisions with wrong and right answers side by side, plus a paragraph arguing which unit each specific decision should use.
  3. Size a Redis 8 cluster for 41 million keys at 1.2 KB median value with a measured Zipf exponent. By simulation rather than assumption, produce the hit rate at 2, 5, 10 and 20 million resident keys, identify the count at which the curve falls below 96 %, then repeat at exponent 0.9 and quantify the extra RAM.
  4. For a three-zone 24-node deployment, compute headroom as per-node utilisation under four scenarios: healthy, one node lost, one rack lost, one zone lost. State which breaks, then find the minimum node count keeping every scenario under 80 % and express the incremental cost as a percentage.
  5. Write a load-test scenario for a service with a documented cache and a measured hot key such that a failure in the hot-key path produces a distinguishable signal within 60 seconds. Run it against staging, report the p99 ratio between healthy and cache-failed states, and set the alerting threshold on that ratio.

3 · Data Storage Engines and Their Trade-Offs

3.1 Choosing a storage engine3.2 Relational engines: PostgreSQL and MySQL3.3 Key-value and in-memory stores3.4 Document stores3.5 Wide-column and LSM engines3.6 Graph, vector and specialised engines

This section compares the storage engines available to a service in 2026 on the dimensions that decide the fit: access-pattern flexibility, write amplification, read amplification, the shape of the tail under load, and the operational burden of scale-out. The argument is that engine selection is not a feature checklist but a match between a measured access-pattern distribution and a data structure, and that most systems need exactly one transactional engine plus at most two specialised stores. Each engine’s failure modes are shown to be structural consequences of its data structure rather than implementation defects, so choosing a structure means accepting its failure modes.

3.1 Choosing a storage engine

This subsection establishes the decision procedure and the questions that must be answered before a choice is made.

Engine selection reduces to three questions, asked in order. First, what are the read access patterns — point reads, range scans, prefix scans, aggregations, or fuzzy retrieval? Second, what is the write shape — single-key update, append-only, blob-heavy, or multi-row transactional mutation? Third, what are the durability and consistency requirements, inherited from section 1 and not negotiable at this stage. The engine that wins is the one whose data structure matches the first two answers, subject to the third. A system needing both point reads and prefix scans is served well by neither a pure hash store nor a pure row store; it is served by an ordered key-value engine, which is why those exist. The third question is the one most often deferred until the design is already committed, and consistency requirements cannot be added later: a store with per-key linearisable writes and one with eventual replication are different products, and moving between them is a rewrite rather than a configuration change.

# The decision procedure, run against a concrete workload
# STEP 1. Access patterns (from ch0.4 instrumentation)
#   point_reads      71%   by PK
#   range_scans       9%   ORDER BY created_at LIMIT 20
#   prefix_scans      6%   LIKE 'user_42%' cross-entity listing
#   aggregations     11%   COUNT/SUM by tenant per hour
#   fuzzy_retrieval   3%   full-text + vector
# STEP 2. Write shape
#   single_key_update  64%  hot key present (p99 skew 380:1)
#   append_only         31%
#   multi_row_txn        5%  payments, inventory
# STEP 3. Hard requirements (from ch1)
#   RPO 0 on the txn class; 60 s on the append class
#   p99 < 30 ms point reads at 1 860 rps
#   consistent prefix scan ordering, no gaps or dupes
# VERDICT
#   primary   PostgreSQL 18  (txn class, point reads, ranges)
#   secondary Redis 8        (hot keys, 63% hit rate)
#   tertiary  wide-column    (fuzzy + rollups, 14% of requests)
#   blobs     object store with versioned keys
# REJECTED
#   document store: range and prefix need index discipline it
#     does not give; the 64% point-read class does not benefit
#   pure key-value: no multi-row transaction, RPO 0 unmet
#   graph: 2-hop maximum, not worth a second datastore
CriterionRelationalKey-valueDocumentWide-column
Point read by key✓ 0.3–2 ms✓ 0.1–0.5 ms✓ 0.3–1 ms✓ 0.2–1 ms
Range scan, ordered✓ native index✓ only if ordered△ index array✓ partition key
Arbitrary prefix scan△ btree on text✗ unless ordered△ index array△ clustering key
Multi-row ACID transaction✓ full serialisable✗ single key only△ single document✗ eventual by design
Ad-hoc join, 3+ tables✓ hash and merge join✗ application side✗ application side✗ application side
Ad-hoc aggregate at scale✗ full scan✗✗✓ precomputed rollups
Secondary indexes, many fields✓ cheap to add, write-costly✗△ per document△ sparse, LSM cost
Schema evolution△ migration required✓ free✓ free✓ free
Operational complexity✓ low, one system✓ low△ medium✗ compaction and repair

Do not adopt a distributed engine because the data is "large". A single PostgreSQL 18 instance handles 5–15 TB, 20–60 k writes per second, and 2 000 concurrent connections comfortably; a wide-column cluster handles the same data with several times the operational surface — compaction tuning, repair traffic, read-repair latency, and a failure mode where one node’s disk failure triggers a multi-hour read repair under load. Distributed engines are justified by write throughput beyond roughly 50 k wps or by the need to failover write capacity independently, not by volume alone.

3.2 Relational engines: PostgreSQL and MySQL

This subsection covers the operational parameters of the two dominant relational engines and when each is the better choice.

The choice between PostgreSQL 18 and MySQL 9.x is narrower than it was a decade ago and is decided by operational fit. PostgreSQL’s advantages are richer index access methods — BRIN, GIN, GiST, and vector indexes — MVCC without undo-log pressure, per-column storage, and the widest extension ecosystem. Its costs are optimiser plan regressions on skewed data and an autovacuum that must be tuned deliberately or it will lag behind a high-write workload. MySQL’s advantages are a simpler replication topology, InnoDB Cluster’s built-in group replication, and a predictable clustered-primary read path. Its cost is that a secondary-index read is covering-or-not, with an implicit clustered-index lookup that MySQL 9.x optimises less aggressively than PostgreSQL’s index-only scans. For both engines, the three parameters that decide the tail are the same: buffer pool sizing relative to the hot index, checkpoint and write-ahead log behaviour under write bursts, and autovacuum or purge lag relative to the write rate.

# PostgreSQL 18 -- the settings that decide the tail
shared_buffers = 64GB            # 25-40% of RAM, no more
effective_cache_size = 192GB     # planner hint, not an allocation
work_mem = 64MB                 # per sort/hash node
maintenance_work_mem = 2GB      # index builds, vacuum
max_connections = 400           # NOT 2 000: 2 000 * 64MB of
                               # work_mem can exhaust the host
random_page_cost = 1.1          # NVMe, not 4.0
effective_io_concurrency = 200
max_parallel_workers = 8
max_parallel_workers_per_gather = 2
wal_compression = zstd          # 3-5x WAL volume reduction
wal_writer_delay = 20ms
checkpoint_timeout = 15min
max_wal_size = 32GB             # space between checkpoints
checkpoint_completion_target = 0.9
# A 15 ms fsync plus a 5-40 ms stall at checkpoint
# completion is the standard cause of a p99.9 ten times the
# p99.9 of an identical system with max_wal_size 3x larger.
autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor = 0.02
autovacuum_analyze_scale_factor = 0.01
autovacuum_vacuum_cost_limit = 4000
autovacuum_max_workers = 5
ALTER TABLE events SET (autovacuum_vacuum_cost_delay = 0);
# The four queries that explain 80% of database p99 problems
-- 1. The ACTUAL plan, not the estimated one
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, WAL, SETTINGS)
  SELECT * FROM orders
  WHERE tenant_id = $1 AND status = 'open'
  ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20;
-- Look for: rows removed by filter; a heap fetch on an index
-- scan (not an index-only scan); estimated vs actual rows
-- differing by more than 100x. The last is a stale statistic
-- and the cheapest fix in the database.

-- 2. What the background is actually doing
SELECT relname, n_live_tup, n_dead_tup,
       round(100.0*n_dead_tup/NULLIF(n_live_tup+n_dead_tup,0),1)
         AS dead_pct,
       last_autovacuum, autovacuum_count
FROM pg_stat_user_tables
WHERE n_dead_tup > 10000 ORDER BY n_dead_tup DESC LIMIT 20;
-- dead_pct above 15% sustained means bloat is in the read
-- path and the index is now larger than the working set.

-- 3. Where the time goes, by statement
SELECT calls, mean_exec_time, total_exec_time, rows,
       shared_blks_hit, shared_blks_read, query
FROM pg_stat_statements
ORDER BY total_exec_time DESC LIMIT 20;
-- total_exec_time, not mean: a 2 ms query called 4 M times
-- costs more than a 200 ms query called 900 times.
ParameterPostgreSQL 18MySQL 9.xEffect of getting it wrong
Buffer poolshared_buffers 25–40 % RAMinnodb_buffer_pool_size 60–75 %Working set evicted; p99 climbs non-linearly
Read amplificationIndex-only with visibility mapCovering index, else clustered lookup2–4× random reads per query under skew
Write-ahead logwal_compression = zstdinnodb_redo_log_capacity 4–8 GBCheckpoint stall, p99.9 ten times p99.9
Bloat controlAggressive autovacuum factorsinnodb_purge_threads 4–8History list grows; purge never catches up
Group replicationPatroni, pg_auto_failoverInnoDB Cluster, built inManual failover, MTTR 5–30 min
Logical replicationPublications and subscriptionsROW binlog with GTIDCross-engine CDC becomes bespoke work
Semi-structured datajsonb, GIN, generated columnsJSON functions, functional indexesFiltering in the application, losing pushdown
Vector similaritypgvector, HNSW and IVFFlatVector type, no index in most buildsBrute-force scan, linear in corpus size

PostgreSQL 18's asynchronous I/O changes the shape of the tail, not the mean. With io_method = io_uring and a raised effective_io_concurrency, concurrent readers queue in the kernel rather than in the backend, so throughput under saturation improves substantially while p50 is unchanged. This changes capacity planning rather than latency planning: the node sustains more I/O operations before degrading, which moves the write-throughput ceiling and therefore moves the threshold at which distribution becomes necessary.

# MySQL 9.x -- the equivalent tuning surface
innodb_buffer_pool_size = 96G
innodb_buffer_pool_instances = 8     # 1 per 8G, avoids mutex
innodb_redo_log_capacity = 8G        # replaces log_file_size
innodb_flush_log_at_trx_commit = 1   # RPO 0; =2 halves fsyncs
                                     # and doubles the RPO
innodb_io_capacity = 4000
innodb_io_capacity_max = 8000
innodb_flush_neighbors = 0           # SSD: no read-ahead pairing
innodb_purge_threads = 8
innodb_adaptive_hash_index = OFF     # a documented source of
                                     # intermittent stalls
performance_schema = ON
slow_query_log = ON
long_query_time = 0.05
# innodb_redo_log_capacity, not innodb_log_file_size: the
# old variable needs a restart, and a redo log that is too
# small stalls every write burst until it is restarted.
# The two MySQL queries that find real problems
SELECT digest_text, count_star,
       sum_timer_wait/1e9/1e3 AS total_s,
       sum_rows_examined / NULLIF(count_star,0) AS rows_per_call
FROM performance_schema.events_statements_summary
WHERE schema_name = DATABASE()
GROUP BY digest_text ORDER BY total_s DESC LIMIT 10;
-- rows_per_call above ~100 means the statement is doing index
-- work it does not need. That ratio finds the missing index
-- faster than any other single query in the diagnostic set.
-- Cross-reference it with count_star: a high rows_per_call on
-- a statement called 40 times is noise; the same ratio on a
-- statement called 4 M times is 40% of your database CPU.
SELECT object_name, index_name, count_star,
       count_star / NULLIF(count_star - count_read, 0) AS reuse
FROM performance_schema.table_index_joins_usage
WHERE object_schema = DATABASE() ORDER BY reuse LIMIT 20;
-- reuse near 1.0: the index serves almost every query touching
-- the table, so extend it to cover more queries.
-- reuse near 0.05: a write-only tax, maintained on every
-- insert and read by almost nothing. Drop it. On an insert-
-- heavy table a 0.05 index can be 20% of total write cost.
-- Both views reset with TRUNCATE, so a weekly snapshot is
-- what makes the reuse ratio a trend rather than a lifetime.

3.3 Key-value and in-memory stores

This subsection covers in-memory key-value stores and the cluster-mode failure modes that differ fundamentally from the single-node case.

Key-value stores are the most used and most under-specified component in modern systems. The specification that matters is not the API but three parameters: value size distribution, access-pattern distribution, and durability requirement. A store holding 200-byte values accessed uniformly is a different product from one holding 40 KiB blobs accessed in a Zipfian pattern, and the second is where cluster mode and hash-slot migration become load-bearing. Memcached remains the better answer for the first case: no persistence, no replication, no clustering complexity, and a slab allocator that handles uniformly small values with lower overhead per byte. Redis 8's single-node model is single-threaded for command execution, so throughput is a function of one core and of value complexity rather than of server size — a 64-core machine running one Redis process is not faster than a 16-core machine. Cluster mode distributes across 16 384 hash slots with a gossip failure detector, and the operational consequence is that resharding, failover, and replica promotion are all asynchronous and none is atomic with respect to the client.

# Redis 8 -- hash slots and the client's duty
# 16 384 slots, CRC16(key) mod 16384. A multi-key operation
# is single-slot ONLY if all keys share a hash TAG:
#   SET user:42:profile  {...}    slot = CRC16("42")
#   SET user:42:settings {...}    slot = CRC16("42")
#   MGET user:42:profile user:42:settings  # ONE slot, atomic
#   MGET user:42:profile user:43:profile  # CROSSES, ERROR
# The hashtag is invisible in the key name and is the single
# most common cause of CROSSSLOT in production. Rule: hash on
# the ENTITY id, never the row id, or every multi-key
# operation on that entity becomes a client-side loop.
redis-cli --cluster create \
  10.0.1.11:6379 10.0.2.11:6379 10.0.3.11:6379 \
  10.0.1.12:6379 10.0.2.12:6379 10.0.3.12:6379 \
  --cluster-replicas 1 --cluster-yes
# Failover is NOT synchronous. The detector needs
# cluster-node-timeout (default 15 000 ms) without a PONG
# before a replica is promoted. In that window writes to the
# failed master error, and the promoted replica may be up to
# one replication offset behind, which is silent data loss.
# Redis 8 -- durability and safety settings
# min-replicas-to-write 1
# min-replicas-max-lag 2
#   Make the master REFUSE writes rather than acknowledge one
#   that a lagging replica may never receive. This trades
#   silent data loss for an availability error, which is the
#   right trade for a cache and the WRONG trade for a session
#   store. The same two settings, opposite verdicts, decided
#   by the durability requirement in the ch1.5 table.
#
# Durability, the three settings that matter
#   appendfsync always    RPO 0,         p99 write 8-15 ms
#   appendfsync everysec  RPO <= 1 s,    p99 write 0.2 ms
#   appendonly no         RPO unbounded, cache only
# Session tokens with a 1 s RPO are a security finding, not a
# tuning choice. Either keep a system of record, or accept
# always plus the latency, and state the cost in the SLO.
#
# Three settings that are not about durability but cause
# incidents anyway:
#   protected-mode yes   blocks remote CONFIG; leave on
#   rename-command       disable FLUSHALL and KEYS in prod;
#                        KEYS is O(n) and will block the single
