#                        command thread for seconds
#   timeout 0            set a value; an idle client holding a
#                        connection will never send a command
#                        and will never release the slot
PropertyRedis 8, single nodeRedis 8, clusterMemcached 1.6
Command threads1, plus I/O threads for reads1 per shard1 per thread, N threads
Max throughput80–150 k ops/s1.3–2.4 M ops/s at 150 shards~500 k ops/s per node
Max dataset~1 TB, RAM-bound~1 TB per shard~1 TB, RAM-bound
Optimal value size< 512 B< 512 B< 1 KiB
Ordered range scan✓ sorted sets, lex range✓ single slot only✗ no ordering
Prefix scan△ SCAN, constant per call△ per shard, merge in client✗ no
Transactions✓ MULTI and EXEC✗ cross-slot✗ no
PersistenceAOF and RDBPer shard✗ none
Eviction policies8, fine-grainedPer shardLRU only
ReplicationAsync, per-shard failoverAsync, 15 s detection✗ none

A cache holding objects the application cannot rebuild is a system of record wearing a disguise. Session state, rate-limit counters, idempotency keys, and one-time tokens are all commonly placed in Redis and all become correctness dependencies. The failure is not the choice of Redis but the absence of a stated durability requirement: a session store with appendfsync everysec silently invalidates a fraction of sessions every second, and a restart loses every session issued in the last second. Name each key’s durability requirement in the same table that names its TTL, and audit that table quarterly against what the code actually does.

# The four Redis diagnostics, and what each is telling you
# 1. Hit rate AS A RATE, not a lifetime counter
redis-cli --raw INFO stats | grep keyspace
# hits / (hits + misses). A LIFETIME hit rate is
# meaningless: 0.999 on a store that was 1.0 last month and
# 0.6 today. Export hits and misses as counters and compute
# the rate in Prometheus; alert on 5 minutes, not on a total.

# 2. Eviction pressure
redis-cli INFO stats | grep evicted_keys
# Must trend to ~0. A rising value means the ch2.4 working set
# was under-estimated. The fix is memory, not a different
# eviction policy.

# 3. Fragmentation: the silent memory leak
redis-cli INFO memory | grep -E 'used_memory_rss|fragmentation'
# ratio = RSS / used_memory. Above 1.5 with expiring keys
# means the allocator holds freed jemalloc arenas; fix with
# activedefrag yes or a rolling restart. Above 3.0 with no
# expirations means the key distribution is pathological, for
# example one multi-gigabyte hash.

# 4. Fork cost, the diagnostic nobody runs
redis-cli INFO persistence | grep -E 'latest_fork|rdb_'
# latest_fork_us above 200 000 means a background save stalls
# the event loop. On a 64 GB instance fork cost is
# proportional to the address space, so a 4 MB/s write rate
# against 64 GB can transiently double memory use through
# copy-on-write. Large instances need
# vm.overcommit_memory = 1 and huge pages off.

3.4 Document stores

This subsection covers the value document stores provide, the index discipline they require, and when they are the wrong answer.

The document model co-locates a variable field set with a document identifier, and its entire appeal is that it removes the schema migration step from the write path. That benefit is real and it is also the source of its characteristic failure: a query touching a field not covered by an index performs a collection scan, and the first such query is discovered under load rather than in review. Document stores therefore require an explicit rule that every field used in a query predicate must be indexed, plus an enforcement mechanism, because the rule is not otherwise maintainable. Multi-tenant document stores also force a specific trade: a shared collection with tenant_id in every index is cheaper to operate and supports unbounded tenant counts, while a collection per tenant gives natural isolation and key locality but makes the index count a function of the tenant count. At 3 000 tenants that design has 3 000 times the metadata, 3 000 times the index rebuild cost, and a background-thread scheduling problem. The crossover is roughly 200–500 tenants.

# Document model -- the discipline that makes it work
# RULE 1. every field in a query predicate is indexed
db.orders.createIndex({ tenantId: 1, createdAt: -1 },
                      { name: "tenant_created" });  # COMPOUND
# RULE 2. every query carries the tenant predicate FIRST
db.orders.find({ tenantId: t, status: "open" })
        .sort({ createdAt: -1 }).limit(20);
# A query missing the tenantId prefix cannot use the compound
# index and, in a shared collection, scans every tenant's
# documents. Enforce in the repository layer, not by habit.

# RULE 3. validate the schema at the boundary
db.createCollection("orders", {
  validator: { $jsonSchema: {
      bsonType: "object",
      required: ["tenantId", "userId", "total", "currency"],
      properties: {
        total:    { bsonType: "long", minimum: 0 },
        currency: { enum: ["GBP", "EUR", "USD"] },
        lines:    { bsonType: "array", maxItems: 500 } } } },
  validationLevel: "strict", validationAction: "error"
});
# Without a validator, "lines" silently becomes an object on
# one code path and an array on another, and the read code
# that assumes an array works right up until it does not.
# Document model -- size and durability rules
# RULE 4. cap the document size. 16 MB is the hard limit; the
# soft limit that matters is ~1 MB, above which:
#   - the working set no longer fits the wire cache
#   - a document can no longer be updated in place, so every
#     update rewrites the whole value and a full journal entry
#   - replay time per replica grows without bound
# Large attribute sets belong in a wide-column or blob store,
# referenced by id from the document. A 4 MB document updated
# once per second is 345 GB/day of journal traffic on a
# dataset of 200 documents.
#
# RULE 5. write concern is a per-operation decision
db.orders.updateOne({ _id: id }, { $inc: { revision: 1 } },
  { writeConcern: { w: "majority", j: true } });
# w:1 is the default and is NOT durable. j:true forces a
# journal flush on every write, adding 8-15 ms. For a ledger
# that is mandatory; for a session cache it is pure waste.
#
# RULE 6. collection per tenant, or shared?
#   < 200 tenants: collection per tenant. Natural key
#     locality, natural isolation, small index builds.
#   > 200 tenants: shared collection, tenant prefix on every
#     key AND every index, and a repository layer that
#     refuses a query without the tenant predicate.

Use jsonb in PostgreSQL before you add a second database. A jsonb column with a GIN index on jsonb_path_ops supports containment and key-existence queries at a fraction of the operational cost of a second engine, and it preserves the ability to join the document back to relational rows. The document store’s advantage — schema freedom — is available in the relational engine at the cost of automatic index discipline, which is a discipline problem rather than a capability problem.

3.5 Wide-column and LSM engines

This subsection covers log-structured merge engines, the compaction behaviour that determines their cost, and the read-repair failure mode unique to replicated LSM designs.

Log-structured merge engines trade read latency for write throughput by buffering writes in an in-memory table, writing sorted immutable runs to disk, and merging those runs in the background. The trade is total: writes are fast and sequential, reads are amplified by the number of overlapping runs, and total system cost is dominated by the bytes moved by compaction rather than by the bytes written by clients. A well-tuned levelled configuration has read amplification near 1 and write amplification of 10–30×; a tiered configuration reduces write amplification to around 5× at the cost of 10–30× read amplification, and requires a large block cache or a read-repair path to be usable. The characteristic failure is compaction debt: when the incoming write rate exceeds the compaction rate, the memtable fills faster than runs merge, run count grows, read amplification grows with it, and read latency degrades while write latency still looks normal. The system is simultaneously out of disk and slow, which is confusing until the right metric exists.

# Compaction is the cost centre. Size it from the write rate.
write_rate = 4 000 wps * 1.2 KiB / 1.2 (zstd) = 4.0 MB/s
levelled_W = 12          # bytes written per byte ingested
compaction = write_rate * levelled_W = 48 MB/s internal
# 48 MB/s * 86 400 * 7 days = 28.3 TiB of compaction traffic
# per week, on top of client writes. A 1 TB NVMe at 2 GB/s
# sequential clears 100 GB in 28 s, so the device is 1.15x
# oversubscribed before a client writes a byte.
# THEREFORE compaction is usually disk-bound, not CPU-bound,
# and the fix is a faster device, a lower levelled W (tiered
# plus a bigger cache), or less write volume.
#
# The three ratios to alert on, not the absolutes:
#  1. bytes_compacted / bytes_written should be ~= W,
#     measured weekly. A 3x drift means the key
#     distribution changed and hotspots are rewritten
#     repeatedly. THIS is the most common cause of a
#     "mysterious 10x write amplification" incident.
#  2. L0 file count against the L0 slowdown trigger. Above
#     the threshold for more than 10 minutes, write
#     admission is throttled, which reaches the client as
#     write latency rather than as compaction lag.
#  3. Age of the oldest un-compacted run. Above 4 hours a
#     full-node compaction is not keeping up and read
#     latency is about to degrade non-linearly.
# Read repair: the LSM failure mode with no relational analogue
# On a quorum read, replicas return their local version. If
# they disagree the coordinator must reconcile. Three choices,
# each with a cost:
#
#  1. Trust the highest timestamp, overwrite the others
#     cost: silently discards a concurrent write that had a
#           later CLOCK but an earlier VALUE -- a lost update
#           no client ever sees.
#     use for: metrics, counters, derived state.
#  2. Return the conflict to the client
#     cost: pushes merge semantics into every caller, and
#           clients surface the conflict to the user, so the
#           availability loss is real.
#     use for: collaborative editing, inventory decrement.
#  3. Reconcile deterministically with a CRDT
#     cost: 2-3x metadata growth for an LWW-registered set,
#           and merge semantics correct only for the
#           operations actually modelled.
#     use for: counters, sets, "last write" fields.
#
# Read repair is ASYNCHRONOUS and runs on the READ path. Under
# a partition, a hot key read on both sides repairs toward
# whichever side the reader was on, in BOTH directions,
# oscillating. The symptom is a value that changes between
# two reads with no intervening write, which users report as
# a bug and engineers spend days looking for in app code.

The right threshold for leaving a relational engine is write throughput, not data volume. Shard when sustained writes exceed roughly 50 k wps on a tuned primary, when the working set no longer fits in RAM and the query mix requires full scans, or when the blast radius of a maintenance window is unacceptable. Data volume is the weakest of the three signals, because a 4 TB table with a 20 GB hot working set and index-only access performs identically to a 40 GB table. Sharding is an expensive one-way door and the trigger should be a number you can watch rather than a size you can observe.

3.6 Graph, vector and specialised engines

This subsection covers the engines that exist for a single access pattern and the criteria for justifying a new datastore.

A specialised engine is justified when one of three conditions holds: the access pattern is unexpressible in the general-purpose engine at acceptable cost, the data model is deeply recursive and the general engine needs six self-joins for one question, or the per-entity volume is large enough that row overhead dominates. Everything else is operational cost paid for elegance. Vector search meets the second condition only above roughly a million vectors or below a 50 ms latency target, because below those thresholds a vector index in the primary database with a brute-force scan is faster than a network round trip to a separate service. Graph databases earn their place on multi-hop traversals and on recursive queries where the schema is itself the query; they lose on reporting over a wide row and on aggregate computation, and the most common production outcome is a graph database serving a two-hop lookup while three other stores serve the same data for reporting.

# Vector search: the decision, and the parameters that matter
# THRESHOLD: do not add a vector service below ~1 M vectors
# or above a 50 ms latency target. Brute-force cosine over
# 1 M x 768 float16 = 1.5 GB, which is ~40 ms of sequential
# memory bandwidth on one core -- FASTER than the network
# round trip to a separate service.
#
# Index choice: recall, latency, memory
#   HNSW   M=16, ef_construction=200
#     recall@10 0.95-0.99, memory 1.3-2x vectors
#     query cost logarithmic in N, dominated by random access
#     use when the corpus is static between rebuilds
#   IVFFlat, lists = sqrt(N)
#     recall@10 0.85-0.95, memory ~1x vectors
#     needs a full retrain when the corpus changes 2-3x
#     use when vectors arrive continuously
# When the embedding MODEL changes, an HNSW index is
# INVALID, not stale: the graph was built over old vectors.
# A new model means a full rebuild -- at 10 M x 768 that is
# 6-14 hours and double the storage bill meanwhile.
#
# The parameter that is always set wrong: ef_search
#   40  -> p99 ~15 ms, recall@10 0.94
#  100  -> p99 ~48 ms, recall@10 0.97
#  200  -> p99 ~110 ms, recall@10 0.985
# Raising ef_search to chase recall is the commonest cause of
# a vector search p99 regression. Set it from a measured
# recall-latency curve and alert on the curve, not on a
# scalar recall number.

A graph database is almost never a replacement for a relational one; it is a read model in front of one. The pattern that works is to keep the system of record relational, project adjacency into the graph store on the change stream, and accept eventual consistency with a lag equal to the stream’s consumer lag. Teams that adopt the graph as the system of record discover, usually during the first audit, that its transactional model cannot express the multi-row constraints the business actually has: uniqueness across a relationship, a total that must net to zero, an ordering constraint on a state machine.

Exercises 4

  1. Run the three-question procedure from 3.1 against a service you have operated, using measured access-pattern percentages rather than estimates. Produce the verdict, then deliberately invert the two highest-confidence answers and show which engine wins instead. Deliverable is a one-page comparison with the sensitivity of the verdict to each answer stated explicitly.
  2. Tune a PostgreSQL 18 instance for 8 000 wps, a 90 GB working set, and a twelve-hour nightly write burst. Set every parameter from first principles, run the four diagnostics from 3.2 before and after, and report which single change moved p99 the most and by how much. Include the plan output for the three heaviest statements in the appendix.
  3. Measure a Redis 8 or Memcached hit rate as a rate rather than a lifetime counter, then deliberately fill the working set past the memory limit and record the hit-rate curve, the eviction rate, and p99 as the hit rate degrades. Produce the alerting thresholds you would set on each of the three signals and justify each level.
  4. Build an LSM compaction cost model for a given write rate and value size, deriving bytes written per byte ingested from a measured compaction backlog, then size the device so compaction plus client writes fit within 70 % of sustained throughput. Repeat for tiered and levelled and state the read-latency cost of each in exchange for the storage saving.
  5. Design a vector search layer for a 4 M-document corpus at a 40 ms p99. Compare pure vector, hybrid lexical-plus-vector, and filtered-partition approaches, measuring recall@10 for each at the latency target. State which deployment the measurement justifies and what would have to change to justify a dedicated service.

4 · Caching, CDNs and Read Scalability

4.1 Where caches belong and what they may contain4.2 Cache layers and the read path through them4.3 Invalidation, TTLs and versioned keys4.4 Stampedes, negative caching and request coalescing4.5 CDN edge behaviour and cache keys4.6 HTTP caching semantics and conditional requests

This section covers the highest-leverage and most frequently misapplied scaling technique available to an online system. The argument is that a cache is not a performance optimisation but a staleness contract, and that every cache decision is a decision about how much staleness a class of read may tolerate and how that staleness is bounded when the invalidation channel fails. The section covers layer placement and the multiplicative arithmetic of hit rates, TTL discipline, five invalidation strategies compared, cache stampede and its four distinct mitigations, negative caching including the null-object trap, CDN edge behaviour with surrogate keys and origin shields, and the HTTP freshness model including conditional requests.

4.1 Where caches belong and what they may contain

This subsection establishes the admissibility test for cached data and the class of value that must never enter a cache.

A value may be cached if and only if it can be reconstructed from a source of truth that is still reachable, or if its loss is acceptable. Everything else — session state not otherwise reconstructible, an idempotency key, a rate-limit counter, a one-time token — is a cache with a correctness dependency, and the distinction matters because a cache’s operational contract quietly assumes it is safe to lose. The three questions to ask before caching any value are therefore: what is the source of truth, what is the maximum tolerable staleness, and what does the caller do when the value is absent. A value that fails the third question is a value whose absence is an error, and caching it converts a normal miss into a dependency failure. Placement follows from those answers: the cheapest correct cache is the one closest to the client, and the most expensive is the process-local map, because it cannot be invalidated centrally, its hit rate is per-instance rather than fleet-wide, and it multiplies memory cost by the instance count.

# The admissibility test, applied to candidate values
# ------------------------------------------------------------
value                 truth      max_stale  on_miss  admissible
----------------------------------------------------------------
product detail        primary    60 s       refetch  yes edge+origin
search results        indexer    5 min      refetch  yes edge
user profile          primary    30 s       refetch  yes origin
order status          primary    0          refetch  origin only,
                                                   no replica
inventory count       primary    0          wait/409 never cache;
                                                   reserve must be
                                                   a transaction
price at checkout     primary    0          refetch  never
session token         primary    0          re-auth  never
idempotency key       primary    7 d        refetch  never -- a
                                                   duplicate
                                                   capture is a
                                                   double charge
rate-limit counter    primary    0          n/a      never;
                                                   approximate
                                                   counts are fine
feature flag          config     60 s       refetch  yes everywhere
# The rule: admissible if reconstructible, or if loss is
# acceptable. A miss must be a normal, cheap event -- never an
# error the caller has to handle specially. If your on_miss
# column says "wait" or "409", the value does not belong in a
# cache, and the "refetch" answer for a zero-staleness value
# is only correct if the refetch is against the primary.
Cache layerHit rate contributionLatency savedInvalidation reachCost per entry
Client, browser or app0–40 %0 ms, offline capableOnly that clientZero, but unsynchronised
CDN edge60–95 % of reads20–120 msGlobal, per key or surrogate keyBilled per GB, no RAM
Reverse proxy, Varnish or Nginx10–40 %1–3 msOne instance, or via a shared storeRAM, ~1.2× value
Application L1 map2–15 %0.05–0.3 msOne process onlyRAM × instance count
Distributed cache, Redis 850–90 % of origin reads0.3–2 msFleet-wide, immediateRAM × 1.35, plus one hop
Read replicaNot a cache; a replica0–5 ms, or slowerReplication lag, 0.05–30 sA full storage copy

An in-process map multiplied by instance count is a memory leak with a hit-rate chart. A 32-instance deployment holding a 500 MB L1 cache is spending 16 GB on data that a single distributed cache would hold once, and each instance’s copy ages independently, so a redefinition invalidates on one instance and not on the other thirty-one. The visible symptom is a support ticket describing data that is correct on retry, which reads as a networking bug. Use the L1 map only for objects smaller than a few kilobytes whose lifetime is a single request, and keep the cacheable set in one tier that can be invalidated centrally.

4.2 Cache layers and the read path through them

This subsection describes the layered read path and shows how per-layer hit rates combine into a fleet hit rate.

Layered caching compounds multiplicatively and the arithmetic is unforgiving. With a 94 % edge hit rate and a 90 % hit rate for the requests that reach the origin, the effective hit rate is 0.94 + 0.06 × 0.90 = 0.994, and only 0.6 % of requests reach the database. The important consequence is that per-layer hit rates are not independent: adding a layer improves the next layer’s apparent hit rate by reducing its traffic, so a layer’s measured hit rate rises as the layer below it improves. This makes per-layer dashboards misleading, and the only figure that should be reported is the fraction of end-user requests that reach the origin. The cost side compounds too, and it is where layered caching usually fails inspection: a proxy-layer miss is not free, costing an extra network hop and an extra serialisation, and a distributed-cache miss costs a round trip plus the origin’s work.

# The two-layer version, and why layer count must shrink
#   edge 0.94        -> 0.060 reach the origin
#   proxy 0.55       -> 0.027 reach distributed
#   distributed 0.90 -> 0.0027 reach the database
#   effective hit rate = 99.73%
#
# But the per-request cost accounting is:
#   0.06 * (0.45 * 0.6 ms    proxy lookup on its miss
#          + 0.027 * 0.6 ms  distributed lookup
#          + 0.0027 * 4.0 ms database)
#     = 0.06 * (0.270 + 0.016 + 0.011) = 0.018 ms
#
# A layer that cuts traffic to 0.27% while adding 0.45 ms of
# its own lookup to 45% of arriving requests is usually a
# mistake: it has made the common case slower to improve an
# already-rare case.
#
# THE RULE: layer count must FALL as the downstream hit rate
# rises. Two layers are right when the downstream hit rate is
# poor and the upstream is good. Four are right when both are
# poor. Four with a 99% downstream hit rate is four layers of
# overhead doing nothing. Re-derive the layer count whenever a
# hit rate moves more than 10 points.
# Varnish 7 -- the read path, explicit
vcl 4.1;
backend origin {
    .host = "origin.internal";   .port = "8443";
    .connect_timeout = 200ms;    .first_byte_timeout = 3s;
    .between_bytes_timeout = 1s; .max_connections = 200;
    .probe { .url = "/healthz"; .interval = 5s; .timeout = 1s;
             .window = 8; .threshold = 3; }  # 3 of 8 -> down
}
sub vcl_recv {
    if (req.method != "GET" && req.method != "HEAD") {
        return (pass);
    }
    if (req.http.Authorization || req.http.Cookie) {
        return (pass);   # NEVER cache per-user responses in a
                         # shared cache. Not "vary by cookie":
                         # the key space explodes and the hit
                         # rate goes to zero.
    }
    if (req.url ~ "^/static/" || req.url ~ "\.(js|css|png)$") {
        return (hash);
    }
    return (pass);
}
# Varnish 7 -- coalescing, grace, and visible age
vcl 4.1;
backend origin { .host = "origin.internal"; .port = "8443"; }

sub vcl_backend_response {
    set beresp.ttl = 60s;
    if (beresp.status == 200 && bereq.url ~ "^/product/") {
        set beresp.grace = 300s;   # serve stale while one
        set beresp.keep  = 600s;   # thread fetches, others wait
        return (deliver);
    }
    if (beresp.status >= 500) {
        set beresp.ttl  = 5s;
        set beresp.grace = 30s;
    }
    return (deliver);
}
sub vcl_deliver {
    set resp.http.X-Cache-Age =
        round((now - obj.accessible) * 1000) + "ms";
    set resp.http.X-Cache-Status = obj.hits > 0 ? "HIT" : "MISS";
}
# keep is the coalescing mechanism and grace is the staleness
# mechanism; they are different and both are required. keep
# without grace makes concurrent requests WAIT, adding their
# latency to the p99; grace without keep makes them all
# REFETCH, which is a stampede. Set both, and set grace from
# the ch1.6 staleness budget rather than a round number.

Never cache a personalised response in a shared cache keyed on the cookie. The failure is a data leak, not a performance problem, and it is not prevented by Vary: Cookie — it is caused by it, because keying on the cookie turns one hot entry into one entry per session, so the hit rate collapses to near zero and the cache becomes a memory leak with the sensitivity of a user-data store. The correct mechanism is to strip the identifying part of the cookie before the cache key, or to make the URL or a header carry only the non-identifying variant. Anything else is a cross-user data leak waiting for the first user who notices.

4.3 Invalidation, TTLs and versioned keys

This subsection compares the invalidation strategies and establishes the rule that a TTL must exist even when invalidation is event-driven.

Five invalidation strategies exist and they are complementary rather than competing. Pure TTL is the baseline and the only one unconditionally correct, because it bounds staleness by construction without depending on any other system being up. Write-through and write-around reduce the TTL’s importance but do not remove it. Event-driven invalidation gives near-immediate consistency for a specific class of change and is the right answer for a read-your-write requirement, at the cost of a dependency on the message bus. Versioned key namespacing solves the problem the other three do not — invalidating a whole class of entries cheaply and without a scan — and its cost is a version lookup on the read path plus the loss of cache warmth across a deploy. Pure event-driven invalidation with no TTL is the failure mode: when the bus is down, entries never expire, and the system serves arbitrarily old data while reporting a healthy hit rate.

StrategyStaleness boundSafe when the bus is downCostUse for
Pure TTLExactly the TTL✓ AlwaysStorage equal to TTL window of writesBaseline, always on
Write-through0 for writes on this path✗ Cache goes staleWrite latency plus failure couplingSmall, hot, write-stable sets
Write-around, delete on writeRead-during-write race window✗ Stale until TTLA delete per write, a miss per readMost write-through caching over a database
Event-driven purgeBus latency, typically 20–500 ms✗ Stale until TTLBus dependency, purge fan-outRead-your-write classes
Versioned key namespacingVersion-lookup latency, 0–5 s✓ Old version still servedA version read per requestBulk invalidation, deploys, migrations
 
# Versioned key namespacing -- the invalidation that scales
# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Writer side, inside the same transaction as the data change
def bump_version(namespace, db):
    row = db.execute(
        "UPDATE cache_version SET v = v + 1 "
        "WHERE ns = %s RETURNING v", (namespace,))
    return row[0][0]          # 17, 18, 19 ...

# Reader side: one cheap lookup, itself cached for 2 s
def cache_key(namespace, ident, version):
    return f"{namespace}:v{version}:{ident}"

def get_product(pid, db, redis):
    v = get_version("product", db, redis)     # 2 s TTL
    k = cache_key("product", pid, v)
    hit = redis.get(k)
    if hit:
        return hit
    row = db.execute("SELECT * FROM product WHERE id = %s", (pid,))
    if not row:
        return None                # see 4.4: negative caching
    redis.setex(k, 300, serialize(row))       # TTL ALWAYS set
    return row
# What it buys: "all product pages are stale" becomes one
# UPDATE, after which 4 200 000 entries are unreachable. No
# SCAN, no DEL loop, no 4.2 M delete round trips. The cost is
# that every key changes name at once, so the cache is cold
# for one TTL: acceptable when the data is wrong, not
# acceptable as a routine deployment step.

A cache with no TTL is a cache with an unbounded correctness risk. Event-driven invalidation makes this feel safe right up until the day the message bus, the change-data-capture pipeline, or the purge worker is down — during an incident, when the bus is busiest — and the cache silently serves data from before the last known-good state. The failure is invisible: the hit rate looks excellent, the latency looks excellent, and the data is wrong. Every cache layer must carry a TTL for the classes where staleness is bounded, and the TTL must be short enough that the bus-outage window is tolerable rather than unbounded. If a value genuinely cannot be stale, it does not belong in a cache at all.

4.4 Stampedes, negative caching and request coalescing

This subsection covers the cache stampede, its four distinct mitigations, and negative caching including the null-object trap.

A cache stampede occurs when an entry expires or is evicted and the concurrent requests that miss it all proceed to the origin simultaneously. At 1 860 rps with a 300-second TTL, an entry requested at 6 rps expires and attracts roughly 6 concurrent misses; on a hot key requested at 300 rps it attracts 300. The origin sees a 50× amplification of one logical read, and because the misses all happen at once they align with any other expiry in the same shard, producing correlated load rather than independent spikes. The mitigation set is four distinct mechanisms that are frequently confused with one another: request coalescing, a distributed lock, probabilistic early expiry, and stale-while-revalidate with jittered TTLs. Negative caching is the same problem in a different guise and carries its own trap: a request for a non-existent key is a miss, it goes to the origin, the origin returns nothing, nothing is cached, and the next request repeats the origin call.

# Request coalescing: local single flight
# Concurrent misses for the same key become ONE origin call.
# Necessary but NOT sufficient: with 32 instances a hot key
# still produces up to 32 origin calls, and if instances
# autoscale to 200 the amplification is 200x.
class SingleFlight:
    def __init__(self):
        self.calls = {}          # key -> Future

    def do(self, key, fn):
        fut = self.calls.get(key)
        if fut is None:
            fut = Future()
            self.calls[key] = fut
            try:
                fut.set_result(fn())          # the ONE origin call
            except Exception as e:
                fut.set_exception(e)
                raise
            finally:
                del self.calls[key]           # key removal IS the
                                              # lock release; in
                                              # finally, or a failed
                                              # call wedges the key
        return fut.result()                   # others await
# Request coalescing: fleet-wide, via a short-lived lock
def get_with_coalescing(redis, key, origin, ttl_s=300):
    v = redis.get(key)
    if v is not None:
        return decode(v)
    token = uuid4().hex
    # SET NX EX: a single winner, and the lock self-expires so
    # a crashed holder cannot wedge the key forever.
    if redis.set(f"lock:{key}", token, nx=True, ex=2) is None:
        # Not the winner. Wait BRIEFLY, then fall back to the
        # origin. The fallback is deliberate: a brief
        # over-fetch beats a 2-second stall, and it bounds the
        # damage if the winner died.
        for wait in (0.02, 0.05, 0.10, 0.20):
            sleep(wait)
            v = redis.get(key)
            if v is not None:
                return decode(v)
        return origin()            # over-fetch, not a stall
    try:
        val = origin()
        redis.setex(key, ttl_s, encode(val))
        return val
    finally:
        # Release only if we still hold it, or we delete a lock
        # a successor has since taken.
        lua = "if redis.call('get',KEYS[1])==ARGV[1] then " \
              "return redis.call('del',KEYS[1]) else return 0 end"
        redis.eval(lua, 1, f"lock:{key}", token)
# Negative caching, and the null-object trap
# A missing key and a cached empty value are DIFFERENT, and
# the encoding must survive every layer to the database.
# WRONG: cache.setex(key, 30, "") -- some serialisers drop
#       it, some clients treat "" as absent, and a key
#       expiring and an empty value become indistinguishable.
# RIGHT: wrap everything.
NULL = b"\x00N"      # cannot collide with any serialised
                     # payload, which always starts "{"
def cache_get(redis, key):
    raw = redis.get(key)
    if raw is None:
        return MISS
    if raw == NULL:
        return None        # a cached NEGATIVE result
    return decode_payload(raw)

def cache_put(redis, key, value, ttl_s):
    if value is None:
        redis.setex(key, 30, NULL)     # short TTL: 5-30 s
    else:
        redis.setex(key, ttl_s, encode_payload(value))
MitigationAmplification floorLatency added on missFailure mode
NoneConcurrent request count on the key0Origin overload, cascading timeout, retry storm
Jittered TTL1 per TTL window, amortised0None; a hot key still renews at 63 % of its TTL
Probabilistic early expiry~1 per renewal cycle0, if the refresh is asynchronousRefresh traffic even when the entry is hot
Local single flightInstance count, e.g. 32×0None; insufficient alone above ~20 instances
Fleet single flight via lock1 per key, plus a 2 s lock-TTL tail0–200 ms on a contended missHolder crash: 2 s of over-fetch, then fallback
Stale-while-revalidate with grace1 per refresh, stale served meanwhile0Data can be ttl + grace old; must be stated in ch1.6
 
# The complete stampede-safe read path
import math, random, threading

def should_refresh(age_s, ttl_s, delta=2):
    """XFetch: renew when delta*log(U) <= -ttl, i.e. with
    probability 1/delta per request. With delta=2 and a 300 s
    TTL each entry refreshes at a random point in its last
    150 s, so the 300 requests that would have hit the expiry
    instant are spread over 150 s and at any moment roughly
    ONE is refreshing."""
    return delta * math.log(random.random()) <= -ttl_s

def get(redis, key, origin, ttl_s=300):
    v = redis.get(key)
    if v is None:
        val = origin()                  # cold: coalesce it
        cache_put(redis, key, val, ttl_s)
        return val
    left = redis.ttl(key)               # integer seconds

4.5 CDN edge behaviour and cache keys

This subsection covers edge cache keys, surrogate keys for bulk purge, and the origin shield that prevents an edge miss storm.

The cache key determines both the hit rate and the correctness of a CDN, and the two decisions are usually conflated. What goes in the key — host, path, query string, selected headers, and sometimes a user or device token — determines the key space, and the key space determines the hit rate. A key that includes the full query string of a product listing with 40 filter parameters has a hit rate near zero; a key that omits a parameter that changes the response is a data leak. The safe procedure is to enumerate every query parameter that changes the response, include all of them, and then measure whether the resulting key space is too sparse — in which case the fix is canonicalisation, not omission. Bulk purge is the second edge problem: deleting a million objects by key is 10⁶ API calls or a prefix purge that not all providers support.

# Edge cache key, canonicalised. What is and is not in it.
# ------------------------------------------------------------
# IN the key:
#   scheme, host, path
#   every query parameter that changes the response
#   Accept-Encoding ONLY if the origin varies the body by it
#   country or device class ONLY if the origin varies by it
#   an authenticated user token ONLY on a per-user route, and
#     then only on a route that must not be shared
#
# OUT of the key, and why:
#   all cookies   -> one entry per session, hit rate ~0, and
#                   any per-user data becomes a cross-user leak
#   utm_*, _ga, fbclid
#                 -> canonicalise away, or every campaign
#                    fragment is a permanently cold key
#   the response body hash -> not a request property
#   Authorization on a public route
#                 -> if the route ignores identity, ignoring
#                    the header is correct AND cheap
#
# Canonicalisation is a correctness operation, not a
# performance one: two spellings of the same request that the
# origin treats identically must land on ONE key.
#   /p?page=1          and  /p?page=01   -> same page
#   /p?sort=n&page=2  and  /p?page=2&sort=n -> same
#   /p                 and  /p/          -> usually the same
# Normalise in the edge rewrite, not in application code, or
# the CDN and the origin disagree about the key space.
# Origin shield, and the edge configuration that matters
# An origin shield is a tier of caches between the edge and
# the origin. Without it an edge MISS means an origin hit, and
# a popularity event makes every edge miss at once: 300 PoPs x
# 1 miss = 300 origin requests for one object. With a shield,
# the first edge miss populates the shield and the other 299
# hit the shield.
upstream shield {
    hash $request_uri consistent;   # sticky per URL, so all
                                   # edges for one object land
                                   # on the same shield node
    server shield-1:8080;
    server shield-2:8080;
    server shield-3:8080;
    keepalive 1024;
}
proxy_cache_path /var/cache/nginx levels=1:2 keys_zone=sh:200m
                 max_size=2000g inactive=24h use_temp_path=off;

An edge with no origin shield turns a popularity event into an origin outage. A product page that goes from 10 rps to 100 000 rps in nine minutes — a front-page placement, a link on a large forum, a bot loop — produces one miss per edge location simultaneously, and 300 points of presence become 300 simultaneous origin requests per object. The origin sees a 30 000× amplification of a single logical read, exhausts its connection pool, and the misses that were going to be answered become timeouts. The fix is structural rather than tuning-based: a shield tier, or aggressive early-expiry renewal on the object before the spike, and the second only works if the object was already hot enough to be renewing before the spike arrived.

4.6 HTTP caching semantics and conditional requests

This subsection covers the HTTP freshness model, ETag validation, and the traps in each directive.

HTTP’s caching model has two independent halves that are frequently conflated. Freshness is a function of Cache-Control alone: the response is considered fresh for the stated lifetime without contacting anyone, which is cheap and lossy. Revalidation is a function of the validator: the client or cache sends If-None-Match with an ETag or If-Modified-Since with a timestamp, and the origin returns 304 if the representation is unchanged, which costs a request but no body. A system that sets a long freshness lifetime has no invalidation mechanism until the lifetime expires; a system that sets a short one is doing full revalidation constantly. Choosing between them is choosing between a cheap stale window and an expensive fresh one, and the answer is usually a moderate lifetime plus ETag revalidation for the paths that matter. The traps are specific and each has been a production incident.

# HTTP caching: the directives and what they actually mean
# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Cache-Control: public      shareable by any cache. Required
#                            on anything behind a CDN.
#                private     no shared cache may store it.
#                            The default for a response with
#                            Set-Cookie if unspecified.
#                no-cache     STORABLE but must be
#                            revalidated before reuse. NOT
#                            "do not cache". The commonest
#                            misreading in HTTP.
#                no-store     no cache may store it at all.
#                max-age=N    freshness in the CLIENT cache.
#                s-maxage=N   freshness in SHARED caches; takes
#                            precedence over max-age. This is
#                            the one that matters behind a CDN.
#                must-revalidate  never serve stale, ever.
#                stale-while-revalidate=N
#                            serve stale for N while one
#                            request refreshes. THIS IS THE
#                            STAMPEDE FIX, and it is an HTTP
#                            directive, not a proxy extension.
#                immutable    never revalidate; for
#                            content-hashed asset names.
# Example, with every number a ch1.6 staleness bound:
#   public, max-age=0, s-maxage=300,
#   stale-while-revalidate=60, stale-if-error=86400
#   browser revalidates every time, the edge holds 5 minutes
#   and may serve 60 s stale while refreshing, and may serve
#   up to 1 day stale if the origin errors.
# Vary traps, and ETag -- the two things that go wrong
# Vary: Accept-Encoding   correct, 2-4 variants
# Vary: Accept-Language   10-30, correct for localised pages
# Vary: User-Agent        20-200 variants, usually wrong
# Vary: Cookie            one variant per session: the kill.
#                         NEVER on a shared cache.
# A Vary on a header the cache does not normalise is a silent
# hit-rate destroyer, invisible because the cache reports HIT
# for each variant it stored.
#
# ETag: a weak validator is cheap and sufficient for caching
ETag: W/"a1b2c3d4-4096-v3"      # weak, for revalidation
ETag: "a1b2c3d4-4096"           # strong, required for ranges
def make_etag(row):
    return 'W/"%d-%d-%d"' % (row.updated_at, row.byte_size,
                              row.schema_version)
# Do NOT emit a strong ETag on a dynamically generated body:
# it forces the body to be hashed on every request, which
# defeats the purpose. The weak form above is free.
#
# THE TRAP: a correct response that is not byte-identical
# between origin and edge produces a new ETag per request, so
# every revalidation returns 200 with a full body and the 304
# path is never taken. Causes:
#   - a body containing a request id or a date

Measure the origin arrival rate, not the hit rate, and make it an SLO. The number that matters is requests reaching the origin per second, and it should have a target derived from the origin’s cost per request. A hit rate is a ratio between two numbers that both move: a cache can show 95 % while origin traffic doubles, and the ratio hides it. Expressing the target as "no more than 12 origin requests per second for reads" is checkable, alerts cleanly when a layer’s hit rate degrades, and maps directly onto the origin’s capacity from section 2. It also makes the correct response obvious: when origin arrival rate rises, fix the layer below the one whose hit rate looks best.

Exercises 5

  1. Apply the admissibility test from 4.1 to every value cached anywhere in a system you have operated. For each, name the source of truth, the maximum tolerable staleness, the behaviour on a miss, and whether the value is a correctness dependency. Produce a corrected configuration for every value that failed the test, including the negative-caching TTL where the failure was an unbounded miss loop.
  2. Measure the distinct cache-key count per route per hour for 24 hours on a system with a CDN, and compute the theoretical maximum hit rate for each route from the access frequency distribution. Identify the three routes with the largest gap between achievable and actual hit rate, and for each state whether the fix is key canonicalisation, a different cache layer, or acceptance that the route is not cacheable.
  3. Build a load test that provokes a cache stampede on a single key at 300 rps with a 60-second TTL, with coalescing disabled. Record the origin request amplification, then enable local single flight and measure again, then add fleet-wide coalescing and measure a third time. Report the three amplification figures and the p99 for each, and state which mitigation you would ship and why.
  4. Implement versioned key namespacing for one namespace of 4 million keys with a dual-version read path, then measure the cold-cache window during a version bump with and without dual-version promotion. State the maximum staleness introduced by each approach in the units of the section 1.6 requirement, and justify the choice against that requirement.
  5. Take a route and instrument it to count distinct ETag values per resource over 100 requests. If more than one appears, identify the non-determinism in the response and eliminate it, then measure the change in 304 rate and in origin bytes sent. Separately, write the full Cache-Control and Vary headers for a personalised page, a static asset, and a product price, and justify each directive against the staleness budget.

5 · Load Balancing, Reverse Proxies and Routing

5.1 Layer 4, layer 7 and where the boundary sits5.2 Selection algorithms and their behaviour under skew5.3 Health checks, ejection and slow starts5.4 Session affinity, and why to avoid it5.5 DNS-based routing, TTLs and health5.6 Service discovery and configuration propagation

This section covers the component that every request passes through and that most teams under-instrument. The argument is that routing is where a system’s failure semantics are decided: the selection algorithm determines whether a failure concentrates or spreads, the health check determines what "failed" means and how quickly it is noticed, and the timeout configuration determines whether a slow dependency becomes an outage. The section compares layer 4 and layer 7 balancing with the specific cost each imposes, works through the selection algorithms including the skew behaviour of least-connections under unequal server cost, and covers slow starts, session affinity, DNS-based routing and its propagation delay, and service discovery with its watch-versus-poll trade-off.

5.1 Layer 4, layer 7 and where the boundary sits

This subsection compares layer 4 and layer 7 balancing on cost, capability, and the failure semantics each produces.

Layer 4 balancing operates on a connection: the load balancer terminates the TCP handshake, selects a backend for the connection, and forwards packets without inspecting HTTP. It cannot read a header, so it cannot route on path, cookie, or method. Its advantage is cost — a connection is a few kilobytes of state and forwarding is a memory copy — so a small instance handles hundreds of thousands of connections per second and is essentially immune to the slowloris class of attack that consumes an application server’s threads. Its limitation is that every connection is pinned to one backend for its lifetime, so HTTP/1.1 keep-alive and HTTP/2 multiplexing make the connection count a poor proxy for load, and a single long-lived HTTP/2 connection can carry a disproportionate share of traffic to whichever backend it was assigned.

Layer 7 balancing reads the request, so it can route on path, host, header, or cookie, and it can perform TLS termination, compression, and response caching at the edge. Its cost is roughly 20–50× that of layer 4 for the same throughput, and the cost is paid in CPU that competes with the same silicon the balancer is trying to protect. The practical boundary is therefore a traffic split rather than a choice: use layer 4 for bulk data — media, large uploads, long-lived streams — and layer 7 for the request traffic where routing decisions and caching matter. A single layer 7 balancer for all traffic is a design decision made for simplicity and paid for in the p99 of the media path.

# The decision, as a traffic split rather than a choice
# ------------------------------------------------------------
# layer 4  (Envoy, HAProxy in tcp mode, an NLB, an L4 LB)
#   cost        0.02-0.10 ms added, ~1-3 vCPU for 100k conn/s
#   can do      connection routing, source affinity, PROXY
#               protocol, TLS passthrough, WebSocket upgrade
#   cannot do   path routing, header routing, caching,
#               compression, rate limiting by route
#   use for     media, uploads, gRPC over HTTP/2, anything
#               where the connection count equals the request
#               count or where the body dominates
#
# layer 7  (Envoy, HAProxy in http mode, Nginx, Varnish)
#   cost        0.15-0.60 ms added, 3-8 vCPU for 20k rps
#   can do      everything above, plus per-route policy,
#               retries, outlier detection, response headers
CapabilityLayer 4Layer 7Consequence of choosing wrongly
Added latency0.02–0.10 ms0.15–0.60 msAt 20 hops, L7 adds 12 ms of pure overhead
Throughput per 4 vCPU300 k conn/s25–60 k rpsL7 needs 6× the fleet for the same traffic
Route by path or header✗✓L4 forces a split by port or by DNS name
TLS termination✗ passthrough only✓L4 pays the TLS CPU in the application
Response caching✗✓See ch4: a layer 4 tier needs a separate cache
Per-route rate limiting✗✓L4 limits per connection, which is meaningless
Retries with idempotency✗✓Retries must live in the application instead
Slowloris exposure✓ immune✗ must be mitigatedL7 with no timeouts is a trivial DoS target
HTTP/2 multiplexing✗ pinned to one backend✓ spread by requestL4 produces a 100:1 skew that looks like 1:1
Per-connection stateYes, cheapYes, expensiveMemory per connection differs by 20×

Terminate TLS once, at the outermost layer, and never re-encrypt inward for routing reasons. Each additional hop that terminates TLS costs a full handshake or a resumed handshake plus the symmetric cost of the connection state, and an environment where the internal hop re-encrypts for a routing header is an environment where the internal certificates are a second secret to rotate. Where mutual TLS is genuinely required at an internal boundary, use a passthrough L4 hop with client certificate validation at the backend rather than a second full proxy hop — the balancer then forwards the already-authenticated connection without ever holding the private key.

# HAProxy 3.0 -- the two tiers, showing the traffic split
# ------------------------------------------------------------
# FRONTEND: layer 4, TLS passthrough. Bulk data, media, and
# anything whose connection count is a fair proxy for load.
frontend media_l4
    bind 0.0.0.0:443
    mode tcp
    option tcplog
    tcp-request inspect-delay 5s
    tcp-request content accept if { req_ssl_hello_type 1 }
    # Pass the client IP and the SNI so the backend can serve
    # the right certificate without terminating here.
    tcp-request content set-var(txn.sni) req_ssl_sni
    default_backend media_origin

backend media_origin
    mode tcp
    balance leastconn
    # PROXY protocol preserves the client address through the
    # L4 hop so the L7 tier behind it still sees it.
    server m1 10.0.1.11:443 check send-proxy-v2 inter 2s

5.2 Selection algorithms and their behaviour under skew

This subsection compares the selection algorithms and shows which ones break when backend cost or request cost is unequal.

Round-robin distributes perfectly only when every backend is equally capable and every request costs the same, and both are usually false. Weighted round-robin corrects for capability but not for cost: a route that takes 300 ms per request and a route that takes 5 ms receive the same share of connections, so the slow route’s queue grows until it is the bottleneck. Least-connections corrects for this, because a slow server accumulates connections and stops receiving new ones, which is the correct behaviour — but it is reactive, so during the interval between a server becoming slow and its connection count rising, the balancer keeps sending it traffic. Least-response-time is the fastest-converging option and is the right default when backend cost varies, at the cost of requiring per-request timing at the balancer.

The failure mode shared by all of them is that they balance connections or requests rather than work. A request that consumes 40 ms of backend CPU and one that consumes 2 ms are treated identically by round-robin and by least-connections, and the only algorithm that sees the difference is one that measures time. This is why per-route backend pools, even when the routes share the same physical servers, are the most reliable form of load isolation: they prevent a cheap route from filling the queue of an expensive one.

AlgorithmState neededBehaviour with unequal backend costBehaviour on a failing backendUse when
Round robinNoneBad: a slow backend is given equal shareChurns the next N requests before ejectionUniform backends, batch traffic
Weighted round robinStatic weightsBetter, but weights are guesswork and go staleSame churn, scaled by weightKnown capacity tiers, static fleet
Least connectionsOne counter per backendGood, but reactive: a 1-second stall still receives trafficStops within one poll intervalGeneral default for mixed request cost
Least response timeEWMA of latency per backendBest: converges in one EWMA windowFastest, and weights by observed latencyLatency targets, heterogeneous routes
Least bandwidthRate counter per backendGood for transfer-bound workSlow to notice, as I/O is burstyMedia, large payloads
Consistent hashRing per backend setWeighted variants need per-request cost inputRemaps only the affected key rangeCache affinity, sharded backends
Power of two choicesTwo random samplesGood approximation, O(log N) per pickOne of two picks may still failVery large fleets, minimal state
RandomNonePoor, but unbiased in expectationOne request per failure, on averageLoad testing, not production
 
# Least response time, the default, with a percentile rather
# than a mean. Implemented on an EWMA per backend.
# ------------------------------------------------------------
class LeastResponseTime:
    def __init__(self, backends, alpha=0.15, max_score_ms=2000):
        self.b = {b: EWMA(None, alpha) for b in backends}
        self.max_score_ms = max_score_ms   # a floor, not a cap:
                                         # a backend with no data
                                         # scores 0 and gets 100%
                                         # of traffic until its
                                         # first sample lands

    def pick(self, healthy):
        # Probe two at random and take the better: O(log N) and
        # statistically indistinguishable from a full scan for
        # N beyond about 30 backends. (Power of two choices.)
        cands = random.sample(healthy, 2) if len(healthy) > 2 \
                else healthy
        return min(cands, key=lambda b: self.b[b].value(
                                            self.max_score_ms))

    def observe(self, backend, latency_ms, ok):
        # Decay the score on failure so a recovered backend
        # earns traffic back gradually rather than instantly.

Balance per route, not per connection, and give each route its own pool when the cost differs by more than 5×. A pool mixing a 2 ms endpoint with a 300 ms endpoint forces one algorithm to serve two incompatible objectives, and whichever objective loses does so invisibly in the aggregate p99. Separate pools cost connections and memory — a few hundred per pool at typical settings — and buy per-route SLOs that can be stated and alerted independently. The diagnostic that finds the cases worth splitting: group routes by their measured p50 into bands, and treat every band boundary as a candidate pool boundary.

# Consistent hashing, for cache and shard affinity. The version
# that is actually used in production: virtual nodes.
# ------------------------------------------------------------
import bisect, hashlib

class ConsistentHash:
    """h virtual nodes per physical node, so that a node of
    different weight takes a proportional share of the ring.

    With h = 100 and 6 nodes, the coefficient of variation of
    the per-key load is about 1/sqrt(h * 6) = 4%. With h = 1
    it is about 41%, and with 6 nodes and h = 1 several keys
    will be 3x the mean. h is the whole game."""

    def __init__(self, nodes, h=100):
        self.h = h
        self.ring = []                     # sorted (hash, node)
        for n in nodes:
            for i in range(h):
                digest = hashlib.sha256(
                    f"{n}#{i}".encode()).digest()
                self.ring.append((int.from_bytes(digest[:8],
                                                 "big"), n))
        self.ring.sort()
        self.hashes = [x[0] for x in self.ring]

5.3 Health checks, ejection and slow starts

This subsection covers the three dimensions of a health check, the ejection state machine, and the parameters that make failover either fast or destructive.

A health check has three independent dimensions and systems that conflate them fail in predictable ways. Liveness asks whether the process is running and can answer at all. Readiness asks whether it can serve traffic now — which fails during warm-up, during a dependency outage, and while draining. Startup asks whether initialisation has completed. A single endpoint conflating the three produces the classic failure where a slow-starting instance is killed by the liveness probe for being slow, restarting forever. The three must be separate endpoints with separate thresholds and separate timings, and the liveness probe must be cheap enough that it cannot itself become a load problem.

Ejection is a state machine with three states — healthy, suspect, ejected — and the transition parameters determine whether the balancer is resilient or flapping. Too eager and a momentary GC pause ejects a good node, redistributing load to nodes that are then themselves loaded, and the fleet oscillates. Too reluctant and a genuinely dead node receives traffic until the failure count crosses the threshold. The standard resolution is a rising ejection that escalates through passives, ignores, and then ejection over successive intervals, plus a quarantine on re-admission so a recovered node is given 10 % of traffic before rejoining fully.

# Kubernetes 1.33 -- three probes, three purposes
# ------------------------------------------------------------
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata: { name: checkout }
spec:
  replicas: 32
  template:
    spec:
      terminationGracePeriodSeconds: 45   # see below
      containers:
        - name: app
          image: checkout:2026.08.14
          ports: [{ containerPort: 8080 }]
          # STARTUP: bounded, and only during initialisation.
          # initialDelaySeconds is deliberately 0 because the
          # startup probe replaces the need for a fixed delay.
          startupProbe:
            httpGet: { path: /healthz/startup, port: 8080 }
            periodSeconds: 2
            failureThreshold: 30      # 60 s to become ready
          # READINESS: fails when the instance cannot serve.
          # A dependency outage MUST fail readiness, or the
          # balancer keeps sending traffic to an instance that
          # will time out on every request.
          readinessProbe:
            httpGet: { path: /healthz/ready, port: 8080 }

# The probe handlers, and what each must actually do
# ------------------------------------------------------------
GET /healthz/live      -- is the process healthy?
#   200 if the event loop is responsive and the process has
#   not exhausted memory. It must NOT check a database, a
#   cache, or a downstream service. It must NOT be slow: a
#   probe that costs 50 ms at 0.5 Hz per instance is free, but
#   a probe that loads a 4 GB table is not.
#   If you need to know about a dependency, that is a
#   separate metric and a separate alert, not a liveness probe.

GET /healthz/ready     -- should this instance get traffic?
#   200 only if ALL of:
#     - initialisation complete (config loaded, migrations
#       verified, caches warm enough to be useful)
#     - connection pools have capacity
#     - the primary database is reachable AND accepting
#     - the circuit breaker on each hard dependency is CLOSED

# Envoy 1.34 -- outlier detection and slow start
# ------------------------------------------------------------
clusters:
  - name: checkout
    connect_timeout: 250ms
    type: ROUND_ROBIN
    lb_policy: least_request
    common_http_protocol_options:
      max_requests_per_connection: 0   # 0 = unlimited
    outlier_detection:
      # Consecutive 5xx or gateway errors.
      consecutive_5xx: 5
      interval: 2000ms        # base_ejection_time x 3 = 30 s
      base_ejection_time: 10s
      max_ejection_percent: 50    # NEVER eject more than half
                                    # the fleet: ejecting all
                                    # turns a partial failure
                                    # into a total one
      # The SUCCESS-RATE criterion, which catches what
      # consecutive errors cannot: a backend that is up, not
      # erroring, and simply too slow.
      success_rate:

A liveness probe that checks a dependency turns a dependency incident into a fleet-wide restart storm. This is the single most common self-inflicted outage in containerised systems, and it is always well-intentioned: someone made the liveness endpoint check the database so that an instance unable to reach it would be recycled. The result is that when the database has a 30-second hiccup, every instance in the fleet restarts simultaneously, and because a restarting instance holds no warm cache, no JIT, and no connection pool, the database’s recovery is now competing with 32 cold starts. The correct division is that liveness checks only what a restart would fix, readiness checks what a restart would not, and the two together are sufficient for every realistic dependency failure.

5.4 Session affinity, and why to avoid it

This subsection covers session affinity mechanisms, the state they hide, and the cases where they are nonetheless correct.

Session affinity — cookie-based or IP-hash-based stickiness — is a solution to a problem that has better solutions, and adopting it makes three things worse at once. It makes the load distribution worse, because a session’s traffic follows the user rather than the capacity. It makes failure worse, because losing one instance loses one third of the sessions instantly rather than a share of the request volume. And it makes scaling worse, because an instance cannot be drained without failing the sessions on it. The IP-hash variant is worse still, since it pins an entire NAT or corporate egress to one instance and produces exactly the hot-key problem section 0.4 described.

The problem affinity appears to solve is that in-memory state is not shared. The correct fix is to make the state shareable or removable: store it in a shared store, encode it in a token the client carries, or make it reconstructible. The cases where affinity is nonetheless correct are narrow — a backend with a connection to a third-party system that permits exactly one concurrent connection per session, or a protocol where a long-lived stateful stream is cheaper than stateless requests. Outside those, affinity is a cost with no benefit and should be removed deliberately rather than left in place by default.

# Removing affinity, in the order that works
# ------------------------------------------------------------
# 1. EXTERNALISE THE SESSION, then remove the cookie.
#    The token carries the state; the server trusts the
#    signature, not the store.
import hmac, hashlib, base64, json, time

SECRET = b"..."  # from a secret manager, rotated, versioned
TTL_S  = 3600

def issue_session(claims):
    body = {**claims, "iat": int(time.time()), "exp":
             int(time.time()) + TTL_S}
    raw = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(
        json.dumps(body, separators=(",", ":")).encode())
    sig = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(
        hmac.new(SECRET, raw, hashlib.sha256).digest())
    return f"{raw.decode()}.{sig.decode()}"   # ~400 bytes

def read_session(tok):
    raw_b64, sig_b64 = tok.split(".")
    raw = base64.urlsafe_b64decode(raw_b64)
    expect = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(
        hmac.new(SECRET, raw, hashlib.sha256).digest())
MechanismDistribution qualityFailure behaviourDrainabilityVerdict
Source IP hash✗ NAT and mobile clients pin en masse✗ One egress = one instance = 100 % loss✗ cannot drain that instanceAvoid entirely
Cookie insert by LB△ reasonable for many users✗ sessions lost on instance loss✗ drain fails live sessionsLegacy; remove
Application session id in token✓ balancer is stateless✓ nothing lost✓ fully drainableCorrect
Signed state token✓ no store at all✓ nothing lost✓ fully drainableCorrect, if state is small
Sticky by application key✗ hot keys pin an instance△ hot key lost✗ hot key pinnedOnly with key replication
Ring hash for shard routing✓ intentional, 4 % CV△ only the affected range moves△ requires draining a key rangeCorrect for shards, not sessions

If a protocol genuinely requires per-session affinity, pin the connection at layer 4 and accept the cost explicitly. WebSocket, gRPC bidirectional streaming, and SSH tunnels are the real cases: a stream is one long-lived connection, so affinity is a property of the protocol rather than an implementation shortcut, and a load balancer that terminates and re-establishes the connection each time is simply broken. The correct configuration is an L4 hash on the connection’s 5-tuple, a long idle timeout matched to the protocol’s heartbeat interval, and no attempt to share those connections across routes. Everything else should be stateless.

5.5 DNS-based routing, TTLs and health

This subsection covers DNS-based routing, the propagation delay it introduces, and why health-checked DNS is not a substitute for a load balancer.

DNS-based routing removes a hop by answering the A query with the load balancer’s address rather than the backends', or by answering with a backend address directly. The cost is that DNS is a caching hierarchy with no invalidation mechanism: a resolver that cached a record will serve it until the TTL expires, and intermediate resolvers frequently ignore the TTL the authority publishes in favour of their own policy. The practical consequence is that a DNS-based cutover has a propagation delay equal to the longest TTL in the chain, which in a corporate environment with an ISP resolver can be fifteen minutes, and during that window a fraction of clients is talking to an instance that no longer exists.

Health-checked DNS, where the record is withdrawn when a health check fails, is a common and dangerous pattern. It converts a 2-second load-balancer ejection into a DNS-propagation-speed event change, which is tens of seconds to minutes. The correct use of health-checked DNS is coarse failover between regions or between datacentres, with a TTL of 60–300 seconds, not fine-grained balancing within a datacentre. Health checking at the DNS level is a routing decision made on an interval measured in minutes, and any incident requiring a response measured in seconds must be handled above that layer.

# DNS-based routing: the arithmetic of propagation
# ------------------------------------------------------------
# A backend is removed at T=0. Clients resolve as follows:
#
#   resolver        TTL   last cached   next refresh
#   -------------------------------------------------------
#   app, no cache   n/a       n/a        immediate
#   app + jitter    30 s     T=-120 s    T=+30 s    (12.5%)
#   ISP resolver    300 s    T=-900 s    T=+300 s   (11.1%)
#   corporate       60 s     T=-30 s     T=+30 s    (50.0%!)
#   stale-while-    86400 s  T=-8000 s   never      (forever)
#     revalidate
#
# The percentages are the fraction of clients still sending
# traffic to the removed backend, for a 30-minute window.
# A 50% figure is not unusual: it is what a corporate proxy
# with a 60-second TTL and unlucky timing produces.
#
# CONSEQUENCE: a 32-instance fleet loses one instance, and
# for up to 5 minutes 11-50% of clients still connect to it.
# Those connections are refused. The error rate is 11-50%

# The record set, when DNS routing is the right mechanism
# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Primary and secondary are health-checked by the provider
# and the record is withdrawn on failure. Use it for regions,
# never for backends within a region.
$ORIGIN checkout.example.com.
$TTL 60
; Regional failover. Health-checked: if health-check-primary
; fails, the secondary answer is served.
checkout-primary.health-check-primary   A   203.0.113.10
checkout-primary.health-check-primary   A   203.0.113.11
checkout-secondary.health-check-secondary A 198.51.100.20
; Weighted split for an intentional percentage rollout
checkout-canary                A   203.0.113.30
checkout-stable.health-check-stable A 203.0.113.10
; Per-region endpoints for a client that resolves once and
; then maintains a long-lived connection or a warm pool.

A rolling restart that moves backends behind DNS is a deliberate five-minute outage. This is not a theoretical concern: a deployment that removes pods from a DNS record and re-adds them under a new name produces a 5–50 % error rate for the duration of the TTL, and the deploy appears successful because the pods came back. The symptom users describe is "the site flickers for a few minutes after every deploy", which is a support-ticket generator and a source of cache warm-up churn. The rule is that DNS resolution must be stable across a deploy: the record names a load balancer, the load balancer’s own membership changes, and the client never learns about it.

5.6 Service discovery and configuration propagation

This subsection covers the watch and poll models, the convergence time budget, and the stale-configuration failure mode.

Service discovery is a distributed consensus problem, and the interesting parameter is convergence time: the interval between a backend becoming healthy and every client knowing it. The two models are watch and poll. Watch — long-lived connections from clients to an authoritative store, with the store pushing changes — has the best convergence, typically 10–100 ms, and the worst failure mode: a partition between a client and the store leaves the client with a frozen view, and if the client treats "no update" as "still valid" it continues to send traffic to instances that were removed minutes ago. Poll — each client listing the store every N seconds — tolerates a partition trivially, because a client that cannot reach the store keeps its last good list, and has a convergence time equal to N plus the store’s own propagation.

The correct answer is a bounded staleness policy rather than a choice, and it is expressed as a maximum age. A client treats its endpoint list as valid for at most 30 seconds; if it has not successfully refreshed within that window it removes the endpoints and fails fast, which converts a silent stale-routing problem into a visible error. This is the same principle as the cache TTL from chapter 4, applied to configuration, and it fails in the same way if omitted: a store outage during a rolling deploy produces requests to a mixture of old and new instances, with a 5xx rate proportional to the fraction of stale clients, and no metric that distinguishes it from a real backend fault.

# Watch with a bounded staleness policy, in full
# ------------------------------------------------------------
class ServiceResolver:
    def __init__(self, store, ttl_s=30, watch_timeout_s=10):
        self.store = store
        self.ttl = ttl_s
        self.watch_timeout = watch_timeout_s
        self.endpoints = []            # (addr, weight, healthy)
        self.last_ok = 0.0

    def update(self):
        try:
            # Watch with a deadline, so a silent store does not
            # leave the client blocked rather than stale. THIS
            # is the important detail: a watch with no timeout
            # is a partition that never surfaces.
            eps = self.store.watch(timeout=self.watch_timeout)
            self.endpoints = eps
            self.last_ok = monotonic()

# HAProxy 3.0 -- service discovery from an SD backend
# ------------------------------------------------------------
backend api_app
    mode http
    balance leastconn
    http-request set-header X-Forwarded-For %[req.hdr(X-Forwarded-For)]
    # server-state-file lets the balancer re-adopt instances
    # it knew before a restart, including their DOWN/MAINT
    # state, so a balancer restart does not re-admit an
    # instance that was deliberately ejected.
    load-server-state-from-file global
    http-check send meth GET uri /healthz/ready
    http-check expect status 200
    default-server inter 2s rise 3 fall 2 maxconn 2000
    # Drain an instance by marking it MAINT, which removes it
    # from selection without dropping existing connections.
    server-template srv 16 10.0.0.0:8080 check resolvers dns
# The four levers, and what each costs
# ------------------------------------------------------------
#   inter 2s fall 2   eject in ~4 s. Faster detection means
#                     more false positives on a GC pause; 4 s

Make the endpoint list’s age a first-class metric and alert on it. The metric is one gauge per consumer, `service_discovery_list_age_seconds`, and the alert is a warning at half the configured TTL and a page at the TTL. This single metric converts an entire class of intermittent, unreproducible production failures — requests to an instance that was removed twenty minutes ago, with no corresponding backend error — from an investigation into a dashboard check. It also makes the staleness policy enforceable, because a policy nobody can observe is a policy that will be raised to a week in the first incident and never lowered again.

Exercises 6

  1. Measure the actual distribution of traffic across backends for a running service, at three granularities: connections, requests, and CPU seconds. Report the coefficient of variation at each. Then change the selection algorithm to least-response-time and re-measure, stating which granularity improved and which did not, and explain the residual imbalance in terms of request cost.
  2. Audit a service’s probes. For each of liveness, readiness, and startup, record the handler’s implementation and what it checks, then classify every dependency the handler touches as hard or soft. Prove the classification with a chaos experiment: fail each dependency in turn and record which instances are ejected, within what time, and whether the fleet survives. Deliverable is the table plus the corrected probe configuration.
  3. Measure session affinity in a system that has it: for a 24-hour window, report the share of sessions whose requests all landed on one instance, the maximum number of concurrent sessions on any single instance, and the number of sessions lost during a rolling restart. Then externalise the state, remove the affinity, and re-measure all three. State the balance-sheet impact of the errors the affinity was causing.
  4. Build a DNS propagation measurement for your environment: resolve a record through the app resolver, the OS resolver, and the corporate proxy, and record the actual observed TTL of each. Then compute, for a 32-instance rolling restart, the expected error rate over five minutes at each observed TTL, and compare with the observed error rate from a real deploy. State the TTL your environment actually supports.
  5. Instrument a service’s service discovery with a list-age gauge and run a rolling restart while recording: time from pod-ready to first traffic received, time from pod-ready to full traffic share, and total error rate during the restart. Compare against the convergence budget from 5.6 and account for the difference, specifically investigating whether connection tracking accounts for it.

6 · Databases at Scale: Replication, Partitioning and Sharding

6.1 Read replicas, async versus sync, and lag6.2 Partitioning: range, hash and list6.3 Sharding, and choosing the shard key6.4 Cross-shard consistency and read-your-write6.5 CAP, PACELC and the 2000-era critique6.6 Polyglot persistence and its failure modes

This section covers the mechanisms by which a relational system is made to exceed a single machine, and the costs each imposes. The argument is that partitioning, replication, and sharding solve different problems and are routinely confused: partitioning is a single-node storage and maintenance strategy, replication is an availability and read-scalability strategy, and sharding is a write-scalability strategy with a correctness cost. The section covers read replicas and replication lag as a first-class observable quantity, the three partitioning strategies with their query implications, shard-key selection as an irreversible decision, the cross-shard consistency problems sharding creates, the CAP theorem and its PACELC refinement, and polyglot persistence with the operational tax it levies.

6.1 Read replicas, async versus sync, and lag

This subsection covers read replicas, the three replication modes and their costs, and replication lag as a quantity that must be measured rather than assumed.

A read replica is a full copy of the data on a second machine, and it is the cheapest available scale-out for a read-heavy workload: it removes read capacity from the primary without any change to the write path, and it can be placed in a separate failure domain at no additional architectural cost. Its limitation is that the replica is a copy at an unknown point in the past, and the distance between now and the replica’s state — replication lag — is a measured quantity, not a constant. Lag is a distribution: at p50 it is frequently 20–80 ms, at p99 a few hundred milliseconds, and under a write burst or a vacuum it can reach tens of seconds. Any design that routes reads to replicas must state a bound on that distribution and enforce it, because the unstated case is unbounded.

Asynchronous replication is the default and should be. Synchronous replication to one peer trades a cross-node round trip — 0.4–2 ms within a zone, 4–20 ms across zones — for RPO 0, and that price is paid on every write whether or not a failure occurs. The correct use of synchronous replication is a narrow, named tier: payment capture, ledger postings, and configuration writes, where RPO 0 is a business requirement rather than a preference.
