The incorrect use is making the whole database synchronous to improve an availability number, which converts every transient network blip into a write outage, because a partition to the synchronous standby blocks writes entirely.

# PostgreSQL 18 -- the three durability modes, priced
# ------------------------------------------------------------
#   synchronous_commit = on      fsync locally, then WAIT for
#                               at least one remote to flush
#     RPO 0 within the replica set
#     cost: +0.4-2.0 ms p50 within a zone, +4-20 ms across
#     failure mode: a partition to the sync standby BLOCKS
#     WRITES unless synchronous_standby_names is empty. This
#     is the setting that turns a 200 ms network blip into a
#     total write outage.
#   synchronous_commit = local   fsync locally, do not wait
#     RPO 0 on the primary; RPO = replay lag on failover
#     cost: ~8-15 ms fsync p99, no cross-node component
#     correct for: almost everything, with async replicas
#   synchronous_commit = off     no fsync; the OS decides
#     RPO = time since the last checkpoint, up to max_wal_size
#     correct ONLY with a replicated log in front of the
#     primary, which is a different design
synchronous_commit = on
wal_keep_size = 4GB
max_wal_senders = 10
max_replication_slots = 10
synchronous_standby_names = 'ledger_sb2, ledger_sb1, read_sb1, read_sb2, read_sb3'
# Order matters: the named standbys must be available or
# writes block. A learner-only read replica must NEVER
# appear in this list: a read replica falling behind would
# then gate writes, the exact coupling the design avoids.
# Replication lag is a distribution. Measure it as one.
# ------------------------------------------------------------
SELECT application_name, state, sync_state,
       write_lag, flush_lag, replay_lag,
       pg_wal_lsn_diff(sent_lsn, replay_lsn) AS bytes_behind
FROM pg_stat_replication ORDER BY bytes_behind DESC;
-- Translate to the number that matters: seconds of loss.
--   potential_RPO = bytes_behind / wal_rate_per_sec
--
# Per-replica lag is a MAX over the set for RPO and a MIN
# for read-your-write, and they are different numbers. RPO
# needs the WORST replica; read-your-write needs the BEST,
# because it needs the replica closest to current. A token
# pinned to "a replica" gives 8 s; one pinned to "the least
# lagged replica" gives 80 ms. That difference is the whole
# argument for pinning to the primary instead.
#
# THE LAG SPIKE, and why it is worse than it looks:
#   steady state      40 ms
#   write burst       2-30 s     primary outruns the network
#   autovacuum/       1-5 s       replay competes for I/O
#     checkpoint
#   replica restart   30-120 s    must replay from the slot
#   after failover    0 s         promoted, no lag
#
# Routing to replicas "unless they are more than 1 s behind"
# gives a read path whose p99 includes a 1 s read whenever a
# burst occurs, and whose tail is a function of the WRITE
# rate rather than of the query. Bound it at 250 ms, accept
# primary's capacity model from chapter 2.
ModeRPOWrite p50 addedWrite p99 addedBlocks writes on partitionCorrect for
Asynchronous, 1 replica≤ 5 s00✗ NoRead scaling, the default
Asynchronous, 2 replicas≤ 5 s0+0.2 ms shipping✗ NoThree zones of reads
Synchronous, 1 peer, same zone0+0.4–2.0 ms+1–5 ms✓ YesLedger, payments, config
Synchronous quorum, 2 of 30+0.8–3.0 ms+2–8 ms✓ YesLedger with zone survival
Synchronous, 1 peer, cross zone0+4–20 ms+10–60 ms✓ YesAlmost never; latency dominates
Semi-synchronous, group replication0, relaxed r > 1+1–6 ms+3–15 ms✗ With r > 1MySQL InnoDB Cluster
Quorum read, any replica0, read side0+1 RTT✗ NoLinearisable reads, LSM engines
Cascading, 3-hop≤ 15 s00✗ NoNever; three hops of lag

Routing reads to replicas without a lag bound converts a write burst into a read outage. The pattern is ubiquitous: "route to a replica if it is healthy, else to the primary", with health defined as "accepting connections". A replica 20 seconds behind is perfectly healthy by that definition and will serve every read 20 seconds stale, and if the endpoint is a price, an availability figure, or an order status, the symptom is users seeing data that contradicts what they were shown two minutes earlier. The health check must include a lag measurement, and the routing decision must be on lag rather than on liveness. The bound belongs in the ch1.6 table, and the fraction of reads it sends to the primary belongs in the primary’s capacity model.

6.2 Partitioning: range, hash and list

This subsection covers the three partitioning strategies, the query plans each produces, and the operations each makes expensive.

Partitioning is a single-node strategy: it divides one logical table into many physical tables, or one partition of a shared namespace, so that the storage engine can maintain, index, vacuum, and evict each division independently. It reduces the cost of maintenance operations and, where the partition key is in the query predicate, reduces the amount of data a query must examine. What it does not do is spread load across machines, and confusing the two is the most consequential misconception in database scaling — a table partitioned into 64 partitions on one machine is still one machine with one set of locks and one buffer pool.

Range partitioning is right when queries carry a range predicate on the partition key, and it is the only choice supporting partition-wise drop and time-based retention: dropping a month is a file unlink rather than a billion-row delete. Its weakness is that any query touching the partition key without a range predicate must visit every partition, and a hot partition at the high end concentrates writes. Hash partitioning is right when writes are uniform and queries are by key rather than range, and it distributes load and storage evenly; its weakness is that dropping a single key requires a scan of every partition. List partitioning is right for a small, changing, known set of values — a status, a region, a tenant tier — and is usually the answer for a multi-tenant table where the tenant count is known and stable.

# PostgreSQL 18 -- all three. Using more than one strategy on
# the same table is almost always a mistake.
# ------------------------------------------------------------
CREATE TABLE event (
  tenant_id  bigint NOT NULL,
  kind       text   NOT NULL,
  payload    jsonb  NOT NULL,
  created_at timestamptz NOT NULL
) PARTITION BY RANGE (created_at);
CREATE TABLE event_2026_08 PARTITION OF event
  FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-08-01') TO ('2026-09-01');

CREATE TABLE session (
  session_key text PRIMARY KEY,
  user_id     bigint NOT NULL,
  created_at  timestamptz NOT NULL
) PARTITION BY HASH (user_id);
CREATE TABLE session_p0 PARTITION OF session
  FOR VALUES WITH (MODULUS 16, REMAINDER 0);

CREATE TABLE invoice (
  invoice_id uuid NOT NULL,
  region      text NOT NULL,
  status      text NOT NULL,
  total_minor bigint NOT NULL,
  PRIMARY KEY (region, invoice_id)
) PARTITION BY LIST (region);
CREATE TABLE invoice_uk PARTITION OF invoice
  FOR VALUES IN ('uk');
-- because uniqueness can only be enforced within a partition.
# The plan consequences, measured rather than assumed
# ------------------------------------------------------------
-- RANGE, no predicate: 12x the index probes.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
  SELECT count(*) FROM event WHERE kind = 'purchase';
-- Append
--   ->  Seq Scan on event_2026_08 ...  (12 partitions)
--       Buffers: shared hit=4 120  read=98 410
-- A single-partition equivalent reads ~3 400 blocks, a 30x
-- difference that grows with the PARTITION count, not with
-- the data.
--
-- HASH, point lookup: the partition is pruned.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE)
  SELECT * FROM session
  WHERE user_id = 42 AND session_key = $1;
-- Index Scan using session_p3_user_id_session_key_idx
-- (never touches the other 15 partitions)
--
-- LIST, unlisted value: SILENTLY EMPTY.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE) SELECT * FROM invoice WHERE region = 'de';
-- 0 rows. NOT an error. A new region missing from the list
-- returns nothing for it. A DEFAULT partition catches it,
-- and then DEFAULT accumulates every forgotten value and a
-- query for one of them scans all of it. Create DEFAULT,
-- alert on its row count, migrate rows out on a schedule.
--
-- THE MAINTENANCE ARITHMETIC, why partitioning exists at all.
# Vacuum cost is proportional to dead tuples in the PARTITION.
# ONE machine. It is not an argument for sharding.

Range, hash and list differ along four axes that decide the choice. Distribution: range leaves a hot partition at the high end of the range, hash is even by construction, list depends on the value frequency. Pruning: range and list prune fully on both range and point predicates, hash prunes only on the partition key, and all three touch every partition when the key is absent from the predicate. Removal: range and list support partition-wise drop at O(1), which hash cannot do at all without a full scan. Growth: range and list accept a new value with no rewrite, hash requires a repartition. The right use follows directly: range for time series, archives and billing periods; hash for uniform high-write tables such as sessions; list for regions, statuses and tenant tiers.

Partition count is a capacity parameter, and the ceiling is usually autovacuum workers rather than partitions. A thousand partitions on a table with five autovacuum workers means each vacuum pass covers two hundred partitions sequentially, and the largest partition may wait hours. The workarounds are a higher autovacuum_max_workers and per-partition storage settings where needed, but the honest answer is that partition counts above 100–200 require the vacuum strategy to be designed rather than inherited. For time-series tables, monthly partitions over seven years is eighty-four and is comfortable; daily partitions over the same period is 2 555 and is not.

6.3 Sharding, and choosing the shard key

This subsection covers the routing layer sharding requires, the shard-key selection criteria, and the cost of choosing wrongly.

Sharding moves partitions of one logical dataset onto separate machines, each with its own primary, its own indexes, and its own failure domain. The write-capacity ceiling of a single primary — around 20–60 k writes per second after tuning, and 5–15 TB — is the trigger, and exceeding it requires either vertical scaling past the point of diminishing returns or distribution. Sharding buys write throughput and a bounded blast radius; it costs that the dataset is no longer queryable by one engine, so every query touching more than one shard must be decomposed, fanned out, and merged, and every cross-shard transaction must be handled by application code or a transaction manager.

The shard key is the single irreversible decision in database design. It determines which queries are cheap, which are impossible, which data lives together so a join is local, and how balanced the load is. Changing it after launch means moving every row, rewriting every query, and invalidating every cache key derived from it. The selection criteria are: high cardinality, so distribution is even; low skew, so no shard is hot; present in the majority of queries, so most queries are single-shard; immutable, so it never needs to change; and naturally aligned with the access pattern, so a query for a user’s data touches one shard rather than all of them.

# Shard-key selection, scored against five criteria
# ------------------------------------------------------------
# multi-tenant orders: 3 000 tenants, 41 M orders, 12 k wps.
#
# criterion            tenant_id  user_id  order_id  created_at
# -------------------  ---------  ------  --------  ----------
# cardinality          3 000      9.2 M   41 M      41 M
# worst/mean writes    1 560      13      291       33
# in most queries?     61%        74%     88%       31%
# immutable?           yes        yes     yes       yes
# locality with joins  GOOD       GOOD    none      none
# -------------------  ---------  ------  --------  ----------
# VERDICT: tenant_id balances the DATA but skews the WRITE
#   LOAD 156x. The largest tenant is 1.9 M orders and 1 400
#   wps on one shard, four times a comfortable single-primary
#   load. order_id distributes perfectly and appears in 88%
#   of queries, but it destroys locality: "list this
#   customer's orders" becomes a 3 000-way fan-out on the
#   most common page in the product.
#
# THE ANSWER, and it is usually a composite:
#   shard by hash(tenant_id) for DATA placement, AND give the
#   largest tenants multiple shards by sub-keying:
#     shard = hash(tenant_id, bucket)
#     bucket = order_id % 16 above a size threshold, else 0
#   This is virtual nodes applied to shards, and it is the
#   only approach that handles a Zipfian tenant distribution
#   without either hot shards or a fan-out.
#
# or a second store rather than a cleverer key.
# The routing layer every sharded design needs
# ------------------------------------------------------------
class ShardRouter:
    def __init__(self, topology, ring, n_vshards=16384):
        # VIRTUAL SHARDS, always. The physical shard count
        # changes as nodes are added; the virtual count does
        # not. Map virtual -> physical in a table, so a
        # rebalance moves a table rather than rehashing keys.
        self.n = n_vshards
        self.ring = ring                    # ConsistentHash
        self.map = topology                 # {vshard: shard_id}

    def shard_for(self, shard_key: str) -> int:
        return self.map[crc32(shard_key.encode()) % self.n]

    def locate(self, shard_key, pk):
        """The single call that resolves to a connection.
        Everything else is a consequence of this returning
        a (shard_id, connection) pair."""
        sid = self.shard_for(shard_key)
        return self.pool[sid], pk
#
# WHY 16384 VIRTUAL SHARDS
#   16384 / 32 physical = 512 virtual per node. The CV of
#   per-virtual load is about 1/sqrt(512) = 4.4%, so at 32
#   physical shards the worst is roughly 1.12x the mean.
#   4096 gives 1.3x at 32 nodes. 16384 costs a 16 384-row
#   topology table, which is nothing.
#
#   with every query and every cache key rewritten: weeks.
Shard key propertyTestGoodBadConsequence of failing it
CardinalityDistinct values ÷ rows> 10⁶< 10⁴Fewer shards than nodes; idle capacity
Evennessmax ÷ mean writes per shard< 1.3> 2.0One hot shard sets the ceiling for the whole system
Query coverage% queries carrying the key> 80 %< 50 %Every query becomes an N-way fan-out
LocalitySingle-shard share of QPS> 80 %< 40 %Joins move into the application, permanently
ImmutabilityCan the value ever change?NeverOccasionallyA rewrite of the whole dataset to fix it
Growth localityDoes one tenant grow in place?Yes, sub-shardableNoA single tenant eventually needs a split migration
Cache friendlinessIs the key a cache key prefix?YesNoCache entries scatter across all shards
Blast radiusShards lost in one zone< 1/3> 1/3Quorum arithmetic fails; see 6.5

A shard key chosen from the data model rather than the query model is the most expensive irreversible decision in database design. The failure always has the same shape: the key is order_id, which is perfectly unique, perfectly even, and perfectly immutable, and the result is that "list this customer’s last twenty orders" — a query every application has, executed on every page load — becomes a scan of all shards with a merge, at a cost of N round trips and a merge in application memory. The tests are simple and must be run before the schema is frozen: write the twenty most-executed queries, identify the key present in the majority, and confirm the resulting shard count exceeds the node count by at least 4×. If no key passes, the data does not want to be sharded by this dimension, and the answer is a read model or a second store.

6.4 Cross-shard consistency and read-your-write

This subsection covers the consistency anomalies sharding creates, the repairs available, and the transaction manager options.

Sharding converts local consistency problems into global ones, and the global problems are qualitatively harder because no storage engine can solve them. A single-node database guarantees that a transaction touching three rows sees a consistent snapshot; across three shards there is no transaction, and the application must decide what the shards' combined state means while they change independently. The anomalies are: read-your-write, where a write to shard A is invisible to a read on shard B; monotonic reads, where a user paging a list sees the cursor regress; lost update, where two concurrent read-modify-writes on the same shard overwrite one another because each shard is individually correct; and phantom reads, where a cross-shard count corresponds to no single point in time.

The repairs are all applications of the consistency models from section 1, restricted to a distributed setting. The routing token is the cheapest and most under-used: a write returns a version and a shard, and a subsequent read carries both, with the router directing it. Version vectors extend this to reads that span shards. A transaction manager — two-phase commit or its optimistic variant — is the only mechanism giving atomicity across shards, and it is correct to use rarely: the availability cost is real, the latency cost is one round trip per participating shard, and the operational cost is a coordinator whose failure blocks every transaction in flight. In practice the correct answer is to avoid the transaction rather than to make it atomic.

# Read-your-write across shards, by routing token
# ------------------------------------------------------------
class ShardedStore:
    def write(self, shard_key, entity, value):
        shard, conn = self.router.locate(shard_key, entity)
        # The PRIMARY advances a per-user revision and returns
        # it, so the client holds a token for the next read.
        rev = self.shard_seq(shard_key)          # 1 increment
        conn.execute("UPDATE t SET v=$1, rev=$2 WHERE id=$3",
                     (value, rev, entity))
        return {"rev": rev, "shard": shard.shard_id}

    def read(self, shard_key, entity, min_rev, max_lag_s=0.25):
        shard, conn = self.router.locate(shard_key, entity)
        if shard.is_replica or shard.lag_s() > max_lag_s:
            shard, conn = self.primary_for(shard_key)
        row = conn.execute("SELECT v, rev FROM t WHERE id=$1",
                           (entity,))
        if row.rev < min_rev:
            # Not yet visible. Three valid responses, and the
            # choice belongs in the requirements (ch1.6):
            #   1. wait, bounded, then 409
            #   2. read the primary unconditionally
            #   3. return 409 immediately with the floor
            # Option 3 preserves the read-path SLO and is
            # correct; the client retries with the same token
            raise NotYetVisible(row.rev, min_rev)
        return row.v
#
# part users notice.
# LOST UPDATE, which no routing token fixes
#   A: SELECT balance -> 100     B: SELECT balance -> 100
#   A: UPDATE SET balance=90       B: UPDATE SET balance=90
#   result 90, and 10 has vanished. Each shard was correct;
#   the operation was not atomic.
#
#   The fix is at the shard, and it is one statement:
UPDATE t SET balance = balance - 10
 WHERE id = $1 AND balance >= 10 RETURNING balance;
#   Atomic, single round trip, returns 0 rows rather than a
#   negative balance. The invariant lives in the WHERE
#   clause, the only place it can be enforced atomically. An
#   application-level read-then-write across a network
#   boundary is a lost update waiting to happen.
#
#   The general form is an optimistic version column:
UPDATE t SET v=$1, rev=rev+1 WHERE id=$2 AND rev=$3;
#   0 rows means someone else won; the client re-reads and
#   retries, bounded, with the bound in the ch1.6 budget.
#
# THE TRANSACTION OPTIONS, priced
#   1. NO TRANSACTION. The default, and correct most of the
#      time. Make the operation a single-shard write, or
#      design it to be idempotent and retryable. Over 80% of
#      real workloads.
#   2. SINGLE-SHARD TRANSACTION. Keep a multi-row mutation on
#      one shard by including the rows' shared parent in the
#      shard key. The highest-leverage design move available:
#      order and order_lines, account and entries.

Prefer single-shard transactions to any distributed mechanism, by choosing the shard key with that in mind. The most effective consistency design work is not a transaction manager but a data model in which related rows share a shard key and therefore live together. An order and its lines, an account and its entries, a conversation and its messages — each must be mutated atomically, and each is atomic for free if the parent identifier is the shard key. The compound key (account_id, entry_id) with the shard taken from account_id gives a single-shard transaction for every account operation, and the only cross-shard operations left are the ones that genuinely are cross-account.

6.5 CAP, PACELC and the 2000-era critique

This subsection states the CAP theorem accurately, presents its 2000-era critique, and shows the PACELC formulation the critique produced.

CAP was stated by Gilbert and Lynch in 2002 as a result about asynchronous distributed systems: when a network partition occurs, a system must choose between remaining available and remaining consistent. The theorem is correct and almost always misapplied, because it is a statement about behaviour during a partition and is read as a statement about general design. The three properties are not a three-way menu either: availability and partition tolerance are not choices, because a system that uses the network is partition-tolerant whether or not it says so. The real design question is what a system does during the seconds or minutes a partition lasts, and CAP is silent about everything outside that window.

The critique is that CAP addresses only the partition case and says nothing about the far more common condition of no partition, where the same trade-off between latency and consistency exists and is far more consequential. Daniel Abadi proposed PACELC in 2010 to correct this: if there is a Partition, the system chooses between Availability and Consistency; Else, it chooses between Latency and Consistency. The else branch is where ordinary systems spend all their time, and it is the branch CAP omits. Every replicated system trades latency against consistency continuously and by a configurable amount, so the design question is not "which of the three" but "how much latency will we trade for how much consistency, and what happens to that trade when the network partitions".

# CAP and PACELC, made operational
# ------------------------------------------------------------
# CAP, correctly stated (Gilbert and Lynch, 2002):
#   A replicated state machine under an ASYNCHRONOUS network
#   cannot provide both CAPABILITY (every request to a
#   non-failing node returns a response) and CONSISTENCY (all
#   nodes see the same state) in the presence of a partition.
#
# WHAT CAP DOES NOT SAY, and the four misreadings:
#   1. "CP" does not mean consistency is free. It means
#      writes BLOCK during a partition. Availability is not
#      lost, it is converted into a timeout: a CP database
#      under partition has a 100% error rate.
#   2. "AP" does not mean eventual consistency is fine. It
#      means conflicting writes are accepted and must be
#      reconciled, with a mechanism someone must choose.
#   3. Partition tolerance is not a choice. A system that
#      communicates over a network has partitions.
#   4. CAP is silent outside the partition window, which is
#      where systems spend 99.99% of their life. This is the
#      critique that produced PACELC.
#
# THE ELSE BRANCH, worked, with real numbers. A write, quorum
# 2 of 3, same-zone round trip 1 ms:
#   synchronously replicated
#     local fsync      8 ms p99
#     quorum ack wait  1 ms p99, parallel with fsync
#     total            9 ms p99, RPO 0
#   asynchronously replicated
#                                     2-30 s p99)
# The else branch, concluded
#   The trade is 1 ms of p99 latency for 2-30 s of p99 data
#   loss. On a 200 ms p99 target, 1 ms is 0.5% of the budget.
#   On an RPO requirement, 30 s may be the whole requirement.
#   THE RATIO OF THESE TWO NUMBERS IS THE DESIGN DECISION,
#   it differs per data class, and that is why the answer is
#   a per-tier decision (ch1.5) rather than a per-system one.
#
# The partition window is short and the latency cost is
# permanent, which is why the else branch usually wins the
# argument. Partitions last seconds to minutes, with a median
# perhaps of 20 seconds for a network event and considerably
# longer for a misrouted route or an expired firewall rule.
# The latency cost of consistency is paid on every write,
# forever. A team that chooses synchrony to survive a
# partition is paying a permanent tax against an event
# measured in minutes, and the arithmetic usually favours
# asynchrony with a reconciliation procedure, provided the
# reconciliation is written, tested, and rehearsed.
SystemDuring a partitionLatency cost of consistency, no partitionReconciliationFits which data class
PostgreSQL sync replica✓ CP: writes block+0.4–2.0 ms p99None neededLedger, payments, config
PostgreSQL async replica✗ AP: writes continue, lag grows0Failover replays the log; RPO = lagEverything else
etcd / Raft group✓ CP: minority side rejects writes+1 RTT, 1–5 msNone; the log is authoritativeConfig, leases, coordination
Cassandra, CL ONE✓ AP: any replica serves0Read repair, hinted handoffDerived state, counters, cache
Cassandra, CL ALL✗ CP: every replica required+N RTTNoneAlmost never; CL QUORUM is right
DynamoDB, eventually consistent✓ AP0Vector clock, last-write-winsCounters, flags, presence
DynamoDB, strongly consistent✗ AP with 50% errors on partition+1 RTTVector clock, last-write-winsUser-visible reads
Distributed SQL, Raft groups✓ CP per group+1 RTT, 2–10 msNone; serialisable across groupsTransactional, when affordable

Quorum arithmetic is the mechanism, and the zone layout is a constraint on the protocol rather than a choice of protocol. For f replicas with read quorum R and write quorum W, strong consistency requires R + W > f. With f = 3 and R = W = 2 the system tolerates exactly one failure and its availability is 1 − C(2,2)/C(3,2) = 0.667 with a single node left, not 99.9 %; a 3-node quorum is not a 99.9 % system, and this is why the six-node figure in ch1.5 is the correct one. With f = 5 and R = W = 3 the system tolerates two failures, at 0.900 with two down. The zone layout must then be chosen so the worst single-zone loss still leaves a quorum: a 2 + 2 + 1 distribution across three zones does, because every single zone loss leaves three or four nodes, whereas 3 + 1 + 1 does not, because losing the three-node zone leaves two and halts all writes.

# The three topology choices, priced in milliseconds
# ------------------------------------------------------------
# QUORUM ARITHMETICS. f replicas, R read quorum, W write
# quorum. Strong consistency requires R + W > f.
#   A(op) = 1 - C(f-k, required) / C(f, required)
#   with k of f replicas alive.
#
# f=3, R=W=2:  R+W=4 > 3, consistent
#   A with 3 alive = 1.000
#   A with 2 alive = 1 - C(1,2)/C(3,2) = 1.000
#   A with 1 alive  = 1 - C(2,2)/C(3,2) = 0.667
#   Tolerates exactly ONE failure. A 3-node quorum system is
#   NOT 99.9%: it is 2/3, which is why the 6-node figure in
#   ch1.5 is the correct one.
#
# f=5, R=W=3:  R+W=6 > 5, consistent
#   A with 5 alive = 1.000
#   A with 4 alive = 1 - C(1,3)/C(5,3) = 1.000
#   A with 3 alive = 1 - C(2,3)/C(5,3) = 1.000
#   A with 2 alive = 1 - C(3,3)/C(5,3) = 0.900
#   Tolerates TWO failures, at 90% with two down.

6.6 Polyglot persistence and its failure modes

This subsection covers when multiple stores are justified, the change-data-capture pattern that keeps them consistent, and the failure modes unique to polyglot systems.

Polyglot persistence means using more than one storage engine because each is right for a different class of data. The justification always has the same shape: one of the engines cannot express the required access pattern at acceptable cost. Search cannot be answered by a B-tree without an inverted index; a time series cannot be aggregated across 100 000 series in a row store; a three-hop graph traversal needs six self-joins; a vector search over 10 M embeddings needs an approximate index. The test is whether the second store is genuinely necessary, because the cost is not the storage — it is the second consistency problem, the second on-call rotation, the second backup and restore procedure, and the second thing to be schema-migrated.

The consistency mechanism between stores is change data capture, and its central property is that it is eventually consistent with a lag equal to the consumer’s position. That lag is a number the requirements must state, and the standard failure is that it is not stated, so a user sees a state visible in one store for two seconds and in the other for none, and the bug is reported as "the data is wrong" rather than "the projection is behind". The deeper failure is that a projection becomes load-bearing: a report is run from it, a support agent reads it, and eventually a business process writes to it. Once anything writes to a projection it is a second source of truth, and the two diverge.

# Change data capture, the standard shape
# ------------------------------------------------------------
-- Source of truth: orders, PostgreSQL 18
CREATE PUBLICATION orders_pub FOR TABLE orders, order_lines;
wal_level = logical
max_replication_slots = 10
max_wal_senders = 10
-- The slot is the failure point: if the consumer stops and
-- the slot is not released, WAL accumulates on the primary
-- until it fills the disk. This is the commonest way a CDC
-- pipeline takes down its own source of truth.
SELECT slot_name, active, restart_lsn, confirmed_flush_lsn,
       pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), confirmed_flush_lsn)
         AS bytes_behind
FROM pg_replication_slots;
-- bytes_behind / wal_rate = seconds of staleness. ALERT at
-- 2 s warning, 10 s page. The consumer position is the
-- requirement from ch1.6, measured rather than assumed.
--
# The consumer, idempotent by primary key:
def on_event(ev):
    key = (ev["table"], ev["pk"])
    if dedupe.seen(key, ev["lsn"]):
        return                      # at-least-once delivery
    if ev["op"] == "D":
        search_index.delete(ev["pk"])
    else:
        search_index.upsert(ev["pk"], to_doc(ev["row"]))
        analytics.append(ev)        # at-least-once, so the
    dedupe.mark(key, ev["lsn"])
# THREE PROPERTIES the consumer must have, and why
#   idempotent       delivery is at-least-once; a duplicate
#                    upsert is harmless, a duplicate
#                    increment is not
#   ordered per key  the log is ordered per transaction; an
#                    update followed by its delete must not
#                    arrive reversed
#   replayable       the slot must be resettable and the
#                    consumer must be able to rebuild its index
#                    from zero in bounded time. A projection
#                    that cannot be rebuilt is a projection
#                    that cannot be repaired, and it will need
#                    to be repaired.
#
# The polyglot system, and its lag budget
#   primary    orders          PostgreSQL 18  RPO 0,  RTO 60 s
#   projection search_index   inverted index  lag <= 5 s
#   projection read_model     wide-column     lag <= 2 s
#   projection analytics_rows wide-column     lag <= 60 s
#   cache      product:v17:42 Redis 8          TTL 300 s
#
# EACH LAG IS A STALENESS BOUND AND EACH MUST APPEAR IN THE
# ch1.6 TABLE, with the read paths permitted to use the
# projection. A projection with no declared consumer is a
# shadow copy: it will be queried eventually, by someone who
# has no idea it is behind.
#   lag <= 2 s    read_model: anything a user reads
#   lag <= 5 s    search_index: results a user ranks
#   lag <= 60 s   analytics_rows: reports and dashboards
#                  anything with an RPO

Every projection must be rebuildable from the source of truth in bounded time, and the bound must be stated before the projection exists. A search index that takes six hours to rebuild is a search index that will never be rebuilt after an incident, so it will silently accumulate corruption — a missing document from a failed consumer, a stale field from a schema change the consumer did not handle — and the results will be quietly wrong in ways that resemble relevance problems rather than consistency problems. Stating the rebuild bound forces the design question at the right time: if the projection is an inverted index over 40 M documents and a full rebuild is six hours, it needs an incremental-repair mechanism and a periodic full reconciliation, and both are far cheaper to build before the index is populated than after.

Exercises 7

  1. Measure replication lag on a real system as a distribution, not a point: sample every 5 seconds for 24 hours and report p50, p99, and maximum per replica, alongside the primary’s write rate at each sample. Identify the two conditions under which p99 exceeds 1 second, and for each state whether the fix is a configuration change, a query change, or an accepted property of the workload.
  2. Implement all three partitioning strategies on the same 100 M row table in PostgreSQL 18, with identical indexes. Measure query p99 with and without the partition key in the predicate, vacuum duration and bloat, and the wall time to drop the oldest month. Report which strategy wins on each metric, and state which metric matters most for your workload and why.
  3. Score five candidate shard keys for a real dataset against the eight criteria in the table from 6.3, computing cardinality, max-to-mean write ratio, query coverage, and single-shard share of QPS from measured data. Select a winner, then design the composite key handling the largest tenant, and state the rebalancing procedure with its estimated duration and row-movement count.
  4. For a read path in your system, determine empirically whether it satisfies read-your-write and monotonic reads under a zone loss. Inject the loss, replay a user session, and record every violation with its user-visible symptom. Then add the routing token and re-run, reporting the latency the token adds and the fraction of reads it sends to the primary.
  5. Write the PACELC analysis for one real write path: measure p50 and p99 with synchronous and with asynchronous replication, compute the RPO of each under a node loss, and express the trade as a ratio of latency cost to data-loss cost in the same units. Then state, for each of four data classes in the system, which mode is correct, justified against that ratio rather than against a principle.

7 · Message Queues, Event Streaming and Async Processing

7.1 When to decouple with a queue7.2 Queue semantics: at-most-once, at-least-once and effectively-once7.3 Broker architectures: Kafka, RabbitMQ, SQS, NATS and Redis Streams7.4 Delivery mechanics: push, pull, prefetch and consumer groups7.5 Ordering, partition keys and hot keys7.6 Dead-letter queues, poison messages and retry ladders

This section moves the request path off the critical path by inserting a durable, asynchronous intermediary between producers and consumers. It fixes the three delivery guarantees precisely, because "at-least-once" is a promise made by the broker and the real risk lives at the consumer edge, where a partial side effect can be observed between a read and a write. The four dominant broker families are compared on storage model, ordering scope, retention and operational surface rather than on marketing throughput, and the section closes on the two failure modes that dominate on-call: unbalanced partitions and poison messages trapped in a retry loop. The argument throughout is that a queue is a buffer with an explicit service level, not a message-bus abstraction, and that its semantics must be designed for rather than assumed from the product name.

7.1 When to decouple with a queue

Establishes the measured conditions under which inserting a broker reduces tail latency and cost, and the conditions under which it merely relocates the failure.

Asynchronous decoupling is justified when at least one of four measured conditions holds. First, peak-to-average ratio: where the arrival rate has a peak-to-trough ratio above roughly 3:1 and the consumer’s cost per unit is fixed — CPU-bound work, a third-party quota, a database connection pool — a buffer converts a capacity problem into a latency problem, and latency can be traded explicitly. Second, burst absorption at a rate-limited sink: a downstream with a hard 200 req/s ceiling facing a 4,000 req/s arrival distribution is not a capacity question but a shaping question. Third, fan-out amplification: an event consumed by forty subscribers costs one write instead of forty reads, and the subscriber count stops being an O(n) tax on the producer. Fourth, availability decoupling: a producer requiring 99.99% availability cannot take a synchronous dependency on a consumer that offers 99.5%, because the composed figure is 99.485%.

The counter-case matters equally. A queue inserted in front of a component that is already fast and rarely contended adds two network round-trips, a broker availability dependency, an at-least-once duplicate surface and a new failure domain, in exchange for smoothing a distribution that was never a problem. Sizing is the first concrete artefact and it is arithmetic, not intuition: peak arrival rate multiplied by the tolerable backlog, divided by the sustainable drain rate, gives the recovery window the operator will need at 03:00.

# Buffer arithmetic for a bounded, observable work queue.
# arrival p99: 4200 msg/s | consumer: 32 workers x 95 msg/s each
# spike to absorb without loss: 90 s of peak arrivals

PEAK_RATE     = 4200          # msg/s, p99 over a 24 h window
WORKERS       = 32
PER_WORKER    = 95            # msg/s measured with the real payload mix
SUSTAINABLE   = WORKERS * PER_WORKER          # 3040 msg/s
SPIKE_SECONDS = 90
REPLAY_BUDGET = 900           # operator must fully catch up in 15 min

BACKLOG_MAX   = PEAK_RATE * SPIKE_SECONDS      # 378 000 messages
DRAIN_SECONDS = BACKLOG_MAX / float(SUSTAINABLE)
print(f"backlog_max={BACKLOG_MAX}  drain={DRAIN_SECONDS:.1f}s")
assert DRAIN_SECONDS <= REPLAY_BUDGET, "buffer too deep to replay in budget"

# The byte budget matters as much as the count: 378k x 1.5 KiB = 553 MiB.
# That must fit inside the broker retention window at the chosen replica
# factor, or you have specified a buffer the disk cannot hold.

The structural requirement is that the consumer is bounded. An unbounded queue — a LinkedBlockingQueue sized to Integer.MAX_VALUE in the JVM, an unbuffered-then-grown chan in Go, an in-memory broker’s arrayAppend — converts a throughput failure into a memory-exhaustion failure and then into an OOMKilled container, which is a strictly worse incident because it destroys the state the queue existed to protect. Admission control must be explicit at every layer, and the rejection signal must be a 429, a nack, or a rejection onto a dead-letter route — never an unbounded block on a request goroutine.

// Bounded worker pool with explicit admission control (Go 1.25).
// jobs is fixed-capacity: Submit never blocks a request goroutine.
type Pool struct {
    jobs           chan Job
    inflightGauge  prometheus.Gauge
    shedCounter    prometheus.Counter
}

func NewPool(workers, queueDepth int) *Pool {
    p := &Pool{jobs: make(chan Job, queueDepth)}
    p.inflightGauge = prometheus.NewGauge(prometheus.GaugeOpts{
        Name: "worker_inflight", Help: "jobs accepted but not yet terminal"})
    p.shedCounter = prometheus.NewCounter(prometheus.CounterOpts{
        Name: "worker_shed_total", Help: "jobs rejected at admission"})
    for i := 0; i < workers; i++ {
        go p.run()
    }
    return p
}

// Submit reports false at saturation so the caller can shed (HTTP 503 +
// Retry-After) rather than pin a request goroutine until the client gives up.
func (p *Pool) Submit(j Job) bool {
    select {
    case p.jobs <- j:
        p.inflightGauge.Inc()
        return true
    default:
        p.shedCounter.Inc()
        return false
    }
}

func (p *Pool) run() {
    for j := range p.jobs {
        ctx, cancel := context.WithTimeout(j.Ctx, 45*time.Second)
        err := j.Execute(ctx)     // bounded work: 45 s hard ceiling
        cancel()
        switch {
        case err == nil:
            j.Ack()
        case errors.Is(err, context.DeadlineExceeded):
            j.Nack(err)            // hand off to the retry ladder, never retry here
        default:
            j.Nack(err)
        }
        p.inflightGauge.Dec()
    }
}

An unbounded queue disguised as resilience. The most common failure mode of asynchronous architecture is a buffer sized by nobody, accumulating four hours of work during an incident and then producing results computed against state that has since changed. Two corrections follow. Instrument age of oldest unacknowledged message, not depth: three million messages that drain in two minutes is an event, whereas three thousand messages whose oldest entry is forty minutes old is an outage, and depth alone cannot distinguish them. And cap the buffer by age in the broker where the technology permits it — Kafka’s retention.ms with a delete cleanup policy, or an SQS MessageRetentionPeriod of one hour — so that overload degrades into data loss you have consciously accepted rather than into silently stale output.

7.2 Queue semantics: at-most-once, at-least-once and effectively-once

Distinguishes the three delivery guarantees at mechanism level and locates the duplicate-creation window that makes at-least-once unsafe without idempotency.

The three guarantees differ in where the risk of loss or duplication is placed, and conflating them is the most expensive naming error in this domain. At-most-once means the broker never redelivers: the message is removed at the moment of delivery, and if the consumer crashes after receiving but before committing its work, the message is gone. The loss window is exactly the consumer’s processing time. This is the correct guarantee for metrics, sampling, cache invalidation and log replication, where loss is preferable to duplication, and it is the default for several managed queue products. At-least-once means the broker retains the message until it receives an explicit acknowledgement, so a consumer crash causes redelivery; the duplicate window is the entire processing duration plus the acknowledgement round-trip. Every durable broker supports it and it is the correct default for anything with financial or state consequences. Effectively-once is not a broker guarantee at all — it is an end-to-end property produced by combining a transactional producer, a read-committed consumer and a deduplicated or idempotent commit, and it holds only within the boundaries the implementation defines.

The failure that motivates all of this is the already-processed-but-unacknowledged crash. A consumer receives message m, performs a debit, and is killed before sending the ack. The broker, correctly, redelivers m, and the consumer debits again. This is not a broker bug; it is the guarantee operating as specified. The only defences are to make the side effect idempotent, to make the commit and the side effect atomic through the transactional outbox and inbox pattern covered in 8.4, or to construct the effect so that repetition is naturally harmless — a keyed PUT rather than an append.

// Effectively-once consume-transform-produce, end to end.
// Three mechanisms are required together; any one alone is insufficient:
//   (1) transactional producer  - publish and offset commit are atomic
//   (2) read_committed consumer - aborted records are never visible
//   (3) the sink must still tolerate a duplicate, because "effectively"
//       is a bound, not a proof.

const producer = &kafka.Writer{
    Addr:         kafka.TCP(brokers...),
    Balancer:     &kafka.Hash{},        // key to partition, see 7.5
    RequiredAcks: kafka.RequireAll,       // wait for the ISR, not just the leader
    // Stable per LOGICAL producer. Changing it on restart defeats fencing
    // and lets a zombie instance commit after a replacement has taken over.
    TransactionalID: "svc-a-tx-3",
    MaxAttempts:    1,                   // the transaction is the retry mechanism
    Async:          false,
}
defer producer.Close()

func process(ctx context.Context, r *kafka.Reader) error {
    m, err := r.FetchMessage(ctx)        // manual commit; never auto-commit
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }

    var out DomainEvent
    if err := json.Unmarshal(m.Value, &out); err != nil {
        // Poison record: advance past it. Retrying forever is the outage.
        return r.CommitMessages(ctx, m)
    }

    if err := withKafkaTransaction(ctx, producer, func(tx *kafka.Writer) error {
        if err := tx.WriteMessages(ctx, kafka.Message{
            Key:   []byte(out.AggregateID),  // preserve the ordering key downstream
            Value: mustMarshal(transform(out)),
        }); err != nil {
            return err
        }
        // Committing the source offset in the same transaction means the
        // offset advances if and only if the output lands.
        return tx.CommitMessages(ctx, m)
    }); err != nil {
        return err                        // offset NOT advanced: redelivery
    }
    return nil
}

// Kafka 4.x defaults that make this work with no extra configuration:
//   enable.idempotence=true                  (default since 3.0)
//   acks=all                                 (implied by idempotence)
//   max.in.flight.requests.per.connection=5  (required by idempotence)
// Deduplication is per producer SESSION, sequence-numbered. It does not
// deduplicate across a producer restart, and not across instances.

# Consumer-side dedupe by business key: the pattern that makes at-least-once
# safe when the effect lives in PostgreSQL 18. The dedupe row and the
# business row are written in ONE local transaction, so dedupe is atomic
# with the effect. No distributed transaction is involved.

BEGIN;

INSERT INTO processed_messages (consumer, message_id, processed_at)
VALUES ('svc-a', $1, now())
ON CONFLICT (consumer, message_id) DO NOTHING
RETURNING 1;

-- Zero rows returned => already processed. ROLLBACK and ack the message.
-- One row returned  => first delivery. Continue with the business work.

UPDATE accounts SET balance_minor = balance_minor - $2 WHERE id = $1;
INSERT INTO ledger_entries (account_id, delta_minor, reason, ref)
VALUES ($1, -$2, 'charge', $3);

COMMIT;
-- Only now is it safe to acknowledge the broker. If the process dies
-- between COMMIT and the ack, redelivery hits the ON CONFLICT path and
-- is a no-op: at-least-once delivery, exactly-once effect.
--
-- Cost: one index probe and one row per message. At 20k msg/s the table
-- grows by 1.7 B rows/day, so partition it by day and drop partitions
-- behind the maximum redelivery window of the slowest consumer.

CREATE TABLE processed_messages (
    consumer     text        NOT NULL,
    message_id   uuid        NOT NULL,
    processed_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    PRIMARY KEY (consumer, message_id, processed_at)
) PARTITION BY RANGE (processed_at);
GuaranteeWhere the risk livesLoss windowDuplicate windowCorrect use
At-most-onceBroker deletes before ackFull consumer processing timeNone by guaranteeMetrics, cache invalidation, sampling
At-least-onceConsumer crashes before ackNoneProcessing + ack round-tripDefault for all state-changing work
Effectively-onceOutside the declared transaction boundaryNone if the tx commitsCommitted-tx redelivery is filteredETL, projections, read-model rebuilds
Exactly-once (claimed)Any cross-system boundaryUnbounded if the sink is non-transactionalUnbounded if the sink is non-transactional✗ Do not claim it; it is unachievable end to end
At-most-once, batchedBroker deletes the whole batchEntire unacked batchNoneIdempotent batch jobs only

Kafka’s idempotent producer is scoped per producer session, not per application. enable.idempotence=true deduplicates retries within one session by sequence number, and max.in.flight.requests.per.connection ≤ 5 preserves ordering across retries. It does not deduplicate across a producer restart, and it does not deduplicate across two distinct producer instances. The transactional producer with a stable transactional.id closes the restart case by fencing older instances, which is exactly why that identifier must be derived from the logical role rather than from a pod name. RabbitMQ has no equivalent: publisher confirms give at-least-once and the deduplication burden falls entirely on the consumer. SQS FIFO’s MessageDeduplicationId is bounded to a five-minute content-hash window and de-duplicates the send, not the effect.

7.3 Broker architectures: Kafka, RabbitMQ, SQS, NATS and Redis Streams

Compares the dominant brokers on storage model, ordering scope, retention, delivery style and operational cost, and identifies the workload shape each was designed for.

Apache Kafka 4.x is a partitioned, replicated, ordered log; the KRaft metadata quorum replaced ZooKeeper entirely in 4.0, so a three-node broker cluster is self-managing. Records are appended to a partition leader and acknowledged after the configured in-sync-replica count; consumers track offsets, committed either to Kafka or held locally. Retention is time- and size-based and independent of consumption, which is what makes replay and reprocessing possible at all. Kafka is the correct answer for a durable event log, high fan-out, replay requirements, and tens of thousands of messages per second per broker with batching. Its cost is operational weight: partition count is effectively fixed at creation, group rebalancing is nontrivial, and JVM plus page-cache discipline is not trivial to run well.

RabbitMQ 4.x is a broker with a rich routing topology — exchanges, queues, bindings, header matching, dead-letter exchanges — and two queue implementations that behave very differently. Classic queues replicate by mirroring, are limited to a single active master, and can lose in-flight unacknowledged messages on failover. Quorum queues replicate through Raft internally, cap at five nodes, and support explicit at-least-once dead-lettering with a delivery limit; they are the only variant that should be used for state-changing work. RabbitMQ is push-based with a per-consumer prefetch, FIFO per queue, and its retention is a consumer-position concept rather than a time window, which makes it a work-distribution system rather than an event log.

Amazon SQS is a managed, API-only, at-least-once service with a 14-day maximum retention, a default quota of 1,200 TPS per queue, and a visibility-timeout model that is the largest single source of duplicated work in SQS-based systems. FIFO queues add per-message-group ordering and a content-hash deduplication window at roughly twice the cost of standard. NATS JetStream 2.11 is a lightweight single-binary system with hierarchical subject routing, consumer modes offering at-most-once, at-least-once and exactly-once-with-dedupe-window, a choice of retention policies including work-queue, and mirror and source configurations for cross-cluster replication. Redis Streams add consumer groups with an explicit pending-entries list and blocking reads inside the Redis 8.x server; they are fast and operationally simple, but durability is bounded by the persistence mode and they should not be a system of record.

DimensionKafka 4.xRabbitMQ 4.xAmazon SQSNATS JetStream 2.11Redis Streams
Storage modelReplicated partitioned logPer-node queues; quorum queues use RaftFully managed, opaqueReplicated stream, memory + diskIn-memory stream, AOF/RDB optional
Ordering scopePer partition, key hashes to partitionFIFO per queueBest-effort standard; per-group FIFOPer stream subject; sequenced optionsPer stream, per consumer group
Delivery stylePull, consumer groups, offset commitsPush with prefetchPush, visibility timeoutPull (durable) or pushPull, XPENDING list

Throughput shape10k–1M msg/s cluster-wide, batchedThousands to tens of thousands msg/s1,200 TPS per queue by defaultMillions msg/s in benchmarkBounded by memory and persistence
Routing flexibilityTopics and key hash only✓ Topic, fanout, direct, headersFIFO name carries the group id✓ Hierarchical subjects with wildcardsSingle stream plus consumer groups

# Kafka 4.x broker configuration: KRaft combined mode, three nodes, no ZooKeeper.
# node.id must be unique and stable; it is the broker's identity in the quorum.
process.roles=broker,controller
node.id=1
controller.quorum.voters=1@kafka-1:9093,2@kafka-2:9093,3@kafka-3:9093
listeners=PLAINTEXT://:9092,CONTROLLER://:9093
advertised.listeners=PLAINTEXT://kafka-1:9092
controller.listener.names=CONTROLLER
listener.security.protocol.map=CONTROLLER:PLAINTEXT,PLAINTEXT:PLAINTEXT

# Durability settings worth stating explicitly rather than inheriting.
log.dirs=/var/lib/kafka
num.partitions=12
default.replication.factor=3
min.insync.replicas=2
offsets.topic.replication.factor=3
transaction.state.log.replication.factor=3
transaction.state.log.min.isr=2
log.cleaner.enable=true
auto.create.topics.enable=false
# Never elect an out-of-sync leader: trading consistency for availability
# is a decision that must be made deliberately, in writing, by a human.
unclean.leader.election.enable=false
group.initial.rebalance.delay.ms=3000
# Leave the page cache in charge of I/O: do not raise -Xmx above ~6 GiB.

# RabbitMQ 4.x: a quorum queue with explicit at-least-once dead-lettering and
# a TTL-based retry loop. Quorum queues are the only variant to run in prod.
# Declared declaratively through the management API so the topology is
# reviewable in version control and diffable across environments.

curl -sS -u "$RMQ_USER:$RMQ_PASS" -H 'content-type: application/json' \
  -X POST http://rabbitmq:15672/api/definitions -d '{
  "vhosts": [{"name": "/prod"}],
  "queues": [
    {"name": "orders.process", "vhost": "/prod", "arguments": {
       "x-queue-type": "quorum",
       "x-delivery-limit": 5,
       "x-dead-letter-exchange": "orders.retry",
       "x-dead-letter-routing-key": "orders.process.retry",
       "x-quorum-initial-group-size": 3}},
    {"name": "orders.process.retry", "vhost": "/prod", "arguments": {
       "x-queue-type": "quorum",
       "x-message-ttl": 60000,
       "x-dead-letter-exchange": "orders",
       "x-dead-letter-routing-key": "orders.process"}},
    {"name": "orders.process.dlq", "vhost": "/prod", "arguments": {
       "x-queue-type": "quorum",
       "x-message-ttl": 259200000}}
  ],
  "bindings": [
    {"source": "orders.retry", "vhost": "/prod",
     "destination": "orders.process", "destination_type": "queue",
     "routing_key": "orders.process.retry"},
    {"source": "orders.dlx", "vhost": "/prod",
     "destination": "orders.process.dlq", "destination_type": "queue",
     "routing_key": "#"}
  ],
  "policies": [
    {"vhost": "/prod", "name": "consumer-timeout", "pattern": ".*",
     "apply-to": "queues",
     "definition": {"consumer-timeout": 1800000}}
  ]
}'

# Read live queue state without consuming, and confirm the queue TYPE:
rabbitmqctl list_queues --vhost /prod name type messages_ready \
  messages_unacknowledged consumers | grep -E 'orders|NAME'
# A classic queue here means the deployment is not what the runbook claims.

Quorum queues versus classic queues is the decision that will bite you. Classic mirrored queues in RabbitMQ 4.x have a single active master, and when that master fails, in-flight unacknowledged messages held on it can be lost regardless of mirroring; the deprecation warnings in the broker log are not optional advice. Quorum queues replicate through Raft, require an odd node count of three or five, cap the group at five replicas, and have a different throughput profile under heavy fan-out — which is why the delivery limit and dead-letter arguments must be configured explicitly at declaration time. If you have inherited classic queues, migrate by draining, redeclaring as quorum, and republishing; not by an in-place conversion.

7.4 Delivery mechanics: push, pull, prefetch and consumer groups

Compares push and pull delivery at mechanism level, explains the consumer-group rebalance protocol, and identifies the prefetch setting that determines whether a slow consumer is throttled or destructive.

Push delivery means the broker writes to a consumer’s socket, which is efficient when message rate and consumer capacity are well matched and pathological when they are not: the prefetch window fills with undelivered messages, a slow consumer’s window grows, memory pressure moves to the broker, and the whole cluster degrades because of one laggard. Pull delivery inverts the control — the consumer asks for at most N messages and is directly responsible for how fast it drains them, so backpressure lives where the capacity constraint actually is. Modern Kafka and NATS are pull-only for exactly this reason; RabbitMQ and SQS are push-with-a-pull-equivalent because the client protocol assumes it, and the prefetch or visibility timeout is the knob that approximates backpressure.

Prefetch is the most consequential setting in the domain and is routinely left at a default that is wrong. A prefetch of 1,000 on a consumer whose handler takes 200 ms means a single in-flight message occupies the consumer for 200 ms while 999 more sit pinned in its unacknowledged set: a rebalance now takes as long as it takes to redeliver a thousand messages, and a crash re-executes a thousand units of work. The rule is prefetch ≥ processing_time × target_throughput_per_consumer — enough to keep the consumer busy and no more. For a 50 msg/s consumer with a 100 ms handler that is 5, not 1,000.

Kafka consumer groups implement work distribution through a group-coordinator protocol on the __consumer_offsets topic: a member joins, the coordinator assigns partitions across members, and any membership change triggers a rebalance. RangeAssignor assigns contiguous partition ranges per topic per consumer and will overload the first consumer when topic count and consumer count are not integral multiples. RoundRobinAssignor balances better but breaks per-topic affinity. StickyAssignor minimises partition movement so cached state survives rebalances. Cooperative sticky rebalancing, selected with partition.assignment.strategy=CooperativeStickyAssignor, performs an incremental two-stage rebalance rather than a stop-the-world one, and is the correct default from Kafka 4.x onward.

# Consumer configuration: the four settings that determine rebalance behaviour
# and duplicate volume. Everything else is a tuning detail.

group.id=order-projection
enable.auto.commit=false          # manual commit only; auto-commit is a footgun
auto.offset.reset=earliest         # replay consumers only; use latest in prod
max.poll.interval.ms=300000        # 5 min: the real deadline for a whole batch
session.timeout.ms=45000           # safely below the broker group.max.session
max.poll.records=200               # batch size, NOT prefetch
partition.assignment.strategy=org.apache.kafka.clients.consumer.CooperativeStickyAssignor
# Cooperative rebalancing: incremental, no stop-the-world, no full re-fetch.
# Trade-off: the first rebalance after enabling it is slower, because the
# member must first revoke everything held under the previous protocol.

# Why max.poll.interval.ms dominates duplicate volume:
#   handler 2 s x 200 records = 400 s, which exceeds the 300 s interval
#   => the member is evicted mid-batch, partitions are reassigned, and the
#      entire uncommitted batch is re-fetched by another member.
# Rule: max.poll.records x p99(handler time) must stay under
#       max.poll.interval.ms with at least 2x headroom.

fetch.min.bytes=1048576            # 1 MiB: batched fetches, fewer syscalls
fetch.max.wait.ms=100              # do not block the poll thread indefinitely
max.partition.fetch.bytes=5242880  # 5 MiB per partition per fetch

// A correct pull loop: bounded fetch, bounded batch, explicit commit AFTER
// the work is durable, and a heartbeater decoupled from processing so a
// slow handler does not get the member evicted.
func (c *Consumer) Run(ctx context.Context) error {
    for {
        batch, err := c.reader.FetchBatch(ctx, 200, 5*time.Second)
        if err != nil {
            return err
        }
        if len(batch) == 0 {
            continue
        }
        var last *kafka.Message
        for _, m := range batch {
            if err := c.handle(ctx, m); err != nil {
                if errors.Is(err, ErrPoison) {
                    c.deadLetter(ctx, m, err)   // advance, never loop
                } else {
                    c.reader.Unread(m)          // return the whole batch
                    return err                  // rebalance, retry later
                }
            }
            last = m
        }
        // Commit only up to the last SUCCESSFUL message. If message 7 of
        // 200 failed, offsets 1..6 commit and 7.. are redelivered.
        if last != nil {
            if err := c.reader.CommitMessages(ctx, last); err != nil {
                return err
            }
        }
    }
}

// Heartbeats must be decoupled from the handler loop. The contract is
// session.timeout.ms comfortably above the heartbeat interval, and
// max.poll.interval.ms comfortably above the batch processing time.
cfg := kafka.ReaderConfig{
    Brokers:        brokers,
    GroupID:        "order-projection",
    Topic:          "orders.v2",
    MinBytes:       1 << 20,
    MaxBytes:       10 << 20,
    MaxWait:        100 * time.Millisecond,
    CommitInterval: 0,                  // synchronous, explicit control
    StartOffset:    kafka.LastOffset,
    WatchPartitionChanges: true,
    Logger:         zap.NewNop(),       // the library is extremely chatty
}
MechanismBroker pushClient pullServer-side consumer

Make processing time a first-class metric. Export consumer_poll_lag_ms — the interval between the poll returning and the batch commit completing — and alert when it exceeds 50% of max.poll.interval.ms. Every eviction-by-timeout incident in a Kafka deployment traces back to this number having been invisible, and the failure presents as mysterious duplicate processing rather than as a timeout, which sends the investigation in entirely the wrong direction.

7.5 Ordering, partition keys and hot keys

Explains that ordering in a partitioned system is scoped to a key and a single partition, and quantifies both the throughput ceiling and the skew failure that keying introduces.

Total ordering over a distributed log is not achievable at scale, and the correct mental model is scoped ordering: the broker guarantees that records with the same partition key arrive at a consumer in production order, and promises nothing across keys. Kafka achieves this by hashing the key to a partition with the default Hash partitioner; RabbitMQ achieves it by routing a consistent subset of messages to a single queue. The design consequence is that the ordering key must be the aggregate identity: order_id for order events, account_id for ledger entries. Keying order events on customer_id is defensible only if all of a customer’s orders genuinely must serialise, and it silently reduces parallelism to the number of active customers, which is usually far below the number of active orders.

The failure mode is the hot key. A partition’s throughput ceiling is the broker’s per-partition limit — on a typical three-replica Kafka cluster roughly 10–40 MB/s per partition and on the order of 50k–200k records per second depending on batch size and acks. A single hot key, whether a national account, a mega-tenant, or a record with no key at all, sends all of its traffic to one partition and one consumer. The tell is a lag histogram where one partition is minutes behind while the others are current. Mitigations in order of preference: salt the key across a bounded number of sub-shards to split load at the cost of ordering within the entity; route the hot stream to a dedicated topic with its own partition budget; or accept the serialisation if the aggregate’s own write rate is genuinely low, which is frequently the case and often the correct answer.

// Key selection is an architectural decision, not a default. Three shapes,
// three different guarantees. Choose by the aggregate, not by the payload.

func PartitionKey(ev Event) string {
    switch ev.Type {
    case OrderPlaced, OrderPaid, OrderShipped:
        // Per-ORDER ordering. Two orders for one customer can be processed
        // concurrently: this is what you want for scale.
        return "order:" + ev.OrderID

    case AccountDebited, AccountCredited:
        // Per-ACCOUNT ordering, non-negotiable. A ledger that interleaves a
        // debit and a credit for one account is not a ledger.
        // Trade: throughput is capped at the account's own event rate.
        return "account:" + ev.AccountID

    case TelemetrySample:
        // No key at all: the default partitioner round-robins and nothing
        // requires ordering. Keying telemetry by device is a classic way
        // to create a hot partition for a fleet of one device.
        return ""
    }
    return ""
}

// Salting: split a hot key across N sub-partitions, sacrificing ordering
// WITHIN the entity for N-fold parallelism. Correct only if the consumer
// tolerates reordering within the entity, for example by sequencing on an
// embedded version number and buffering until the gap closes.
func SaltedKey(key string, shards int) string {
    h := fnv.New32a()
    _, _ = h.Write([]byte(key))
    return fmt.Sprintf("%s-%02d", key, h.Sum32()%uint32(shards))
}

# Find the hot partition before your customers do. Run under load, not in
# production, and remember that per-partition figures are asymmetric.

# 1. Per-partition consumer lag, worst first.
kafka-consumer-groups.sh --bootstrap-server kafka-1:9092 \
  --describe --group order-projection --offsets 2>/dev/null \
  | awk 'NR==1 || $6+0 > 1000 {print $1, $2, $3, "LAG="$6}' \
  | sort -t= -k2 -rn | head -20

# 2. Key-distribution check: hash the keys, bucket by partition, look for skew.
kcat -b kafka-1:9092 -t orders.v2 -C -o -1 -e -q -f '%k\n' 2>/dev/null \
  | python3 -c "
import sys, collections
b = collections.Counter()
for line in sys.stdin:
    k = line.rstrip('\n')
    h = 0
    for ch in k:                     # murmur2, matching the Java partitioner
        h = (h * 31 + ord(ch)) & 0xFFFFFFFF
        h ^= h >> 16
    b[h & 11] += 1                 # 12 partitions
tot = sum(b.values())
for p, n in sorted(b.items()):
    print(f'p{p}: {n:>9} {100*n/tot:5.1f}%')
print(f'expected per partition: {100/12:.1f}%')
"
# Healthy on 12 partitions is 8.3% +/- 1% each. Anything above 20% on one
# partition is a hot key and a scheduled incident.

# 3. Broker-side view, per-partition log size and offline replica count.
kafka-topics.sh --bootstrap-server kafka-1:9092 --describe --under-replicated-partitions \
  --topic orders.v2

Keying everything by tenant or customer id is the most common cause of a partitioned broker that cannot scale. A system with 12 partitions and three enterprise customers generating 40% of traffic between them has three partitions doing most of the work, a consumer group with 12 members where 3 are saturated and 9 are idle, and headroom that exists on paper only. The second warning is subtler: ordering across keys is a real business requirement far more often than teams expect. If two events about the same order can be processed out of order, then "the order is shipped" can be applied before "the order is paid", and the invariant you believed the queue gave you was never there. Write the invariant as a test against the consumer, because the queue will not enforce it for you.

7.6 Dead-letter queues, poison messages and retry ladders

Specifies the retry ladder as an exponential sequence of bounded delay queues, and the detection and quarantine procedure for messages that no retry can fix.

A dead-letter queue is not an error handler; it is a quarantine boundary. Its purpose is to move a message that cannot currently be processed out of the main flow so that one bad record does not consume consumer capacity indefinitely, while preserving the payload and enough context to diagnose it later. A DLQ without a replay path is a data-loss bucket with extra steps, so the design question is never "do we have a DLQ" but "who reads it, how quickly, and by what mechanism is it replayed". The operational requirement is an alarm on DLQ depth greater than zero, routed to a human, because a DLQ that fills silently is the failure mode this pattern exists to eliminate.

Retries belong in a ladder, not in the consumer loop. In-process retry with a sleep is the worst option: it occupies the worker, delays every message queued behind the poison one, and multiplies time-to-detect by the retry count. A ladder republishes the message to a delay queue whose TTL expires and whose dead-letter route returns it to the main queue, so the delay happens in the broker where it consumes no consumer capacity. Three or four rungs is the practical maximum — for example 10 s, 60 s, 10 min — after which the message is a genuine poison and belongs in the DLQ. Every rung must be a separate queue, because a single shared retry queue with per-message TTLs suffers head-of-line blocking where a long-TTL message holds a short-TTL one behind it.

Classification matters more than the retry count. A message that fails because a downstream returned 503 is transient and belongs on the ladder. A message that fails because its payload violates a schema is permanent, and retrying it five times just delays discovery by five minutes while producing five identical alerts. Distinguish by error class, not by attempt count: retry only on timeouts, connection resets, 429 and explicit 503; route deserialization failures, constraint violations and 404 on a required parent straight to the DLQ with a reason code.

// Retry ladder: classify first, then count. Sleeping in the consumer is wrong
// because it holds the worker and delays every message behind the poison.
type Router struct {
    main  publisher.Publisher
    rungs []Rung          // one delay queue per rung: 10s, 60s, 10m
    dlq   publisher.Publisher
    clock func() time.Time // injected, so the ladder is testable
}

type Rung struct {
    Delay time.Duration
    Queue string
}

func (r *Router) Handle(ctx context.Context, m Message, err error) error {
    if IsPermanent(err) {
        // Schema violation, 404 on a required parent, constraint failure.
        // Retrying these is pure cost: identical failure, delayed discovery.
        r.dlq.Publish(ctx, m.WithReason(ReasonOf(err)))
        return nil                       // ack: it will never succeed
    }

    attempt := m.Headers.Attempts()       // survives the dead-letter round trip
    if attempt >= len(r.rungs) {
        r.dlq.Publish(ctx, m.WithReason(ErrExhausted).
            WithAttempts(attempt).WithLastError(err.Error()))
        metrics.Poison.Inc("queue", m.Topic, "reason", ReasonOf(err))
        return nil
    }

    rung := r.rungs[attempt]
    next := m.WithAttempt(attempt + 1)
    next.Headers.Set("x-first-seen", r.clock().UTC().Format(time.RFC3339))
    next.Headers.Set("x-original-topic", m.Topic)
    next.Headers.Set("x-last-error", truncate(err.Error(), 512))
    r.main.Publish(ctx, next, PublishTo(rung.Queue))  // TTL enforced in broker
    return nil                             // ack: the ladder owns it now
}

func IsPermanent(err error) bool {
    var pe *PermanentError
    return errors.As(err, &pe) ||
        errors.Is(err, ErrSchemaViolation) ||
        errors.Is(err, ErrForeignKeyMissing)
}

# SQS: native DLQ plus an explicit redrive policy, and a replay procedure.
# The native DLQ catches messages rejected or expired past MaxReceiveCount;
# the redrive policy is what lets you move them back without hand-rolling
# a consumer. Use both: the DLQ is the safety net, the policy is the path.

aws sqs create-queue --queue-name orders-process-dlq \
  --attributes '{"MessageRetentionPeriod":"1209600"}'   # 14 days, the max

aws sqs create-queue --queue-name orders-process \
  --attributes '{"VisibilityTimeout":"120","MessageRetentionPeriod":"345600"}'
aws sqs set-queue-attributes --queue-url "$MAIN_URL" --attributes '{
  "RedrivePolicy": "{\"deadLetterTargetArn\":\"'"$DLQ_ARN"'\",\"maxReceiveCount\":\"5\"}"
}'

# Alert on DLQ depth >= 1, not on a ratio: any poison is worth a human.
aws cloudwatch put-metric-alarm --alarm-name orders-dlq-nonempty \
  --namespace AWS/SQS --metric-name ApproximateNumberOfMessagesVisible \
  --dimensions Name=QueueName,Value=orders-process-dlq \
  --statistic Maximum --period 60 --evaluation-periods 1 \
  --threshold 1 --comparison-operator GreaterThanOrEqualToThreshold \
  --alarm-actions "$SNS_ARN"

# Replay with a hard cap and full audit, so a bad replay cannot re-poison
# the main queue. Drain to a file, inspect, then re-send.
aws sqs receive-message --queue-url "$DLQ_URL" \
  --max-number-of-messages 10 --visibility-timeout 30 \
  --message-attribute-names All > dlq-batch-1.json
jq -r '.Messages[].Body' dlq-batch-1.json | head -3 | jq .
aws sqs send-message-batch --queue-url "$MAIN_URL" \
  --entries file://replay-entries.json
# Use the original MessageId as the batch Entry Id, so a replay cannot
# duplicate within the 5-minute dedupe window. Then re-check SenderFault:
#   jq -r '.Messages[] | [.MessageId, .Attributes.SenderFault] | @tsv'
# SenderFault=true means the SQS API rejected it: a bad payload, not a
# consumer bug. That distinction saves hours of downstream investigation.
Replay strategyMechanismOrdering preservedDuplicate riskUse when
Offset resetSeek the consumer group to a timestamp✓ Yes, from that point onRe-processes everything after itBug in a projection; Kafka only
DLQ drain and re-sendReceive, inspect, send back✗ Reordered by SQSPossible; use MessageId as batch IdIsolated poison set
Side-stream replayRe-publish from the original log✓ YesNone if the log is the sourceFull reprojection, Kafka/NATS
Compensating re-driveA consumer drains the DLQ at low rateApproximateNone: the DLQ is consumed onceSustained DLQ pressure
Dead-letter and dropAlert, then deleten/aDeliberate data loss✗ Only with a signed-off retention policy

SQS visibility timeouts are a correctness parameter, not a performance knob. The default is 30 s, and if your handler’s p99 exceeds it the message reappears on another consumer while the first is still processing — a guaranteed duplicate under load, precisely when it is least affordable. Set the visibility timeout comfortably above the p99.9 handler time, and if a handler must run longer, extend it from inside the handler with ChangeMessageVisibility rather than globally. Note the interaction with the DLQ: MaxReceiveCount counts receipts, so a message that times out three times before its first successful processing is already one step closer to being discarded. Instrument the receipt-count distribution, not just the DLQ depth.

Exercises 7

  1. Stand up the same workload — 20,000 msg/s, 1 KiB payload, one consumer group — on Kafka 4.x, RabbitMQ 4.x quorum queues, NATS JetStream 2.11 and Redis Streams on comparable hardware. Deliverable: sustained throughput, p99 produce and consume latency, and CPU per million messages, with the batch size each system required to reach it.
  2. Demonstrate replay as a first-class capability for exactly one of the four brokers and document precisely what makes it impossible in the other three. Deliverable: a rebuild-a-read-model procedure for the winner with measured time-to-complete, and a written argument per loser.
  3. Convert a classic RabbitMQ queue to a quorum queue in a real environment: drain, redeclare, republish, verify counts at each step. Deliverable: the runbook, the message-count reconciliation, and a written point-in-time recovery plan for a failure halfway through the migration.
  4. Characterise SQS exactly-once processing empirically by sending bursts with duplicated MessageDeduplicationId values straddling the five-minute window. Deliverable: a results table across the window boundary, and the consequent design rule for idempotency keys.
  5. Build a four-rung retry ladder with error classification in front of it, inject a mix of transient and permanent failures at known rates, and measure the consumer-time consumed by each failure class. Deliverable: the comparison against in-process retry with sleep, quantifying the wasted capacity avoided.

8 · Distributed Transactions, Sagas and Consistency Models

8.1 Two-phase commit and the blocking problem8.2 Two-phase locking and its concurrency costs8.3 Sagas: choreography and orchestration8.4 The transactional outbox and idempotency keys8.5 Compensation and semantic rollback8.6 Choosing a consistency model: decision matrix

This section enumerates the mechanisms that can span a database boundary, then establishes why almost all of them are unavailable in practice at scale. Two-phase commit is analysed at mechanism level including the in-doubt window and the coordinator-failure failure mode; the pessimistic cousin that holds row locks across the same boundary is treated separately because its cost profile differs and its failure mode is deadlock rather than blocking. The saga pattern is presented in both choreography and orchestration form with the operational tax of each made explicit. The transactional outbox and the idempotency key are given as the two production primitives that make at-least-once delivery safe, and the section terminates in a decision matrix mapping business consequence to consistency strength.

8.1 Two-phase commit and the blocking problem

Analyses the two-phase commit protocol at mechanism level and identifies the coordinator-failure window that disqualifies it as a general cross-service transaction primitive.

Two-phase commit separates a distributed transaction into a prepare phase, in which every participant durably records a vote and takes its locks, and a commit phase in which the decision is broadcast. The safety argument is short: once every participant has voted to commit, the commit must hold, because a participant that voted to commit is forbidden from ever rolling back. The availability argument fails in one specific and unavoidable place.

The failure window is this. If the coordinator crashes after a quorum of participants have voted to commit but before the decision is durably recorded and delivered to all participants, then participants that voted are holding locks and waiting, while participants that have not yet voted are free to proceed or abort. A recovering coordinator must resolve this from its durable log; if the decision was never persisted, the correct action is to abort, but participants that already voted commit must honour their vote. Recovery therefore requires every participant to retain a durable record of its vote and to accept a coordinator connection for the full in-doubt period, bounded by transaction_timeout — 10 minutes in PostgreSQL 18, and effectively 60 seconds in MySQL 9.x where binary-log and lock timeouts dominate.
