A participant that cannot reach its coordinator during that window is blocked: it can neither commit nor roll back, and it continues to hold its locks.

Phase 1 (PREPARE)                       Phase 2 (COMMIT)
----------------                       ----------------
C: fsync "prepare" record   ---> P1: write undo/redo, fsync,
 |                                       acquire locks,
 |                                       fsync "VOTE COMMIT"
 |<----------------------------------  VOTE COMMIT
 |   (3 of 5 participants responded)
 |
 X  C CRASHES HERE  -- in-doubt window -->  P2..P5 still waiting
 |
 +-- P1 holds row locks and cannot proceed
 +-- P2 has not voted. Under REPEATABLE READ or SERIALIZABLE it BLOCKS
 |   on P1's locks. Under READ COMMITTED it does NOT block, and may read
 |   a state that is subsequently rolled back: an anomaly 2PC does not
 |   prevent and that READ COMMITTED papers over.
 +-- Resolution requires a reachable coordinator, or an operator running
     COMMIT PREPARED / ROLLBACK PREPARED by hand against each gid.

PostgreSQL 18:
  max_prepared_transactions = 100          # 0 disables 2PC entirely
  idle_in_transaction_session_timeout = '60s'
  lock_timeout = '5s'
MySQL 9.x:
  innodb_rollback_on_timeout = OFF        # default; setting ON is a footgun

The performance profile is the second disqualifier. A distributed transaction converts N local commits into a two-round network protocol plus N forced fsyncs, and lock hold time rises from microseconds to the duration of the slowest participant’s round-trip. On cloud inter-zone networking at 1–2 ms RTT, a five-participant 2PC adds 12–40 ms of pure coordination latency, and every row those participants hold is unavailable to all other writers for that entire window. Above approximately one participant that is not co-located, the throughput and tail-latency cost of 2PC exceeds re-executing the business transaction under idempotency, which is the entire premise of the saga pattern.

-- PostgreSQL 18: two-phase commit, used deliberately and at low volume.
-- Correct for cross-database migration cutover. Not for the request path.
ALTER SYSTEM SET max_prepared_transactions = 100;
ALTER SYSTEM SET idle_in_transaction_session_timeout = '60s';
ALTER SYSTEM SET statement_timeout = '30s';
ALTER SYSTEM SET lock_timeout = '5s';
SELECT pg_reload_conf();

BEGIN;
INSERT INTO ledger (account_id, delta_minor, ref)
  VALUES (1001, -4999, 'order-7f3a');
PREPARE TRANSACTION 'order_7f3a_capture_2026-09-29T11:04:19Z';
-- The transaction is now IN DOUBT: locks are held, the session is gone,
-- and only COMMIT PREPARED or ROLLBACK PREPARED can end it.

-- Operator view of the in-doubt set, with age so the worst case is visible:
SELECT gid,
       prepared,
       now() - prepared AS age,
       database,
       owner
FROM pg_prepared_xacts
ORDER BY prepared;

COMMIT PREPARED 'order_7f3a_capture_2026-09-29T11:04:19Z';
-- Any row in that list older than 10 minutes is an incident: that is the
-- default transaction_timeout, and it has almost certainly been blocking
-- writers on those rows for the whole period.

Where two-phase commit is still the right answer. The protocol is correct in a narrow set of situations: cross-shard and cross-database migrations where a cutover must be all-or-nothing; XA-coordinated writes into legacy systems that expose no idempotency; and coordination inside an existing consensus-replicated log, where a quorum already exists and 2PC adds no new failure domain. It is also, and this is the point most often missed, the correct answer inside a single database, where it is not a distributed protocol at all — a single-node or primary-replica PostgreSQL commit is atomic by construction because the write-ahead log makes it so, and durability and atomicity come from the same mechanism. The mistake is generalising that local property across a network boundary where the log is no longer shared.

PropertySingle-DB transactionTwo-phase commitSaga with compensation
Atomicity✓ True, indivisible✓ True, indivisible✗ Eventual; per-step atomic only
Added latency0.2–2 ms, one WAL fsync12–40 ms for 5 participantsSum of step latencies, off the path
Behaviour under partitionn/a, single node✗ Blocks on the coordinator✓ Degrades to local progress
Primary failure modeTransaction aborts, no residueIn-doubt lock retentionA compensation step itself fails
Isolation from readersMVCC or locks, well definedSame, but held for the whole protocolNone; readers see intermediate states
Implementation costNone, already presentXA managers, prepared-xact runbookPer-step compensation plus idempotency keys

8.2 Two-phase locking and its concurrency costs

Distinguishes pessimistic locking from the optimistic concurrency that distributed systems actually adopt, and quantifies the cost of lock escalation and deadlock on a contended workload.

Two-phase locking is the classical protocol underlying shared-nothing SQL databases: a transaction acquires shared locks for reads and exclusive locks for writes during a growing phase, and cannot acquire new locks during a shrinking phase, which is what guarantees that lock graphs remain acyclic and deadlocks cannot form. The protocol is correct, and its problem is entirely one of cost. Lock acquisition is a durable, ordered, replicated operation in any serious database — a row lock in PostgreSQL 18 with max_connections above 1 acquires tuple locks in shared buffers and may escalate, an X row lock participates in a predicate-lock mechanism under SERIALIZABLE, and MySQL 9.x’s InnoDB records locks in a dedicated structure. The write amplification is real: an UPDATE of one row in a table of ten million is one row touched but three modified pages — the heap page, the index page, and WAL — plus the lock record.

The distributed-systems objection is different from the database objection. In a single database, pessimistic locking is affordable because the lock manager is in the same address space and lock waits resolve in microseconds. Once the lock manager is separated by a network hop, a lock wait becomes a timeout, a timeout becomes a retry, and a retry under contention increases contention. The practical rule is that pessimistic locking does not scale across a network boundary, and no serious design attempts it. The alternative is optimistic concurrency control: read without a lock, validate at commit, and on a conflict either retry or fail. PostgreSQL’s MVCC gives snapshot isolation for free, with write-write conflicts detected as a serialization failure at commit; serializable isolation adds predicate locking, which detects phantoms at the cost of SSI bookkeeping and an abort rate that must be measured, not assumed.

-- Optimistic concurrency in PostgreSQL 18: no read locks, conflict at write.
-- The WHERE clause on version IS the compare-and-swap. One round trip, one
-- index probe, and a conflict is a zero-row update rather than a wait.

UPDATE inventory
   SET available = available - 1,
       version   = version + 1
 WHERE sku = $1
   AND version = $2          -- the version the caller read
RETURNING version, available;
-- 0 rows => someone else won the race. The caller decides: retry, or
-- surface a 409. Never sleep and retry blindly; that is where lock
-- convoys are born.

-- Optimistic locking in the ORM layer (Go 1.25 + sqlc-generated code):
--   1 row updated  -> proceed
--   0 rows updated -> ErrOptimisticLock, propagate as HTTP 409
UPDATE accounts
   SET balance_minor = $2, version = version + 1
 WHERE id = $1 AND version = $3;
-- rowsAffected == 0 means version mismatch: the caller read a stale
-- balance and must re-read. This is the entire conflict-detection story
-- and it costs nothing but a column.

-- SERIALIZABLE: add real predicate locks. Measure the abort rate before
-- enabling it on a hot path; 1-5% is normal and acceptable, 20% is a
-- design problem that no amount of retrying will fix.
SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE;
-- On 40001 serialization_failure, the whole transaction is retried
-- from the top, bounded at 3 attempts, then surfaced as 503.

# Deadlock and lock-contention diagnostics, PostgreSQL 18.
# These are the queries to have in the runbook before the incident, not
# after it. Both are safe to run on a live primary.

-- 1. Current lock graph. Every granted row in pg_locks, with the
--    transaction that holds it. Blocked rows show granted = false.
SELECT blocked.pid              AS blocked_pid,
       blocked.wait_event_type,
       blocked.wait_event,
       blocking.pid             AS blocking_pid,
       blocked.query            AS blocked_query,
       now() - blocked.query_start AS blocked_for
  FROM pg_stat_activity blocked
  JOIN pg_locks bl ON bl.pid = blocked.pid AND NOT bl.granted
  JOIN pg_locks kl ON kl.locktype = bl.locktype
                  AND kl.database IS NOT DISTINCT FROM bl.database
                  AND kl.relation IS NOT DISTINCT FROM bl.relation
                  AND kl.page IS NOT DISTINCT FROM bl.page
                  AND kl.tuple IS NOT DISTINCT FROM bl.tuple
                  AND kl.transactionid IS NOT DISTINCT FROM bl.transactionid
                  AND kl.pid != bl.pid
                  AND kl.granted
  JOIN pg_stat_activity blocking ON blocking.pid = kl.pid
 WHERE cardinality(pg_blocking_pids(blocked.pid)) > 0;

-- 2. Longest-running transactions: the usual root cause of a lock convoy.
SELECT pid, state, now() - xact_start AS xact_age, wait_event_type,
       left(query, 120) AS query
  FROM pg_stat_activity
 WHERE xact_start IS NOT NULL
   AND state != 'idle'
 ORDER BY xact_start
 LIMIT 20;
-- Anything over 60 s in READ COMMITTED is a bug, not a slow query.
-- The cure is a statement_timeout, not a bigger statement_timeout.

-- 3. Deadlock detection: PostgreSQL logs the full graph automatically.
SELECT * FROM pg_stat_database WHERE datname = current_database();
-- deadlocks > 0 means lock ordering is inconsistent between code paths.
-- The fix is a documented global lock order, enforced by a test.
Isolation levelPostgreSQL 18MySQL 9.x InnoDBBlocked readsWrite conflicts
READ UNCOMMITTEDMaps to READ COMMITTED✓ SupportedDirty readsLast write wins
READ COMMITTED✓ Default; statement snapshot✓ Default✗ No dirty readsLost update possible
REPEATABLE READ✓ Transaction snapshot✓ Default; gap locks added✗ NoneSerialization failure
SERIALIZABLE✓ SSI predicate locksAll locks, plain reads locked✗ NoneAbort with 40001; measure rate
SKIP LOCKED✓ Work-queue claim pattern✓ SupportedSkips locked rowsTwo workers, one row, no wait
NOWAIT✓ FOR UPDATE NOWAIT✓ NOWAIT✗ Errors 55P03Fails fast rather than queueing

Two-phase locking across services is not a slow design, it is a broken one. The moment the lock manager is behind a network hop, a lock wait is a timeout; a timeout is a retry; a retry under contention increases contention, and the system enters a state where throughput is inversely proportional to load. SELECT FOR UPDATE on a row is the correct tool inside one database and has no meaning across two. Related trap: SELECT FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED is the correct pattern for a PostgreSQL 18 work queue and a terrible pattern for a business invariant, because skipping the locked row means the invariant was never checked. If the row is missing you have discovered it should have existed, which is a different problem from the one you queued for.

8.3 Sagas: choreography and orchestration

Contrasts the event-driven and command-driven saga topologies on coupling, failure visibility and recovery, and specifies the orchestrator state machine that makes a long-running transaction observable.

A saga is a distributed transaction decomposed into a sequence of local transactions, each committing independently, with a compensating local transaction defined for each forward step. There is no atomic commit and there is no rollback in the database sense: at every instant the system is in a state that is consistent with some prefix of the intended sequence, and the goal is to reach the full sequence or to reach a compensated state that is acceptable. The pattern is the correct default for any workflow that crosses a service boundary and involves a business step that cannot be rolled back by any database operation — sending an email, charging a card, dispatching a shipment, calling a third-party API.

The two topologies differ in who holds the sequence. Choreography distributes the sequence: each service subscribes to an event, reacts, and publishes the next. The coupling is low and there is no central component to deploy, but the workflow is implicit — the actual sequence lives in the union of six subscribers' code, and answering "what is the current state of this order?" requires a trace across all of them. Orchestration centralises the sequence in a saga coordinator that issues commands to each participant and records each step’s outcome. The coupling is explicit and the workflow is a first-class, queryable object, at the cost of a new stateful component that itself needs to be highly available. The practical rule is that choreography works to a depth of roughly three to four participating services and becomes unmaintainable beyond that; past that threshold, orchestrate.

// Orchestrator: the saga is a durable state machine, not a function.
// Persist every transition before acting, so a coordinator crash resumes
// rather than restarts. (Rust 1.90, tokio + SQLx against PostgreSQL 18.)

#[derive(Clone, Copy, PartialEq)]
enum Step { ReserveStock, ChargeCard, AllocateShipment, ConfirmOrder }

struct SagaInstance {
    id:         Uuid,
    order_id:   Uuid,
    current:    Step,
    completed:  Vec<Step>,   // the compensation stack, in reverse order
    version:    i64,           // optimistic lock on the row itself
    deadline:   DateTime<Utc>,
}

impl SagaOrchestrator {
    async fn step(&self, saga_id: Uuid) -> Result<(), SagaError> {
        // 1. Claim the next step under a row lock, with a deadline so a
        //    crashed saga cannot sit in a terminal limbo forever.
        let mut tx = self.pool.begin().await?;
        let saga: SagaInstance = sqlx::query_as!(
            "SELECT id, order_id, current, completed, version, deadline
               FROM saga_instance
              WHERE id = $1 AND status = 'running' AND version = $2
                AND deadline > now()
              FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED", saga_id, expected_version)
            .fetch_optional(&mut *tx).await?
            .ok_or(SagaError::NotClaimable)?;

        let outcome = self.execute(saga.current, &saga).await;   // idempotent step

        // 2. Record the outcome and the compensation to run if a later
        //    step fails. Persist BEFORE acking the triggering event.
        sqlx::query!(
            "UPDATE saga_instance
                SET completed = completed || $2::step[],
                    current = $3, version = version + 1, updated_at = now()
              WHERE id = $1",
            saga.id, vec![saga.current], next_step(saga.current))
            .execute(&mut *tx).await?;
        tx.commit().await?;

        match outcome {
            Ok(_) => self.advance(saga_id).await,        // schedule the next step
            Err(e) => self.compensate(saga_id, e).await, // unwind the stack
        }
    }

    // Compensation runs the stack in REVERSE and must tolerate its own
    // failures: a failed compensation is retried, not abandoned.
    async fn compensate(&self, saga_id: Uuid, cause: SagaError) -> Result<(), SagaError> {
        for step in self.completed_stack(saga_id).await?.into_iter().rev() {
            match self.compensate_step(step).await {
                Ok(_) => metrics.Compensated.WithLabel(step.as_str()).inc(),
                Err(e) => {
                    // A failed compensation is an incident with a human,
                    // not a retry with exponential backoff. Escalate.
                    self.raise_manual_reconciliation(saga_id, step, e).await?;
                }
            }
        }
        self.mark(saga_id, "compensated", cause).await
    }
}

# Choreography: the same workflow, event-driven. Six services, six
# subscribers, and the sequence exists only as the union of their code.
# This is the shape that works; note that every step is a pure function
# of one event plus local state, so replay is safe.

# orders.v3 (order placed)          -> inventory  -> inventory.v1 (reserved)
# inventory.v1 (reserved)           -> payments   -> payments.v1 (captured)
# payments.v1 (captured)            -> fulfilment -> fulfilment.v1 (allocated)
# fulfilment.v1 (allocated)         -> orders     -> orders.v3 (order confirmed)
#
# Every subscriber MUST be idempotent, because every one of these events
# is delivered at least once. Pattern per subscriber:

# inventory service, on receiving orders.v3:
def on_order_placed(msg: OrderPlaced) -> None:
    if not idempotency_claim(msg.event_id, "inventory", ttl=7 * 86400):
        return                       # duplicate; the claim is the dedupe
    try:
        with db.transaction():       # single local transaction
            order = orders_repo.get_for_update(msg.order_id)
            if order.status != "placed":
                return               # already advanced: state-driven, not
            order.status = "reserving"   # event-driven. This is what makes
            reserve_all_lines(order)     # choreography replay-safe.
            outbox.emit("inventory.v1",
                        InventoryReserved(order_id=order.id,
                                          lines=order.lines))
    except TransientError:
        release_claim(msg.event_id)   # retryable: undo the claim, let the
                                     # broker redeliver
    except PermanentError as e:
        outbox.emit("inventory.v1",
                    InventoryRejected(order_id=msg.order_id, reason=str(e)))

# The state check inside the transaction, not just the idempotency claim,
# is what protects you when the upstream event is REPLAYED from the log
# during a full reprojection. The claim expires; the state does not.
DimensionChoreographyOrchestrationTwo-phase commit
Sequence held byUnion of all subscribersOne coordinatorOne coordinator plus a log
Coupling✓ Low; publishers know no consumers✗ Coordinator knows all steps✗ Every participant knows the protocol
Workflow visibility✗ Requires cross-service trace✓ One queryable row per saga✓ Distributed transaction log
New event subscribers✓ No change to producers✗ Coordinator and its contract change✗ Protocol change
Failure diagnosis✗ Hard; causal graph is implicit✓ One instance, one timeline✓ Log-based
Maximum sane participants3–48–15 before splitting3–5 before the latency dominates
Timeout control✗ Per consumer, uncoordinated✓ Central deadline per saga✗ Distributed
Compensation support✓ Natural (reply events)✓ Explicit compensation stackn/a, true rollback

A saga’s compensating step is a business action, not a database rollback. "Roll back" a card capture by issuing a refund — a different transaction, with its own failure modes, its own latency, and possibly a different party involved. "Roll back" a dispatched shipment by issuing a recall, which may take three days and may fail. This is why compensation must be specified per step before the saga is implemented, and why a saga whose steps are all reversible by DELETE is rare. The design review question is not "can this be compensated" but "what does the customer see while the compensation is in flight, and who is paged if it never completes".

8.4 The transactional outbox and idempotency keys

Presents the transactional outbox as the mechanism that makes a local transaction and an event publication atomic, and the idempotency key as the mechanism that makes the resulting at-least-once delivery safe to consume.

The dual-write problem is the reason the outbox exists. Writing to the database and publishing to a broker are two independent operations with no shared transaction, and the interleavings produce two bad outcomes: publish succeeds and the database commit then fails, so consumers act on an event for a transaction that never existed; or the commit succeeds and the publish fails, so the event is silently lost. Neither is detectable after the fact, and the second is far more damaging. The transactional outbox resolves this by making the event a row: the business write and the outbox insert commit together in one local transaction, and a separate relay process publishes rows to the broker and marks them sent.

The relay is where the correctness details live. Publishing and then marking is at-least-once and is correct; marking and then publishing is at-most-once and loses events on crash, so the order matters. Duplicate publication is expected and must be handled by the consumer. A production relay needs three things beyond the naive loop: ordered publication per aggregate, so the relay must not publish event 7 for an order before event 6; concurrency control so two relay instances do not publish the same row, using FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED or an advisory lock keyed on the aggregate; and backpressure measured as outbox age, because a relay that silently falls behind produces stale state that no alert will catch.

-- PostgreSQL 18: the outbox write is part of the business transaction.
-- Either both rows exist or neither does. There is no window in which
-- the order is committed and its event is not.

BEGIN;

UPDATE orders
   SET status = 'placed', placed_at = now(), version = version + 1
 WHERE id = $1 AND version = $2;

INSERT INTO outbox (aggregate_type, aggregate_id, event_type, payload,
                    created_at, available_at, published_at, attempts)
VALUES ('order',        $1,        'OrderPlaced', $3::jsonb,
        now(),          now(),     NULL,         0);

COMMIT;
-- Two facts, one commit. The relay cannot observe one without the other.

CREATE TABLE outbox (
    id             bigserial   PRIMARY KEY,
    aggregate_type text        NOT NULL,
    aggregate_id   uuid        NOT NULL,
    seq            bigint      NOT NULL,   -- per-aggregate monotonic
    event_type     text        NOT NULL,
    payload        jsonb       NOT NULL,
    created_at     timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    available_at   timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    published_at   timestamptz,
    attempts       int         NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    last_error     text,
    UNIQUE (aggregate_id, seq)              -- dedupe on replay
);
CREATE INDEX ON outbox (available_at)
  WHERE published_at IS NULL;              -- partial: the hot set is small

// The relay (Go 1.25). Three invariants: publish before marking,
// one publisher per aggregate, and a bounded batch so a lag spike does
// not produce a publish storm that re-creates the overload.
func (r *Relay) Run(ctx context.Context) error {
    for {
        // Claim a batch. SKIP LOCKED lets N relays run without contending;
        // advisory_lock(x) serialises per aggregate so ordering survives.
        rows, err := r.claimBatch(ctx, 500)
        if err != nil {
            return err
        }
        if len(rows) == 0 {
            select {
            case <-ctx.Done():
                return ctx.Err()
            case <-time.After(250 * time.Millisecond):
            }
            continue
        }
        for _, ev := range rows {
            // Publish FIRST, mark SECOND. Reversing this loses events.
            err := r.publish(ctx, ev)          // idempotent producer session
            if err != nil {
                // Backoff in the row itself: available_at moves forward.
                r.markFailed(ctx, ev, err, backoff(ev.Attempts+1))
                continue
            }
            if err := r.markPublished(ctx, ev); err != nil {
                // Published but unmarked. Next cycle republishes. This is
                // the at-least-once window and the consumer MUST tolerate it.
                metrics.DuplicatePublish.Inc("type", ev.EventType)
            }
        }
    }
}

func (r *Relay) claimBatch(ctx context.Context, n int) ([]OutboxEvent, error) {
    tx, err := r.pool.Begin(ctx)
    if err != nil { return nil, err }
    defer tx.Rollback(ctx)
    rows, err := tx.Query(ctx, `
        SELECT id, aggregate_id, seq, event_type, payload, attempts
          FROM outbox
         WHERE published_at IS NULL
           AND available_at <= now()
         ORDER BY aggregate_id, seq        -- per-aggregate ordering
         LIMIT $1
         FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, n)
    // After this transaction commits, another relay can claim the SAME
    // rows. Guard with a session-level advisory lock on aggregate_id so
    // only one publisher emits a given aggregate's events at a time.
    if err != nil { return nil, err }
    if err := tx.Commit(ctx); err != nil { return nil, err }
    return rows, nil
}

// Outbox age is the only SLI that matters here:
//   SELECT max(now() - created_at) FROM outbox WHERE published_at IS NULL;
// Alert at 30 s, page at 5 min. A relay that falls behind by minutes is
// serving stale state to every downstream consumer, invisibly.
Publication strategyAtomic with commit?Duplicate riskLoss riskVerdict
Commit, then publish✗ NoLowTotal on crash between✗ Never do this
Publish, then commit✗ NoLowPhantom events on rollback✗ Also wrong
Change data capture (Debezium)✓ Via the WALYes, handled downstreamNone✓ Good; couples to logical decoding
Transactional outbox✓ One local transactionYes, expectedNone✓ Default for service-to-service
Two-phase commit✓ TrulyNoneNone✗ Unavailable cross-service
 
-- Idempotency keys: the consumer-side half. One table, one constraint,
-- and the entire duplicate problem becomes a no-op. This is the pattern
-- that makes an outbox relay's at-least-once publication safe.

CREATE TABLE idempotency_keys (
    key          uuid        NOT NULL,
    consumer     text        NOT NULL,
    request_hash bytea       NOT NULL,   -- guards against key reuse
    status       text        NOT NULL CHECK (status IN ('in_flight','done')),
    response     jsonb,
    created_at   timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    completed_at timestamptz,
    PRIMARY KEY (consumer, key)
);

-- In the handler, inside the same transaction as the business write:
--   1. INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING
--   2. if 0 rows: SELECT the row; if status='done' return the stored
--      response verbatim (replay), else return 409 in_flight
--   3. if 1 row:  do the work, UPDATE status='done', store the response
--
-- request_hash is the detail teams omit. Without it, a client that
-- reuses one key for two different requests gets the first response
-- silently, which is a data-corruption bug that surfaces weeks later.
INSERT INTO idempotency_keys (key, consumer, request_hash)
VALUES ($1, 'order-api', digest($2::text))
ON CONFLICT (consumer, key) DO NOTHING
RETURNING key;

-- Key retention: 24 h is standard for HTTP APIs. Beyond the maximum
-- client retry window, a duplicate cannot arrive and the row is dead
-- weight on the hot write path. Prune on a schedule, not inline.
DELETE FROM idempotency_keys
 WHERE created_at < now() - interval '24 hours'
   AND status = 'done';
-- Cost of the whole mechanism: one extra index probe and one row per
-- request, both in the same transaction that was already happening.

Put the idempotency key on the request, not on the internal event. Clients retry with a key they generate; the server maps that key to an internal event id and propagates the mapping through the outbox payload. Internal event ids are per-aggregate and monotonically increasing, which gives you a second, free deduplication mechanism: a consumer that stores the highest seq it has applied for an aggregate can drop anything at or below it in constant time, with no lookup table at all. For a single-writer, ordered consumer this is strictly cheaper than a per-message dedupe table and is worth the extra field in the schema.

8.5 Compensation and semantic rollback

Classifies saga steps by compensability, and specifies the partial-compensation states that a naive implementation gets wrong.

Not all steps are equal, and the compensability classification should drive the design rather than follow it. A step is compensable if a business-level inverse exists and is not itself irreversible — reserving inventory is undone by releasing it, and both are local transactions in the same aggregate. A step is non-compensable but retryable if the forward action is idempotent and safe to repeat: sending a webhook with a stable idempotency key, or incrementing a counter with a natural key. A step is irreversible if the effect has already escaped the system: a card capture, an email sent, a pallet picked, a payment to a third-party bank. An irreversible step placed in the middle of a saga is a design error, because the only recovery from its failure is manual reconciliation, and the only recovery from a later step’s failure is a compensation that the system cannot perform.

The rule that follows is that irreversible steps go last, and the saga is split at that boundary. Everything before the irreversible step is a normal saga with full compensation. The irreversible step itself is the point of no return, and everything after it runs on a "best effort, retry forever, alert a human" basis because a failure there is a business incident rather than a transaction. This is exactly the shape of a payment saga: reserve stock, validate fraud, authorise card, then create the order and enqueue fulfilment — and only the last two are irreversible.

// Compensation is a state machine with its own failure states, not a
// stack pop. A compensation that fails must be visible, retried, and
// eventually escalated - never silently swallowed.
type CompensationStatus int

const (
    CompPending CompensationStatus = iota
    CompInFlight
    CompSucceeded
    CompFailedPermanent   // requires a human: refund window closed
    CompAbandoned         // retried past the deadline; needs a decision
)

type SagaState struct {
    SagaID        uuid.UUID
    State         SagaStateKind  // Running | Compensating | Compensated | Failed
    Completed     []Step         // forward steps done, in order
    Compensation  []CompensationState
    Deadline      time.Time
    Owner         string
}

// Rule: the compensation stack is the reverse of the completed stack,
// and each entry carries its own deadline and its own retry count.
// A step whose compensation is still in flight at the saga deadline
// moves to CompAbandoned and raises a reconciliation ticket.
func (s *SagaState) Plan() []Action {
    if s.State == SagaRunning {
        return []Action{Action{Step: s.Next(), Kind: Forward}}
    }
    var plan []Action
    for i := len(s.Compensation) - 1; i >= 0; i-- {
        c := s.Compensation[i]
        if c.Status == CompSucceeded || c.Status == CompAbandoned {
            continue                        // already terminal
        }
        if c.Status == CompFailedPermanent {
            // Do not retry: escalate and continue unwinding. Blocking the
            // whole saga on one impossible refund is strictly worse than
            // finishing the unwind and reconciling that one item later.
            plan = append(plan, Action{Kind: Escalate, Ref: c})
            continue
        }
        plan = append(plan, Action{Kind: Compensate, Ref: c})
    }
    return plan
}

-- Partial saga: a status an honest implementation must model, and that
-- most hand-rolled versions do not. Five states, not two.

type SagaStatus string

const (
    SagaRunning     SagaStatus = "running"      // forward progress
    SagaCompensating SagaStatus = "compensating" // unwinding
    SagaCompensated SagaStatus = "compensated"  // clean: forward effects undone
    SagaPartial     SagaStatus = "partial"      // THE one teams omit
    SagaFailed      SagaStatus = "failed"       // terminal, needs a human
)

-- What "partial" means: some forward effects were NOT compensated,
-- and the system is knowingly inconsistent on purpose, with a record.
CREATE TABLE saga_reconciliation_queue (
    saga_id        uuid        PRIMARY KEY,
    step           text        NOT NULL,
    reason         text        NOT NULL,
    business_impact text       NOT NULL,  -- "customer charged, not shipped"
    amount_minor   bigint,                 -- money at risk, for triage
    detected_at    timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    resolved_at    timestamptz,
    resolved_by    text,
    CHECK (resolved_at IS NULL OR resolved_by IS NOT NULL)
);

-- SLO for this queue: 99% of partial sagas reconciled within 24 h.
-- It is a customer-facing promise, so it belongs in the dashboard next
-- to the availability SLI, not in a separate ops view nobody watches.
SELECT count(*) FILTER (WHERE resolved_at IS NULL)          AS open,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE resolved_at IS NULL
                          AND now() - detected_at > interval '24 h') AS breached
  FROM saga_reconciliation_queue;
-- A non-zero "breached" count is a page. It means a customer is in a
-- state nobody has looked at, and the number grows silently otherwise.
Step classExampleCompensationSaga positionFailure handling
CompensableReserve inventory✓ Release, same aggregateAnyAutomatic, same saga
Compensable, cross-aggregateAllocate shipment✓ Deallocate, remote callEarlyAutomatic, may itself fail
Retryable onlyEmit analytics event✗ None neededAnyRetry with backoff forever
Non-compensableSend email / SMS✗ Cannot unsendAfter the point of no returnBest effort; alert on failure
Irreversible, financialCard capture, bank transfer✗ Refund is a new transactionLastManual reconciliation on failure
Irreversible, physicalPallet picked, courier dispatched✗ Recall, days, may failAbsolute lastBusiness process, not code

Never place a non-compensable step before the end of a saga. The moment an irreversible step is followed by a fallible one, the system has a reachable state in which money has left and the order does not exist, and the only recovery is a person with a refund tool. The standard example is capturing payment before reserving inventory: if the reservation then fails, the customer has been charged for nothing and the fix is a refund, which is a worse customer experience than a declined payment and a worse operational burden than a failed authorisation. The compensating transaction also must not assume the forward step succeeded partially — an order that captured a card and then failed to write its own row is a state your compensation logic will never see unless you model it explicitly.

8.6 Choosing a consistency model: decision matrix

Converts consistency into an explicit per-operation business decision, and supplies the decision matrix that maps consequence to required strength.

The purpose of the decision matrix is to move consistency out of the vocabulary of taste and into the vocabulary of consequence. For each operation, name the consequence of a stale read, the consequence of a lost write, and the cost of failing the request. Those three numbers determine the model, and the answer is almost never uniform across operations in the same system. A product page that serves a 30-second-stale price is fine; a checkout that accepts a 30-second-stale price is a financial loss and a legal exposure. The same row of the same table can be strongly consistent on write and eventually consistent on read, and that asymmetry is the normal outcome rather than an inconsistency in the design.

The formal vocabulary is worth using precisely because it is precise. Linearizability is the strongest practical real-time guarantee: operations appear to take effect instantaneously at a single point between invocation and response, and it is the correct choice for anything where a user could otherwise observe an impossible order of events. Sequential consistency preserves real-time order per client but not global order, and is appropriate for a single replicated log. Causal consistency preserves only happens-before relationships and is the right read model for social feeds and collaborative editing. Read-your-writes and monotonic reads are session guarantees: they are the practical minimum for any UI that displays data the user just edited, and they are far cheaper than linearizability because they constrain only a single session’s observations.

OperationStale read consequenceRequired modelMechanismBudget
Card capture / balance debitFinancial loss, double-spendLinearizable, single writerPostgreSQL 18 primary + row lockMust not fail: 99.999%
Inventory reservation at checkoutOversell, customer harmLinearizable per SKUSingle aggregate owner, version CAS99.99%
Order total at paymentCharged price ≠ shown priceRead-your-writes, pinned snapshotOrder aggregate read, no cache99.99%
Product page and searchStale description, minorEventual, 30 sRead replica or cache with TTL99.9%
Order history listNew order not yet visibleRead-your-writes, sticky routingSession affinity to the writer99.9%
Analytics and reportingLate or double-counted dataEventual, idempotent aggregationStream into a warehouse, recompute99.5%
Feature flag evaluationStale variant, minorEventual, 60 sLocal cache, periodic refresh99.9%
Fraud score on checkoutBad order acceptedLinearizable read of latestSynchronous call, cached 5 s max99.95%
 
// Express the chosen model as code, per operation, and make violations
// impossible rather than unlikely. (Go 1.25, read-path policy object.)
//
// The mistake this replaces: one global "consistency: strong" config
// applied to every table, which costs an order of magnitude of latency
// on the 80% of reads that never needed it.

type ReadPolicy int

const (
    ReadLinearizable ReadPolicy = iota  // must hit the primary
    ReadYourWrites                       // sticky route, then possibly replica
    ReadEventualWithTTL                  // cache, bounded staleness
    ReadBestEffort                       // no guarantee; used for telemetry
)

var orderReadPolicy = map[Operation]ReadPolicy{
    OpGetOrderForCheckout:  ReadLinearizable,   // the price must be the price
    OpGetOrderForDisplay:   ReadYourWrites,      // user may have just changed it
    OpListRecentOrders:     ReadYourWrites,
    OpGetProductPage:       ReadEventualWithTTL,
    OpSearchProducts:       ReadEventualWithTTL,
    OpEmitTelemetry:        ReadBestEffort,
}

func (r *Reader) Load(ctx context.Context, op Operation, id uuid.UUID) (*Order, error) {
    switch orderReadPolicy[op] {
    case ReadLinearizable:
        return r.primary.QueryRow(ctx, getOrderSQL, id).Scan(&order)

    case ReadYourWrites:
        // Session affinity: the session's own writes went to this writer,
        // so route there. Fall back to the primary, never to a laggy replica.
        if w := r.sessionWriter(ctx, id); w != nil {
            return r.onWriter(w).QueryRow(ctx, getOrderSQL, id).Scan(&order)
        }
        return r.primary.QueryRow(ctx, getOrderSQL, id).Scan(&order)

    case ReadEventualWithTTL:
        if v, ok := r.cache.Get(ctx, "order", id); ok {
            return v, nil
        }
        // Replica read is permitted here, but the staleness bound must be
        // enforced, not hoped for: check replication lag before serving.
        if lag := r.replicaLagSeconds(); lag > 5 {
            return r.primary.QueryRow(ctx, getOrderSQL, id).Scan(&order)
        }
        return r.replica.QueryRow(ctx, getOrderSQL, id).Scan(&order)
    }
    return nil, ErrUnsupportedOperation
}

# Verify the consistency you claimed. Every entry in the matrix above needs
# a test that would FAIL if the guarantee regressed, otherwise the SLO is
# an aspiration rather than a property.

-- 1. Replication lag, the hard bound on any "eventual" read model.
SELECT client_addr, state, sent_lsn, replay_lsn,
       pg_wal_lsn_diff(sent_lsn, replay_lsn) AS replay_lag_bytes,
       extract(epoch FROM now() - reply_time) AS reply_age_s
  FROM pg_stat_replication;
-- Bytes alone is meaningless without the apply rate; convert:
--   lag_seconds = replay_lag_bytes / pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(),
--                                                    replay_lsn) * 1s

-- 2. Prove read-your-writes actually holds for a session. This is the
--    test that catches a load balancer change silently breaking it.
BEGIN;
INSERT INTO orders (id, customer_id, status, version)
VALUES (gen_random_uuid(), $1, 'draft', 1) RETURNING id \gset
SELECT set_config('app.session_id', :'customer_id', true);
-- In the SAME session: read back and assert the row is visible.
DO $$
DECLARE found int;
BEGIN
    SELECT count(*) INTO found FROM orders WHERE customer_id = current_setting('app.session_id');
    IF found = 0 THEN
        RAISE EXCEPTION 'read-your-writes VIOLATED on replica read';
    END IF;
END $$;
COMMIT;
-- A replica read in this test must be able to fail it. If it cannot, the
-- test is not testing the guarantee.

-- 3. Lost-update detection under concurrency, 20 racing writers.
UPDATE inventory SET available = available - 1 WHERE sku = 'SKU-1';
-- Run in 20 concurrent sessions; final available must be exactly
-- start - 20. Anything else means a read-modify-write escaped the CAS.

Eventual consistency is a property with a bound, and the bound must be written down. "Eventually" without a number is indistinguishable from "never". A defensible statement is: read-model staleness is bounded at p99 under 30 seconds measured by the replication_lag_seconds SLI, and the escalation path when that bound is breached is to serve from the primary, which is what the read policy in the code above does. Systems that claim eventual consistency without a bound discover it during an incident, from customers, and treat that as a correctness bug — which it is.

Exercises 8

  1. Build a three-node PostgreSQL 18 XA harness with an injected coordinator crash inside the in-doubt window, capture the retained locks, and resolve with COMMIT PREPARED. Deliverable: the lock-state transcript before and after, the measured lock hold time, and the list of rows that were blocked in the interim.
  2. Measure the coordination cost curve for N in {1, 2, 4, 8} participants and plot p50 and p99. Deliverable: a fitted per-participant cost model and the N at which 2PC stops being cheaper than compensate-and-retry, computed from your measurements rather than assumed.
  3. Implement a transactional outbox relay with FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED, per-aggregate advisory locking and backoff in available_at, then kill the relay mid-batch. Deliverable: proof that no event was lost, the count of duplicates produced, and the age of the oldest unpublished row at the moment of the kill.
  4. Implement idempotency-key handling with a request_hash guard and prove it by firing 50 concurrent identical requests plus 5 with the same key and different bodies. Deliverable: test output showing exactly one side effect for the first group and five rejections for the second.
  5. Classify every step of one real multi-service workflow by compensability, place any non-compensable step at the end, and design the partial-state reconciliation path. Deliverable: the classification table, the reordered sequence, and the reconciliation query with its SLO.

9 · API Design: REST, gRPC, GraphQL and Protocol Choice

9.1 REST resource modelling and HTTP semantics9.2 GraphQL: schema design, N+1 and persisted queries9.3 gRPC and protobuf contract design9.4 Event contracts: AsyncAPI, Avro and schema registries9.5 Versioning and backward-compatible evolution9.6 Error models, budgets and observability contracts

This section treats the wire protocol as an architectural decision with a cost model rather than a default. It establishes correct use of HTTP semantics on the public edge, the failure characteristics of GraphQL’s field-resolution model, and the code-generation contract discipline that makes gRPC’s performance worth its coupling. Event contracts receive the same treatment as request contracts, with schema-registry compatibility modes named explicitly. The section closes on the least-specified and most-mismanaged part of any API: the error contract, including RFC 9457 problem documents and the rate-limit headers that determine whether a client is a good citizen.

9.1 REST resource modelling and HTTP semantics

Fixes the mapping from business operations to HTTP methods, status codes and headers, and identifies the modelling choices that make an interface unsafe to retry.

The quality of a REST interface is determined almost entirely by whether method and status semantics are correct, because those semantics are the only contract a generic client, proxy, CDN and load balancer can reason about. Concretely: GET and HEAD must be safe, PUT and DELETE must be idempotent, and POST is neither. A POST that creates a resource should return 201 Created with a Location header, and it should be made idempotent by requiring an Idempotency-Key rather than by trusting clients not to retry — which, with mobile networks and aggressive SDK timeouts, they always will. The HTTP specification defines idempotence as the same intended effect on the server state, not as the same response bytes, so a PUT that returns a fresh ETag each time is still idempotent if the second call writes the same state.

Status code discipline is a design decision with operational consequences. 422 for semantic validation failures carrying a machine-readable field map, 409 for state-machine violations such as shipping a cancelled order, 412 for a failed If-Match precondition, 429 with Retry-After for shaping, and 503 with Retry-After for load shedding are not interchangeable. Returning 400 for all of them forces every client into bespoke parsing and removes your ability to distinguish a client bug from a transient dependency failure on a dashboard, which is the single most valuable signal during an incident.

openapi: 3.1.0
info:
  title: Order API
  version: 3.4.0
servers:
  - url: https://api.example.com/v3
paths:
  /orders/{orderId}:
    parameters:
      - name: orderId
        in: path
        required: true
        schema: { type: string, format: uuid }
    get:
      operationId: getOrder
      # Safe by contract: no side effects, cacheable, repeatable.
      responses:
        '200':
          description: The order
          content:
            application/json:
              schema: { $ref: '#/components/schemas/Order' }
          headers:
            ETag: { schema: { type: string } }
            Cache-Control: { schema: { type: string } }
        '304': { description: Not modified }
        '404': { $ref: '#/components/responses/Problem' }
        '429': { $ref: '#/components/responses/Problem' }
    patch:
      operationId: patchOrder
      parameters:
        - name: If-Match
          in: header
          required: true
          description: Opaque version token from the last GET
          schema: { type: string }
      requestBody:
        required: true
        content:
          application/merge-patch+json:
            schema: { $ref: '#/components/schemas/OrderPatch' }
      responses:
        '200': { description: Patched }
        '409': { $ref: '#/components/responses/Problem' }  # state-machine violation
        '412': { $ref: '#/components/responses/Problem' }  # If-Match failed
    delete:
      operationId: cancelOrder
      parameters:
        - name: Idempotency-Key
          in: header
          required: true
          schema: { type: string, maxLength: 64 }
      responses:
        '204': { description: Cancelled }
        '409': { $ref: '#/components/responses/Problem' }
        '503':
          description: Shed
          headers:
            Retry-After: { schema: { type: integer } }
components:
  responses:
    Problem:
      description: RFC 9457 problem document
      content:
        application/problem+json:
          schema: { $ref: '#/components/schemas/Problem' }

// Idempotency-key middleware (Node 24, Express) backed by Redis 8.x.
// A retried POST with the same key returns the ORIGINAL status and body,
// which is what makes a client-side retry loop safe to write.
import { createClient } from 'redis';

const redis = createClient({ url: process.env.REDIS_URL });
await redis.connect();

const IDEM_RE = /^[A-Za-z0-9_-]{16,64}$/;

export function idempotency({ ttlSeconds = 86_400 } = {}) {
  return async (req, res, next) => {
    if (!['POST', 'PUT', 'PATCH'].includes(req.method)) return next();

    const key = req.get('Idempotency-Key');
    if (!key || !IDEM_RE.test(key)) {
      return res.status(400).type('application/problem+json')
        .json({ type: 'urn:problem:invalid-idempotency-key', status: 400 });
    }
    // Scope by tenant AND route: the same key on two routes is two
    // different operations and must not collide.
    const scoped = `idem:${req.tenantId}:${req.method}:${req.baseUrl}${req.path}:${key}`;
    const bodyHash = createHash('sha256')
      .update(JSON.stringify(req.body ?? null)).digest('base64url');

    const claimed = await redis.set(scoped,
      JSON.stringify({ state: 'IN_FLIGHT', bodyHash, ts: Date.now() }),
      { NX: true, EX: ttlSeconds });

    if (claimed === null) {
      const prior = JSON.parse((await redis.get(scoped)) ?? '{}');
      if (prior.bodyHash && prior.bodyHash !== bodyHash) {
        // Same key, different body: a client bug. Never return the stored
        // response here, because it would silently apply the wrong request.
        return res.status(422).type('application/problem+json')
          .json({ type: 'urn:problem:key-reused-different-body', status: 422 });
      }
      if (prior.state === 'DONE') {
        res.set('Idempotent-Replay', 'true');
        return res.status(prior.status)
          .type('application/problem+json').json(prior.body);
      }
      return res.status(409).type('application/problem+json')
        .json({ type: 'urn:problem:request-in-flight', status: 409 });
    }

    const chunks = [];
    res.on('data', (c) => chunks.push(c));
    res.on('finish', () => void redis.set(scoped, JSON.stringify({
      state: 'DONE',
      status: res.statusCode,
      body: Buffer.concat(chunks).toString('utf8'),
      bodyHash,
    }), { EX: ttlSeconds }));

    next();
  };
}
OperationMethodSuccess statusClient may retry?Required header
Create a resourcePOST201 + LocationOnly with Idempotency-KeyIdempotency-Key
Full replacePUT200 or 204✓ Naturally idempotentIf-Match optional
Partial updatePATCH200✓ With merge semanticsIf-Match or If-Unmodified-Since
ReadGET200 + ETag✓ AlwaysIf-None-Match → 304
Delete or cancelDELETE204✓ Idempotent by definitionIdempotency-Key for side effects
Long-running operationPOST202 + status URLPoll the status resourceRetry-After on 503
Bulk batchPOST207 or 202Per-item status requiredIdempotency-Key per batch
State violationany409✗ Fix the request firstProblem type URI

Do not encode actions in GET, and do not version by URL alone. GET /orders/123/cancel breaks every cache, prefetcher and crawler in the path and converts a side effect into an apparently safe operation; a browser extension’s speculative prefetch becomes a cancellation. Where action semantics are unavoidable, model the action as a sub-resource — POST /orders/123/cancellations — which is retryable with an idempotency key and produces an auditable entity. Separately, URL path versioning without a deprecation date is a promise nobody intends to keep: put Deprecation and Sunset headers on every response from a versioned surface, give each a named owner, and attribute traffic per consumer so the cutover is provable rather than hopeful.

9.2 GraphQL: schema design, N+1 and persisted queries

Explains the field-resolution execution model that makes GraphQL both flexible and dangerous, and specifies the mitigations that make it viable on a hot path.

GraphQL’s advantage is that a client asks for the fields it needs in one round-trip, which removes over-fetching and under-fetching and lets a client evolve without a server deploy. Its cost follows from the same mechanism. The server executes a query as a tree of field resolvers, and nothing in the GraphQL specification prevents a client from expressing a query that is, in relational terms, a cartesian product. A naive resolver that performs one database query per parent row turns a single request into thousands, and the p99 goes from 12 ms to 4 s. This is the N+1 problem, and it is not an implementation bug — it is the default consequence of a tree-execution model with no cost model in the query language.

The mitigations are specific and non-optional for a public API. DataLoader batching collapses sibling field resolvers into a single query keyed on the batched parent ids, using a per-request cache so a nested selection does not re-query the same parent. Query depth and complexity limits cap the tree: a max depth of 7 and a complexity budget computed from field weights, where a list field multiplies by its typical page size. Persisted queries remove arbitrary query text from the request path entirely, replacing it with a hash, which eliminates query-injection surface, enables per-operation rate limiting, and allows query-level caching and optimisation. Automatic persisted queries is the pragmatic middle: clients send a hash, the server returns a one-time registration challenge if unknown, and after one round-trip the query is registered and thereafter only the hash travels. Field-level authorisation must be enforced in resolvers rather than assumed from the top-level guard, because a field can be reached through four different paths.

// DataLoader: the non-negotiable mitigation for GraphQL on a relational
// backend. Batches the whole tick's sibling resolvers into one query and
// caches per request so nested selections do not re-fetch.
import DataLoader from 'dataloader';

const orderById = new DataLoader(async (ids) => {
    // ONE query for N parents, not N queries. Placeholders, not interpolation.
    const rows = await db.query(
      'SELECT id, customer_id, status, total_minor, placed_at
         FROM orders WHERE id = ANY($1::uuid[])', [ids]);
    const byId = new Map(rows.map((r) => [r.id, r]));
    // Must return one element per input id, in order, with nulls for misses.
    return ids.map((id) => byId.get(id) ?? null);
});

export const resolvers = {
    Query: {
        order: (_p, { id }, ctx) => ctx.loaders.orderById.load(id),
        orders: async (_p, { first = 20, after }, ctx) => {
            // Relay-style cursor pagination: stable under concurrent writes,
            // unlike OFFSET, which skips or repeats rows as data changes.
            return ctx.repo.ordersPage({ first, after });
        },
    },
    Order: {
        // Resolved through the loader, so 20 orders in a list cost 1 query.
        customer: (order, _a, ctx) => ctx.loaders.customerById.load(order.customer_id),
        lines:    (order, _a, ctx) => ctx.loaders.linesByOrder.load(order.id),
    },
    // A fresh loader per request: sharing across requests is a
    // cross-tenant data-leak bug waiting to happen.
    createLoaders: () => ({ orderById, customerById, linesByOrder }),
};

# Depth, complexity and cost limits, plus automatic persisted queries.
# Apollo Server 5 / graphql-js on Node 24.

const server = new ApolloServer({
    schema,
    introspection: false,               # disable in production
    plugins: [
      ApolloServerPluginLandingPageLocalDefault(),
      // 1. Depth: 7 is generous for a mobile client and stops the
      #    {a{b{c{d{e{f{g{h}}}}}}}} amplification.
      new ApolloServerPluginComplexity({ maxDepth: 7 }),
      // 2. Complexity: a list field multiplies by its real page size.
      //    Weights are per field, not per query, so cost scales with the
      //    shape the client can actually express.
      new ApolloServerPluginComplexity({
        estimators: {
          Query: { defaultComplexity: 1, maximumComplexity: 300 },
          Mutation: { defaultComplexity: 20 },   # writes cost more
        },
        scalarCosts: { Int: { defaultCost: 1, maxCost: 5000 } },
      }),
      // 3. Per-operation cost budget, enforced against a rate limit so a
      //    legitimate-but-expensive query cannot monopolise the service.
      new ApolloServerPluginOperationCost({ maximumCost: 500 }),
      // 4. Automatic persisted queries: after one registration round-trip
      //    the client sends only a sha256 hash. The server can then cache,
      //    rate-limit and pre-compile per operation, and the query text
      #    never reaches the parser again.
      new ApolloServerPluginAPQ({
        apq: { keyGeneratorFn: (query) => sha256(query), persist: true },
      }),
    ],
    formatError: (formatted, err) => {
      // Never leak internals: stack traces and resolver paths in a public
      // GraphQL response are a reconnaissance gift.
      const isPublic = !err.extensions?.code || PUBLIC_CODES.has(err.extensions.code);
      return isPublic ? formatted : { message: 'Internal error', path: formatted.path };
    },
});

# Execute a legitimate complex query and count the actual SQL statements.
# If this is not close to 1 + (number of distinct loader types), the
# DataLoader wiring is broken somewhere in the resolver tree.
EXPRESS_OPERATION_COUNT=true node --enable-source-maps ./dist/server.js

# Diagnosing GraphQL performance: the three measurements that matter.

# 1. Field-level execution time. This is the only way to find a slow
#    resolver that is invisible at the query level.
curl -sS -X POST http://gateway:4000/graphql \
  -H 'content-type: application/json' \
  -H 'apollographql-client-name: perf-probe' \
  -d '{"query":"{ order(id:\"...\" ){ id status customer{ id name } lines{ id sku qty } } }"}' \
  | jq '.extensions.tracing.version, .data.order.id'

# 2. N+1 detection: the same query, same shape, 1 parent vs 20 parents.
#    If DB query count scales with the number of parents, you have an N+1.
EXPRESS_OPERATION_COUNT=true node ./dist/server.js
#   1 parent  -> 3 SQL statements
#  20 parents -> 63 SQL statements   <-- the bug, plainly visible

# 3. Query shape census across production traffic. APQ makes this easy
#    because you already store the hash to text mapping.
psql -d app -c "
  SELECT apq_hash,
         count(*)                          AS calls_7d,
         avg(total_ms)::numeric(8,1)       AS avg_ms,
         percentile_cont(0.99) WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY total_ms) AS p99_ms,
         left(text, 120)                   AS query_preview
    FROM graphql_operation_log
   WHERE ts > now() - interval '7 days'
   GROUP BY apq_hash, text
   ORDER BY calls_7d DESC
   LIMIT 20;"

# The row to act on: a query with a huge call count and a p99 above 500 ms
# is a denial-of-service vector you deployed yourself. Either cap its
# cost, optimise the resolver, or deprecate the field it selects.
DimensionREST (OpenAPI 3.1)gRPC 1.7xGraphQLAsyncAPI / Avro
TransportHTTP/1.1, HTTP/2HTTP/2, prior knowledgeHTTP/1.1, HTTP/2Kafka, AMQP, MQTT, NATS
SchemaOpenAPI, JSON Schema.proto, protocSDL, introspectionAsyncAPI + Avro/Protobuf
TypingOptional; JSON Schema is descriptive✓ Strong, generated✓ Strong, schema-first✓ Strong per event
Over-fetching✗ Common✗ Common✓ Solved by constructionn/a, whole events
N+1 riskNot applicableNot applicable✗ High, needs DataLoaderNot applicable
CodegenPartial, from OpenAPI✓ Full, all languages✓ Full from SDL✓ From schema
Browser support✓ Native✗ Needs grpc-web proxy✓ Nativen/a
Query cost control✗ Endpoint-level only✗ Per-method✓ Depth, complexity, persisted✗ Per consumer quota
Best fitPublic, cacheable, CRUDInternal service-to-service, low latencyAggregating gateways, mobile clientsService-to-service async

A GraphQL gateway is an unauthenticated database interface until you say otherwise. Introspection exposes the entire schema to anyone who asks, and nested selections let a single unauthenticated request reach twenty resolvers in a depth that was never designed. Disable introspection in production, enforce depth and complexity limits at the gateway rather than inside resolvers, apply per-field authorisation in the resolver tree because a field is reachable through many paths, and put a hard per-operation cost budget against a rate limit. If the answer to "is this query expensive?" requires reading a comment in a resolver, the answer is that the query is unbounded and the gateway will be the thing that falls over.

9.3 gRPC and protobuf contract design

Specifies protobuf contract design and explains which gRPC features are cheap in a datacentre and expensive across a WAN.

gRPC is HTTP/2 with a binary framing format, a schema-first contract, and four call types whose semantics must be respected or the reliability properties you believe you have are absent. Unary is a request-response. Server-streaming sends many responses to one request. Client-streaming sends many requests for one response. Bidirectional streaming sends both. The properties that matter: unary and client-streaming calls are not retryable by default, because a retry after an ambiguous failure may execute the operation twice; all calls are cancellable, and a handler that ignores cancellation keeps burning a connection and a goroutine after the client has gone; and deadlines propagate, with the client-supplied deadline travelling to the server, which is the mechanism that makes a 30-second budget enforceable across four services instead of a hope.

The wire characteristics that determine suitability: a protobuf message with ten small fields costs roughly 30–80 bytes versus 400+ for the equivalent JSON, and serialisation is allocation-light in Go, Rust and Java but is a real cost in Python without upb. HTTP/2 multiplexing removes the head-of-line blocking at the transport level, and gRPC’s own flow control is per-stream, so a slow reader throttles its own stream. For a datacentre with sub-millisecond RTT and streaming requirements, gRPC is clearly correct. Across a WAN with 80–150 ms RTT and per-call overhead in the low single-digit milliseconds, it is usually still correct for internal traffic; across a mobile edge, grpc-web or REST is generally the better answer, and a streaming call over a mobile network is a misdesign.

syntax = "proto3";
package orders.v3;

import "google/protobuf/timestamp.proto";
import "google/protobuf/empty.proto";
import "google/protobuf/field_mask.proto";

option go_package   = "github.com/acme/orders/api/ordersv3;ordersv3";
option java_package = "com.acme.orders.v3";
option java_multiple_files = true;

// Field numbers are the wire contract. NEVER renumber and NEVER reuse a
// number after deletion: mark it reserved to prevent accidental reuse.
message Order {
  string id           = 1;
  string customer_id  = 2;
  State  state        = 3;
  int64  total_minor  = 4;          // minor units; never a float for money
  string currency      = 5;         // ISO 4217, three uppercase letters
  repeated Line lines = 6;
  google.protobuf.Timestamp placed_at = 7;
  // 8 was "coupon_code": removed 2026-04. Never reuse.
  reserved 8;
  reserved "coupon_code";
  map<string, string> metadata = 9;  // untyped escape hatch; bound its size
  uint64 version       = 10;         // optimistic concurrency token

  message Line {
    string sku       = 1;
    int64  qty       = 2;
    int64  unit_minor = 3;
    reserved 4; reserved "discount_pct";   // removed: implied a rounding rule
  }
}

enum State {
  STATE_UNSPECIFIED = 0;   // required: proto3 defaults to zero, and a
                            // zero-valued state is a silent bug generator
  STATE_DRAFT   = 1;
  STATE_PLACED  = 2;
  STATE_PAID    = 3;
  STATE_SHIPPED = 4;
  STATE_CANCELLED = 5;
}

message GetOrderRequest  { string id = 1; }
message ListOrdersRequest {
  string customer_id = 1;
  int32  page_size   = 2;    // server clamps to [1, 200]
  string page_token  = 3;
}
message ListOrdersResponse {
  repeated Order orders = 1;
  string next_page_token = 2;
}

service OrderService {
  rpc GetOrder(GetOrderRequest) returns (Order);
  rpc ListOrders(ListOrdersRequest) returns (ListOrdersResponse);
  rpc UpdateOrder(UpdateOrder) returns (Order);
  // Deadlines and cancellation are part of the contract, not options.
  rpc WatchOrders(WatchOrdersRequest) returns (stream Order);
}

message UpdateOrder {
  string id     = 1;
  Order order   = 2;
  google.protobuf.FieldMask update_mask = 3;  // explicit partial update
  uint64 expected_version = 4;                // CAS: 0 means "no check"
}

// Server interceptor chain: the four cross-cutting concerns that every
// production gRPC service needs, in one place. (Go 1.25, grpc-go 1.7x.)

func UnaryInterceptor(ctx context.Context, req any,
    info *grpc.UnaryServerInfo, h grpc.UnaryHandler) (any, error) {

    // 1. Deadline: REJECT, do not ignore. A handler with no deadline in a
    //    fan-out chain is how a 2-second user request becomes a 30-second
    //    resource leak across four services.
    deadline, ok := ctx.Deadline()
    if !ok {
        return nil, status.Error(codes.DeadlineExceeded,
            "server requires a deadline; client must set one")
    }
    budget := time.Until(deadline)

    // 2. Per-method budget: the outer edge gets 800 ms, so this hop may
    //    not spend more than 500 ms, or the sum exceeds the user's budget.
    methodBudget := 500 * time.Millisecond
    if budget > 20*time.Millisecond && budget < methodBudget {
        methodBudget = budget - 10*time.Millisecond
    }
    ctx, cancel := context.WithTimeout(ctx, methodBudget)
    defer cancel()

    // 3. Auth: identity from verified metadata, never from a request field.
    md, _ := metadata.FromIncomingContext(ctx)
    if err := authn.Authorize(ctx, md); err != nil {
        return nil, status.Error(codes.Unauthenticated, "invalid credentials")
    }

    // 4. Observability: propagate the trace context, record the method
    #    and outcome, and count retries explicitly so duplicate effects
    #    are visible in the metrics rather than only in the traces.
    ctx = propagator.Extract(ctx, md)
    start := time.Now()
    resp, err := h(ctx, req)
    code := status.Code(err)

    metrics.RPCDuration.WithLabelValues(
        info.FullMethod, code.String()).Observe(time.Since(start).Seconds())
    metrics.RPCRetries.WithLabelValues(info.FullMethod).Inc(
        attemptFromMetadata(md))
    if code != codes.OK {
        // Return the user-safe message only. Never an internal error string.
        err = mapError(err)
    }
    return resp, err
}

// Unary calls are NOT retried automatically. Retrying a non-idempotent
// Unary call after an ambiguous timeout can execute it twice. If you want
// gRPC's built-in retry, the service config must name an idempotent method
// and the application must make it idempotent first.
const retryPolicy = `{
  "methodConfig": [{
    "name": [{"service": "orders.v3.OrderService", "method": "GetOrder"}],
    "timeout": "0.5s",
    "retryPolicy": {
      "maxAttempts": 3,
      "initialBackoff": "0.05s", "maxBackoff": "0.4s", "backoffMultiplier": 2,
      "retryableStatusCodes": ["UNAVAILABLE", "RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED"]
    }
  }]
}`

# gRPC operational tooling. These five commands cover most incidents.

# 1. Reflection-based exploration: what does the server actually serve?
#    (Do not enable reflection on a public endpoint.)
grpcurl -plaintext -d '{"id":"7f3a-..."}' \
  localhost:8080 orders.v3.OrderService/GetOrder

# 2. Timing breakdown and status codes over a real window. grpcurl's
#    verbose flag is the equivalent of a per-call trace.
grpcurl -vv -d '{"customer_id":"c-1","page_size":50}' \
  localhost:8080 orders.v3.OrderService/ListOrders 2>&1 \
  | grep -E 'headers|trailers|code:|grpc-status'

# 3. Load generation with per-method latency and status distribution.
#    Compare p99 against the deadline budget you set in the interceptor.
ghz --insecure --proto ./api/orders.proto \
    -n 50000 -c 64 -z 30s \
    --data '{"id":"7f3a-..."}' \
    orders.v3.OrderService.GetOrder \
  | tee ghz-getorder.txt | grep -E 'p50|p95|p99|Error|Status code distribution' -A4

# 4. Latency histogram per method, from the server's own metrics.
curl -s localhost:9090/metrics | grep grpc_server_handled_seconds \
  | awk '{print $1, $NF}' | sort -k2 -rn | head

# 5. Deadlock and cancellation audit: how many calls ended because the
#    CLIENT went away? A rising number means a leak upstream, and the
#    server is paying for work nobody is waiting for.
curl -s localhost:9090/metrics | grep grpc_server_handled_total \
  | grep -E 'CANCELLED|DEADLINE_EXCEEDED' \
  | awk '{sum+=$NF} END {print "cancelled+deadline:", sum}'
# Fix is on the client side: propagate the deadline instead of using a
# fixed 30s context that outlives the user's request.

Bidirectional streaming over HTTP/2 is a connection-level resource commitment, not a convenience. Each stream is a state machine held in server memory with its own buffers, and the HTTP/2 SETTINGS_MAX_CONCURRENT_STREAMS limit means a few hundred idle streams can exhaust a server’s stream budget and starve new calls entirely — a slow-loris pattern applied to your own infrastructure. Bound the number of active streams per connection, set grpc.keepalive parameters on both ends so dead peers are reaped, and never multiplex thousands of logical subscriptions onto one connection. The complementary trap is treating streaming as a substitute for a queue: a server-streaming WatchOrders that fans out to two hundred database connections per subscriber is a self-inflicted connection-exhaustion incident, and the durable answer is a broker with consumer groups.

9.4 Event contracts: AsyncAPI, Avro and schema registries

Treats event schemas with the same rigour as request contracts, and specifies the compatibility modes that make a registry a deployment gate rather than a museum.

An event contract is harder to evolve than a request contract because the producer and consumer are deployed independently and neither can observe the other. The schema registry is the mechanism that converts that risk into a build-time failure: every subject or topic is registered with a schema, and CI runs compatibility checking against the previous registered version before the producer is allowed to deploy. The compatibility mode is the policy, and choosing the wrong one is the difference between a safe rollout and a 03:00 page. BACKWARD — a new consumer can read data written by the previous producer — is the correct default for a log where history persists. BACKWARD_TRANSITIVE is stricter and correct when the consumer will replay from offset zero, because it checks against all prior versions, not just the last. FORWARD is the mirror image and is rarely what you want. FULL and FULL_TRANSITIVE are the correct choices for a request/response topic where the broker retains nothing.

Encoding choice follows from the same analysis. Avro is compact, supports schema resolution rules that tolerate added fields with defaults, and is the practical default for Kafka when the consumer set is heterogeneous. Protobuf with a registry gives faster codecs and a simpler wire type, at the cost of a resolution story that is weaker and less standardised. JSON Schema on a broker topic is the worst of both: the largest payloads and no binary codec, though it remains reasonable at the public edge for low-volume integration events where human readability is genuinely worth more than throughput.

// Avro schema for a domain event. Note which fields carry defaults: without
// a default, ADDING the field is a FORWARD-incompatible change, because a
// consumer running the older schema hits an unknown field it cannot skip.
{
  "type": "record",
  "name": "OrderPlaced",
  "namespace": "com.acme.orders.v3",
  "doc": "Emitted exactly once per order, outbox-guaranteed.",
  "fields": [
    { "name": "event_id",   "type": "string",
      "doc": "UUIDv7. Primary dedupe key for the consumer." },
    { "name": "occurred_at","type": { "type": "long", "logicalType": "timestamp-millis" } },
    { "name": "aggregate_id", "type": "string" },
    { "name": "seq",        "type": "long",
      "doc": "Monotonic per aggregate. Consumers drop seq <= highest_seen." },
    { "name": "customer_id","type": "string" },
    { "name": "total_minor", "type": "long" },
    { "name": "currency",   "type": "string" },
    { "name": "schema_version", "type": "int", "default": 3 },
    { "name": "channel",    "type": ["null", "string"], "default": null,
      "doc": "Union with null + default: added in v3, old readers skip it." }
  ]
}

# AsyncAPI 3.0: the event-side contract, in the same shape as OpenAPI.
# It is documentation, tooling input, and a design review artefact.

asyncapi: 3.0.0
info:
  title: Orders Event API
  version: 3.2.0
  description: |
    Order lifecycle events. Producers are outbox-relayed; consumers MUST
    be idempotent on event_id. Replay is supported: every event is
    retained 7 days and may be re-consumed by resetting the group offset.
channels:
  orderEvents:
    address: orders.v3
    messages:
      placed:  { $ref: '#/components/messages/OrderPlaced' }
      paid:    { $ref: '#/components/messages/OrderPaid' }
      shipped: { $ref: '#/components/messages/OrderShipped' }
operations:
  receiveOrderPlaced:
    action: receive          # "receive" = this app consumes
    channel: { $ref: '#/channels/orderEvents' }
    messages:
      - $ref: '#/channels/orderEvents/messages/placed'
components:
  messages:
    OrderPlaced:
      name: OrderPlaced
      title: Order placed
      contentType: application/vnd.apache.avro+json
      correlationId:
        location: $message.payload#/event_id
      payload:
        $ref: '#/components/schemas/OrderPlaced'
  schemas:
    OrderPlaced:
      type: object
      required: [event_id, occurred_at, aggregate_id, seq, total_minor]
      properties:
        event_id:      { type: string, format: uuid }
        occurred_at:   { type: string, format: date-time }
        aggregate_id:  { type: string, format: uuid }
        seq:           { type: integer, format: int64, minimum: 1 }
        total_minor:   { type: integer, format: int64, minimum: 0 }
        currency:      { type: string, pattern: '^[A-Z]{3}$' }
        channel:       { type: [string, 'null'] }
      additionalProperties: false
      # Client-side compliance is testable: reject anything that does not
      # match, and send a poison message to the DLQ with the reason.

# Schema registry as a deployment gate. Compatibility is checked in CI,
# before the producer ships, against the CURRENTLY REGISTERED version.

# Confluent Schema Registry REST API.
# Register and check compatibility in one call:
curl -sS -X POST "$SR_HOST/subjects/orders.v3-OrderPlaced-value/versions" \
  -H 'content-type: application/vnd.apache.avro+json' \
  -d "$(cat order-placed.avsc)" | jq -r '.id'

curl -sS -X POST "$SR_HOST/compatibility/subjects/orders.v3-OrderPlaced-value/versions/latest" \
  -H 'content-type: application/vnd.apache.avro+json' \
  -d "$(cat order-placed.avsc)" | jq '.is_compatible'
# {"is_compatible":true}

# Set the policy explicitly. BACKWARD_TRANSITIVE is the correct mode when
# any consumer may replay from the beginning of the retention window:
# it validates against ALL prior versions, not just the latest.
curl -sS -X PUT "$SR_HOST/config/orders.v3-OrderPlaced-value" \
  -H 'content-type: application/vnd.schemaregistry.v1+json' \
  -d '{"compatibility":"BACKWARD_TRANSITIVE"}'

# The gate that matters: fail the build on incompatibility.
npx --yes @asyncapi/cli validate asyncapi.yaml
# or, for Avro specifically:
npx --yes avro-tools compatibility -s order-placed.avsc \
  orders.v3-OrderPlaced-value -p BACKWARD_TRANSITIVE || exit 1

# Re-serialise historical data to the newest schema before changing a
# field that resolution cannot bridge (renames, type widening, enum
# removal). This is the only operation that genuinely needs a migration.
curl -sS -X POST "$SR_HOST/subjects/orders.v3-OrderPlaced-value?deleted=true" \
  -H 'content-type: application/vnd.schemaregistry.v1+json' \
  -d "$(cat order-placed.avsc)" | jq -r '.version'

# Consumer-side: fetch the writer's schema, not a hard-coded copy.
curl -sS -H 'Accept: application/vnd.schemaregistry.v1+json' \
  "$SR_HOST/subjects/orders.v3-OrderPlaced-value/versions/latest" \
  | jq -r '.schema' > /tmp/writer-schema.json
# A consumer pinned to a schema ID cannot read a topic whose writer
# upgraded. Schema resolution is the mechanism that saves you; a pinned
# ID is what breaks it.
Compatibility modeChecks new againstPasses whenCorrect whenBreaks when
BACKWARDPrevious versionNew consumers read old dataLog with recent history onlyA consumer replays beyond one version
BACKWARD_TRANSITIVEAll prior versionsNew consumers read all history✓ Replayable logs (Kafka, JetStream)Any old version is unreadable
FORWARDPrevious versionOld consumers read new dataShort-lived, no retained historyNew data must be readable by old readers
FULLPrevious, both directionsEither direction worksRequest/response, ephemeral retentionEither side needs one-way evolution
NONENothingAnything✗ Never, except for a genuinely new subjectEverything, silently

Adding a field to an Avro schema is only backward-compatible if it has a default. Avro resolution matches by field name and uses the reader’s schema; an unknown field in the data is skipped by the reader, so a new optional field with a default is read fine by an old consumer. The exceptions that bite: renaming a field (resolution cannot match it, so the value is lost), changing a field’s type to an incompatible one (long to string does not resolve), adding an enum symbol (an old reader encountering the new symbol gets a decode error, not a null), and promoting a field to required. Each of these is a breaking change that the registry will reject in BACKWARD_TRANSITIVE mode — which is the point of having the gate in CI rather than discovering it from a consumer’s error queue at 03:00.

9.5 Versioning and backward-compatible evolution

Enumerates the changes that are safe, the changes that require coordination, and the deprecation procedure that makes a break survivable.

Versioning is a policy question with three parts: what signals a version, what a version means, and who is allowed to break one. The commonest and most damaging confusion is treating a major version number as a deployment unit. A major bump in a REST path is a compatibility statement, and bumping it while both versions are served is pure cost; not bumping it while changing the contract is an outage. The four safe changes in REST are: adding a response field, adding an optional request field, adding a new endpoint, and adding a new enum value to a response that consumers are documented to handle unknown values. The four unsafe changes are: removing or renaming a field, narrowing a type or an accepted range, changing a field’s meaning while keeping its name, and changing a required field to optional.

For protobuf and Avro the rules are different in one important respect: the wire format identifies fields by number, so renaming a field is wire-compatible even though it is semantically breaking, and changing a field’s type is usually wire-incompatible. This is the classic trap — a .proto rename with a JSON transcoding layer breaks the JSON clients while the binary path keeps working, and the bug is only visible in one of the two transports. For protobuf the safe set is: add fields with new numbers, add enum values with reserved protection on deleted numbers, and reserve the numbers and names of everything removed. For Avro, add a field with a default, add an enum symbol only if no old reader exists, and never rename.

# The compatibility gate. Fails the build on a breaking change; this is
