# what makes "we will version later" a policy rather than a hope.

#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail

NEW="$1"           # api/orders/v3/orders.proto
OLD="api/orders/v2/orders.proto"

if [ -f "$OLD" ]; then
  # buf breaking --against is the protobuf-native check: it understands
  # field numbers, reserved ranges, and service/method removals.
  if ! buf breaking "$NEW" --against "$OLD" --config buf.yaml; then
    echo "FAIL: breaking change in orders.proto"
    echo "  Allowed: add fields, add enum values, add methods."
    echo "  For anything else: bump the package, keep the old package"
    echo "  deployed, and run the deprecation procedure below."
    exit 1
  fi
fi

# Reserved-range check: catches a developer reusing a deleted field number,
# which is the one protobuf break that wire-compat tests will NOT catch
# because the number still parses.
python3 - "$NEW" <<'PY'
import re, sys
src = open(sys.argv[1]).read()
for m in re.finditer(r'reserved\s+(\d+)\s*;', src):
    used = re.search(rf'=\s*{m.group(1)}\s*;', src)
    if used:
        sys.exit(f"FAIL: field number {m.group(1)} is reserved and in use")
for m in re.finditer(r'reserved\s+"(\w+)"\s*;', src):
    if re.search(rf'\b{m.group(1)}\s*=\s*\d+\s*;', src):
        sys.exit(f"FAIL: field name {m.group(1)} is reserved and in use")
PY

echo "OK: no breaking change detected"

# Deprecation as an operational procedure, not a header. Four stages, with
# a hard date at the end of each. The traffic attribution step is what
# makes the cutover provable instead of hopeful.

# Stage 1: announce. Headers on EVERY response, plus a documentation page.
#   Deprecation: true
#   Sunset: Tue, 31 Mar 2027 23:59:59 GMT
#   Link: </docs/v2-deprecation>; rel="deprecation"

# Stage 2: observe. Per-consumer attribution is the whole point; you
# cannot sunset what you cannot attribute.
psql -d edge -c "
  SELECT consumer_id,
         count(*)                       AS calls_30d,
         max(called_at)                 AS last_seen,
         min(response_status)            AS min_status
    FROM api_call_log
   WHERE api_version = 2
     AND called_at > now() - interval '30 days'
   GROUP BY consumer_id
   ORDER BY calls_30d DESC;"
# The row to act on: a consumer with one call in six months. Email it
# individually; do not wait for it to migrate on its own.

# Stage 3: throttle. Make staying on v2 a cost, without breaking anyone.
curl -sS -X PATCH "$GATEWAY/admin/v2/rate" \
  -H 'content-type: application/json' \
  -d '{"rps":50,"sunset_enforced":true,"retry_after":300}'
# 50 rps is a migration forcing function, not an outage. Agree the date
# with each named consumer before enabling enforcement.

# Stage 4: remove. Only when the query above returns zero rows for 14
# consecutive days, and the removal has its own rollback path: keep the
# handler compiled behind a feature flag for one more release, so a
# surprise reappearance is a flag flip rather than a redeploy.
ChangeREST / JSONProtobufAvroRequires coordination
Add a field✓ Safe on responses; optional on requests✓ New number only✓ Must carry a defaultNo
Rename a field✗ Breaking✗ Wire-ok, semantics break✗ Value lost on readYes, dual-write then dual-read
Remove a field✗ Breaking⚠ Reserve the number and name⚠ Tolerated; document itDeprecation period
Change a type✗ Breaking✗ Breaking✗ Only within a promotion setYes, new field + migration
Add an enum value⚠ Only if clients ignore unknowns✓ Safe✗ Old readers fail to decodeConfirm every reader
Add an endpoint✓ Safe✓ Safen/aNo
Change default value✗ Breaking✗ Breaking✗ BreakingYes, explicit migration

Semantic breaks are invisible to every automated compatibility check. A field renamed in the .proto is wire-compatible and passes buf breaking; an Avro field whose meaning changes from gross to net keeps the same name, type and number and passes every registry check; a REST response field that changes from cents to dollars is textually identical to the client. None of these are caught by a diff. The only defences are a written definition per field that the schema comments reference, a golden-file test that asserts the values not just the shape, and a human review step that asks what the number means, not whether it changed type.

9.6 Error models, budgets and observability contracts

Specifies the error contract as a first-class API surface and the telemetry that makes client-visible failures diagnosable without server access.

Error responses are the most-read part of any API and the least designed. RFC 9457 application/problem+json is the current standard and should be the only shape on the wire, whatever the internal error type: a type URI that identifies the problem class and is dereferenceable to documentation, a title that is a short human-readable summary, an status mirroring the HTTP status, a detail specific to this occurrence, and an instance that is a URI identifying this specific occurrence. What must not be in it: a stack trace, an internal hostname, a SQL fragment, or a raw driver error — all four are reconnaissance, and all four appear in real incidents because someone added them "temporarily".

Two contracts matter as much as the body. First, the retry contract: the combination of status code, Retry-After and a documented backoff expectation is what a well-written client uses to decide whether to retry, and getting it wrong produces both retry storms and silent data loss. A 429 without Retry-After invites clients to retry immediately and amplifies the overload that caused it. Second, the observability contract: every error response should carry a correlation identifier that also appears in the server’s structured logs and traces, so a user-reported failure is resolvable from a screenshot. That single field is worth more than any amount of log retention.

// RFC 9457 problem documents. One constructor, one shape, no ad-hoc
// JSON error bodies anywhere in the codebase. (TypeScript / Node 24.)

export class Problem extends Error {
  constructor(
    readonly status: number,
    readonly type: string,          // stable URI, dereferenceable to docs
    readonly title: string,
    readonly detail: string,
    readonly instance: string,
    readonly meta: Record<string, unknown> = {},
  ) { super(detail); }

  static fromDomain(e: DomainError, req: RequestContext): Problem {
    switch (e.kind) {
      case 'validation':
        return new Problem(422, 'urn:problem:validation-failed',
          'Request validation failed', e.message, req.instance,
          { errors: e.fieldErrors });      // { field, code, message }[]
      case 'conflict':
        return new Problem(409, 'urn:problem:state-conflict',
          'Operation not allowed in current state', e.message,
          req.instance, { currentState: e.current, attempted: e.attempted });
      case 'precondition':
        return new Problem(412, 'urn:problem:precondition-failed',
          'Precondition failed', 'The resource changed since you read it',
          req.instance, { expectedEtag: e.expected, currentEtag: e.current });
      case 'rate_limited':
        return new Problem(429, 'urn:problem:rate-limited',
          'Too many requests', e.message, req.instance,
          { limit: e.limit, remaining: e.remaining, resetAfterSeconds: e.reset });
      case 'shed':
        // 503 + Retry-After, distinct from 500: the request was refused
        // deliberately, and retrying later is the correct client action.
        return new Problem(503, 'urn:problem:overloaded',
          'Service temporarily unavailable',
          'The service is shedding load; retry after the interval given',
          req.instance);
      default:
        // Log the detail server-side; return a generic public message.
        log.error({ err: e, corrId: req.correlationId, instance: req.instance });
        return new Problem(500, 'urn:problem:internal',
          'Internal server error',
          'An unexpected error occurred. Quote the instance URI when reporting it.',
          req.instance);
    }
  }

  toResponse(res: Response, correlationId: string): void {
    res.status(this.status)
       .type('application/problem+json')
       .set('x-correlation-id', correlationId)   // THE observability contract
       .json({ type: this.type, title: this.title, status: this.status,
               detail: this.detail, instance: this.instance, ...this.meta });
  }
}

# Rate-limit and budget headers, per RFC 9331 (RateLimit) and the older
# X-RateLimit-* family. Emit BOTH during a migration: new clients read
# the standard, old clients read the legacy names.
#
#   RateLimit: limit=100, remaining=42, reset=17
#   RateLimit-Policy: 100;w=60, 1000;w=3600
#   Retry-After: 17          (seconds, on 429 and on 503 shed)
#   X-RateLimit-Limit: 100
#   X-RateLimit-Remaining: 42
#   X-RateLimit-Reset: 17
#
# Why "remaining" must be exact: a client that reserves budget based on a
# stale value will overshoot, and a client that does not see a remaining
# value cannot pace itself, so it either bursts or stalls.
#
# Per-client budgets at the gateway, in priority order:
#   1. Tenant identity from a verified token, not from an X-Forwarded-For
#      header, which any caller can set.
#   2. Route class: /checkout is exempt from the global limiter and has
#      its own, smaller, budget.
#   3. Two simultaneous limits: a short burst window (100/s) to absorb
#      legitimate bursts, and a long sustained window (10k/hour) to cap
#      total cost. A single limit cannot do both.
#   4. Response headers always present, including on 200, so a client can
#      self-pace before it is throttled rather than after.
#
# Client obligations, stated in the documentation and enforced by
# monitoring: exponential backoff with full jitter, honour Retry-After
# over any local backoff, and a hard retry ceiling of 3 per request.
# A client that retries a non-idempotent POST without an Idempotency-Key
# will duplicate writes, and no server-side limiter can prevent that.

# Error-budget observability: the queries that answer "what is broken and
# for whom" without access to server logs. Prometheus 3.x.

# 1. Top error classes by problem type URI, not by message. The URI is
#    stable; messages are not, and grouping by message fragments over time.
#      count by (problem_type) (rate(http_responses_total{status=~"4..|5.."}[5m]))
- record: api:error_rate:by_type5m
  expr: |
    sum by (problem_type, route) (
      rate(http_responses_total{status=~"4..|5.."}[5m])
    )

# 2. Retry-After compliance: are clients honouring the throttle, or are
#    they amplifying it? A rising ratio of 429-followed-by-immediate-retry
#    is the signature of a retry storm forming in real time.
- record: api:throttle_amplification:ratio5m
  expr: |
    sum(rate(http_requests_total{status="429"}[5m])
        and on(instance) rate(http_requests_total{status="429", retry_immediately="true"}[5m]))
    /
    clamp_min(sum(rate(http_requests_total{status="429"}[5m])), 1)

# 3. Which CONSUMER is affected. Attribution is per API key, not per IP:
#    NAT and mobile carriers make IP attribution meaningless.
- record: api:errors:by_consumer1h
  expr: |
    sum by (consumer_id, status) (
      rate(http_responses_total{status=~"4..|5.."}[1h])
    ) * 3600

# 4. The one that finds silent data loss: successful writes that were
#    never confirmed downstream. A rising count here with flat error
#    rates means the failure is invisible from the outside.
- record: api:unconfirmed_writes:count1h
  expr: |
    sum(rate(outbox_created_total[1h])) - sum(rate(outbox_published_total[1h]))
Error classStatusRetryableRetry-AfterClient obligation
Validation failure422✗ NeverAbsentFix the request; field map in errors
Authentication missing401✗ Not blindAbsentObtain a token; do not loop
Authorisation denied403✗ NeverAbsentRequest access; retrying is pointless
Not found404✗ NeverAbsentCheck the identifier; may be eventual
State conflict409✗ NeverAbsentRe-read state, then decide
Precondition failed412✓ After re-readAbsentRe-fetch ETag and re-apply
Rate limited429✓ YesRequiredHonour Retry-After, back off with jitter
Shed / overloaded503✓ YesRequiredBack off; this is deliberate, not a fault
Dependency timeout504✓ YesRecommendedBack off; only if the call is idempotent
Internal error500✓ CautiouslyAbsentQuote the instance URI in a report

Distinguish 503 shed from 500 failure in your own dashboards. A shed 503 is the load-management system working, and it should appear as a green line on an availability graph with a red annotation on the latency graph. If shed responses are counted against the availability SLI, the SLI becomes a measure of your own shedding policy rather than of user experience, and the incentive quietly inverts: the cheapest way to hit the SLO becomes refusing all traffic. Track them as separate series and page only on the 500 rate.

Exercises 9

  1. Audit an existing REST surface for method and status correctness: classify every endpoint as safe, idempotent or unsafe, and flag every violation including missing Location on 201 and missing Retry-After on 429. Deliverable: the classification table and a rewritten OpenAPI 3.1 document for the ten worst offenders.
  2. Implement idempotency-key handling with a request-hash guard backed by Redis 8.x, then fire 50 concurrent identical requests plus 5 reusing one key with different bodies. Deliverable: test output showing exactly one side effect for the first group and five 422 rejections for the second, with the stored response replayed verbatim.
  3. Build a GraphQL endpoint with DataLoader, depth limiting at 7 and complexity capping at 300, then measure SQL statement count for a one-parent and a twenty-parent query of the same shape. Deliverable: the statement counts before and after, and the persisted-query hash you would enable in production.
  4. Set up a schema registry with BACKWARD_TRANSITIVE and a CI gate, then attempt a breaking change by renaming an Avro field and by reusing a reserved protobuf field number. Deliverable: the failing build output for each attempt and the migration you would run instead.
  5. Implement RFC 9457 problem documents end to end with correlation identifiers, and verify the whole contract by triggering ten error classes and checking that each carries the right status, type URI, retryability and correlation id. Deliverable: the ten captured responses as a test fixture, plus the retry contract table published to API consumers.

10 · Service Boundaries, Coupling and the Monolith-to-Microservices Path

10.1 Finding boundaries: bounded contexts, DDD and team topology10.2 Decomposition heuristics and data ownership10.3 Coupling metrics and contract-first design10.4 The modular monolith as a deliberate target10.5 Strangler fig: the extraction sequence10.6 Microservices anti-patterns

Boundaries are the highest-leverage decision in a distributed system and the one most often made for the wrong reasons. This section grounds boundary selection in business capability and team topology rather than in entity count, and supplies the measurable coupling metrics that let an existing codebase be audited instead of argued about. The modular monolith is presented as a legitimate destination rather than a failure state, and the extraction sequence is given as a reversible, incrementally deployable procedure with traffic mirroring as the verification tool. The section names the failure patterns explicitly, because a distributed monolith — services coupled by synchronous chatty calls and shared tables — costs more than either of the two designs it imitates.

10.1 Finding boundaries: bounded contexts, DDD and team topology

Establishes that service boundaries should follow business capability and organisational ownership, and that the ubiquitous language is the artefact that makes a boundary defensible.

A service boundary is a claim about which changes may be made independently. If two components must be deployed together, they are one component, and splitting them buys the appearance of autonomy while adding a network hop, a partial-failure mode and a contract to maintain. The reliable signals that a boundary is genuine are organisational: the components are changed by different teams, on different schedules, and one team’s release is never blocked by another’s. Conway’s law is not a pessimistic observation here; it is a design tool. If you cannot find a boundary that maps to a team, you have found a deployment unit rather than a service.

Within the codebase, the Domain-Driven Design vocabulary remains the most precise tool available for naming what a boundary is. A bounded context is a region of the model in which one particular meaning of a word holds: "Customer" in the sales context has a billing address and a credit limit, while "Customer" in the support context has a communication history and a satisfaction score, and unifying them is a category error that produces the worst kind of schema — a customer table with forty nullable columns, each existing for one context’s benefit. The practical test for whether two contexts are correctly separated is whether they use the same noun to mean different things. If they do, the noun is not the boundary, and encoding it in a shared table guarantees the coupling.

Two supporting concepts complete the toolset. An anti-corruption layer is a translation boundary that stops one context’s model from leaking into another’s, and it is the correct answer when integrating with a legacy or third-party model you do not control. A shared kernel is a deliberately small, jointly owned, exceptionally stable piece of code that two contexts genuinely share; it is a legitimate design and it is also the most common source of a slow-burn coupling problem, because shared kernels grow. The operational rule is that a shared kernel must have a named owner, a stated stability promise, and a review gate — without all three it is just a common library that everyone depends on.

// Boundary enforcement by the type system, not by convention. Two contexts,
// two meanings of "Customer", and nothing that lets them be confused.
//
// package order/  (ORDERING bounded context)
type CustomerRef struct {
    ID       uuid.UUID        // opaque; no cross-context foreign key
    Billing  address.Postal
    CreditLimitMinor int64    // currency in minor units, never float64
    PaymentTermsDays int
}

// package support/  (SUPPORT bounded context)
type ContactPoint struct {
    ID       uuid.UUID
    Customer uuid.UUID        // a REFERENCE, not a shared entity type
    Channel  Channel          // Email | Phone | Chat
    Verified bool
}
type InteractionLog struct {
    ID       uuid.UUID
    Contact  uuid.UUID
    At       time.Time
    Summary  string
}

// Neither file imports the other. The compiler enforces it; a review
// comment does not. If a change requires both, the change is telling you
// the boundary is in the wrong place, which is a finding worth more than
// the feature.
//
// The test that proves the boundary is real:
//   go build ./...   with an import of support in order -> FAILS
//   The failure is the deliverable of this exercise, not an accident.

# Context map: bounded contexts, their relationships, and the anti-patterns
# visible in the arrows. Keep this in the repo next to the code and update
# it in the same pull request that changes a dependency.
#
#              publishes OrderPlaced        +----------------+
#   +----------------+ ---------------------> |   FULFILMENT   |
#   |   ORDERING     |<---------------------- | (bounded ctx)  |
#   | Owns: Order,   |   ShipmentDispatched   | Owns: Shipment,|
#   |  OrderLine,    |                         |  Carrier, Pkg  |
#   |  PricingRule   |                         +----------------+
#   +-------+--------+                                  |
#           | OrderConfirmed                           | DeliveryAttempted
#           v                                          v
#   +----------------+                        +------------------+
#   |   BILLING      |                        |  NOTIFICATION    |
#   | Owns: Invoice, |                        | Owns: Template,   |
#   |  Payment,      |                        |  ChannelPref,    |
#   |  LedgerEntry   |                        |  DispatchLog     |
#   +-------+--------+                        +------------------+
#           | PaymentCaptured
#           v
#   +----------------+
#   | CUSTOMER       |  SUPPORT owns ContactPoint + InteractionLog.
#   | (support ctx)  |  Deliberately NOT the same entity as BILLING's Payer.
#   +----------------+
#
# Rules the map makes visible:
#   - Unidirectional arrows. A pair of arrows in both directions between
#     two contexts means a cyclic dependency: pick the direction of
#     authority and route the other way through the broker.
#   - No arrow that skips the broker for cross-context notifications. A
#     synchronous arrow is a coupling with a latency cost and a
#     availability dependency.
#   - No shared table between any two contexts. That is not a boundary,
#     that is a shared-memory bug with a network in the middle.
#
# Tooling: the map is generated from the service registry plus static
# analysis of imports and topics, so it cannot silently diverge from
# reality the way a diagram in a wiki always does.

Team topology is the primary constraint, not an afterthought. The two-pizza heuristic and its critics both make the same underlying point: if a service is owned by six teams, no single team can change it safely, and the true boundary is somewhere coarser. The number of independently deployable units a platform can support scales roughly with the number of platform engineers, not with team count, which is why unqualified enthusiasm for one-repository-per-service frequently reverses into a monolith with network latency. A service that needs a full platform team to deploy is not a service; it is a component with extra build steps.

Boundary signalEvidence to look forVerdictWhat to do
Independent change cadenceCommit histories on two components never touch the same file✓ Genuine boundaryExtract
Single owning teamDisjoint CODEOWNERS entries✓ Genuine boundaryExtract
Distinct ubiquitous languageSame noun, different glossary per context✓ Two contextsDo not merge; add an ACL
Different availability needOne component needs 99.99%, another 99.5%✓ SeparateExtract, isolate blast radius
Different scaling profileOne is IO-bound, the other CPU-bound✓ SeparateExtract; different node pools
Shared write tableTwo services hold INSERT on one relation✗ Fictional boundarySplit ownership, publish events
Synchronous fan-out ≥ 4Call graph crosses a service 4+ times per request✗ Distributed monolithCollapse or go async
Shared entity moduleOne entities package imported everywhere✗ Shared kernel gone wrongSplit by context, version it

Exercises 10

  1. Produce a context map for a real domain you work in: at least four bounded contexts, the ubiquitous-language terms in each, and every term that means something different in two contexts. Deliverable: the map plus a glossary of conflicting terms with both definitions written out in full.
  2. Overlay your team topology on the context map and find every context split across teams or joined between them. Deliverable: a proposed realignment with the deployment-ordering consequence of each move stated explicitly.
  3. Take a table written by two services and design the ownership split: which service owns which columns, what event contract replaces the direct write, and how the read model is rebuilt. Deliverable: the ownership table and a migration script that runs against PostgreSQL 18 with no downtime.
  4. Write the domain model for one bounded context such that another context cannot import its types, enforced by the compiler rather than by a review rule. Deliverable: the induced build failure and the CI check that keeps it induced.
  5. Argue in writing whether one specific component in your system is a service or a module, citing the three strongest pieces of evidence from the coupling metrics in 10.3. Deliverable: a one-page decision record a reviewing engineer could reasonably argue with.

10.2 Decomposition heuristics and data ownership

Supplies the heuristics that select what to extract first, and the data-ownership pattern that makes an extraction possible without a shared database.

Decomposition heuristics that hold up in practice, in the order they should be applied. Start where the change rate is highest: the component that changes most often is the one whose release coordination costs the most, and extracting it produces the fastest payback. Start where the scaling profile differs: a CPU-bound image processor and an IO-bound order API sharing a deployment unit forces both to scale on the larger resource’s curve. Start where the data boundary is already clear: a component that already owns a coherent set of tables is a mechanical extraction; one that shares rows with four other components is a research project. Start where a dependency is one-way: if component A calls B and B never calls A, the extraction of A is contained. A cycle means you must break the cycle first, and breaking a cycle usually means a design decision, not a code move.

Data ownership is the part that determines whether an extraction is real. The rule is that one service owns a data set exclusively: no other service holds write credentials on those tables, and no other service reads them directly. Reads across a boundary go through an API or a purpose-built read model, and the reason is not architectural purity — it is that a direct cross-service database read creates a dependency that no amount of network isolation can make safe, because the reader is relying on the writer’s schema and the writer has no idea who is reading. The mechanical pattern for a read-heavy cross-boundary query is a local read model populated by the owning service’s event stream, which trades a bounded staleness for the complete removal of the coupling.

-- The data-ownership cutover, in order, with no window in which two
-- services can write the same table. PostgreSQL 18.

-- STEP 1. Move the table to a dedicated schema. No access change yet;
-- this is the cheap, reversible part.
ALTER TABLE public.orders SET SCHEMA orders_core;

-- STEP 2. Revoke everything from the old owner's application role, and
--         grant only what the new owner needs. Do this BEFORE the new
--         service is live: an unused grant is harmless, a moment with two
--         writers is not.
REVOKE ALL ON SCHEMA orders_core FROM order_api_app;
GRANT  USAGE ON SCHEMA orders_core TO orders_service_app;
GRANT  SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE ON ALL TABLES IN SCHEMA orders_core
       TO orders_service_app;
ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES IN SCHEMA orders_core
       GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE ON TABLES TO orders_service_app;

-- STEP 3. The other services stop reading the table. Create the read
--         model on each consumer side, populated from the outbox stream.
CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW legacy_order_summary AS
  SELECT customer_id, count(*) AS order_count, max(placed_at) AS last_order
    FROM orders_core.orders GROUP BY customer_id;
-- Refresh via a small job, or drop it entirely in favour of a projection
-- built from orders.v3 events. The MV is the bridge, not the destination.

-- STEP 4. Delete the MV once every consumer reads from the projection.
--         Leaving a bridge in place indefinitely is how systems acquire
--         two sources of truth.

-- What must never happen in this sequence: a period during which both
-- services hold write credentials, even briefly. Two writers on one
-- table is a split-brain with no detection, because neither service
-- knows the other exists. Revoke first, then cut over.

# The traffic-shed route that lets you extract a service without changing
# clients. Envoy 1.34 fronting an existing monolith, adding one route.
# This is the first half of the strangler sequence in 10.5.

apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: HTTPRoute
metadata:
  name: orders-routing
  namespace: prod
spec:
  parentRefs:
    - name: public-gateway
      namespace: infra
  hostnames: ["api.example.com"]
  rules:
    # Everything not matched below continues to serve from the monolith.
    # Traffic SHADOWS so the new implementation is exercised in production
    # at full fidelity without a single user depending on it.
    - matches:
        - path:
            type: PathPrefix
            value: /v3/orders
      filters:
        - type: requestMirror
          requestMirror:
            backendRef:
              name: orders-service-v2
              port: 8080
            percent: 100          # mirror 100% of traffic
            fraction:
              numerator: 5
              denominator: 100    # but only 5% of mirrored RESPONSES
                                  # are compared; the rest are discarded
      backendRefs:
        - name: monolith
          port: 8080

---
# The comparison is the entire point. Same request, two implementations,
# and a diff on the fields that matter.
apiVersion: v1
kind: ConfigMap
metadata:
  name: shadow-diff-config
  namespace: prod
data:
  fields: |
    id,status,total_minor,currency,lines[].sku,lines[].qty
  ignore: |
    timestamps,request_id,trace_id,etag   # legitimately differ
  tolerance: |
    total_minor: 0        # must be EXACT for money
    status: exact         # must be EXACT for a state machine
  alert_after: "5 consecutive mismatches within 60 s"
HeuristicSignal to measureExtract first?Reason
Highest change rateCommits per week, last 6 months✓ YesRelease coordination is the real cost
Differing scale profileCPU-to-IO ratio per endpoint✓ YesForces a shared sizing decision otherwise
Clear data boundaryTables touched exclusively by one module✓ YesMechanical extraction, low risk
One-way dependencyCall graph has no cycle through it✓ YesContained blast radius
Differing compliance scopeAudit scope, data classification✓ YesIsolates the certification boundary
Shared tables, many writersColumn-level usage across modules✗ LastResearch project, not a refactor
Dependency cycleCall graph cycle detection✗ Break firstRequires a design decision
Low traffic, low changeCommits and rps both near zero✗ No benefitCosts a service, buys nothing

Do not extract a component that is still changing three times a week, unless the extraction itself reduces that cost. A service extracted mid-flight forces every subsequent change into a distributed transaction of human coordination — a PR here, a schema change there, a coordinated deploy — and the team pays that tax immediately in exchange for a benefit that arrives later. The exception is a component whose dependency is the problem: a module pulled in by everything is expensive to change regardless of its own rate, and extracting it converts an implicit dependency into an explicit contract. The other half of this warning is about the reverse mistake: extracting a component that is stable and isolated produces a network hop, a deployment pipeline, an on-call rotation and a runbook, in exchange for the ability to deploy it slightly more independently — which it was already, because nothing else was changing.

10.3 Coupling metrics and contract-first design

Defines the measurable coupling metrics that make boundary quality auditable, and specifies contract tests as the mechanism that keeps an interface honest.

Coupling should be measured, not debated, because the debate always resolves in favour of the architecture already in place. The metrics that carry information are: fan-in and fan-out at the module and service level, where high fan-out is a liability and high fan-in is a liability of a different kind (a widely depended-upon service is a single point of failure for everyone); affinity, the percentage of a component’s changes that co-occur in the same commit as changes to another, which is the empirical test of whether two modules are one unit; instability, defined as fan-out divided by the sum of fan-in and fan-out, which correctly flags that a highly stable high-fan-in module is fine while a highly unstable high-fan-out module is a hazard; and temporal coupling, the degree to which two components must be deployed within a short window of each other, which is the metric that most honestly distinguishes a service from a module.

Contract-first design inverts the workflow: the interface is specified and reviewed before the implementation, the specification is the artefact both sides build against, and the tests generated from it run on both sides. In practice this means a provider publishes a contract, a consumer writes a test against it, and CI runs the consumer’s test against the provider’s real implementation — which catches the break at the provider’s commit rather than at the consumer’s deploy. The tool that does this most completely is Pact, and the discipline that makes it work is that a contract is only as good as its verification: a provider can break a contract it never had a consumer test for, so contract coverage must be a metric that is itself tracked, because a green build with 40% contract coverage is a green build that proves very little.

# Coupling metrics from a real codebase. This is an audit tool, not a
# design tool: it tells you what the boundaries actually are, regardless
# of where the diagram says they are. (Python 3.14, stdlib only.)
import ast, collections, pathlib, json, sys

def module_name(path: pathlib.Path, root: pathlib.Path) -> str:
    rel = path.relative_to(root).with_suffix('')
    return '.'.join(rel.parts[:-1]) if rel.name == '__init__' \
           else '.'.join(rel.parts)

def imports(path: pathlib.Path) -> set[str]:
    tree = ast.parse(path.read_text(encoding='utf-8'))
    out = set()
    for node in ast.walk(tree):
        if isinstance(node, ast.Import):
            out.update(a.name for a in node.names)
        elif isinstance(node, ast.ImportFrom) and node.level == 1 and node.module:
            out.add(node.module)
    return out

root = pathlib.Path(sys.argv[1])
edges: list[tuple[str, str]] = []
for py in root.rglob('*.py'):
    if '.venv' in py.parts or 'tests' in py.parts:
        continue
    src = module_name(py, root)
    for dep in imports(py):
        if dep.split('.')[0] in ('os', 'sys', 'json', 'typing', 'asyncio'):
            continue
        edges.append((src, dep))

fan_in  = collections.Counter(d for _s, d in edges)
fan_out = collections.Counter(s for s, _d in edges)
nodes   = set(fan_in) | set(fan_out)

def instability(n: str) -> float:
    i, o = fan_in[n], fan_out[n]
    return o / (i + o) if (i + o) else 0.0

print(f"{'module':<44}{'in':>5}{'out':>5}{'I':>7}")
for n in sorted(nodes, key=lambda x: -fan_out[x]):
    flag = '  <-- extracted-service candidate' \
           if fan_out[n] >= 12 and instability(n) > 0.8 else ''
    print(f"{n:<44}{fan_in[n]:>5}{fan_out[n]:>5}{instability(n):>7.2f}{flag}")

# The number that decides whether a boundary is real:
cycles = [e for e in edges if (e[1], e[0]) in set(edges)]
print(f"\nbidirectional (cyclic) import pairs: {len(cycles)}")
for a, b in cycles[:20]:
    print(f"  {a} <-> {b}   # a cycle: these cannot be separated as-is")

// Contract-first: the consumer writes the contract, the provider verifies it
// against its real implementation. The break is caught at the PROVIDER's
// commit, not at the consumer's deploy. (JVM / Go / Node: Pact works
// across all three via pact-jvm, pact-go and pact-js.)

// ---- CONSUMER SIDE: this is the test that defines the contract ----
// It asserts only what this consumer actually depends on, which is the
// point: a contract asserts the dependency, not the whole API.
describe('OrderService', () => {
  beforeAll(() => provider.setup());

  it('returns an order with the fields checkout needs', async () => {
    await provider.given('an order 7f3a exists in state placed')
      .when('the consumer calls GetOrder')
      .then('the response status is 200')
      .and('the response has an id equal to 7f3a')
      .and('the response has a state of placed')
      .and('the response has a total_minor that is an integer')
      // And the constraint that must hold, which no schema expresses:
      .and('the response total_minor is greater than or equal to 0')
      // And the field the consumer added in v2, which pins the version:
      .and('the response has an etag');
  });

  it('returns 404 for an unknown order, never 500', async () => {
    await provider.given('no order with id 00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000000')
      .when('the consumer calls GetOrder')
      .then('the response status is 404')
      // The specific failure this catches: a backend that throws rather
      // than returning empty is indistinguishable from an outage.
      .and('the response has no body other than the problem document');
  });
});

// ---- PROVIDER SIDE: CI runs the pact against the real service ----
// The provider cannot merge a change that breaks a pact in `main`.

// Contract coverage: the metric that makes "we have contracts" honest.
pact-broker can-i-deploy --pacticipant OrderService-v2 \
                        --version "$(git rev-parse HEAD)" \
                        --to prod

// If this prints "yes", every breaking change is already verified against
// every consumer's expectations for the environment being deployed. If it
// prints "no", the deploy is blocked - and that is the entire value of
// having the broker in the path at all.
Coupling typeMetricHealthy valueWhat excess meansRemedy
Structural, outboundFan-out per module< 12God module; everything depends on itSplit by bounded context
Structural, inboundFan-in per moduleDeliberate, not accidentalOne team’s change breaks manyNarrow the interface
Instabilityout / (in + out)< 0.7 unless deliberately volatileVolatile and widely depended onStabilise or invert the dependency
TemporalDeploys within 24 h of each other< 10%✗ Not a service; it is a moduleMerge, or formalise the contract
Change affinityCo-changing commit fraction< 15%Two modules, one unitDo not extract
DataTables written by more than one service0Split-brain with no detectionAssign ownership, publish events
Temporal, in codeSynchronous hops per request≤ 3Distributed monolithMerge or go asynchronous

Run the affinity metric before every extraction, and again after. If two modules co-change in more than 15% of commits, extracting one of them does not create independence — it creates a distributed transaction of human coordination, which is strictly more expensive than the shared repository it replaced. The measurement takes ten minutes, the extraction takes two quarters, and the metric is the only reliable predictor of whether the quarter was well spent. Equally, run it on the whole candidate list rather than on your favourite: the highest-affinity pair is always the pair you should not split first.

10.4 The modular monolith as a deliberate target

Presents the modular monolith as a defensible destination with specific enforcement mechanisms, and states the conditions under which it is the correct choice.

A modular monolith is a single deployable with enforced internal boundaries: modules communicate through explicit public interfaces, the build fails on a forbidden import, each module owns its tables, and cross-module access goes through an interface or an in-process event. It is not a compromise and it is not a staging state — for a system with fewer than about ten independently-changing capabilities and a team count below the number at which coordination becomes the dominant cost, it is the correct architecture, and extracting microservices from it is a downgrade in both operability and transaction simplicity.

The three conditions that make a modular monolith the right target are measurable. Change coupling is low: the affinity metric from 10.3 shows modules changing independently. Scaling profiles are similar: no module needs an order of magnitude more CPU or IO than the others, so one deployment unit is not a compromise. The team is small enough that a full-system understanding is achievable — as a rule of thumb, fewer than about eight teams, since beyond that the coordination cost exceeds the deployment-unit benefit. Fail any of the three and the boundary should become a real network boundary, at which point the same discipline applies: explicit interface, enforced ownership, no shared state.

The enforcement mechanism is what separates a modular monolith from a well-organised ball of mud. Documentation does not work, because a documentation rule is violated under deadline pressure and nothing catches it. Compile-time or test-time enforcement does work, and the tools are language-specific: ArchUnit in Java, module boundary tests in Go using package visibility rules, `import-linter` in Python, and internal/ package visibility in Rust. The rule set should be small enough to be understood — forbidding imports across a fixed list of module pairs is enforceable; a general "no cross-module access" rule produces exceptions that become the new normal.

// Enforcing module boundaries in Go 1.25. Go's answer is package
// visibility plus a test that asserts the dependency direction, because
// Go has no way to make a package private within a module other than by
// placing it under internal/.

/*
repo layout:
  cmd/api/                    # composition root: the only place that wires
  internal/
    pricing/                  # module: public interface + implementation
    orders/
    fulfilment/
    platform/
      boundary_test.go        # the enforcement lives here
  contracts/                  # interfaces each module publishes
*/

// contracts/pricing.go: the ONLY thing orders may know about pricing.
package contracts

type QuoteRequest struct {
    SKU      string
    Qty      int64
    Customer string
}

type Quote struct {
    UnitMinor    int64
    Currency     string
    ExpiresAtUnix int64
}

type Pricer interface {
    Quote(ctx context.Context, req QuoteRequest) (Quote, error)
}

// internal/orders/service.go imports contracts, never internal/pricing.
import "acme/orders/contracts"

// internal/pricing/service.go implements it, importing contracts.
// Neither module imports the other. If pricing's internals leak, the
// compile fails, which is the entire value of the arrangement.

// The boundary test. This is the artefact that makes "modular monolith"
// a real architecture rather than a directory layout.
package boundary_test

import (
    "go/build"
    "go/parser"
    "go/token"
    "strings"
    "testing"
)

// allowed declares the permitted dependency direction. Small, explicit,
// and enumerable: a general rule would produce exceptions that erode it.
var allowed = map[string][]string{
    "internal/orders":     {"internal/pricing", "internal/platform", "contracts"},
    "internal/pricing":    {"internal/platform", "contracts"},
    "internal/fulfilment": {"internal/platform", "contracts"},
    "internal/billing":    {"internal/platform", "contracts"},
    "internal/platform":   {},
    "cmd/api":             {"internal/orders", "internal/pricing",
                            "internal/fulfilment", "internal/billing",
                            "internal/platform"},
}

// forbidden is the thing people actually do, spelled out so the failure
// message can name the specific violation rather than "import not allowed".
var forbidden = map[string]string{
    "internal/orders":     "orders must reach pricing only through contracts.Pricer",
    "internal/pricing":    "pricing must not import domain modules",
    "internal/fulfilment": "fulfilment must reach orders only through contracts",
    "internal/billing":    "billing must not import fulfilment internals",
}

func TestModuleBoundaries(t *testing.T) {
    fset := token.NewFileSet()
    pkgs, err := parser.ParseDir(fset, "./", nil, parser.ImportsOnly)
    if err != nil {
        t.Fatal(err)
    }
    violations := 0
    for pkgPath, allowedDeps := range allowed {
        pkg, ok := pkgs[pkgPath]
        if !ok {
            t.Fatalf("module %s not found: the rule set has drifted", pkgPath)
        }
        for _, f := range pkg.Files {
            for _, imp := range f.Imports {
                path := strings.Trim(imp.Path.Value, `"`)
                if !strings.HasPrefix(path, "acme/orders/") { continue }
                rel := strings.TrimPrefix(path, "acme/orders/")
                if contains(allowedDeps, rel) { continue }
                violations++
                t.Errorf("BOUNDARY VIOLATION: %s imports %s\n  reason: %s\n" +
                         "  fix: depend on an interface in contracts/, or move\n" +
                         "       shared code into internal/platform/",
                    pkgPath, rel, forbidden[pkgPath])
            }
        }
    }
    if violations > 0 {
        t.Errorf("%d boundary violation(s). The build fails here so that "+
                 "the review discussion does not have to.", violations)
    }
}

// Table ownership, the other half. A second test asserts that each
// migration directory belongs to exactly one module, so a table cannot
// silently acquire a second writer.
func TestTableOwnershipIsSingleWriter(t *testing.T) {
    owners := map[string]string{}  // table -> module, from CODEOWNERS
    for _, m := range migrations.List() {
        for _, tbl := range m.TablesTouched() {
            if prev, dup := owners[tbl]; dup {
                t.Errorf("TABLE %s is written by both %s and %s\n"+
                         "  fix: assign ownership, publish an event instead",
                         tbl, prev, m.Module)
            }
            owners[tbl] = m.Module
        }
    }
}
DimensionModular monolithMicroservicesDistributed monolith
Cross-module callFunction call, ~50 nsNetwork, 0.5–50 msNetwork, 4+ hops per request
Cross-module transaction✓ One local transactionSaga or 2PC✗ Chained synchronous 2PC
Deploy units110–20010–200, must be in lockstep
Boundary enforcement✓ Compiler and tests✓ Network and contracts✗ None; conventions only
Local debugging✓ One process, one stack trace✗ Distributed trace required✗ Worse than either
Independent scaling✗ One unit✓ Per service✗ Uniform, which is the point
Operational load per unitLowHigh, per service✗ Highest; all the cost, no benefit
Correct for< 8 teams, low change couplingMany teams, divergent scale or complianceNothing

A modular monolith without a boundary test is a monolith with folders. The failure is gradual and always for the same reason: a developer needs one field from another module, adds a direct import rather than extending an interface, the coupling is invisible because everything still compiles and still deploys together, and six months later the "modules" are mutually recursive with a 4,000-line import graph that no one can reason about. The mitigation is a test that fails the build, run on every pull request, with an error message that states what to do instead — "orders must reach pricing only through contracts.Pricer" rather than "import not permitted". The cost is one test file per repository; the cost of not having it is the death of the architecture, discovered during the first large refactor rather than in advance.

10.5 Strangler fig: the extraction sequence

Specifies the eight-step extraction sequence, with traffic mirroring as the verification mechanism and a rollback path at every stage.

The strangler fig pattern replaces functionality incrementally by routing traffic at the edge, and its value is that every step is independently reversible. The sequence below is ordered so that the risky steps happen while the blast radius is smallest, and each step has an explicit exit criterion. The order is not arbitrary: data extraction comes last precisely because it is the only step that cannot be cleanly reversed.

# The eight steps, with exit criteria. Each is independently reversible.
#
# 1. CHARACTERISE. Freeze the current behaviour as a contract.
#    Record: p50/p95/p99 per route, the response body shape for 100% of
#    real traffic over 7 days, and the error rate per route.
#    EXIT: a golden-file corpus of 10k real responses, committed.
#    WHY FIRST: without this you cannot prove the new service is equivalent;
#    you can only assert it is.
#
# 2. SEAM. Introduce the routing layer. Put Envoy or the ingress in front
#    with a 100% route to the existing monolith. No behaviour change.
#    EXIT: p99 latency of the new hop added to the baseline, < 2 ms.
#    ROLLBACK: delete the route.
#
# 3. SHADOW. Mirror 100% of traffic to the new implementation with the
#    response discarded. Compare on the fields that matter.
#    EXIT: 7 days, zero mismatches on the constrained fields
#          (id, status, total_minor, currency, lines[].sku).
#    NOTE: total_minor and status must match EXACTLY. If they do not,
#    the difference is a rounding rule or a state-machine bug, and both
#    are worth finding before any user depends on it.
#    ROLLBACK: set mirror percent to 0.
#
# 4. CANARY READS. Route 1% of reads to the new service.
#    EXIT: error rate and p99 at the new service are within 10% of the
#          monolith, for 48 hours, including a peak-traffic period.
#    ROLLBACK: set the read weight to 0. Takes effect in seconds.
#
# 5. CANARY WRITES. This is the irreversible-ordering step and it comes
#    only after reads are proven. Two options:
#      (a) DUAL WRITE with the new service as the target and a CDC or
#          outbox relay replicating back to the monolith's tables.
#      (b) WRITE-THEN-COMPENSATE, which is worse and only acceptable
#          where the write is idempotent.
#    EXIT: zero divergence over 72 hours at peak.
#    ROLLBACK: stop the relay, copy the new service's state forward once.
#    THIS IS WHERE PROJECTS FAIL. Do not skip step 4.
#
# 6. DUAL READ. Both implementations serve; compare every response and
#    alert on divergence. This is a bug-finding phase, not a check-box.
#    EXIT: 14 days with zero P1 divergence.
#
# 7. CUTOVER. 100% traffic to the new service. The monolith path stays
#    deployed but unrouted, behind a flag.
#    EXIT: one full business cycle, including month-end if it is relevant.
#    ROLLBACK: the flag. A flag flip, not a redeploy.
#
# 8. DELETE. Remove the monolith path only after the rollback flag has
#    been off for a full release cycle. Anything sooner and you have
#    traded a working system for an unverified one.

# Step 5 in detail: the write cutover, which is where extractions die.
# The requirement is ONE authoritative writer at all times, with a
# reversible relay between them during the transition.

# Phase A: the monolith remains the sole writer. The new service's tables
# are populated by a relay FROM the monolith, read-only on the new side.
#   This is safe: two databases, one writer, no divergence possible.
#   Verify by comparing row counts and checksums hourly, continuously.
SELECT count(*) AS rows,
       sum(hashtext(t::text)::bigint) AS content_hash
  FROM orders_core.orders
 WHERE updated_at > now() - interval '1 hour';
# A hash mismatch means the relay is behind or dropping rows. This query
# is the canary for the whole phase, and it must run on a schedule.

# Phase B: the new service becomes the writer. The relay REVERSES,
# replicating new writes back into the monolith's tables. Two writers now
# exist, but only one is authoritative and the other's writes are being
# overwritten - which is fine for the duration of the canary, and
# disastrous if it is left running. The relay must have a hard TTL.
#   Set the relay's own expiry as a Kubernetes CronJob with
#   concurrencyPolicy: Forbid and a deadline, so it cannot outlive the
#   canary window even if the person running it forgets.

# Phase C: the monolith becomes read-only and is then deleted.
#
# THE DUAL-WRITE ALTERNATIVE, and why it is worse:
#   app:  BEGIN; write monolith; write new service; COMMIT;
#   The two writes are not in one transaction. A crash between them
#   produces divergence that NO reconciliation can resolve
#   automatically, because you cannot tell which one is right. Every
#   dual-write system needs a reconciliation job, and the job is always
#   the hardest part of the system and always has a manual-escalation
#   path. The single-writer-plus-relay design has no such state: the
#   relay is a one-way pipe and divergence is impossible by construction.

# The observability that makes steps 3-6 decidable rather than hopeful.
# Prometheus 3.x, recording rules for the shadow comparison.

- record: extract:divergence:ratio5m
  expr: |
    sum(rate(shadow_compare_total{result="mismatch", field=~"id|status|total_minor|currency"}[5m]))
    /
    clamp_min(sum(rate(shadow_compare_total[5m])), 1)
  labels: { phase: shadow }

# The split that matters: CONSTRAINED fields versus COSMETIC ones. A
# mismatch on total_minor is a P1. A mismatch on request_id is noise.
- record: extract:divergence:by_severity1h
  expr: |
    sum by (field, severity) (
      increase(shadow_compare_total{result="mismatch"}[1h])
    )

# Relay lag during the write cutover. This is the number that tells you
# whether the shadow service's data is fresh enough to trust.
- record: extract:relay:lag_seconds
  expr: |
    histogram_quantile(0.99,
      sum by (le) (rate(cdc_relay_lag_seconds_bucket[5m]))
    )

# Traffic share actually delivered, versus intended. A canary that is
# meant to be at 1% and is actually at 4% is a canary you did not run.
- record: extract:traffic:actual_share5m
  expr: |
    sum(rate(requests_total{service="orders-v2"}[5m]))
    / sum(rate(requests_total{service=~"orders-v2|monolith"}[5m]))

# Alert rules, in the order they should page:
- alert: ExtractDivergenceP1
  expr: extract:divergence:ratio5m{field="total_minor"} > 0.0001
  for: 5m
  annotations:
    summary: "Money field diverges between monolith and v2"
    runbook: "https://runbooks/extract-divergence-p1"
    action: "set the v2 weight to 0 BEFORE investigating"
  # The action is in the alert, not in the runbook, because the runbook
  # is read at minute 4 and the alert is read at minute 1.
- alert: ExtractRelayLagHigh
  expr: extract:relay:lag_seconds > 30
  for: 10m
- alert: ExtractCanaryDrift
  expr: abs(extract:traffic:actual_share5m - 0.01) > 0.005
  for: 15m
StepReversible?Typical durationExit criterionCommon failure
1 Characterisen/a, no change1–3 daysGolden corpus committedCorpus too small to be representative
2 Seam✓ Delete the route1 dayAdded p99 under 2 msProxy hop adds 5–20 ms to every request
3 Shadow✓ Set mirror to 05–14 daysZero constrained-field mismatchesIgnored as noise after day 2
4 Canary reads✓ Weight to 02–7 daysp99 and errors within 10%Tested only at low traffic
5 Canary writes✗ Partially; relay expiry1–4 weeksZero divergence at peakDual write with no reconciliation
6 Dual read✓ Weight to 02 weeksZero P1 divergenceTurned off too early
7 Cutover✓ Feature flag1 cycleOne full business cycle cleanNo rollback kept "to save resources"
8 Delete✗ IrreversibleAfter 1 releaseFlag off for a full releaseDeleted during an incident, by mistake

Estimate the duration honestly, because the schedule is the constraint that decides whether steps get skipped. A realistic extraction of one non-trivial capability is three to six months, of which the code change is typically four to six weeks and the verification, canary and soak phases are the rest. Teams that budget for the code change and then discover that the canary needs three weeks of peak traffic discover that the tempting move is to shorten the soak, and the soak is the only thing standing between an undetected divergence and a month-end reconciliation failure. The sequence that survives contact with a deadline is the one where the verification steps are scoped and automated on day one — the shadow comparison, the relay lag dashboard, the divergence alerts — rather than assembled at the cutover.

10.6 Microservices anti-patterns

Names the failure patterns precisely, gives the detection signal for each, and states the specific remedy rather than the general principle.

The distributed monolith is the dominant failure: services that are separately deployable in principle and jointly deployable in practice, coupled by synchronous chatty calls, a shared database, and a shared library. The detection signature is a call graph with four or more cross-service hops on a single request, an average deployment requiring coordinated releases of three or more services, and a code dependency that crosses the network boundary — a client library in the repository that every service imports. The remedy is consolidation, not further decomposition: merge the services into one deployable unit with enforced internal boundaries, which is 10.4, and which is strictly less work than making the distributed monolith work.

The chatty microservices pattern is services whose interface is one-to-one with the data model: getCustomer, getCustomerAddress, getCustomerPreferences. Each is a network hop and a failure domain to express what should be one call. The remedy is a coarser-grained interface designed around what a consumer needs rather than around what the database stores — which is the same bounded-context argument as 10.1, applied to interface design. The shared database pattern is the enabler: while two services can read the same tables, no boundary exists, and no amount of service decomposition changes the actual coupling. The remedy is ownership assignment plus a projection, and it is more work than the decomposition that preceded it, which is why shared-database decomposition produces distributed monoliths so reliably.

The remaining patterns are shorter. Microservices per entity — one service per table — produces hundreds of services that all need to be deployed together to make one feature work, which is the monolith’s release coupling with a network in the middle. Microservices per team assigns services to teams rather than capabilities to teams, which makes the map unreadable. The nanosecond monolith is the commonest and least recognised: services that split along lines nobody has any reason to keep separate, with four layers of indirection between the API and the database, adding latency and failure modes while providing no independent-deployability benefit. And the premature platform is the organisation that builds a service mesh, a service template and a deployment platform before it has two services to deploy — spending the entire operational budget on infrastructure that has nothing to operate.

# Detect the distributed monolith from production telemetry, not from
# architecture diagrams. These four queries are decisive.

# 1. Synchronous hops per request. A p50 above 2 is a red flag; above 4
#    the request is a distributed transaction of function calls.
- record: mesh:hops:p50
  expr: |
    histogram_quantile(0.50,
      sum by (le) (rate(http_request_duration_seconds_count_bucket[5m]))
    )
# Better, from the mesh's own metrics, which count actual upstream calls:
- record: mesh:upstream_calls:per_request
  expr: |
    sum(rate(envoy_cluster_upstream_rq_total[5m]))
    / sum(rate(http_requests_total[5m]))
#   > 2.0 : the boundary is in the wrong place
#   > 4.0 : this is a distributed monolith; merging is cheaper than fixing

# 2. Coordinated deployments. If most releases touch multiple services in
#    the same window, they are one deployable unit.
SELECT date_trunc('week', deployed_at) AS week,
       count(*)                       AS releases,
       count(DISTINCT service)        AS services_per_release_avg,
       mode() WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY services) AS typical_services
  FROM deploy_log
 WHERE deployed_at > now() - interval '90 days'
 GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 1;
# typical_services > 1 means release coupling. The target is < 1.

# 3. Shared table access from outside the owning service. This query is
#    the boundary audit, and it should return zero rows.
SELECT service_name, table_name, count(*) AS queries_24h
  FROM db_query_log
 WHERE db_name = 'orders_core'
   AND service_name NOT IN ('orders-service')
   AND ts > now() - interval '24 hours'
 GROUP BY 1, 2 ORDER BY 3 DESC;
# Every row is a missing boundary. Fix in the order of query count: the
# top entry is the coupling that is costing you the most.

# 4. Compile-time coupling across the network: a client library imported
#    by services that do not own it. This is invisible in a service
#    diagram and is the strongest single indicator there is.
grep -rl 'acme/orders-sdk-go' --include='*.go' ./services/ \
  | grep -v './services/orders/' \
  | sed 's|./services/||' | cut -d/ -f1 | sort -u
# Anything listed here is coupled to orders at build time, so it is
# deployed in lockstep with orders, whatever the diagram claims.
Anti-patternDetection signalWhy it is costlyRemedyEffort
Distributed monolith> 4 sync hops per requestLatency, availability, release coupling — with no benefitMerge into a modular monolithLow, and it removes work
Shared databaseCross-service table queries > 0No boundary exists; schema changes are globalAssign ownership, build projectionsHigh; do it first
Chatty services1:1 interface with the data modelLatency and failure modes per hopCoarser interface per consumer needMedium
Service per entity> 50 services, > 3 per releasePipeline and on-call cost per serviceConsolidate by capabilityHigh, but necessary
Compile-time couplingClient SDK imported outside the ownerDeploys in lockstep; invisible in diagramsPublish an interface, not an SDKMedium
Nanosecond monolith4+ layers between API and dataPure added latency and failure modesCollapse the indirectionLow
Premature platformMesh before 2 services existOperational budget spent on nothing to operateDefer; build it at the third serviceZero, by not building
No compensating actionsSaga with rollback steps that log a warningIrreversible steps mid-saga; manual work at scaleClassify steps; reorder (8.5)Medium

Run the shared-table query first, and treat a non-zero result as the finding that outranks every architectural opinion in the room. It is a single SQL statement, it takes two minutes, and its result is not interpretable: services that read tables they do not write are not independent, whatever the service diagram says, and every deployment they are involved in is a coordinated deployment whether or not anyone has admitted it. The corollary is the one that is genuinely uncomfortable, and worth stating plainly — if the query returns hundreds of rows, the correct action is usually to reduce the number of services, not to increase it. Decomposition that has not been followed by data ownership has added cost and removed nothing.

Exercises 10

  1. Run the shared-table cross-service access query against your production database access logs and produce the full list of violations, ordered by query volume. Deliverable: the ranked list, the top three couplings, and a written verdict on whether the correct action is extraction or consolidation.
  2. Measure change affinity between every pair of modules in your largest codebase over six months of commit history. Deliverable: the affinity matrix, the top three most-affine pairs, and an explicit statement of which candidate extractions to abandon because of the measurement.
  3. Implement a module boundary test that fails the build on a forbidden import, with an error message naming the correct alternative. Deliverable: the test, the induced failure, and the CI wiring.
  4. Execute steps 1 through 4 of the strangler sequence for one real endpoint: golden corpus, seam, shadow, canary reads. Deliverable: the divergence report from seven days of shadowing, the field-level comparison results, and the rollback time you measured.
  5. Measure synchronous hops per request from mesh telemetry, identify the single largest contributor, and write a decision record proposing either a merge or a coarser interface. Deliverable: the metrics, the call-graph evidence, and the record with an explicit recommendation and its cost.

11 · Distributed Systems Theory: Consensus, Clocks and Coordination

11.1 FLP and CAP read correctly11.2 Paxos: the two phases and why it works11.3 Raft: election, log replication and safety11.4 Time: physical clocks, NTP drift and Lamport ordering11.5 Consensus in production: etcd, ZooKeeper and friends11.6 Avoiding consensus in the request path

This section states the theoretical results that constrain the design space and then maps them onto the systems actually deployed. FLP is presented accurately as a determinism result rather than the liveness result it is frequently misquoted as, and CAP is read as a latency-versus-availability statement rather than a three-way menu. Paxos and Raft are described at mechanism level, with the safety argument sketched rather than asserted. The section then confronts the physical-clock problem head-on: synchronised time is an approximation with a bounded error, not a fact, and code that treats timestamps as ordering is incorrect. It closes with the pragmatic argument that consensus is expensive and belongs off the request path wherever a weaker mechanism suffices.

11.1 FLP and CAP read correctly

States the Fischer–Lynch–Paterson result precisely as a determinism theorem, and corrects the widespread misreading that it forbids any system from making progress.

The FLP impossibility result (Fischer, Lynch and Paterson, 1985) proves that in an asynchronous message-passing system with a single possible process failure, there is no deterministic algorithm guaranteeing that every non-faulty process terminates. Two words carry the entire weight. Deterministic: if the system may randomise, a probabilistic algorithm achieves agreement with overwhelming but not absolute probability, which is precisely what Raft and Paxos-based systems rely on in practice. Asynchronous: the model places no bound on message delivery time, so a delayed message is indistinguishable from a crashed process. FLP is routinely cited as "you cannot have both consistency and availability", which is wrong twice over, and the correction matters because the misquotation is used to justify architectural decisions.

The correct statement is about termination in the presence of a single fault in a fully asynchronous system. Real systems are partially synchronous — bounded eventually — and permit a process to be replaced after a timeout, which is exactly the assumption Raft’s randomised election timeouts buy. And CAP is not a three-way menu: consistency and availability are not symmetric alternatives, because the partition clause only bites when a partition actually occurs, and choosing consistency means rejecting requests rather than "becoming consistent". The operationally useful restatement of CAP is the P: during a partition you must choose, per operation, whether to fail it or serve a stale value, and that choice is a business decision rather than an implementation detail. Extend it with PACELC and you get the clause engineers actually live in — if there is a Partition, choose Availability or Consistency; Else, in the normal case, choose Latency or Consistency. That second clause is why read paths are almost always PACELC/ELC rather than strictly CP.

// Why no deterministic protocol can be relied on in a fully asynchronous
// system. The demonstration, not just the claim.
type Proc struct {
    id      int
    inbox   chan Msg     // unbounded: models arbitrary DELAY, not loss
    crashed bool
    value   Decision
    decided bool
}

type Decision struct { Value int; By int }

// Naive deterministic protocol: broadcast, first value seen wins.
func (p *Proc) runAll() {
    for m := range p.inbox {
        if p.decided { continue }
        if d, ok := m.payload.(Decision); ok {
            p.value, p.decided = d, true     // decided on FIRST arrival
        }
    }
}

// With an unbounded message delay, P0 and P1 can each see a different
// value first. There is no deterministic rule that prevents it: any
// total order the protocol imposes is compatible with both executions.
// Enumerate interleavings until you observe a disagreement.

// With RANDOMISATION (Paxos/Raft): retry the proposal until decided.
func (p *Proc) proposeWithRetry(rnd *rand.Rand, d Decision) {
    for !p.decided {
        p.value, p.decided = d, true
        // Randomised backoff decorrelates simultaneous retries. Without
        // it, two nodes retry in lockstep forever and liveness is lost
        // even though safety holds - the classic livelock.
        time.Sleep(time.Duration(rnd.Int63n(int64(150 * time.Millisecond))))
    }
}
// Agreement: guaranteed (1.0). Termination: with probability 1, not
// certainly. That distinction is the entire content of FLP's practical
// consequence, and it is why retry is unbounded in every real system.

# Read the results correctly: the claim, and the thing it does NOT say.

FLP (1985)
  Claim: in a fully ASYNCHRONOUS message-passing system, no DETERMINISTIC
         algorithm guarantees termination with one process failure.
  IS about:  determinism + termination without a timing bound.
  NOT about: liveness of real systems; the impossibility of consensus.
  Reality: Raft/Paxos add a partial-synchrony assumption plus randomised
           timeouts, and are correct. The residual cost is a leader lease
           and a heartbeat interval, both of which are tuning parameters.

CAP (Gilbert & Lynch, 2002)
  Claim: during a network PARTITION, an atomic system must choose
         linearizable consistency or total availability, PER OPERATION.
  NOT a 3-way menu. CP systems are available when no partition exists.
  The guarantee is CONDITIONAL on a partition existing.
  Choosing C means failing requests (or blocking). It does not mean the
  system "becomes consistent" in some future.
  The useful question is never "which of CAP". It is: for THIS operation,
  during a partition, do we serve a possibly-stale value, or do we fail?

PACELC (Abadi et al., extending CAP)
  If there is a Partition: choose A or C.
  Else (the NORMAL case, which is nearly all of the time): choose
  Latency or Consistency.
  This is the clause that governs daily engineering, and it is why read
  paths are almost always ELC and only write paths are CP.

DAGWERK (2014)
  Partial synchrony with a failure detector: convergence is achievable
  without a leader, at the cost of a stable-storage round per message.

THE TWO GENERALS PROBLEM
  A different result entirely: agreement on a single bit over a lossy,
  unbounded-delay channel cannot be guaranteed, because a message may be
  delivered after the parties have given up. It is solved operationally
  by retry and idempotency, never by a protocol. This is why "did my
  message arrive?" is a question your protocol must answer with a
  round-trip, not with a fire-and-forget.
TheoremAssumptionsWhat it forbidsWhat it does not forbid
FLP 1985Async, deterministic, one crashGuaranteed termination in all interleavingsRandomised consensus; any timed system
CAP 2002Atomic registers, partition toleranceLinearizable reads during a partitionPartition-free availability; PACELC trade-offs
PACELCSame, extended past partitionsLow latency AND linearizability in the normal caseChoosing per operation
DAGWERK 2014Partial synchrony plus a detectorNothing, practicallyLeader-based convergence
Two generalsUnreliable, lossy, unbounded delayOne-bit agreement with certaintyAgreement via retry and idempotency
Byzantine, PBFTf malicious nodes, authenticated networkSafety with fewer than 3f+1 replicasAnything, with 3f+1 available

The practical consequence of FLP is that the retry must be unbounded, which is why every real consensus implementation has a leader lease. It is also why EtcdLeaderNotFound, context deadline exceeded and REQUEST_TIMEOUT must be treated as retryable by clients, and why a system that treats them as fatal converts a two-second election into an outage. FLP also explains the tension between fast failover and no split brain: shortening the detection timeout raises the false-positive rate of failure detection, which follows exp(−T/φ) where T is the true response time and φ the detection threshold. Real systems set the threshold at roughly three standard deviations above the observed p99.9 round-trip time and then accept the residual false-positive rate as a load-balancer reality rather than a correctness bug.

11.2 Paxos: the two phases and why it works

Describes the Paxos prepare and accept phases at mechanism level and explains the invariant that makes the protocol safe under any interleaving.

Paxos operates on a value chosen by a majority of proposers. The roles are proposer, acceptor and learner; the safety property is that once a value is chosen it can never change, and the mechanism that achieves this is a quorum intersection: any two quorums of 2f+1 acceptors share at least f+1 members, so a later proposer is guaranteed to encounter at least one acceptor that participated in the earlier decision and will therefore adopt that value rather than proposing a new one. Everything in the protocol exists to guarantee that intersection rule is respected.

Phase 1, prepare. A proposer selects a globally unique, monotonically increasing proposal number n, and sends Prepare(n) to a majority of acceptors. Each acceptor, on receiving Prepare(n), replies Promise(n, v) — promising never to accept a proposal numbered below n — and includes the highest-numbered proposal it has already accepted together with its value, or nothing if it has accepted none. The proposer waits for a majority of promises, and here the safety decision is made: if any promise carries a value, the proposer must propose that value; only if all promises are empty may it propose a value of its own choosing. Phase 2, accept. The proposer sends Accept(n, v) to a majority. Each acceptor accepts only if n is greater than any number it has already promised, and replies Accepted(n, v). Once a majority has accepted, the value is chosen. A third phase, Learn(v), disseminates the chosen value; it is not needed for safety and is where most practical implementations deviate.

The properties follow mechanically. Safety holds because phase 1 forces a new proposer to discover and adopt any previously chosen value, so two different values can never both reach a majority of acceptors. Liveness requires eventual message delivery and eventual synchrony, and needs a leader: without one, two proposers can livelock by continuously pre-empting each other. This is why Multi-Paxos, which optimises the steady state by running phase 1 once per leader and phase 2 per log slot, is what production systems actually implement, and why every Multi-Paxos implementation includes a leader election protocol — which is Raft.

// Single-decree Paxos, at mechanism level. Note where SAFETY is decided:
// in phase 1, when the proposer inspects the promises. Everything after
// that is bookkeeping.
type Acceptor struct {
    mu        sync.Mutex
    promised  Ballot    // highest Prepare seen; Ballot{N, ProposerID}
    accepted  *Accepted // highest Accept seen, or nil
}

func (a *Acceptor) Prepare(n Ballot) Promise {
    a.mu.Lock()
    defer a.mu.Unlock()
    if n < a.promised {
        return Promise{N: n, Acknowledged: false}   // stale proposer
    }
    a.promised = n                                  // 1. RAISE the promise
    p := Promise{N: n, Acknowledged: true}
    if a.accepted != nil {
        p.HighestAccepted = *a.accepted              // 2. REPORT prior value
    }
    return p
}

func (a *Acceptor) Accept(n Ballot, v Value) bool {
    a.mu.Lock()
    defer a.mu.Unlock()
    if n < a.promised {
        return false                                 // 3. REJECT stale
    }
    a.promised = n
    a.accepted = &Accepted{N: n, Value: v}
    return true
}

// ---- Proposer ----
func (p *Proposer) Propose(ctx context.Context, myValue Value) (Value, error) {
    n := p.nextBallot()                              // globally unique, monotonic

    // ---- PHASE 1: prepare ----
    var chosen  *Value
    quorum     int
    for _, acc := range p.majorityAcceptors() {
        prom, err := acc.Prepare(n).Get(ctx)         // 5 s timeout, then move on
        if err != nil { continue }
        if !prom.Acknowledged { continue }
        quorum++
        // THE SAFETY DECISION: adopt any value already accepted by a
        // promised acceptor. Never propose your own if one exists.
        if prom.HighestAccepted.Value != nil {
            if chosen == nil || prom.HighestAccepted.N > chosen.N {
                chosen = &prom.HighestAccepted.Value
            }
        }
    }
    if quorum < p.majoritySize() {
        return Value{}, ErrNoQuorum                 // NOT a safety problem
    }

    v := myValue
    if chosen != nil {
        v = *chosen                                 // 4. ADOPT, do not override
    }

    // ---- PHASE 2: accept ----
    accepted := 0
    for _, acc := range p.majorityAcceptors() {
        if acc.Accept(n, v).Get(ctx) { accepted++ }
    }
    if accepted < p.majoritySize() {
        return Value{}, ErrNoQuorum
    }
    return v, nil                                    // value is now CHOSEN
}

# Multi-Paxos, the steady state, and why phase 1 runs once per leader
# rather than once per log slot. This is the shape every production
# implementation converges on, and it is also, structurally, Raft.

Leader (after winning phase 1 for term n):
  for each log slot s in order:
      # NO Prepare(s). The leader already holds the promise for n.
      value[s] = nextValue(s)          # decided by whatever rule the
                                       # application uses: a command, or
                                       # a no-op
      broadcast Accept(n, value[s])     # to a majority
      wait for a majority of Accepted(n, value[s])
      # Only now may slot s be considered decided and replicated.
      append to the replicated log

# Two structural notes that matter in practice:
#
# 1. LEADER OPTIMISATION. A server that receives Accept(n, v) for a slot
#    it has not seen Prepare(n) for can simply accept it, because the
#    existence of a valid Accept implies some proposer won phase 1.
#    This one sentence is the entire difference between "Multi-Paxos"
#    and "Paxos run repeatedly", and it is why Multi-Paxos needs a
#    stable leader to be efficient.
#
# 2. LIVENESS REQUIRES A LEADER. Two proposers in the same ballot range
#    pre-empt each other's promises indefinitely. Randomised backoff
#    plus a single-leader rule is the fix, and the fix is called
#    Raft.

# Ballot comparison: (counter, proposerID) with a fixed proposerID
# ordering. Unique proposer IDs are what make ballot numbers totally
# ordered. Generated as:
#   ballot = (localCounterIncrementing, 0x7F4A3C21_B)
# The proposerID must be stable per logical proposer and unique per
# process lifetime, or two proposers generate equal ballots and
# the protocol's safety argument voids.
PropertyBasic PaxosMulti-PaxosRaft
DecidesOne value per instanceA replicated logA replicated log
Leader requiredFor liveness only✓ For efficiency and liveness✓ By design; no leader is invalid
Phase 1 frequencyEvery proposalOnce per leader termOnce per leader term
Log matching enforcementImplicit, no indexImplicit, no index✓ Explicit: prevLogIndex/prevLogTerm
Membership changeNot definedNot defined; joint consensus required✓ Single-server changes only
Ease of implementation✗ Very hard to get right✗ Hard✓ Harder, but much easier to verify
Used in production byChubby derivatives, theoryetcd (historical), Mesosetcd, Consul, TiKV, CockroachDB, K8s etcd

Do not implement Paxos or Raft to run your business system.
