The failure modes are subtle, the safety argument is a proof rather than a test, and the cost of getting it almost right is a data-corruption bug that appears once every few months under a specific timing window. Use etcd, Consul, or a database’s own replication layer. If you believe you need your own consensus, the question to answer first is why none of the existing implementations fit — and the honest answer is usually that you want a simpler mechanism, which is the subject of 11.6.

11.3 Raft: election, log replication and safety

Walks through Raft’s three sub-problems, specifies the election pseudocode, and states the invariants that make a Raft log safe.

Raft decomposes consensus into three sub-problems and solves each with a mechanism that is easier to verify than Paxos’s. Leader election: every server begins as a follower with a randomised election timeout between 150 and 300 ms; a follower that hears nothing from a leader for its timeout becomes a candidate, increments its term, votes for itself, and requests votes. A candidate wins on a majority of the cluster. Log replication: the leader appends a client entry to its own log, then sends it to every follower with the index and term of the preceding entry; a follower accepts only if its own log at prevLogIndex has a matching prevLogTerm, and on mismatch it decrements the index and retries, which is the log-matching property that makes the log eventually consistent. Safety: a server never votes twice in one term, and terms are monotonically increasing, so at most one leader exists per term.

The randomised election timeout is the load-bearing detail. A fixed timeout causes split votes — several candidates each gathering 2 of 5 votes, none reaching 3 — and the cluster livelocks until a retry happens to align. Randomisation decorrelates the candidates, and the split vote counter in etcd is the metric that tells you the election range is too narrow. The other two details that repeatedly cause production incidents: a leader steps down when it cannot reach a quorum, and refuses to serve reads and writes, because a partitioned leader that continues serving is a split brain; and followers reject entries whose term is older than their own, so a returning partitioned node’s stale log is overwritten rather than merged.

// Raft follower election, at mechanism level. (Go 1.25, for reading;
// DO NOT use this in production. Use etcd.)
const (
    electionTimeoutMin = 150 * time.Millisecond
    electionTimeoutMax = 300 * time.Millisecond  // the randomisation range
    heartbeatInterval  = 50 * time.Millisecond   // ~1/3 of the MIN timeout
)

func (s *Server) run() {
    for {
        switch s.state {
        case Follower, Candidate:
            // Randomised: this is the anti-livelock mechanism. A fixed
            // timeout makes split votes a certainty under load.
            s.resetTimer(electionTimeoutMin, electionTimeoutMax)

            select {
            case <-s.heartbeatCh:                 // leader is alive
                s.becomeFollower(s.currentTerm, s.leaderID)
            case <-s.electionTimer.C:
                s.startElection()
            case msg := <-s.rpcCh:
                s.stepFollower(msg)
            }

        case Leader:
            s.appendNoopEntry()                    // see the note below
            ticker := time.NewTicker(heartbeatInterval)
        leaderLoop:
            for {
                select {
                case <-ticker.C:
                    if !s.heartbeat() {
                        s.becomeFollower(s.currentTerm, LeaderIDNone)
                        break leaderLoop
                    }
                case req := <-s.clientCh:
                    s.handleClient(req)
                case msg := <-s.rpcCh:
                    s.stepLeader(msg)
                }
            }
        }
    }
}

func (s *Server) startElection() {
    s.state = Candidate
    s.currentTerm++                 // monotonic: a term is a logical epoch
    s.votedFor = s.id               // vote for self: at most one per term

    lastIdx, lastTerm := s.log.lastIndexAndTerm()
    args := RequestVoteArgs{
        Term:         s.currentTerm,
        CandidateID:  s.id,
        LastLogIndex: lastIdx,
        LastLogTerm:  lastTerm,     // the "up-to-date" check
    }
    votes := 1
    for _, peer := range s.peers {
        go func(p Peer) {
            rep, err := p.RequestVote(args, 3*time.Second)
            if err != nil { return }
            if rep.Term > s.currentTerm {   // saw a newer term: step down
                s.becomeFollower(rep.Term, LeaderIDNone); return
            }
            if rep.VoteGranted && s.voteTally.Add(1) > s.majority() {
                s.becomeLeader()               // won; no re-check needed
            }
        }(peer)
    }
    s.voteTally.Store(votes)
}

func (s *Server) canVote(req RequestVoteArgs) (bool, int) {
    if req.Term < s.currentTerm            { return false, s.currentTerm }
    if req.Term > s.currentTerm            { s.currentTerm = req.Term; s.votedFor = None }
    if s.votedFor != None && s.votedFor != req.CandidateID {
        return false, s.currentTerm                     // already voted this term
    }
    // Up-to-date check: a candidate whose log is behind cannot win, or
    // it would overwrite committed entries. Compare term first, then index.
    myIdx, myTerm := s.log.lastIndexAndTerm()
    if req.LastLogTerm < myTerm { return false, s.currentTerm }
    if req.LastLogTerm == myTerm && req.LastLogIndex < myIdx {
        return false, s.currentTerm
    }
    s.votedFor = req.CandidateID
    s.persist()                                 // MUST be durable before replying
    return true, s.currentTerm
}

# The four Raft invariants, stated as assertions. These are the properties
# a Raft implementation is actually required to have; everything else is
# mechanism.

# 1. ELECTION SAFETY: at most one leader per term.
#    Enforced by: votedFor persisted before replying, one vote per term.
#    Violation symptom: two leaders in one term; both accept writes.
#    This is the invariant that makes split brain impossible.

# 2. LOG MATCHING: if two logs contain an entry with the same index and
#    term, the logs are identical in all entries up to and including it.
#    Enforced by: the follower rejects AppendEntries when
#      log[prevLogIndex].term != prevLogTerm
#    and decrements prevLogIndex until it matches. The consequence is
#    that a log is a prefix-consistent set of blocks, so a log index is a
#    globally meaningful position.

# 3. LEADER COMPLETENESS: a committed entry is present in the log of
#    every leader.
#    Enforced by: the up-to-date check in RequestVote.
#    Proof sketch: a leader was elected by a majority, so any later
#    election's majority intersects it, and the intersection holds a
#    committed entry, so the new leader's log contains it.

# 4. STATE MACHINE SAFETY: no two clients see the same index applied
#    with different values.
#    Enforced by: log matching plus leader completeness. Committed is
#    final; apply in index order, exactly once, to a deterministic state
#    machine.

# ---- Operational consequence: the no-op entry ----
# A new leader APPENDS AN EMPTY ENTRY to its log on election. Reason:
# without it, a leader that has not yet replicated any of its own entries
# may not know the true commitIndex of the old term, because
# commitIndex = index of the last entry replicated to a majority, and
# entries from previous terms are not committed merely by being stored on
# a majority. The no-op entry is committed by the new leader's own
# replication, which advances commitIndex and retires those entries.
# Skipping it causes committed entries to be lost after a leader change.

# Where to look when diagnosing a Raft-based system:
kubectl -n kube-system logs etcd-0 | grep -E 'lost leader|elected|health check'
# etcd metrics: etcd_server_leader_changes_seen_total
#   A rising counter is normal at low rate and pathological at high rate:
#   it is the direct measure of network health or of too tight a
#   heartbeat interval relative to the round-trip time.

# etcd operational inspection. These are the commands to have in the
# runbook, because the failure modes are not obvious from the outside.

# 1. Cluster health and member list, including the raft term.
ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --endpoints=https://etcd-0:2379 \
  endpoint status --write-out=table
#   IS LEADER / IS LEARNER / raft TERM / RAFT INDEX / RAFT APPLIED INDEX
# A gap between RAFT INDEX and RAFT APPLIED INDEX that grows is a stalled
# apply loop: writes are accepted but not applied, which reads as a
# phantom-success bug to every client.
ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --endpoints=https://etcd-0:2379 \
  member list -w table

# 2. Leader changes in the last hour. The single most useful health
#    number for a consensus-backed dependency.
curl -s localhost:2381/metrics | grep etcd_server_leader_changes_seen_total
curl -s localhost:2381/metrics \
  | grep -E 'etcd_server_proposals_(pending|failed)_total'
#   proposals_pending climbing steadily = a stuck or slow apply path.
#   proposals_failed rising = quorum is not available, or fsync is slow.

# 3. The database is small but the latency distribution is everything.
curl -s localhost:2381/metrics | grep etcd_disk_backend_commit_duration_seconds \
  | awk '{print $1, $NF}' | sort -k2 -rn | head
#   etcd is a strong-CONSISTENT store. Its commit latency is the fsync
#   latency. On a network block device with a volatile write cache, this
#   is 0.1 ms; on a busy shared SAN it is 20 ms, and every write in the
#   cluster pays it. This number explains more incidents than any other.

# 4. Slow requests: the exact requests over 1 s in the last 10 min.
ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --endpoints=https://etcd-0:2379 \
  endpoint status --write-out=json \
  | jq -r '.[].Status.header.revision'
curl -s localhost:2381/debug/metrics?mode=basic | head -30

# 5. Corruption: raft log gaps mean an invariant was violated somewhere.
ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --endpoints=https://etcd-0:2379 \
  alarm list
#   NOSPACE, CORRUPT, and a raft index gap are all page-inducing. The
#   runbook must say exactly what to do, and it must have been rehearsed.
FailureDetectionBehaviour in RaftOperator action
Leader partitionetcd_server_leader_changes_seen_total risesLeader steps down, writes rejectedNone; expect a new leader in 1 election timeout
Follower partitionRAFT INDEX falls behindCaught up by log replication on healNone; check for a network policy
Leader crashesNo heartbeatsNew leader after 150–300 msNone; verify restart policy
Quorum lostproposals_failed_total rises✓ Refuses writes; never splitsRestore a majority; writes are unavailable by design
Slow fsyncbackend_commit_duration p99Commits slow; latency rises for allFix storage; do not raise timeouts
Clock skewNTP offset alertsIgnored: Raft uses terms, not timeFix NTP; no correctness impact
Disk fullNOSPACE alarmAlarm; refuses writes until compact+defragCompact, defrag, alarm disarm

Clock skew must never influence a Raft decision, and in most implementations it must not influence any consensus decision at all. Raft’s notion of ordering is the term counter, not the timestamp; two servers with clocks an hour apart behave identically to two with synchronised clocks. Systems that layer expiry on top — session TTLs, lease durations, heartbeat timeouts — reintroduce time, and each such parameter is a place where a clock jump becomes a correctness bug. A common and serious configuration error is setting the election timeout below the observed p99.9 round-trip time, which converts a brief network blip into a leader change; the correct relationship is election_timeout > 3 × p99.9_RTT measured on the actual network path, not on loopback.

11.4 Time: physical clocks, NTP drift and Lamport ordering

Establishes that synchronised time is a bounded approximation rather than a fact, and specifies Lamport timestamps and vector clocks as the correct tools for ordering and causality.

Physical time cannot be synchronised perfectly, and the reason is not engineering slowness. Any two clocks run at slightly different rates because of manufacturing tolerance, temperature and voltage, and the cheapest sources — the crystal oscillators in servers and phones — drift by tens of parts per million. A clock accurate to 50 ppm accumulates one second of error per roughly 5.8 hours. Network delay only makes synchronisation worse, and Cristian’s algorithm bounds the achievable accuracy at half the round-trip time: if a round trip takes 2 ms across a WAN, the best possible estimate is accurate to about 1 ms, and that is the floor, not the aspiration. Every practical system therefore has a bounded clock error, and code that behaves differently at different clock offsets is incorrect.

The consequences follow directly. Timestamp ordering is not causality. If machine A’s clock is 200 ms fast, an event stamped on A appears to happen after an event on B that actually followed it. Any logic that sorts by timestamp to reconstruct order — "last write wins" using updated_at, an audit log ordered by time, a cache that invalidates on the newest timestamp — is broken under skew. Elapsed-time measurement must use a monotonic clock. Wall-clock time can jump backwards when NTP steps the clock, which turns a timeout into an infinite wait and a rate limiter into a burst allowance. Duration and rate computations must never use wall time. And timeouts must be derived from a measured percentile, not from a round number: a 100 ms timeout against a 2 ms p50 and a 40 ms p99.9 is a timeout that fires 0.1% of the time and manufactures a false failure exactly when the system is already loaded.

// The distinction that must be respected everywhere: WALL clock for
// human-facing timestamps, MONOTONIC clock for every interval.
// (Go 1.25.)
func measureBoth() {
    // WRONG for any duration, rate, or timeout:
    //   wall gives absolute time, but NTP can step it BACKWARDS, so a
    //   subtraction can be negative and a timeout becomes an infinite wait.
    t0 := time.Now()
    doWork()
    elapsedWall := time.Since(t0)   // may be negative or wildly wrong

    // CORRECT: monotonic reading; immune to clock steps and NTP slew.
    t1 := time.Now()
    elapsedMono := t1.Sub(t0)
    metrics.WorkDuration.Observe(elapsedMono.Seconds())

    // CORRECT for a persisted timestamp, and only that:
    //   note the explicit UTC and the "Z". A stored local time is a bug
    //   waiting for the first DST transition or the first team in
    //   another region.
    record := TimestampedAt{At: t1.UTC(), Wall: t1.UTC().Format(time.RFC3339Nano)}
    _ = elapsedWall
    _ = record
}

// Never do this. Two servers with a 200 ms clock offset produce this
// order, which is not the order the events happened in:
func lastWriteWinsBad(a, b Record) Record {
    if a.UpdatedAt.After(b.UpdatedAt) { return a }
    return b
}

// The correct version: a monotonic counter owned by the aggregate,
// advanced atomically. No clock involved, so no skew involved.
func lastWriteWinsGood(ctx context.Context, id uuid.UUID, patch Patch) (Version, error) {
    var v int64
    err := db.QueryRowContext(ctx, `
        UPDATE records
           SET data = $2, version = version + 1
         WHERE id = $1
        RETURNING version`, id, patch).Scan(&v)
    return v, err     // version is the only ordering that is trustworthy
}

# NTP discipline, measured. The configuration is easy; the measurement is
# what tells you whether it is working.

# chrony: prefer an internal NTP source, and record the actual offset.
# Multiple sources reduce the chance of a single-source failure.
pool ntp-1.corp.internal iburst maxsources 4
pool ntp-2.corp.internal iburst maxsources 4
server fallback.pool.ntp.org iburst

# These four parameters are the difference between "NTP is running" and
# "NTP is actually correct". Without them, chrony accepts large offsets
# that then cause a step, and a step moves the clock.
maxslewrate        1000.0     # parts per million: slew slowly, don't step
makestep           1.0 3      # step only if off by more than 1 s for 3 days
rtcsync            -          # kernel timestamps; 0 disables it
leapsectz          right      # handle leap seconds without stalling

# The four numbers to put in the runbook, from `chronyc tracking`:
#   Reference ID    : the actual source in use
#   Stratum         : distance to the reference (1 = local hardware clock)
#   Ref time (UTC)  : when the local clock was last corrected
#   System time     : the residual error. Alert above 50 ms, page above
#                     500 ms. On a well-disciplined fleet it is < 1 ms.

chronyc tracking
chronyc sources -v
chronyc sourcestats -v

# Measure the drift rate directly: this is the number that determines how
# quickly an un-disciplined clock accumulates error.
chronyc tracking | grep -i 'system time'
# 50 ppm => 1 s of error per 5.8 hours of un-disciplined operation.
# 500 ppm (a cheap crystal, a hot chassis) => 1 s per 33 minutes.

# The consequence to write into the architecture: any design whose
# correctness depends on clock agreement is wrong, because agreement is
# an operational property and correctness is a design property. If the
# system breaks when chrony fails, the design is the bug.

// Lamport timestamps: a logical clock that captures happens-before
// without any physical clock at all. Total order is consistent with
// causal order, which is exactly what distributed systems need.
type LamportClock struct {
    mu   sync.Mutex
    last int64
}

func (c *LamportClock) Tick() int64 {              // local event
    c.mu.Lock(); defer c.mu.Unlock()
    c.last++
    return c.last
}

func (c *LamportClock) Observe(remote int64) int64 { // received a message
    c.mu.Lock(); defer c.mu.Unlock()
    // The merge rule: local = max(local, remote) + 1. This guarantees
    // that if a happened-before b, then L(a) < L(b). The converse is
    // FALSE, and that is the crucial caveat.
    if remote > c.last { c.last = remote }
    c.last++
    return c.last
}

// Consequence you must design for: L(a) < L(b) does NOT mean a caused b.
// Two causally independent events can be ordered arbitrarily. So you
// cannot use Lamport time to DETECT concurrency; for that you need
// vector clocks. Lamport orders what is comparable and is silent about
// what is not.

// Practical use: total order for a log, deterministic replay, and
// "happened-before" reasoning in tests. It is the correct clock for
// debug output, since it reproduces identically on every replay.
func (o *Order) processEvent(ev Event, c *LamportClock) {
    ts := c.Tick()                                  // this local event
    if ev.CausedBy > 0 && ev.CausedBy >= ts {
        return                                     // causality violation
    }
    if o.LastLamport > ts {
        // A causally independent event arrived late. NOT an error, and
        // NOT something to reject: the correct handling is a merge or
        // an anti-entropy reconciliation pass, not a rejection.
        o.mergePending(ev)
    }
    o.LastLamport = ts
    o.events = append(o.events, ev)
}
MechanismWhat it ordersNeeds physical time?Detects concurrency?Cost
Wall clockNothing reliably under skew✗ Yes, and it is unreliable✗ NoFree, and wrong
Monotonic clockDurations only, local process✗ Never; immune to steps✗ NoFree, correct for intervals
Vector clockCausality, per entity✗ No✓ Yes, definitivelyVector grows with entity count
Lamport clockTotal order consistent with causality✗ No✗ No; comparable ≠ concurrentSingle integer
Hybrid logical clockTotal order plus wall-clock approximationPartly; bounded error✗ No64 bits
Database sequenceInsertion order at one database✗ No✗ No, and gaps existOne sequence
Raft term + log indexTotal order across the cluster✗ No✗ NoBuilt into the protocol

Hybrid logical clocks are the pragmatic answer for audit logs, and they are worth understanding before adopting one. an HLC stores a physical timestamp plus a logical counter and advances the counter whenever the physical clock would not move the value forward. It gives timestamps that are monotonically increasing within a process, approximately wall-clock comparable across processes, and independent of clock steps — the combination of properties that a distributed audit log needs and that neither a wall clock nor a Lamport clock provides alone. The caveat to state in the design: the cross-process comparability is approximate to the clock offset, so an HLC is not a source of truth about causal order; it is a source of truth about local order plus a good approximation of real time.

11.5 Consensus in production: etcd, ZooKeeper and friends

Compares the available consensus stores on their operational profiles and identifies the correct and incorrect places to put one.

Three systems dominate. etcd is a Raft implementation in Go, single-binary, with a gRPC or HTTP/JSON v3 API, a watch API that is the reason most people use it, and a strict limit of roughly 2 GB of data and 10,000 events per second in the documented design range — it is a configuration store, not a database. It is also the store Kubernetes uses for all cluster state, which means a cluster with many watch-heavy clients puts etcd under load that has nothing to do with the application’s data. ZooKeeper is the older ZAB protocol, Java, watch-based with one-shot semantics that make missed events a client problem, and it is in maintenance mode for new adoption. Consul adds service discovery and a DNS interface on top of Raft, with a session and locking model that is convenient and that several groups have learned to regret at scale.

The correct uses are narrow and specific: leader election, distributed locks with fencing tokens, configuration and feature flags, cluster membership, and coordination for a scheduler. A lock is the sharpest trap. A distributed lock held via a lease is only as safe as the lease duration, and a client that pauses — a long GC, a descheduled container, a network partition — can hold the lock past its expiry and resume work believing it is still protected. The only correct lock is one whose holder re-validates ownership before every side effect, and even that is only sound if the resource being protected supports a fencing token, which a database row can and an external HTTP API cannot. The deeper point is that locks are usually the wrong abstraction: most uses are more directly solved by a unique constraint, a compare-and-swap, or a queue that already provides the mutual exclusion.

# etcd: election, the canonical correct use. The lease is the safety
# mechanism, and its duration is a real design parameter.
ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --endpoints=https://etcd-0:2379 \
  auth disable

# Acquire an election with a 15 s lease. A leader that cannot renew within
# the lease loses it automatically; the failover cost is one lease period.
ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --endpoints=https://etcd-0:2379 \
  lease grant 15 --keepalive-without-refresh
# lease 8f3a granted with TTL(15s)

ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --endpoints=https://etcd-0:2379 \
  election elect node-1 lease 8f3a --listen
# node-1 f7c2... elected

# Watch, so a client learns of a leadership change rather than discovering
# it on its next write.
ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --endpoints=https://etcd-0:2379 \
  watch --rev=1 --prefix /election/ --interactive
# : {"type":"PUT","kv":{"key":"f7c2.../node-1","value":""}}

# Diagnostics for the three real etcd problems.
curl -s localhost:2381/metrics | grep -E 'etcd_server_has_leader|etcd_server_is_leader'
#   has_leader = 0 means NO LEADER: the cluster is unavailable for writes.
#   is_leader on more than one member means a split brain: page.
curl -s localhost:2381/metrics | grep etcd_debugging_mvcc_total
#   keys_total: approaching ~100k means the store is over its design
#   range. Kubernetes in a large cluster can reach this with leases alone.

# THE FENCING PROBLEM, demonstrated. A lock without a fencing token is
# not a lock; it is a suggestion.
#
#   t=0.00  Client A acquires lock L, lease TTL 5 s. A is descheduled.
#   t=0.10  Client B calls acquire, gets a failure, retries at 250 ms.
#   t=5.00  A's lease EXPIRES. B acquires L successfully.
#   t=5.01  B begins the critical section, writes to the database.
#   t=9.50  A is rescheduled, believes it still holds L, and resumes.
#   t=9.51  A writes to the database. BOTH A and B are inside the
#           critical section. The lock has failed silently.
#
# The only sound fix is a fencing token that the protected resource
# enforces. PostgreSQL 18 makes this trivial, because the write itself
# can carry the token.

CREATE TABLE lease_fence (
    resource  text        PRIMARY KEY,
    fence     bigint      NOT NULL DEFAULT 0
);

-- Client B, on acquiring the lease, increments and reads the token.
UPDATE lease_fence SET fence = fence + 1
 WHERE resource = 'invoice-42' RETURNING fence;   -- B gets fence = 2

-- Every write inside the critical section must present it:
UPDATE invoices
   SET status = 'issued', fence = 2
 WHERE id = 'inv-42' AND fence < 2;
-- A's late write carries fence = 1 and matches ZERO rows. It cannot
-- proceed, cannot corrupt, and can detect that it has lost the lock.
-- The protection is in the DATA, not in the coordinator: the resource
-- enforces the invariant even if the coordinator is unreachable, the
-- client is paused, or the client is wrong.

-- Compare with a lock on an external HTTP API: there is nowhere to put
-- the token, so the lock is unsound and the operation must be made
-- idempotent with a conditional instead. If the API offers neither,
-- the design is wrong; the lock cannot be made correct.
SystemProtocolData scale limitWatch modelUse it forNot for
etcdRaft~2 GB, ~10k events/s✓ Continuous, resumableKubernetes state, config, leases, electionsGeneral-purpose data storage
ZooKeeperZAB~1 GB, Znode limitsOne-shot; misses are client-sideLegacy Hadoop, Kafka (pre-4.0)New systems; high write rate
ConsulRaft + Serf gossip~1 GB practical✓ Blocking queries and watchesService discovery, DNS, prepared queriesHot data, many small values
NATS JetStream 2.11Raft per streamDisk-bound, replicas ≤ 5✓ Consumer pull, orderedEvent streams, work queues, KVSub-10 ms global writes
Redis 8.x clusterGossip + async replicationMemory-boundPub/Sub, streamsCaching, sessions, rate limitsAnything needing consensus

etcd is a configuration store with a small design range, and Kubernetes clusters regularly exceed it. The documented working range is a few thousand writes per second and a total data size under 2 GB; a watch on the full keyspace is a write amplification of the entire store on every change, which is why a cluster with hundreds of controllers produces a steady write load that has nothing to do with application data. The operational symptoms arrive in a specific order: etcdserver: mvcc: database space exceeded, then etcdserver: too many requests, then leader elections caused by fsync latency on a loaded disk. The mitigations are compaction (--auto-compaction-mode=periodic, --auto-compaction-retention=5m), defragmentation on a schedule, dedicated disks, and putting anything high-volume in its own etcd instance rather than sharing the cluster one.

11.6 Avoiding consensus in the request path

Argues that most systems that reach for a distributed lock or a leader have a simpler correct alternative, and specifies the mechanisms that replace consensus.

The general principle is that consensus is a strong, expensive mechanism, and the burden of proof runs the wrong way: it should be clear why a problem needs it, not assumed because the problem is hard. In practice, the majority of uses of a coordinator are solving one of four problems, and each has a cheaper correct answer. Mutual exclusion is usually a uniqueness constraint — a UNIQUE index in PostgreSQL 18 is a distributed mutex with no coordinator, no lease, no pause hazard, and no additional round-trip. Leader election for scheduled work is usually a queue with consumer groups, where the broker already does the assignment and the work is idempotent so double-delivery is harmless. Configuration and coordination is usually a version-stamped value in a store that is already there. Sequential ordering across writers is usually a single-writer design: partition the data by key and give each key exactly one owner, which converts a global ordering problem into N local ones.

Where a weak mechanism is genuinely insufficient, the right primitive is not consensus but a gossip protocol. Gossip propagates membership and state in O(log n) rounds with no leader, no quorum requirement, no ordering guarantee, and no blocking behaviour: a node that cannot reach a majority continues to serve reads from local state, and the inconsistency resolves when the partition heals. SWIM — the protocol behind HashiCorp Serf and NATS membership — combines a gossip failure detector with an independent probe round per node, which gives roughly n-scaled failure detection without the false-positive rate that pure gossip accumulation produces. The tradeoff is the one you would expect: gossip gives eventual consistency and no linearizability, so it is correct for membership, service discovery, cache invalidation and state merging, and incorrect for anything where two concurrent writers must not both succeed.

// The cheap correct answer to "I need a distributed mutex", in order of
// preference. Each is strictly stronger than a lock for the job it fits.

// 1. UNIQUENESS CONSTRAINT. No coordinator, no lease, no network round-trip
//    beyond the write you were already making.
func reserveSlot(ctx context.Context, slotID string, orderID uuid.UUID) error {
    _, err := db.ExecContext(ctx, `
        INSERT INTO shipment_slots (slot_id, order_id)
        VALUES ($1, $2)
        ON CONFLICT (slot_id) DO NOTHING
        RETURNING slot_id`, slotID, orderID)
    if errors.Is(err, sql.ErrNoRows) {
        return ErrSlotTaken    // exactly one writer wins, always
    }
    return err
}
// This is a correct distributed mutex. Two processes on two continents
// racing for the same slot produce exactly one winner, with no lock
// service, no lease expiry, and no failure mode where both proceed.

// 2. COMPARE-AND-SWAP on a version column. The general case of (1).
func transitionOrder(ctx context.Context, id uuid.UUID, from, to State) error {
    tag, err := db.ExecContext(ctx, `
        UPDATE orders SET state = $3, version = version + 1
         WHERE id = $1 AND state = $2`, id, from, to)
    if tag == 0 { return fmt.Errorf("%w: expected %s", ErrConflict, from) }
    return err
}

// 3. SKIP LOCKED WORK QUEUE. The broker does the assignment; idempotency
//    makes a duplicate harmless, so the guarantee is only ever weakened.
func claimNext(ctx context.Context) (*Job, error) {
    var j Job
    err := db.QueryRowContext(ctx, `
        WITH claimed AS (
          SELECT id FROM jobs
           WHERE status = 'ready' AND run_after <= now()
           ORDER BY run_after
           LIMIT 1
           FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED      -- no worker ever blocks another
        )
        UPDATE jobs SET status = 'running', claimed_at = now(), claimed_by = $1
         WHERE id IN (SELECT id FROM claimed)
        RETURNING id, payload, attempts`).Scan(&j.ID, &j.Payload, &j.Attempts)
    return &j, err
}
// The queue is the lock, the database is the coordinator, and the system
// degrades to at-least-once rather than to a stall when a worker dies.

// Gossip (SWIM-style): membership and failure detection WITHOUT consensus.
// No leader, no quorum, no ordering, no blocking. This is the right tool
// when "eventually consistent" is acceptable, which is most of the time.
type Node struct {
    id      string
    members map[string]*Member       // id -> last known state
    clock   LamportClock
}

type Member struct {
    Status    Status                // Alive | Suspect | Dead
    Incarnation uint64               // monotonic per node, for suspicion
    LastSeen  time.Time
}

func (n *Node) probe(target string) {
    // One random member probes, rather than every member probing every
    // other: this is the SWIM insight, and it turns O(n^2) per round
    // into O(n) with the same detection time.
    m := n.members[target]
    if m.Status != Alive { return }

    ok := n.ping(target, m.Incarnation)
    if ok {
        n.confirm(target, m.Incarnation)
        return
    }
    // Direct probe failed. Do NOT declare dead: re-probe k random others
    // (k = 3 in SWIM) and take a majority. This is what separates SWIM's
    // low false-positive rate from naive gossip's.
    acks := 0
    for _, peer := range n.randomMembers(3) {
        if n.pingVia(peer, target, m.Incarnation) { acks++ }
    }
    if acks*2 > len(n.randomMembers(3)) {
        n.mark(target, Suspect, m.Incarnation)   // suspicion, not death
        // Suspect is a distinct state precisely so a brief partition does
        // not cause two nodes to declare each other dead and both leave.
    } else {
        n.mark(target, Dead, m.Incarnation)
    }
}

func (n *Node) gossip() {
    // Push a small random subset, plus the delta since the last push.
    for _, peer := range n.randomMembers(3) {
        n.push(peer, n.randomMembers(3), n.deltaSinceLastPush())
    }
    // Convergence: O(log n) rounds for all state to reach all nodes under
    // normal conditions. NO GUARANTEE under partition, which is the
    // correct and accepted behaviour: a partitioned node keeps serving
    // from local state rather than blocking.
}
NeedReach forRound-tripsFails howVerdict
One winner among manyUNIQUE constraint0 extraConstraint violation, cleanly✓ Always first choice
Compare-and-swapVersion column in the UPDATE0 extraZero rows affected, cleanly✓ Always first choice
Exclusive work claimFOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED0 extraAnother worker claims it✓ First choice for queues
Partition work across consumersKafka / NATS consumer group1 rebalanceRebalance, then continue✓ First choice
Single writer per keyKey-partitioned log0 extraReorders within the partition✓ Strong and cheap
Membership, discoveryGossip (Serf, SWIM)0, asyncStale entries, never blocks✓ Correct for the job
Leader for scheduled jobsConsumer group with one member0 extraSplit brain, harmless if idempotent✓ Often sufficient
Config and flagsVersion-stamped store1 read, cachedStale for the TTL✓ Usually sufficient
Linearizable multi-key txnetcd / database txn2+Blocks without a quorum⚠ Only with a stated reason
Exactly-once across systemsImpossible; use idempotency——✗ Not a design option

When you do keep a consensus-backed dependency, keep it off the request path. The correct architecture is to consult it periodically — a background refresher every few seconds populating an in-process read-only view — and have the request path read only the local view. Kubernetes does this with informer caches, service meshes do it with xDS pushes, and DNS-based discovery does it with a resolver cache. The property you gain is that a consensus leader election becomes a brief local staleness rather than a 300 ms latency spike on every request, and a total partition of the coordinator becomes a degraded read rather than an outage.

Exercises 11

  1. Build the asynchronous simulator from the first code block with unbounded inboxes, a seeded PRNG and a fault injector, and enumerate interleavings until two processes decide different values. Deliverable: the seed and the interleaving trace producing the disagreement, plus the argument for why no deterministic fix exists.
  2. Measure the false-positive rate of leader election in a real cluster. Set the detection threshold to 3× the observed p99.9 RTT and run for 24 hours. Deliverable: the rate predicted by exp(−T/φ) beside the observed leader-churn count, and the threshold you would ship.
  3. Write a two-page internal note correcting your organisation’s stated understanding of CAP and FLP, using one real incident from your own history as the worked example. Deliverable: the note, plus every runbook line and dashboard annotation that currently misstates the result, with corrections.
  4. Replace a distributed lock in one real service with a UNIQUE constraint and measure the change. Deliverable: latency before and after, the number of round-trips removed, and the failure-mode analysis showing which hazard the constraint does not have.
  5. Implement SWIM-style failure detection with a three-peer re-probe, then partition a five-node cluster and measure false-positive death declarations against plain gossip. Deliverable: the detection latency and false-positive count for both, and the recovery behaviour when the partition heals.

12 · Containerisation, Orchestration and Kubernetes Workloads

12.1 Container images, layers and build hygiene12.2 Core Kubernetes objects and the controller pattern12.3 Scheduling: requests, limits, QoS and placement12.4 Networking: CNI, Services, Gateway API and service mesh12.5 Autoscaling: HPA, VPA, KEDA and the cluster autoscaler12.6 Configuration and secrets management

Kubernetes is treated here as a reconciliation substrate with a specific programming model, not as a virtual-machine manager with extra YAML. The section covers image construction as a supply-chain and layer-caching problem, the controller and desired-state model that makes the platform self-healing, and the request and limit mechanics that determine both scheduling behaviour and eviction priority. Networking is traced from CNI through ClusterIP to the Gateway API, and the service mesh is assessed on the specific guarantees it adds versus the latency it costs. Autoscaling is decomposed into its three independent loops, because scaling the wrong one is the commonest cause of a scaling system that does not scale. Secrets close the section with the specific claim that a base64-encoded environment variable is not a secret.

12.1 Container images, layers and build hygiene

Explains image layer semantics as they affect build caching, pull latency and vulnerability scanning, and specifies a reproducible multi-stage build.

A container image is an ordered set of content-addressed layers, each a tar diff plus a JSON manifest, and the union of those diffs is the root filesystem. Three consequences follow, and each is a design constraint rather than an implementation detail. First, an unchanged layer is shared by digest across every image containing it, so layer ordering determines how much of an image is a cache hit on a typical CI runner: a COPY go.mod before a COPY . . makes the dependency layer survive a source-only change, and reversing those two lines makes every build cold. Second, a layer cannot be removed by a later layer — a RUN rm creates a new diff containing whiteout entries, so secrets and build artefacts must never enter a layer in the first place. Third, each layer is a separate pull unit, so an image with forty tiny layers is measurably slower to pull than the same bytes in three.

The correct construction pattern is multi-stage with a minimal runtime base pinned by digest. Pinning by tag is a supply-chain hole: alpine:3.22 is mutable, so two builds of the same commit can produce different bytes, and an audit performed against one image does not apply to the other. Pinning by digest makes the build reproducible and turns the tag into a resolvable alias, which is the property that lets an SBOM be meaningful over time. Reproducibility also requires a nightly --no-cache build compared by digest, because a build that is only reproducible while the cache is warm is not reproducible at all.

# syntax=docker/dockerfile:1.19
# ---- Stage 1: build -------------------------------------------------------
FROM golang:1.25.6-bookworm AS build
WORKDIR /src

# Copy manifests FIRST so `go mod download` caches independently of source.
# Reversing these two stanzas makes every source change a cold build.
COPY go.mod go.sum ./
RUN --mount=type=cache,target=/go/pkg/mod \
    go mod download && go mod verify

COPY . .
ARG VERSION=dev
ARG COMMIT=unknown
# CGO off yields a static binary. -trimpath removes host paths so the
# output is bit-reproducible across machines, not merely on one runner.
RUN --mount=type=cache,target=/root/.cache/go-build \
    --mount=type=cache,target=/go/pkg/mod \
    CGO_ENABLED=0 GOOS=linux go build \
        -trimpath \
        -ldflags="-s -w -X main.version=${VERSION} -X main.commit=${COMMIT}" \
        -o /out/svc ./cmd/svc

# ---- Stage 2: verify. Runs in CI; never becomes an image layer. ----------
FROM build AS verify
RUN go vet ./... && go test -race -count=1 ./...

# ---- Stage 3: runtime -----------------------------------------------------
FROM gcr.io/distroless/static-debian12:nonroot@sha256:9f6a0c1d4e2b7a3f5c8d1e0b4a7c6d3e2f1a0b9c8d7e6f5a4b3c2d1e0f9a8b7
COPY --from=build /out/svc /usr/local/bin/svc
USER nonroot:nonroot
EXPOSE 8080
ENTRYPOINT ["/usr/local/bin/svc"]
# Result: ~12 MB, two layers, no shell, no package manager, non-root by
# default. A debugger must be attached from outside, which is the point:
# an attacker with a shell in a distroless container has to bring one.

# Build with BuildKit, emit an SBOM and provenance, scan, then push.
docker buildx build \
  --platform linux/amd64,linux/arm64 \
  --build-arg VERSION="$VERSION" --build-arg COMMIT="$GIT_SHA" \
  --sbom true --provenance mode=max \
  --output type=image,name="$REGISTRY/svc",push-by-digest=true \
  -t "$REGISTRY/svc:$GIT_SHA" -t "$REGISTRY/svc:edge" .

# Layer audit. A 300 MB layer is a misconfigured COPY that will dominate
# pull time for every pod start, on every node, forever.
docker history --no-trunc --human "$REGISTRY/svc:$GIT_SHA"

# Vulnerability gate: fail on HIGH and CRITICAL where a fix exists.
trivy image --severity HIGH,CRITICAL --ignore-unfixed \
  --exit-code 1 "$REGISTRY/svc:$GIT_SHA"

# Confirm nothing secret entered a layer, INCLUDING deleted layers.
# `rm` in a later layer does not remove bytes from earlier ones.
docker save "$REGISTRY/svc:$GIT_SHA" | gitleaks detect --stdin --no-git -v

# Reproducibility check, nightly, with no cache at all. Two digests must
# be identical or the build is not reproducible and no SBOM is trustworthy.
docker buildx build --no-cache --provenance=false \
  -t "$REGISTRY/svc:repro-a" --push . 
docker buildx build --no-cache --provenance=false \
  -t "$REGISTRY/svc:repro-b" --push .
docker inspect -f '{{index .RepoDigests 0}}' "$REGISTRY/svc:repro-a"
docker inspect -f '{{index .RepoDigests 0}}' "$REGISTRY/svc:repro-b"
Base imageSizeShellCVE surfaceUse when
golang:1.25-bookworm~850 MB✓High, full toolchainBuild stage only, never runtime
node:24-slim~210 MB✓MediumRuntime debugging in production is required
node:24-alpine~135 MB✓Medium; musl not glibcNative modules exist for musl only
distroless/static-debian12:nonroot~3 MB + app✗MinimalStatic Go or Rust binaries; default
distroless/base-debian12~20 MB + app✗Minimal.NET 10 or JVM needing glibc and tzdata
chainguard/wolfi-base~8 MB✓Very low, rebuilt dailyWhen the CVE SLA is measured in hours

A layer is immutable and a tag is a promise you did not make. Deleting a file in a later layer does not remove it from the image: anyone can recover the contents from docker history or by extracting the layer tarball. This is why build secrets passed as ARG, a .npmrc containing a token, and a COPY . . written before a .dockerignore exists are credential-disclosure incidents waiting to happen — and they are routinely discovered by an external researcher rather than internally. Pin runtime bases by digest, maintain a written inventory of which base images the fleet consumes, and re-pin on a schedule. An unpinned base silently changes the bytes under a tag someone already audited, which means the audit result has an expiry date that nobody recorded.

12.2 Core Kubernetes objects and the controller pattern

Explains the reconciliation programming model that underpins every controller, and specifies the workload objects with their correct uses and their failure behaviours.

Every Kubernetes controller implements the same loop: read the desired state from the API server, observe the actual state from the world, compute the difference, and act to reduce it, then repeat. This is a control loop with a convergence property, not a command dispatcher, and the distinction explains most Kubernetes operational behaviour. A Deployment does not execute "start three pods"; it observes that three of the desired replicas do not exist and creates them. If you delete a pod, the controller notices and replaces it — the self-healing behaviour is a consequence of the model, not a feature that was added. The practical implications follow directly: convergence is eventual rather than immediate, the API server is the coordination point and therefore a scaling and availability concern, and anything you can express as a difference between desired and actual can be managed declaratively.

The workload objects differ in what they promise. A Deployment manages a ReplicaSet and gives rolling updates with maxUnavailable and maxSurge; its pods are interchangeable, which is what makes it the right object for a stateless service. A StatefulSet provides stable network identities, ordered rollout, and per-pod persistent volumes — and the cost is that rollout is sequential, so a ten-pod StatefulSet takes ten times the update duration, and a stuck pod blocks the entire rollout. A DaemonSet runs one pod per node for node-level agents such as log shippers and CNI plugins, with the useful built-in scheduling features of node affinity, tolerations and maxUnavailable. A Job runs to completion and a CronJob schedules them; a CronJob’s concurrencyPolicy and startingDeadlineSeconds are the settings that prevent a backlog from compounding, and both are routinely left at defaults that do the wrong thing.

# The reconciliation loop, stated once, because every controller is this.
# (Go 1.25, client-go 1.33.)
func (c *Controller) Run(ctx context.Context) error {
    inform := c.kube.InformerFor(c.targetGVK())   // shared cache + watch
    informer.AddEventHandler(cache.ResourceEventHandlerFuncs{
        AddFunc:    func(any) { c.enqueue(struct{}{}) },
        UpdateFunc: func(any, any) { c.enqueue(struct{}{}) },
        DeleteFunc: func(any) { c.enqueue(struct{}{}) },
    })
    informer.Run(ctx.Done())

    work := c.rate.NewQueue("reconcile")
    go func() {                              // single-worker by default
        for c.processNext(ctx, work) {
        }
    }()
    <-ctx.Done()
    return nil
}

func (c *Controller) processNext(ctx context.Context, q workqueue.TypedRateLimitingInterface[reconcileKey]) bool {
    key, shutdown := q.Get()
    if shutdown { return false }
    defer q.Done(key)
    if err := c.sync(ctx, key); err != nil {
        // Rate-limited requeue with exponential backoff. NEVER a tight
        // retry: a controller that hot-loops against a failing API server
        // is how one bad deployment takes down a cluster.
        q.AddRateLimited(key)
        return true
    }
    q.Forget(key)   // success: reset the backoff
    return true
}

// sync: read desired, observe actual, diff, act. Reconcile is LEVEL-based,
// not EDGE-based: one Create of the Deployment causes the ReplicaSet
// controller and the scheduler to each do their own reconcile. That
// decomposition is why the system scales across controller types.
func (c *Controller) sync(ctx context.Context, key reconcileKey) error {
    desired, err := c.kube.Get(ctx, key)
    if apierrors.IsNotFound(err) {
        return c.deleteAllFor(key)     // GC by owner reference
    }
    if err != nil { return err }
    if desired.Status.ObservedGeneration < desired.Generation {
        // Status has not caught up with spec: the controller has not yet
        // processed this change. Skipping prevents a second reconcile of
        // work already in flight.
        return nil
    }
    return c.reconcileChildren(ctx, desired)
}

# A correct workload specification, Kubernetes 1.33. Note what is explicit:
# resources, probes with real thresholds, topology spread, and a PDB.
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: order-api
  namespace: prod
  labels: { app: order-api, tier: edge }
spec:
  replicas: 6                       # not 3: with maxUnavailable 1 and a
  revisionHistoryLimit: 5           # 1-node drain, 3 replicas is 33% churn
  strategy:
    type: RollingUpdate
    rollingUpdate:
      maxSurge: 2                   # 2 extra pods during rollout
      maxUnavailable: 0             # NEVER below 1 in production:
                                    # 0 means a full outage at the moment
                                    # old pods terminate
  minReadySeconds: 10               # a pod must stay ready 10 s before it
                                    # counts; kills the crash-loop-release
  progressDeadlineSeconds: 600
  selector:
    matchLabels: { app: order-api } # immutable after creation
  template:
    metadata:
      labels: { app: order-api, tier: edge }
    spec:
      serviceAccountName: order-api
      automountServiceAccountToken: false   # no API access needed
      securityContext:
        runAsNonRoot: true
        runAsUser: 10001
        fsGroup: 10001
        seccompProfile: { type: RuntimeDefault }
      # Spread across nodes AND zones. Without this, 6 replicas can land
      # on 2 nodes and a single node failure is a 67% capacity loss.
      topologySpreadConstraints:
        - maxSkew: 1
          topologyKey: kubernetes.io/hostname
          whenUnsatisfiable: DoNotSchedule
          labelSelector:
            matchLabels: { app: order-api }
        - maxSkew: 1
          topologyKey: topology.kubernetes.io/zone
          whenUnsatisfiable: ScheduleAnyway
          labelSelector:
            matchLabels: { app: order-api }
      containers:
        - name: api
          image: registry.internal/order-api@sha256:9f6a0c1d4e2b7a3f...
          imagePullPolicy: IfNotPresent
          ports: [{ containerPort: 8080, name: http }]
          resources:
            requests: { cpu: 500m, memory: 512Mi }  # SCHEDULING + QoS
            limits:   { cpu: "2",  memory: 1Gi }    # ENFORCEMENT + QoS
          securityContext:
            allowPrivilegeEscalation: false
            readOnlyRootFilesystem: true
            capabilities: { drop: [ALL] }
          startupProbe:                # protects slow starts from the
            httpGet: { path: /healthz/startup, port: http }
            failureThreshold: 30
            periodSeconds: 2           # 60 s to start: slow Java/.NET
          livenessProbe:               # restarts ONLY on true undeath
            httpGet: { path: /healthz/live, port: http }
            periodSeconds: 10
            failureThreshold: 3        # 30 s to detect
          readinessProbe:              # removes from Service on overload
            httpGet: { path: /healthz/ready, port: http }
            periodSeconds: 5
            failureThreshold: 2
          lifecycle:
            preStop:
              exec: { command: ["/bin/sh","-c","sleep 5"] }
                                    # 5 s: SIGTERM and the Service endpoint
                                    # removal race, handled by sleep, not hope
---
apiVersion: policy/v1
kind: PodDisruptionBudget
metadata: { name: order-api, namespace: prod }
spec:
  minAvailable: 4      # a node drain can take 2 of 6; capacity remains
  selector:
    matchLabels: { app: order-api }

# StatefulSet: the object for anything with identity, and the one whose
# operational characteristics differ most from a Deployment.
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: StatefulSet
metadata: { name: ledger-writer, namespace: prod }
spec:
  serviceName: ledger-writer          # governing headless Service
  replicas: 5
  podManagementPolicy: OrderedReady   # Parallel is faster and DANGEROUS:
                                      # simultaneous writer election
                                      # produces a split brain
  updateStrategy:
    type: RollingUpdate
    rollingUpdate:
      partition: 4                    # upgrade pods 0..3, leave 4 running:
                                      # this is how a Raft leader change is
                                      # orchestrated, one member at a time
  template:
    spec:
      terminationGracePeriodSeconds: 120   # > drain time, always
      containers:
        - name: writer
          resources:
            requests: { cpu: "1", memory: 2Gi }
            limits:   { cpu: "2", memory: 4Gi }
---
# The trap, stated concretely: a StatefulSet rolls out SEQUENTIALLY.
# 5 replicas, 90 s per pod, 2 min termination grace = ~19 minutes for a
# rollout. A Deployment does the same change in 3 minutes. If your SLO
# requires fast rollback, a StatefulSet's speed is a design constraint
# you accepted when you chose the object.
kubectl rollout status statefulset/ledger-writer --timeout=30m
kubectl get pods -l app=ledger-writer -o wide   # watch the ordinal
# The rollback lever: set partition back, then patch each pod downward.
kubectl rollout undo statefulset/ledger-writer --to-revision=41
ObjectPod identityStorageRolloutUse forNot for
DeploymentEphemeralNone or sharedParallel, all at onceStateless servicesAnything needing stable identity
StatefulSet✓ Stable, ordinal✓ Per-pod PVCSequential, reverse orderDatabases, brokers, ledgersStateless services; fast rollback
DaemonSetPer nodeHost path or noneRolling, per nodeAgents: CNI, log shipper, metricsAnything needing more than one per node
JobOne-shotOptionaln/aMigrations, backfills, one-time workLong-running services
CronJobPer runOptionaln/aScheduled batch and maintenanceSub-minute schedules
PodEphemeralNonen/aDebugging, one-off tasksAnything at all: no self-healing
ReplicaSetEphemeralNoneParallelUsually managed by a DeploymentDirect use, unless you need a custom rollout

The liveness probe is the most dangerous field in a Kubernetes manifest, and the failure mode is a cascading outage. A liveness probe that checks anything reachable from outside the process — a downstream database, a cache, a third-party API — will restart every pod in the fleet simultaneously when that dependency degrades, converting a partial dependency outage into a total one and destroying the connection pools and caches that were keeping the surviving pods serving. The rule is that liveness answers exactly one question: is this process irrecoverably wedged such that only a restart helps? Readiness is the probe that checks dependencies, because failing readiness removes the pod from the Service without killing it. The corollary is the three-way probe discipline: startupProbe to cover slow initialisation, livenessProbe for process death only, readinessProbe for dependency health and deliberate shedding.

12.3 Scheduling: requests, limits, QoS and placement

Explains how requests determine scheduling and limits determine enforcement, and how the two quality-of-service classes determine eviction order under node pressure.

A container’s requests answer a scheduling question — how much of a node this pod reserves — and its limits answer an enforcement question — what the kernel will cap. They are different mechanisms serving different purposes, and the commonest scheduling mistake is setting them equal. The scheduler places a pod on a node only if the sum of requests of all already-placed pods plus this pod’s request fits within the node’s allocatable capacity. limits play no part in that decision at all. So a pod with requests.cpu = 100m and limits.cpu = 4 is scheduled onto a busy node believing it needs a tenth of a core, and then consumes four — which is precisely the mechanism behind a noisy-neighbour incident. Set requests from measured p95 usage and limits from measured p99.9, and accept that the two will differ.

The second consequence is the quality-of-service classes, which determine eviction order when a node runs out of resources. A pod whose requests equal its limits in both CPU and memory is Guaranteed and is the last class evicted. A pod with any request or limit set equal to none is BestEffort and is evicted first, in a load-dependent order. Everything in between is Burstable, and its eviction order within the class is proportional to how far actual usage exceeds the request, so a pod requesting 100m and using 3 cores is evicted before one requesting 1 core and using 1.1. The practical consequence is that a cluster running mostly Burstable pods has no meaningful eviction order, and node-pressure events take down arbitrary pods; a cluster that wants deterministic behaviour under pressure needs Guaranteed pods for the workloads it cannot afford to lose, and that is a QoS decision rather than a resource-sizing one.

Placement is the third axis and it is where the majority of real incidents originate. nodeSelector is a hard label match; affinity and antiAffinity are the general form and are what most production policies should use; taints and tolerations control which pods may run where at the node level, and are how dedicated node pools are built. The combination that is routinely missed is topology spread: with no spread constraint, the scheduler’s LeastAllocated scoring will usually spread replicas, but it is a heuristic and it makes no guarantee — a rolling update or a burst of scheduling can place four of six replicas on one node, and a single node failure then removes two-thirds of capacity in an instant. topologySpreadConstraints with maxSkew: 1 converts that from likely to prevented.

# The scheduling settings that determine behaviour under pressure, with the
# reasoning for each. Kubernetes 1.33.
      containers:
        - name: api
          resources:
            # REQUEST: the scheduler's arithmetic. Must reflect the p95
            # of real usage. Set too low and the pod is co-scheduled with
            # a neighbour that will contend for the same physical cores.
            requests:
              cpu: 500m          # measured p95 across a full traffic week
              memory: 512Mi      # memory requests are NOT compressible:
                                 # over-requesting wastes the node,
                                 # under-requesting gets you OOMKilled
            # LIMIT: kernel enforcement, and the QoS class determinant.
            # CPU limit throttles at 100 ms periods, which adds tail
            # latency; memory limit is a hard cgroup limit and the OOM
            # killer's trigger.
            limits:
              cpu: "2"          # 4x request: burst headroom for GC and
                                 # startup, without unbounded contention
              memory: 1Gi
      # GUARANTEED QoS requires limits == requests for BOTH cpu and memory.
      # Without that equality, the pod is Burstable and its eviction order
      # under node pressure is proportional to overcommit. If a workload
      # genuinely cannot be interrupted by a node drain, it must be
      # Guaranteed, and that is a deliberate trade of node utilisation
      # for determinism.
---
# Placement: dedicated pools by taint, spread by topology.
      tolerations:
        - key: workload
          operator: Equal
          value: latency-critical
          effect: NoSchedule
      affinity:
        nodeAffinity:
          requiredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution:
            nodeSelectorTerms:
              - matchExpressions:
                  - key: node.kubernetes.io/instance-type
                    operator: In
                    values: [c7i.4xlarge]   # dedicated, predictable hardware
        podAntiAffinity:
          preferredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution:   # SOFT: never
            - weight: 100                                      # leave a
              podAffinityTerm:                                 # node empty
                topologyKey: kubernetes.io/hostname
                labelSelector:
                  matchLabels: { app: order-api }
      # And the HARD constraint that actually guarantees spread. A
      # preferred anti-affinity is a hint; only topologySpreadConstraints
      # with DoNotSchedule is a guarantee, and during a surge the
      # difference is the difference between surviving a node failure and
      # not.

# Diagnostics: the five commands that answer most scheduling questions.
# All of these are safe on a live cluster and all belong in a runbook.

# 1. Where did the pods actually land, and is the spread acceptable?
kubectl get pods -n prod -l app=order-api \
  -o custom-columns='NAME:.metadata.name,NODE:.spec.nodeName,ZONE:.metadata.labels.topology\\.kubernetes\\.io/zone'
# Group by zone. Two zones with 5 and 1 replica is a finding, not a
# detail: a single-zone failure removes 83% of capacity.

# 2. Why is a pod Pending? The scheduler's own explanation, which names
#    the predicate that failed.
kubectl describe pod -n prod order-api-7d4f9 | sed -n '/Events:/,$p'
#   "0/12 nodes are available: 3 Insufficient cpu, 9 node(s) had
#    untolerated taint {workload=latency-critical}."
# The second half of that message is the interesting half: it is a
# PLACEMENT failure, not a capacity failure, and adding nodes will not
# help.

# 3. Are requests and limits real, or aspirations? This is the query
#    that reveals overcommit before it becomes an incident.
kubectl top pods -n prod --containers \
  -l app=order-api --sort-by=cpu
# Compare against the manifest:
kubectl get deploy order-api -n prod -o jsonpath='{.spec.template.spec.containers[0].resources}'
# 6 pods requesting 500m = 3 cores reserved, limits allow 12. On a
# 16-vCPU node, that is 75% theoretical overcommit at the limit.

# 4. Node pressure and eviction. The QoS class determines the victim.
kubectl get nodes -o custom-columns='NAME:.metadata.name,PRESSURE:.status.conditions[*].type'
kubectl get events -n prod --field-selector reason=Evicted --sort-by=.lastTimestamp | tail -20
# An Evicted event names the pod and, in the message, the QoS class and
# the threshold. "The node was low on resource: memory."

# 5. Node allocatable versus actually allocatable. Reserved and consumed
#    system resources mean allocatable is not capacity.
kubectl describe node c7i.4xlarge-3 | sed -n '/Allocatable:/,/^Events/p'
# Compare against what your DaemonSets actually consume: a log shipper
# at 200 Mi on 40 nodes is 8 GiB of the cluster that no Deployment can
# schedule, and it is invisible in the Deployment's resource requests.

# The four questions, in the order to ask them:
#   1. Is the pod Pending, and why?          (placement predicate)
#   2. Are the pods where I think they are?  (topology)
#   3. Do the requests match reality?        (overcommit)
#   4. Is anything being evicted, and at what QoS?  (pressure)
QoS classCriteriaEviction orderCPU behaviourUse for
GuaranteedRequests = limits for CPU and memoryLastNo throttling; full isolationLatency-critical, cannot be interrupted
BurstableSome request or limit setProportional to usage over requestThrottled at the CFS quota periodDefault for most workloads
BestEffortNo requests, no limitsFirst, load-dependentUnlimited, unbounded contention✗ Only for proven-safe batch
CPU throttlinglimits.cpu set, usage exceeds itn/aThrottled every 100 ms periodCauses p99 spikes, not p50 problems
Memory pressurelimits.memory or node pressurecgroup then node-level OOMn/aOOMKilled is silent and instant; set limits deliberately
Disk pressurenode ephemeral or imagefsLowest priority firstn/aNodeDiskPressure evicts even Guaranteed pods

Set memory limits deliberately, and know that the failure is silent. A container that exceeds its memory limit is killed by the kernel with no application-level signal: the process gets SIGKILL, no graceful shutdown, no flush, no trace, and the pod restarts into an CrashLoopBackOff that looks like a startup bug. The consequence for a service holding unflushed state is data loss at the moment of death, which is why a memory limit below what the working set needs is worse than no limit at all in a system where the kernel will evict the pod anyway under pressure. The correct sequence is: measure the true peak including a full GC cycle, add 30–50% headroom, set the limit, and verify the container_memory_working_set_bytes distribution never approaches it. OOMKilled with a nonzero exit code of 137 and no application log is the signature to look for first when a pod restarts with no explanation.

12.4 Networking: CNI, Services, Gateway API and service mesh

Traces the packet path from CNI through Service to the Gateway API, and specifies the guarantees a service mesh adds against the latency it costs.

Kubernetes networking has four layers and conflating them is the source of most confusion. The CNI is a plugin that gives each pod its own IP address on the node’s network namespace, typically by creating a veth pair and a bridge. The kube-proxy programs the node’s iptables or nftables rules so that a ClusterIP virtual address is load-balanced across the ready endpoints behind a Service; this is per-node, so a Service with 20 endpoints across 6 nodes has each node balancing over its own subset. The Service object is the stable virtual address and the selector that maintains the endpoint set. DNS resolves the name to the ClusterIP through CoreDNS. The two properties that surprise people are that a Service IP is not a pod and never moves, and that connection reuse across a Service is per-node: a long-lived connection from one client pod to a Service pins to one endpoint, so load balancing is per-connection rather than per-request unless the client or the proxy is configured otherwise.

Gateway API is the current-generation replacement for Ingress, and the improvement is architectural: the spec separates the role that provisions and configures the proxy from the role that configures routes, with GatewayClass, Gateway, HTTPRoute and ReferenceGrant as separate objects with separate owners. That separation is what allows a platform team to own the load balancer and an application team to own the routes without either editing the other’s manifests. Service mesh adds a sidecar or an ambient proxy per workload and gives mutual TLS, traffic policy, retries with budgets, outlier detection and fine-grained telemetry. The cost is real and should be quantified before adoption: an Envoy 1.34 sidecar consumes roughly 40–60 MB of memory per pod, adds 1–3 ms of p50 latency per hop and more at p99 under load, and multiplies the control-plane surface that must be healthy. A mesh is justified by a requirement you cannot otherwise meet — zero-trust mTLS across teams, or uniform retry and outlier policies — and not by the desire for better observability, which the same telemetry stack provides more cheaply.

# Gateway API: platform owns the Gateway, the app team owns the route.
# The separation is the entire point of the API.
apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Gateway
metadata:
  name: public-gateway
  namespace: infra
  annotations:
    cert-manager.io/cluster-issuer: letsencrypt-prod
spec:
  gatewayClassName: istio            # owned by the platform team
  listeners:
    - name: https
      protocol: HTTPS
      port: 443
      hostname: "*.example.com"
      tls:
        mode: Terminate
        certificateRefs:
          - kind: Secret
            name: wildcard-tls
            namespace: infra
---
# Owned by the application team. Changing a weight here requires no
# change to the Gateway, and no coordination with the platform team.
apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: HTTPRoute
metadata:
  name: order-api
  namespace: prod
spec:
  parentRefs:
    - name: public-gateway
      namespace: infra                # cross-namespace needs a ReferenceGrant
      sectionName: https
  hostnames: ["api.example.com"]
  rules:
    - matches:
        - path: { type: PathPrefix, value: /v3/orders }
      filters:
        - type: RequestHeaderModifier
          requestHeaderModifier:
            set: [{ name: x-app, value: order-api }]
        - type: ResponseHeaderModifier
          responseHeaderModifier:
            set:
              - { name: Strict-Transport-Security, value: "max-age=31536000" }
      timeouts:
        request: 2s
        backendRequest: 1800ms         # LESS than request, so the backend
                                       # error is specific rather than
                                       # surfacing as a gateway 504
      retry:
        codes: [502, 503, 504]
        attempts: 2
        backoff: 50ms
      backendRefs:
        - name: order-api
          port: 8080
          weight: 100
---
# Cross-namespace reference permission. Without this, the route is
# rejected at admission with a message that does not name the cause.
apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1beta1
kind: ReferenceGrant
metadata:
  name: allow-prod-to-infra
  namespace: infra
spec:
  from:
    - group: gateway.networking.k8s.io
      kind: HTTPRoute
      namespace: prod
  to:
    - group: gateway.networking.k8s.io
      kind: Gateway
      name: public-gateway

# Istio 1.27 / Envoy 1.34: the policies that actually change behaviour.
# Everything else in the mesh config is documentation.
apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1
kind: PeerAuthentication
metadata:
  name: default
  namespace: prod
spec:
  mtls:
    mode: STRICT        # never PERMISSIVE in a namespace with real data:
                        # PERMISSIVE allows plaintext and the mesh will
                        # happily serve it, defeating the entire point
---
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1
kind: VirtualService
metadata:
  name: order-api
  namespace: prod
spec:
  hosts: [order-api.prod.svc.cluster.local]
  http:
    - match:
        - uri: { prefix: /v3/orders }
      route:
        - destination: { host: order-api.prod.svc.cluster.local }
          weight: 90
        - destination: { host: order-api-v2.prod.svc.cluster.local }
          weight: 10        # canary, without a per-request header
      timeout: 1800ms
      retries:
        attempts: 2
        perTryTimeout: 800ms
        retryOn: "5xx,reset,connect-failure,refused-stream"
---
# The two settings that prevent the two worst mesh failures.
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1
kind: DestinationRule
metadata:
  name: order-api
  namespace: prod
spec:
  host: order-api.prod.svc.cluster.local
  trafficPolicy:
    connectionPool:
      tcp:
        maxConnections: 200      # upstream connection cap: the circuit
                                 # breaker, and the protection against a
                                 # retry storm multiplying connections
      http:
        http1MaxPendingRequests: 100
        http2MaxRequests: 500    # HTTP/2 multiplexing-aware; setting this
                                 # too low throttles legitimate traffic
        maxRequestsPerConnection: 0   # 0 disables it; set >0 to spread load
                                      # across upstream connections
    outlierDetection:
      consecutive5xxErrors: 5     # eject a genuinely broken endpoint
      interval: 5s
      baseEjectionTime: 30s
      maxEjectionPercent: 50     # NEVER 100: ejecting every host turns
                                 # one bad deployment into a total outage
      minHealthPercent: 50       # always leave at least half the fleet
---
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1
kind: PeerAuthentication
metadata: { name: default, namespace: prod }
spec:
  portLevelMtls:
    # Prometheus scraped in PLAINTEXT: metrics are not a reason to run a
    # mixed-mode mesh, and the scrape path is the one path that must
    # never require a valid client certificate to diagnose a failure.
    - port: 15020
      mtls: { mode: DISABLE }

# Networking diagnostics. The four questions, in order.
# 1. Is the Service selecting the pods you think it is?
kubectl get endpointslices -n prod -l kubernetes.io/service-name=order-api \
  -o custom-columns='ADDR:.endpoints[*].addresses[*],READY:.endpoints[*].conditions[*].ready,NODE:.nodeName'
# Empty endpoints with non-empty pods means the selector does not match
# the pod LABELS. It is the most common Kubernetes networking fault and
# the message never says so.
#   kubectl get pod -l app=order-api --show-labels
#   kubectl get svc order-api -o jsonpath='{.spec.selector}'

# 2. Is the ClusterIP reachable at all, from inside the cluster?
kubectl run netdebug --rm -it --image=nicolaka/netshoot --restart=Never -- \
  sh -c 'nslookup order-api.prod.svc.cluster.local;
         curl -sv --max-time 3 http://order-api.prod.svc.cluster.local:8080/healthz'

# 3. Is the sidecar the problem? Compare with and without.
istioctl proxy-config endpoints order-api-7d4f9 -n prod
istioctl proxy-status
#   SYNCED means the proxy has the current config. STALE means a control
#   plane problem, and a STALE proxy is a proxy making decisions from
#   old configuration, which is worse than no proxy.
istioctl proxy-config log order-api-7d4f9 --level connection_pool:debug,outlier_detection:debug

# 4. Where is the time actually going? The mesh adds latency and it must
#    be attributed, not assumed.
istioctl proxy-config listener order-api-7d4f9 -n prod --port 8080 -o json \
  | jq '.[] | {name, filterChain: .filterChains[0].filters[0].name}'
kubectl exec -n prod order-api-7d4f9 -c istio-proxy -- \
  pilot-agent request GET stats/prometheus \
  | grep -E 'envoy_cluster_(upstream_rq_time|upstream_rq_pending|outlier_detection)'
# p50 delta below 1 ms means the mesh is not the problem, whatever it
# feels like during an incident.

# 5. Ambient mode, if used, replaces the sidecar. Check for a CNI node
#    that has not been labelled: an unlabelled node silently gets no
#    capture, and the pods on it appear to bypass the mesh entirely.
kubectl get nodes -l istio.io/dataplane-mode=ambient
# Nodes missing from that list while pods are labelled ztunnel-ready is
# a silent, partial mesh rollout, and it is worse than no mesh.
LayerObjectScopeLoad-balancing granularityTypical failure
L3/L2 attachCNI pluginNodeNoneCNI DaemonSet not ready; all pods pending
L4 virtual IPService ClusterIPCluster, per-node iptablesPer connection, not per requestEndpoints empty: selector mismatch
DNSCoreDNS, headless ServiceClusterHeadless returns all pod IPsCoreDNS throttled at 1,000 QPS/node default
L7 ingressGateway API, GatewayCluster edgePer requestCross-namespace ReferenceGrant missing
L7 meshEnvoy 1.34, Istio 1.27WorkloadPer request, L7 awareStale config from a control-plane outage
Layer 7 policyVirtualService, DestinationRuleServiceWeighted, per requestmaxEjectionPercent: 100 ejects the whole fleet

Connection pooling means your load balancing is not what you think it is. A client with a keep-alive connection pool of 100 sending 1,000 req/s to a Service with 6 pods distributes those requests across 6 connections — 167 req/s to one pod, 0 to the others, and no configuration anywhere that controls which 6. Load imbalance of this kind is invisible in the Service’s endpoint list, which is perfectly balanced, and it produces a pod that is CPU-saturated while its neighbours are idle. The mitigations are maxRequestsPerConnection on the mesh side, per-request load balancing at the client, or an explicit round-robin service with a client that does not reuse connections. The same mechanism explains the opposite symptom: a rolling update that appears to have changed nothing, because every client is still pinned to an endpoint that has not been terminated. A preStop hook that sleeps, and a Service whose endpoints drain before the pod dies, are both required — a sleep in the application is a mitigation, not a fix.

12.5 Autoscaling: HPA, VPA, KEDA and the cluster autoscaler

Decomposes autoscaling into its three independent loops and identifies which loop is responsible for which failure.

There are three autoscalers and they operate on different objects, which is why a scaling system usually fails in a way that looks like one component not working. The Horizontal Pod Autoscaler scales replica count from a metric — CPU or memory by default, or a custom or external metric — and it requires a metrics pipeline: metrics-server for resource metrics, or Prometheus Adapter for custom ones. Its critical property is that it reacts to a lagging signal, so for a request-driven workload it is always late by roughly one metrics-scrape interval plus one stabilisation window, and the correct response is to scale on a leading indicator rather than to make the reaction faster. The Vertical Pod Autoscaler sets requests and limits from observed usage and can restart a pod to apply a change, which is why its default mode is Off and why a Recreate policy is a genuinely dangerous setting. The cluster autoscaler adds and removes nodes; it is bounded by the node group’s maximum size, which is the real capacity limit of the whole system and is routinely set from a guess.

KEDA is the fourth that most teams end up with, and it exists because the HPA’s metric model does not fit queues. KEDA scales to and from zero, which the HPA cannot do, and it scales on the metrics that actually drive queue-based work: queue depth, queue age, and Prometheus queries. Queue depth alone is a poor signal because it is bimodal — a queue at 0 with arrivals arriving, versus a queue at 0 because nothing is being produced, look identical and require opposite responses. Queue age is the signal that matters, because it directly measures the user-visible consequence, and it is the metric to scale on for a system with a service-level agreement about processing latency.

# HPA on a leading indicator rather than CPU. CPU is a LAGGING signal:
# at 8 rps per pod and 400 ms service time, saturation arrives in ~1 s
# and the metric pipeline adds 15-30 s. The queue is the leading signal.
apiVersion: autoscaling/v2
kind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler
metadata: { name: order-api, namespace: prod }
spec:
  scaleTargetRef:
    apiVersion: apps/v1
    kind: Deployment
    name: order-api
  minReplicas: 4
  maxReplicas: 60
  metrics:
    - type: Resource
      resource:
        name: cpu
        target: { type: Utilization, averageUtilization: 60 }
  behavior:
    scaleUp:
      # Aggressive on the way up: being late is a user-visible outage.
      stabilizationWindowSeconds: 15
      policies:
        - type: Pods
          value: 8
          periodSeconds: 15        # 8 pods per 15 s: from 4 to 60 in ~105 s
        - type: Percent
          value: 100
          periodSeconds: 30
      selectPolicy: Max
    scaleDown:
      # Very conservative on the way down: a flapping scaler is worse
      # than a slightly over-provisioned one. 300 s of stability.
      stabilizationWindowSeconds: 300
      policies:
        - type: Percent
          value: 25               # never remove more than 25% at once
          periodSeconds: 60
      selectPolicy: Min
---
# VPA: recommendations only by default. Auto with Recreate restarts pods,
# and a restart storm in a system where pods hold warm caches is a
# latency incident caused by a memory optimisation.
apiVersion: autoscaling.k8s.io/v1
kind: VerticalPodAutoscaler
metadata: { name: order-api, namespace: prod }
spec:
  targetRef:
    apiVersion: apps/v1
    kind: Deployment
    name: order-api
  updatePolicy:
    updateMode: "Off"      # "Auto" + "Recreate" restarts; "InPlaceOrRecreate"
                           # is safer on K8s 1.33 but still disruptive
  resourcePolicy:
    containerPolicies:
      - containerName: api
        minAllowed: { cpu: 250m, memory: 384Mi }
        maxAllowed: { cpu: "4", memory: 4Gi }
        controlledResources: [cpu, memory]
        controlledValues: RequestsOnly   # never let VPA set LIMITS:
                                         # a VPA-set limit turns a memory
