                                         # spike into an OOMKill

# KEDA 2.17 on Kubernetes 1.33: scale on queue AGE, not queue depth, and
# scale to zero. This is the correct shape for a queue-driven consumer.
apiVersion: keda.sh/v1alpha1
kind: ScaledObject
metadata: { name: order-projection, namespace: prod }
spec:
  scaleTargetRef:
    name: order-projection        # a Deployment, so replicas can be 0
  minReplicaCount: 0              # scale to ZERO: the single capability
                                  # the HPA cannot do, and the reason to
                                  # use KEDA at all
  maxReplicaCount: 40
  pollingInterval: 15             # KEDA's reaction latency: this plus
                                  # the SLO window IS your lead time
  cooldownPeriod: 180             # stay scaled up 3 min after the burst
  fallback:
    replicas: 3                   # if the scaler cannot reach Prometheus,
                                  # run 3, not 0 and not 40
  triggers:
    - type: prometheus            # AGE: the user-visible consequence
      metadata:
        serverAddress: http://prometheus.monitoring:9090
        metricName: orders_queue_oldest_message_age_seconds
        query: |
          max(
            time() - orders_queue_oldest_unacked_timestamp_seconds
          )
        threshold: '30'           # 30 s of age: at risk of the SLO
        activationThreshold: '5'  # below 5 s, scale to zero
    - type: prometheus            # DEPTH: still useful, but secondary
      metadata:
        metricName: orders_queue_depth
        query: sum(orders_queue_messages{queue="order-projection"})
        threshold: '1000'
---
# The cluster autoscaler is the outer loop, and its ceiling is the real
# capacity limit of the system. Node max must be set from a number, not
# from a guess.
apiVersion: eks.amazonaws.com/v1
kind: NodeGroup
metadata: { name: prod-general, namespace: prod }
spec:
  scalingConfig:
    minSize: 12
    maxSize: 80          # THIS is the capacity ceiling. At 60 HPA
                         # replicas x 2 CPU each, 80 nodes x 32 vCPU is
                         # 2,560 vCPU of ceiling. If the HPA can ask for
                         # more than the group allows, pods sit Pending
                         # for 3-4 minutes while nodes provision. That
                         # delay IS your scaling lead time.

# Autoscaling diagnostics. The three loops fail in three distinct ways and
# each has a different signature.

# 1. Is the HPA even seeing a metric? The most common silent failure.
kubectl describe hpa order-api -n prod | sed -n '/Metrics:/,$p'
#   "cpu: unable to get metrics for resource cpu: no metrics available"
# means metrics-server is not installed, not ready, or cannot reach the
# kubelet. Everything else in the autoscaling stack is irrelevant.
kubectl top pods -n prod -l app=order-api     # must return data first
kubectl -n kube-system get deploy metrics-server
# Also check the flag that disables it on many managed clusters:
kubectl -n kube-system get deploy metrics-server \
  -o jsonpath='{.spec.template.spec.containers[0].args}'

# 2. Is it scaling, and is the metric the right one? Plot both.
kubectl get hpa order-api -n prod -w
# Compare HPA replicas against the actual target metric over the same
# window. If CPU is at 60% and no scaling is happening, the target is
# wrong. If CPU is at 20% and scaling is happening, the metric is
# misconfigured or a second trigger is active.
kubectl get hpa order-api -n prod \
  -o jsonpath='{.status.currentMetrics}' | jq .
# Compare replica count against a leading indicator:
promql:
  sum(rate(http_requests_total{service="order-api"}[1m])) / 8   # rps/pod
  # A flat rps/pod with rising replicas means the pods are NOT the
  # constraint: the constraint is a downstream pool, a lock, or a
  # connection ceiling. More replicas will not help and will make the
  # queue worse.

# 3. Is the cluster able to accept the new replicas? The outer loop.
kubectl get pods -n prod --field-selector=status.phase=Pending \
  -o custom-columns='NAME:.metadata.name,REASON:.status.conditions[-1].message'
kubectl get events -n prod --sort-by=.lastTimestamp | grep -i 'failedscheduling'
#   "0/12 nodes are available: 3 Insufficient cpu" = the HPA asked and
#   the cluster cannot deliver. The fix is nodes, or a smaller request,
#   or a higher maxReplicas is not the answer at all.
# The provisioning delay is the lead time: EKS managed node groups
# typically take 2-4 minutes, Karpenter 30-60 s, Cluster API bare metal
# several minutes. Whatever that number is, the HPA's scaleUp window must
# anticipate it or it will always be late.

# 4. Is the scaler flapping? Watch for oscillation, which is worse than
#    either extreme.
promql:
  changes(deriv(kube_deployment_status_replicas_ready{deployment="order-api"}[5m])[30m:1m])
# A non-zero value over a long window is flapping. The cause is almost
# always a scaleDown window shorter than the workload's own periodic
# variation: a 5-minute cron job's traffic peak, or a nightly batch.
# Widen the window past the period, or exclude the metric, or the system
# will spend its capacity provisioning and deprovisioning.
ComponentScaling targetSignalReaction latencyPrimary failure
HPA on CPUPod replicasLagging resource metricScrape 15 s + window 15 sToo late; saturation precedes the signal
HPA on custom metricPod replicasExternal metric via adapterAdapter dependentAdapter down: no scaling, silently
VPArequests and limitsObserved usage percentileHoursRecreate mode: pod restart storm
KEDADeployments, to zeroQueue depth, age, PrometheusPolling 15 s + SLO windowFallback replicas too low during a metrics outage
Cluster autoscalerNode countPending pods2–4 min on managed groupsmaxSize ceiling hit; pods Pending
KarpenterNodes, dynamically shapedPending pods, cost30–60 sCapacity type gap: ARM nodes, no local disks
Pod disruption budgetNothing; it constrainsn/an/aBlocks node drains and upgrades indefinitely

Scale on the thing that hurts users, not on the thing that is easy to measure. CPU is easy to measure and almost never the right signal, because it is a proxy for demand that only correlates with load when the service is CPU-bound — and most services are not. The right signals are, in order: queue age for asynchronous work, concurrency in use for services with a bounded resource, and request rate per pod for a service with a known throughput ceiling. A useful test for a proposed metric is to deliberately overload the service and check whether the metric crosses its threshold before users report degradation; if it does not, the metric is a lagging indicator with extra infrastructure attached, and the fix is a different signal, not a faster scrape.

12.6 Configuration and secrets management

Distinguishes configuration from secret material, and specifies the rotation model that a base64-encoded environment variable cannot provide.

The distinction is between configuration — non-sensitive values that vary by environment and are safe in version control — and secret material — anything whose disclosure is a security incident: passwords, API keys, TLS private keys, signing keys, connection strings with embedded credentials. Kubernetes provides a ConfigMap for the first and a Secret for the second, and the second is where almost every real breach happens, because the most common deployment pattern, a base64 value in a Secret mounted as an environment variable, provides no secret management at all. A base64 string is an encoding, not encryption; anyone with get secret, with read access to the etcd backup, or with shell access to a pod, reads it in one command. The default answer should be an external secret manager with a controller that projects short-lived credentials into pods, so that no long-lived secret exists in the cluster at all.

Even with a real secret store, the operational problems remain and they are the ones that cause incidents. Rotation: a secret that never rotates is a liability with no expiry date, and rotation must be automated and tested because the first time anyone runs the rotation procedure is the day it is needed. Propagation: an environment variable is read once at process start, so rotating a secret does not affect running pods; a mounted file updates within roughly a minute, so the application must re-read it, which is an application design requirement and not a platform one. Audit: who read which secret, when, is a question that must be answerable, and a Secret in etcd answers it only in the sense that the API server logs the read. Scope: a secret mounted into a pod that does not need it is available to an attacker who compromises that pod, and the principle of least privilege applies to secrets as strictly as to permissions.

# External Secrets Operator projecting from HashiCorp Vault or AWS Secrets
# Manager. The secret NEVER exists in etcd; the pod receives a projected,
# short-lived credential refreshed by the controller.
apiVersion: external-secrets.io/v1beta1
kind: ExternalSecret
metadata:
  name: order-api-db
  namespace: prod
spec:
  refreshInterval: 1h                # re-sync; the VALUE has its own TTL
  secretStoreRef:
    name: vault-backend
    kind: ClusterSecretStore
  target:
    name: order-api-db
    creationPolicy: Owner            # clean up the Secret if this is deleted
    template:
      type: kubernetes.io/basic-auth   # the app gets typed credentials,
      engineVersion: v2                # not an opaque base64 blob
  data:
    - secretKey: username
      remoteRef:
        key: prod/orders/db
        property: username
    - secretKey: password
      # The credential is DYNAMIC, not static: Vault issues a database
      # role credential that is itself short-lived and rotated by the
      # database. Nothing long-lived exists anywhere.
      remoteRef:
        key: prod/orders/db-creds
        property: password
---
# Mount it, and note the settings that determine the blast radius.
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata: { name: order-api, namespace: prod }
spec:
  template:
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: api
          env:
            - name: DB_PASSWORD_FILE     # the *_FILE convention: point at
              value: /etc/secrets/db/password   # the file, not the value
          volumeMounts:
            - name: db-creds
              mountPath: /etc/secrets/db
              readOnly: true
      volumes:
        - name: db-creds
          projected:
            defaultMode: 0400           # owner read only
            sources:
              - secret:
                  name: order-api-db
                  items:
                    - key: password
                      path: password
# The application reads the file at startup. If it re-reads on SIGHUP, a
# Vault rotation propagates to a running pod with no restart. If it does
# not, rotation requires a rollout, and that fact must be in the runbook
# for the secret, not discovered during the rotation.

# Two alternatives worth knowing, with different trade-offs.
#
# ---- SOPS + age: git is the source of truth, decryption happens at
#      deploy time. Good for small teams; the encrypted values are
#      reviewable in diffs and the rotation story is a re-encrypt.
apiVersion: v1
kind: Secret
metadata:
  name: order-api-db
  namespace: prod
  annotations:
    # The ONLY annotation the controller needs.
    config.kubernetes.io/encrypted: |
      apiVersion: v1
      kind: EncryptedSecret
      spec:
        encryptedData:
          password: ENC[AES256_GCM,data:8fQ2...,iv:...,tag:...,type:str]
          username: ENC[AES256_GCM,data:Kx7p...,iv:...,tag:...,type:str]
        # Plaintext never leaves the developer's machine.
        # Age recipients, one per person, so access can be revoked
        # individually. No shared master key, which is the failure mode
        # of a single sops recipient list.
---
# ---- Sealed Secrets: one-time encrypted, cluster-scoped. The controller
#      holds only the private key; nobody else can read the Secret.

# In the cluster, ONCE:
kubeseal --controller-name sealed-secrets --controller-namespace kube-system \
        --fetch-cert --validate > cluster-cert.pem

# At deploy time, from CI, with the key NEVER in git:
kubeseal --controller-name sealed-secrets --controller-namespace kube-system \
  --cert cluster-cert.pem \
  --format yaml < secret.yaml > secret.sealed.yaml
git add secret.sealed.yaml && git commit -m "rotate db password"
# Git history holds ciphertext; the private key lives in one Secret in
# kube-system and nowhere else. Rotation is a re-seal and a commit.

# Secret hygiene: the four checks, and the specific evidence each produces.
# Run all four against production on a schedule, not on a request.

# 1. Is anything sensitive in a ConfigMap? ConfigMaps are NOT encrypted
#    at rest by default in every distribution and are world-readable to
#    anyone with namespace read. A credential here is a credential in
#    plaintext in etcd.
kubectl get configmap -A -o json | jq -r '
  .items[] | . as $cm |
  (.data // {} | to_entries[]) |
  select(.key | test("pass|secret|token|key|cred";"i")) |
  "\($cm.metadata.namespace)/\($cm.metadata.name) key=\(.key)"'
# A non-empty result is a finding. The fix is immediate: move it to a
# Secret, then rotate it, because it has been readable.

# 2. Are the Secrets actually encoded, or actually encrypted?
kubectl get secret -A -o json | jq -r '
  .items[] | . as $s |
  ($s.data // {} | keys[]) as $k |
  ($s.data[$k] | @base64d) as $v |
  select($v | test("^(postgres|mysql|redis)://[^:]+:[^@]+@";"i")) |
  "\($s.metadata.namespace)/\($s.metadata.name):\($k) CONTAINS INLINE CREDENTIALS"'
# A connection string with an embedded password is the specific finding,
# because it defeats every other control: the string is logged by the
# driver, printed in error messages, and visible in any crash dump.

# 3. Which pods can actually reach which secrets? The reachability audit
#    that a breach post-mortem always asks for and that nobody has ready.
kubectl get pods -n prod -o json | jq -r '
  .items[] | .metadata.name as $pod |
  (.spec.containers[]? | select(.env[]?.valueFrom.secretKeyRef != null)
   | .env[] | select(.valueFrom.secretKeyRef != null)
   | "\($pod) -> \(.valueFrom.secretKeyRef.name)/\(.valueFrom.secretKeyRef.key)")' \
  | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn
# Anything in the output that surprises you is a least-privilege finding:
# a pod that can read a secret it has no code path to use.

# 4. Is etcd itself encrypted and backed up, and is the backup encrypted?
#    Everything above assumes etcd is the trusted store. If the etcd
#    backup is an unencrypted file in an S3 bucket, every Secret in the
#    cluster is in that file, and the External Secret above is theatre.
kubectl -n kube-system get pod -l component=etcd \
  -o jsonpath='{.items[0].spec.containers[0].command}' | tr ' ' '\n' | grep -A1 peer
# Look for --cipher-suite and --cert-file / --key-file. Then verify the
# etcdctl snapshot is encrypted separately, and that its key is in a
# different system with different access controls.
ApproachEncryptionRotationGitOps friendlyBest for
Secret with base64✗ None; an encodingManual; env vars need a restart✗ Never commit plaintext✗ Non-secret values only
Sealed Secrets✓ One-time, asymmetricRe-seal and commit✓ Ciphertext in gitTeams wanting git as the source of truth
SOPS with age✓ Client-side, per-recipientRe-encrypt; per-person access control✓ Reviewable encrypted diffsSmall teams, mixed config and secret data
External Secrets Operator✓ In the external manager✓ Automatic, short-lived credentials✓ Reference-only in gitProduction; dynamic credentials
CSI Secret Store✓ Provider-side; mounted as a file✓ Provider-driven, with sync✓ Reference-onlyGKE, AKS, EKS native providers
Vault Agent injector✓ In Vault; in-memory or tmpfs✓ Vault lease management⚠ Sidecar is extra stateDynamic DB credentials at scale
Sealed key in an image✗ Reversible by anyone with the imageRebuild and repush✗✗ Never

A base64 value in a Secret mounted as an environment variable is not a secret, and the environment variable specifically is the worst possible delivery mechanism for one. Base64 is an encoding, so the value is readable by anyone with kubectl get secret, by anyone reading an etcd snapshot, and by anyone with shell access to a pod. Worse, an environment variable is inherited by every child process, is printed in full by most crash dumpers, appears in /proc/<pid>/environ to anything running as the same user, and is frequently logged verbatim by a crash reporter, an APM agent or a shell that runs set -x. Mount the secret as a file with mode 0400 and read the file, or use a projected volume with a short TTL so there is no long-lived value anywhere. And state the rotation procedure for each secret in the same repository as the secret, because the first execution of a rotation procedure is always the one that discovers it does not work.

Exercises 12

  1. Convert a real service to a multi-stage distroless build pinned by digest and measure the difference. Deliverable: the Dockerfile, before/after size and layer count, and cold-pull time from your registry on a fresh node.
  2. Prove reproducibility: build the same commit twice on two different machines with --no-cache and compare image digests. Deliverable: both digests and a written explanation of any difference plus the flag that fixes it.
  3. Audit your images for baked secrets using docker history and gitleaks over the exported tarball. Deliverable: the audit result and, if anything is found, the credential rotation you executed.
  4. Fix the request and limit mismatch on one deployment using measured p95 and p99.9 values, then verify the scheduling headroom with kubectl describe node. Deliverable: the before/after overcommit ratio and the measured p99 latency change over one week.
  5. Run the full secret-hygiene audit from the third code block against your cluster: credentials in ConfigMaps, inline credentials in connection strings, and per-pod secret reachability. Deliverable: the findings ranked by exposure, and the rotation you performed for each real credential found.

13 · Reliability: SLIs, Resiliency Patterns, Graceful Degradation and Chaos

13.1 SLIs, SLOs, SLAs and the error budget13.2 Circuit breakers and adaptive concurrency limits13.3 Bulkheads, queues and load shedding13.4 Rate limiting: token bucket, leaky bucket, sliding window13.5 Graceful degradation and fallback ladders13.6 Chaos engineering and multi-window multi-burn-rate alerting

Reliability is treated here as an engineering discipline with a budget rather than as an aspiration. The section defines the indicator, objective and agreement hierarchy and makes the error budget a release-governance mechanism rather than a report. The four load-management mechanisms are analysed individually — circuit breakers, bulkheads, rate limiting and shedding — and then as a composition, because the order in which they engage determines whether a system degrades or collapses. Graceful degradation is specified as a designed ladder of reduced-capability modes with explicit business triggers. Chaos engineering closes the section together with the alerting scheme that is actually compatible with an SLO: multi-window, multi-burn-rate, which pages on budget consumption rate rather than on symptom thresholds that fire during normal operation.

13.1 SLIs, SLOs, SLAs and the error budget

Defines the SLI, SLO and SLA hierarchy and establishes the error budget as the mechanism that converts a reliability target into a release policy.

The three terms are distinct, and conflating them causes measurable damage. An SLI is a measured quantity: "the proportion of valid requests to GET /v3/orders/{id} that returned a non-error status within 800 ms, measured at the edge, over a rolling 30-day window". An SLO is a target for an SLI over a window: 99.9% over 30 days. An SLA is a contractual commitment with financial consequence, normally looser than the SLO precisely so that engineering has headroom to fail over or degrade without immediately owing money. The critical discipline is measurement location: the SLI must be observed at a point that reflects the user’s experience — a load balancer in the user’s region, or a synthetic probe from outside — and not an in-cluster histogram, which misses DNS, TLS termination, edge proxy errors and the network between the user and the cluster.

Two SLI types carry most of the weight. Availability measures the proportion of requests producing a valid answer. Latency is treated as a first-class reliability indicator rather than a performance detail, and the correct formulation is the proportion of requests under a threshold — the good-events ratio from the SRE workbook — because that quantity is directly comparable to the objective. A third, under-used SLI is freshness: for a dashboard fed by a stream, or a search index, or a recommendation model, the age of the data is often the only thing the user perceives, and it is the SLI that will be breached first. Correctness is a fourth, and it is the one most often absent: a system returning wrong answers at 100% availability has an availability SLI of 100% and a reliability posture of zero.

# Prometheus 3.x: SLIs as first-class, queryable recording rules.
# "Good" = valid response AND within the latency threshold. Everything
# else is bad, including 5xx, timeouts, and requests dropped at the edge.

groups:
  - name: order-api-sli
    interval: 30s
    rules:
      # VALIDITY: non-5xx responses over all responses.
      - record: sli:order_api:valid:ratio_rate5m
        expr: |
          sum(rate(http_requests_total{
                service="order-api", route="/v3/orders/{id}",
                code!~"5.."
              }[5m]))
          /
          sum(rate(http_requests_total{
                service="order-api", route="/v3/orders/{id}"
              }[5m]))

      # LATENCY as a good-events ratio, not an average. Averages hide
      # exactly the behaviour the SLO exists to prevent.
      - record: sli:order_api:latency:good_rate5m
        expr: |
          sum(rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{
                service="order-api", route="/v3/orders/{id}", le="0.8"
              }[5m]))
          / sum(rate(http_request_duration_seconds_count{
                service="order-api", route="/v3/orders/{id}"
              }[5m]))

      # COMBINED: the SLO is on min(valid, fast). A response that is
      # valid but took 4 s is not a good event, and treating it as one
      # is the single most common SLI definition error.
      - record: sli:order_api:good:ratio_rate5m
        expr: |
          min(
            sli:order_api:valid:ratio_rate5m,
            sli:order_api:latency:good_rate5m
          )
        labels: { slo: order-api-availability, window: 5m }

      # FRESHNESS: the SLI that a synchronous dashboard actually has.
      - record: sli:dashboard:freshness_seconds
        expr: |
          time() - max(orders_projection_last_applied_timestamp_seconds)

# Burn rate: the rate at which the error budget is being consumed. This is
# the number an SLO-based alert is actually built on.
#
#   burn_rate = (1 - SLI) / (1 - SLO_objective)
#
# A burn rate of 1.0 exhausts the whole budget exactly at the end of the
# window. A burn rate of 14.4 exhausts 2% of a 30-day budget in 1 hour,
# which is the threshold used by the fastest-paging multi-window alert.

- record: sli:order_api:burn_rate5m
  expr: |
    (1 - sli:order_api:good:ratio_rate5m) / (1 - 0.999)

# Budget arithmetic, stated once, for the runbook:
#   30-day window, 99.9% SLO  -> error budget = 43.2 minutes of total
#                                unavailability per 30 days
#   28-day window, 99.9% SLO  -> 40.3 minutes
#   99.99%                    -> 4.32 minutes
#   99.95%                    -> 21.6 minutes
#   99.999%                   -> 25.9 SECONDS
#
#   14.4x burn for 1 h  consumes 2.0% of a 30-day budget  -> PAGE
#    6.0x burn for 6 h  consumes 1.2% of a 30-day budget  -> PAGE
#    3.0x burn for 1 d  consumes 1.0% of a 30-day budget  -> TICKET
#    1.0x burn for 3 d  consumes 0.5% of a 30-day budget  -> TICKET
#
#   At 99.99% (4.32 min/month), a single 10-minute incident consumes 23%
#   of the monthly budget. The arithmetic is why very high availability
#   targets require a different management style, not merely better code.

# The policy these numbers drive, in the same file so it cannot drift:
#   budget consumed < 25%   : normal release cadence, feature flags off
#   budget consumed 25-75%  : releases require an SRE reviewer
#   budget consumed 75-100% : feature freeze, reliability work only
#   budget consumed > 100%  : freeze, post-mortem required before release
SLO targetError budget, 30 dError budget, 28 dMax incidentMax deploys/month at 5% each
99.0%7 h 12 m6 h 43 m7 h~10
99.5%3 h 36 m3 h 22 m3 h 36 m~10
99.9%43.2 m40.3 m43.2 m~10
99.95%21.6 m20.2 m21.6 m~5
99.99%4.32 m4.03 m4.3 m~1
99.999%25.9 s24.2 s25 s~0.1

The SLO is not the SLA, and the error budget is not a target. Teams that set the SLO equal to the SLA leave no room to deploy, discover a regression and roll back without immediately breaching a contractual commitment. Teams whose measured availability sits permanently at 99.5% while their SLO says 99.9% have discovered that the SLO was fiction. The honest response to the second situation is to renegotiate the SLO to the value the system can actually hold, and then to treat the gap between the current value and the SLO as the funded reliability backlog, with an owner and a date. What must not happen is a target that is permanently unachievable, because the effect is that every engineer learns the SLO is decorative, and that learning is unrecoverable.

13.2 Circuit breakers and adaptive concurrency limits

Specifies the circuit-breaker state machine and its parameter derivation, and explains why adaptive concurrency limits are strictly better in most cases.

A circuit breaker converts a failing dependency from a per-request cost into a bounded, immediate cost, which is the only way the calling service can degrade rather than join the failure. The state machine has three states and two thresholds. Closed: requests flow and outcomes are counted. At a failure threshold over a rolling window, transition to Open: requests fail immediately without touching the dependency, for a configured interval. On expiry, transition to Half-Open and permit a small, fixed number of trial requests. If the trial succeeds, close; if it fails, return to open with a longer interval. The three properties that separate a correct implementation from a common broken one: the half-open probe must be a limited number of requests rather than a percentage of traffic, or the recovery flood is worse than the outage; the open interval should grow with each successive failure within a time window, because a dependency that failed once for 2 s and a dependency that has been down for an hour need very different recovery behaviour; and the failure criterion must measure recent behaviour, since a breaker driven by a 24-hour error rate never opens after a short incident.

The parameter derivation is where most implementations are wrong because the values are chosen rather than computed. The minimum number of requests before the breaker can trip should be large enough that the failure count is statistically meaningful — for a low-traffic endpoint, 10 failures over an hour is a rounding error, not a signal. The rolling window should be roughly ten times the expected inter-arrival time of requests, so that a real outage is not diluted by an hour of prior success. And the open duration should exceed the dependency’s p99.9 recovery time, with a ceiling below the caller’s own timeout budget, because a breaker that opens for longer than the caller’s deadline has no effect on latency — it is just a different error. The most common misconfiguration is an open duration shorter than the dependency’s typical transient failure, which produces a flapping breaker that converts a brief blip into repeated partial outages spread over an hour.

The more interesting development is adaptive concurrency limiting, which replaces the question "how many requests should I allow?" with "how many are completing successfully right now?". The algorithm maintains a minimum concurrency floor and a limit; the limit grows by 1 for each successful completion and halves on every failure or queueing event. A service with ten healthy dependencies converges to a stable limit just above its actual parallelism requirement, and any degradation reduces the limit geometrically within milliseconds — far faster than any fixed threshold can react. It is provably better than a fixed semaphore for protecting a saturated resource, and it removes the configuration problem entirely, which in practice matters more than the theoretical advantage. The operational caveat is that it must be measured in requests currently in flight including queueing, and it must be per dependency, not global.

// Circuit breaker state machine, with the three properties that separate a
// correct implementation from a common broken one. (Go 1.25.)
type State int32

const (
    StateClosed State = iota
    StateOpen
    StateHalfOpen
)

type Breaker struct {
    state        atomic.Int32
    // 1. The rolling window must be roughly 10x the inter-arrival time
    //    of requests. A 24-hour window never opens after a 2-minute
    //    incident; a 10-second window opens on noise.
    failures     atomic.Int64
    successes    atomic.Int64
    windowStart  atomic.Int64 // unix nanos

    // 2. minRequests gates the decision statistically. Without it, 2
    //    failures out of 3 requests opens the breaker.
    minRequests  int64
    failRatio    float64      // e.g. 0.5

    // 3. The open interval GROWS on repeated failure within a window.
    //    A dependency that blipped for 2 s and one that has been down
    //    for an hour need very different recovery behaviour.
    openUntil    atomic.Int64
    baseOpen     time.Duration
    maxOpen      time.Duration
    consecutive  atomic.Int32

    // Half-open permits a FIXED small number of probes, never a
    // percentage of traffic. A percentage floods the recovering
    // dependency and re-outages it.
    halfOpenProbes  atomic.Int32
    halfOpenAllowed int32
    halfOpenInFlight atomic.Int32
}

func (b *Breaker) Allow() error {
    switch State(b.state.Load()) {
    case StateOpen:
        if time.Now().UnixNano() < b.openUntil.Load() {
            return ErrCircuitOpen            // immediate, no network call
        }
        // Transition to half-open, once, via CAS. Multiple goroutines
        // must not each open their own probe window.
        if b.state.CompareAndSwap(int32(StateOpen), int32(StateHalfOpen)) {
            b.halfOpenInFlight.Store(0)
            b.halfOpenProbes.Store(0)
        }
        fallthrough
    case StateHalfOpen:
        if b.halfOpenInFlight.Add(1) <= b.halfOpenAllowed {
            return nil
        }
        return ErrCircuitOpen
    default:
        return nil
    }
}

func (b *Breaker) Record(err error) {
    if err == nil {
        b.successes.Add(1)
        if State(b.state.Load()) == StateHalfOpen {
            // A success closes it. One success, not a majority: the
            // dependency has already demonstrated it can serve.
            b.state.Store(int32(StateClosed))
            b.failures.Store(0)
            b.consecutive.Store(0)
        }
        return
    }
    b.failures.Add(1)
    if State(b.state.Load()) == StateHalfOpen {
        b.trip(true)                        // a failed probe re-opens
        return
    }
    b.rotateWindowIfStale()
    total := b.successes.Load() + b.failures.Load()
    if total >= b.minRequests &&
       float64(b.failures.Load())/float64(total) >= b.failRatio {
        b.trip(false)
    }
}

func (b *Breaker) trip(duringProbe bool) {
    d := b.baseOpen << min(b.consecutive.Add(1), 8)   // exponential
    if d > b.maxOpen || d <= 0 { d = b.maxOpen }
    b.openUntil.Store(time.Now().Add(d).UnixNano())
    b.state.Store(int32(StateOpen))
    b.successes.Store(0)
    b.failures.Store(0)
}

// Adaptive concurrency limiting: STRICTLY better than a fixed semaphore
// for protecting a saturated resource, and it needs no configuration.
// Converges to just above the dependency's real parallelism requirement;
// any degradation reduces the limit geometrically, in milliseconds.
type Limiter struct {
    limit        atomic.Int64
    inFlight     atomic.Int64
    minLimit     int64   // the floor: a small permanent allowance so a
                         // total stall can still probe for recovery
    lastUpdate   int64   // unix nanos, for the queue-time correction
}

func NewLimiter(minLimit int64) *Limiter {
    l := &Limiter{minLimit: minLimit}
    l.limit.Store(minLimit)
    return l
}

// Do runs fn if a slot is available. MUST be the only path to the
// dependency: a limit that does not cover all call sites measures
// nothing.
func (l *Limiter) Do(ctx context.Context, fn func(context.Context) error) error {
    current := l.limit.Load()
    next := l.inFlight.Add(1)
    // If we are above the limit, the request is QUEUED, which is a
    // signal of saturation just as much as a failure is.
    if next > current {
        l.decrease()
    }
    if next > current {
        l.inFlight.Add(-1)
        return ErrOverloaded          // shed immediately, do not wait
    }
    defer func() {
        l.inFlight.Add(-1)
        l.lastUpdate = time.Now().UnixNano()
    }()
    return fn(ctx)
}

// Called AFTER every successful completion. Growing by 1 per success is
// what makes the limit track the dependency's real capacity rather than
// a guessed number.
func (l *Limiter) Succeeded(wasQueued bool) {
    if wasQueued {
        // Queued requests show the limit was too low: grow.
        l.limit.Add(1)
        return
    }
    // Gradient heuristic: only grow if the previous round showed no
    // queueing, so the limit does not oscillate upward without evidence.
    if time.Since(time.Unix(0, atomic.LoadInt64(&l.lastUpdate))) <
       5*time.Millisecond {
        l.limit.Add(1)
    }
}

// Called on failure or on queueing. Halving is fast by design: the goal
// is to stop overwhelming a struggling dependency within milliseconds,
// not to reach a stable state gracefully.
func (l *Limiter) decrease() {
    next := l.limit.Load() / 2
    if next < l.minLimit {
        next = l.minLimit
    }
    l.limit.Store(next)
    metrics.ConcurrencyLimit.Set(float64(next))
}

// Convergence, stated: with N healthy dependencies of true capacity C,
// the limit rises to C + small headroom and stops. When one dependency
// degrades, the limit halves within one request, then again, until the
// caller's total in-flight across all dependencies is below the sum of
// the surviving capacities. Recovery is symmetric and automatic, and
// there is no threshold to misconfigure.

# Circuit-breaker and resilience configuration, in the three major stacks.
# Note what is DERIVED from measurement rather than chosen.

# --- resilience4j (JVM / .NET-adjacent Spring) ---
resilience4j.circuitbreaker.instances.inventory:
  slidingWindowType: COUNT_BASED
  slidingWindowSize: 100          # ~10x the inter-arrival time
  minimumNumberOfCalls: 20        # below this, never trip on noise
  failureRateThreshold: 50        # percent
  slowCallRateThreshold: 60       # a call over 800 ms counts as a failure
  slowCallDurationThreshold: 800ms
  waitDurationInOpenState: 10s    # > the dependency's p99.9 recovery
  permittedNumberOfCallsInHalfOpenState: 3   # FIXED count, not a percentage
  automaticTransitionFromOpenToHalfOpenEnabled: true
  maxWaitDurationInHalfOpenState: 30s
  registerHealthIndicator: true
  recordExceptions:
    - java.io.IOException
    - java.util.concurrent.TimeoutException
    # NOT RuntimeException broadly: a 400 Bad Request from a validation
    # error is not a dependency failure and must not trip the breaker.
  ignoreExceptions:
    - com.acme.BadRequestException
    - com.acme.NotFoundException

---
# --- Polly (.NET 10) ---
services.AddHttpClient("inventory", c => {
    c.BaseAddress = new Uri("http://inventory.prod");
    c.Timeout = TimeSpan.FromMilliseconds(800);   // > the breaker wait
    // Timeout MUST exceed waitDurationInOpenState, or the caller's own
    // deadline fires before the breaker can do anything useful.
}).AddStandardResilienceHandler(o => {
    o.Retry.MaxRetryAttempts = 2;                 // 3 attempts total
    o.Retry.BackoffType = DelayBackoffType.Exponential;
    o.Retry.Delay = TimeSpan.FromMilliseconds(50);
    o.CircuitBreaker.FailureRatio = 0.5;
    o.CircuitBreaker.SamplingDuration = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(10);
    o.CircuitBreaker.MinimumThroughput = 20;
    o.CircuitBreaker.BreakDuration = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(10);
    // Attempt TIMEOUTS must be strictly decreasing so the total fits the
    // caller's budget: 800, 500, 250 ms = 1.55 s, and the caller's own
    // deadline is 1.8 s.
    o.AttemptTimeout.Timeout = TimeSpan.FromMilliseconds(800);
    o.TotalRequestTimeout.Timeout = TimeSpan.FromMilliseconds(1800);
    o.Retry.ShouldHandle = new PredicateBuilder().Handle<TimeoutRejectedException>();
    // Retry ONLY timeouts and 503. Retrying a 400 or a 409 is
    // incorrect and, for a non-idempotent call, harmful.
});

---
# --- Resilience for Node 24 (native, no library) ---
const breaker = new CircuitBreaker({
  failureThreshold: 0.5, minRequests: 20, windowMs: 10_000,
  openMs: 10_000, halfOpenProbes: 3, backoffFactor: 2, maxOpenMs: 60_000,
  isFailure: (e) => e.code === 'ECONNREFUSED' ||
                     e.code === 'ETIMEDOUT'   ||
                     e.status >= 500,
  // Timeout and 5xx trip it. A 4xx is a client error and must not.
});
MechanismTriggerReaction timeConfigurationBest for
Fixed timeoutElapsed timeEqual to the timeoutOne number, derived from a percentileEvery call, always; it is not optional
Retry with backoffRetryable errorExponential; multiplies loadCount, backoff, retryable setIdempotent calls, transient faults only
Circuit breakerFailure ratio over a windowSeconds to open, then immediateFive numbers, all derivedHard dependency failure
Adaptive concurrency limitQueueing and failureMilliseconds, geometricOne minimum; nothing else✓ Preferred for saturating resources
Fixed semaphoreConcurrency countImmediate on excessOne guessed numberHard cap on a known-fixed resource
Queue with backpressureBuffer fullImmediate reject or blockCapacityAbsorbing bursts, never a stall
HedgingLatency above a percentileAfter the hedge delayPercentile, delay, multiplierTail latency on idempotent reads only

Derive the timeout budget from the latency SLO, and distribute it across the call chain before writing any code. If the edge SLO is 800 ms and a request makes four downstream calls in series, the total budget for all four plus overhead is 800 ms, so a uniform 800 ms timeout on each is wrong by a factor of four and guarantees that the caller’s own deadline fires before any of its dependencies' do. The common allocation is roughly 60/15/15/10 percent along the critical path, with each downstream receiving an absolute deadline passed down as context — because a deadline that is not propagated is a deadline that is not enforced. The measurable test is that p99.9 of total request time sits below the timeout, and that no single dependency’s p99.9 approaches the timeout it was given; a dependency sitting at 95% of its budget is a dependency whose next incident is a cascading timeout failure.

13.3 Bulkheads, queues and load shedding

Specifies bulkheads as resource partitioning and explains why load shedding must be designed as a priority system rather than a threshold.

A bulkhead partitions a shared resource so that one class of failure cannot consume the whole. The mechanism is usually a semaphore or a separate worker pool per partition, and the partition is usually by tenant, by request class, or by dependency. The pattern’s origin is naval architecture and the analogy is exact: a hull divided into watertight compartments means one breach floods one compartment rather than the vessel. In software the same reasoning applies to a thread pool, a connection pool, or a rate-limit bucket that one noisy tenant can exhaust. The critical property is that the partition must be a hard resource boundary, not a probabilistic one — a soft bulkhead that permits 10% overshoot is not a bulkhead when the failure mode is a 10× overshoot.

Load shedding is the policy decision layered on top: when total capacity is insufficient, which requests are refused? This must be a designed priority order and not a random rejection, because the two have completely different user outcomes. The order is always business-determined: checkout before browse, authenticated before anonymous, paid tier before free tier, writes that the user is waiting on before analytics. The mechanism has three viable forms and the choice matters. Rejection — return 503 with Retry-After — is the most honest and forces the client to slow down. Degradation — serve a reduced-quality answer, a cached value, or a partial result — preserves the success rate metric at the cost of correctness, which is appropriate only when the reduction is genuinely acceptable to the user. Queuing — admitting the request into a bounded buffer — converts refusal into latency, which is the right choice when the buffer is short and the user is waiting anyway, and catastrophically wrong when the buffer is long, because it converts a fast failure into a slow one and increases the number of in-flight requests competing for the same constrained resources.

The signal that triggers shedding is the most consequential decision and the most frequently wrong. Queue depth is a poor signal because it is bimodal. CPU utilisation is a poor signal because it lags. Concurrency in flight is a poor signal because it does not predict completion time. The signals that work are the ones tied to the user-visible consequence: queue age, in-flight requests divided by measured service rate as a predictor of queueing delay, and — for synchronous services — adaptive concurrency limit, which is a shedding mechanism in its own right. The most robust general design sheds on a combination: a hard ceiling that guarantees the system never queues unboundedly, and a priority-based admission decision in the region below it.

// Bulkheads as hard resource partitions. One exhausted compartment must
// not affect the others. (Go 1.25.)
type Bulkhead struct {
    sem       chan struct{}   // hard boundary; the capacity IS the limit
    queue     chan Request    // separate, small, bounded
    rejected  atomic.Int64
    queueFull atomic.Int64
}

func NewBulkhead(concurrency, queueDepth int) *Bulkhead {
    return &Bulkhead{
        sem:   make(chan struct{}, concurrency),
        queue: make(chan Request, queueDepth),
    }
}

// Admit implements the three-way policy: run, queue briefly, or refuse.
// NEVER block indefinitely: a request that blocks on a full bulkhead is a
// request holding a connection and a goroutine while making no progress.
func (b *Bulkhead) Admit(ctx context.Context, r Request) (ticket, error) {
    // 1. Immediate admission if there is room.
    select {
    case b.sem <- struct{}{}:
        return ticket{b}, nil
    default:
    }
    // 2. Brief queue. A SHORT queue converts a burst into a little
    //    latency; a LONG queue converts a fast failure into a slow one
    //    and multiplies in-flight requests, which is strictly worse.
    select {
    case b.queue <- r:
        b.queueFull.Add(1)
        select {
        case <-time.After(b.queueTimeout):   // typically 50-200 ms
            return ticket{}, ErrShed
        case r := <-b.queue:
            return b.tryAcquireWithRetry(r)
        case <-ctx.Done():
            return ticket{}, ctx.Err()
        }
    default:
        // 3. Refuse. 503 + Retry-After is the honest signal: it tells the
        //    client to slow down, which is what actually fixes a stampede.
        b.rejected.Add(1)
        return ticket{}, ErrShed
    }
}

func (b *Bulkhead) Release() { <-b.sem }

// The property that makes this a bulkhead rather than a queue: the
// semaphore is a HARD partition. A request holding a checkout ticket
// cannot be starved by 10,000 analytics requests, because the analytics
// bulkhead has its own semaphore of a different size entirely.

// Priority-based load shedding. The priority order is a BUSINESS decision
// and belongs in a table a product owner has signed, not in a code review.
var classPriority = []string{
    "checkout",        // 0: the user is spending money right now
    "order-status",    // 1: the user is waiting on something they did
    "auth",            // 2: everything else requires this to work
    "search",          // 3: valuable, and there are alternatives
    "browse",          // 4: valuable, and the page can cache
    "recommendations", // 5: degradable to a static list
    "analytics",       // 6: never user-facing; shed first, always
}

type SheddingDecision struct {
    Admit   bool
    Class   string
    Reason  string
    Load    float64   // the signal value that drove the decision
}

// Compute is pure so it is exhaustively testable. Load is a normalised
// 0..1 saturation signal; the thresholds are per-class so a low-value
// class sheds long before a high-value one.
func Compute(class string, load float64, inflight, capacity int) SheddingDecision {
    p := indexOf(classPriority, class)
    if p < 0 {
        return SheddingDecision{Admit: false, Reason: "unknown class"}
    }
    // Each class has its own threshold: analytics sheds at 40% load,
    // checkout sheds at 95%, and in between the intermediate classes
    // shed in order. A single global threshold cannot express this,
    // because it cannot shed analytics while admitting checkout.
    threshold := 0.40 + 0.55*float64(p)/float64(len(classPriority)-1)
    if load > threshold {
        return SheddingDecision{Admit: false, Class: class,
            Reason: "class over its saturation threshold", Load: load}
    }
    if inflight > capacity {
        return SheddingDecision{Admit: false, Class: class,
            Reason: "no admission capacity remaining", Load: load}
    }
    return SheddingDecision{Admit: true, Class: class, Load: load}
}

// The load signal itself. In-flight divided by CONCURRENT CAPACITY, not
// by request rate: this predicts whether the NEXT request will queue,
// which is what determines whether the user waits. Request rate as a
// signal lags by exactly the amount of time you need to react.
func Saturation(inflight, concurrencyLimit int) float64 {
    if concurrencyLimit <= 0 { return 1.0 }
    return float64(inflight) / float64(concurrencyLimit)
}

# Load shedding in Envoy 1.34, which is where the edge should shed.
# Local rate limiting in the proxy is faster than shedding in the
# application: it costs no application resources, which is the resource
# you are short of.
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1
kind: EnvoyFilter
metadata:
  name: edge-load-shed
  namespace: istio-system
spec:
  configPatches:
    - applyTo: HTTP_FILTER
      match:
        context: GATEWAY
        listener:
          filterChain:
            filter:
              name: envoy.filters.network.http_connection_manager
      patch:
        operation: INSERT_BEFORE
        value:
          name: envoy.filters.http.local_ratelimit
          typed_config:
            "@type": type.googleapis.com/envoy.extensions.filters.http.local_ratelimit.v3.LocalRateLimit
            stat_prefix: http_local_rate_limiter
            token_bucket:
              max_tokens: 100          # burst allowance
              tokens_per_fill: 100
              fill_interval: 1s
            filter_enabled:
              runtime_fraction:
                default_value:         # shed ONLY under pressure
                  numerator: 0          # 0% by default: NO shed at all
                  denominator: 100
            filter_enforced:
              runtime_fraction:
                default_value:         # once enforced, reject 100%
                  numerator: 100
                  denominator: 100
            # The whole mechanism: when the upstream's adaptive
            # concurrency limit drops below its normal value, the proxy
            # starts shedding. It is the load signal, taken from the
            # component that actually knows the capacity.
            local_rate_limit_per_downstream_connection: false
---
# Chaos experiments: shed deliberately and measure the blast radius.
# This is the experiment that validates the priority table above.
- name: shed-analytics-under-load
  weight: 1
  targets: { namespace: prod }
  duration: 10m
  steadyState: 5m
  faults:
    - fault: "podAnnotations"
      target:
        selector:
          app: edge-gateway
      actions:
        - type: updateEnv
          env:
            - name: SHED_THRESHOLD_OVERRIDE
              value: "0.40"
# Steady state: checkout p99 < 250 ms. Chaos: checkout p99 < 300 ms
# while analytics throughput drops to zero. Pass criteria: the checkout
# SLO holds throughout. A failure here means the priority table is wrong
# and the shedding signal is too late.
Isolation techniqueGranularityOverheadProtects againstLimitation
Bulkhead: separate poolPer class or dependencyLowStarvation across classesTotal capacity is the sum; a common dependency is still shared
Bulkhead: separate queuePer classLowA slow consumer blocking othersNeeds a shed policy; queues can starve each other
Rate limit per tenantPer tenantVery lowOne tenant consuming all capacity✗ Does not bound a single large tenant’s burst
Connection pool capPer dependencyNoneExhausting a downstream connection poolPool too small causes its own timeouts
Adaptive concurrency limitPer dependencyVery lowSaturating a struggling dependencyMust cover all call sites to be valid
Cell-based isolationPer tenant cellHigh, N-fold infraTotal tenant isolation, blast radiusCross-cell operations need a saga
Thread pool isolationPer task typeLowOne slow task type blocking allPool sizing is itself a guessing problem

Queueing under load is not graceful degradation; it is a slower outage with a worse failure mode. When a service is at capacity, admitted requests queue, queueing delay grows without bound, every queued request holds a connection, a goroutine and a memory slot, and the effective capacity of the system falls as the queue grows because context switches, cache eviction and GC pressure all worsen. The result is that a service that would have shed 30% of traffic and stayed healthy instead accepts 100% and falls over, and the recovery is slower because the queue has to drain before the system can stabilise. The rule is that a queue may absorb a burst measured in seconds and must be bounded by depth and by age; anything longer belongs in an explicit asynchronous pipeline with its own durability, not in the request path. The corollary for clients: return 503 with Retry-After rather than accepting work you cannot complete, because an honest fast failure is the mechanism that lets the client back off and the system recover.

13.4 Rate limiting: token bucket, leaky bucket, sliding window

Compares the three rate-limiting algorithms on their burst and smoothing properties, and specifies the two-limit configuration that most production systems actually require.

The three algorithms differ in a single property each, and choosing between them is choosing which behaviour is acceptable. Token bucket refills at a constant rate into a bucket of bounded capacity, and each request consumes one token. If tokens are available, the request proceeds; the bucket therefore permits a burst up to its capacity and enforces an average rate equal to the refill rate. This is the correct default for protecting a service from a client that needs bursts: the refill rate is the sustained limit and the capacity is the burst allowance, and the two are independent. Leaky bucket drains at a constant rate and produces a smooth, constant output rate, discarding or queueing anything that arrives faster. It is the right choice when the downstream genuinely cannot tolerate bursts even briefly — a batch window, a rate-limited third-party API with a per-second hard ceiling, a metering system. The cost is added latency for every request, because the leak introduces a queue by construction. Sliding window counts requests in a rolling interval and is the algorithm to use when the limit is contractual and expressed as "N per hour" — a billing quota, an API plan, a fair-use policy. Logarithmic bucketing is the efficient implementation: rather than a fixed window, which permits 2N across a window boundary, it keeps N logarithmic sub-buckets per window and weights each by how much of it falls in the current interval, giving a true sliding window with bounded memory.

The configuration almost every system needs is two limits, not one. A sustained limit over a long window establishes the contractual rate, and a burst limit over a short window establishes the tolerance for legitimate spiky behaviour. A single limit cannot express both: a limit of 1,000 per minute either permits a 1,000-request instant — which will break a downstream with a 100 req/s ceiling — or permits only 16.7 per second, which will break a legitimate client that needs to batch. With both configured, the client can burst to 1,000 in a second and is held to 1,000 per minute over the long run, and the two together are exactly the guarantee the downstream needs. The remaining decisions are scope, which is always the one that causes arguments: per user, per API key, per IP, per tenant, or some combination. Per IP is wrong for any mobile client behind carrier-grade NAT, where thousands of users share one address; per API key is wrong for a service with anonymous traffic. The correct default is per authenticated identity, with per-IP as an additional limit for unauthenticated endpoints specifically.

// Token bucket with a monotonic clock and no locking on the hot path.
// (Go 1.25.) Correctness depends on using a monotonic clock: a wall
// clock that steps backwards refills negative tokens.
type TokenBucket struct {
    mu         sync.Mutex
    capacity   float64     // burst allowance
    tokens     float64
    refillRate float64     // tokens per second = the sustained rate
    last       time.Time   // from time.Now(), which is MONOTONIC
    now        func() time.Time // injected, so the tests are deterministic
}

func NewTokenBucket(ratePerSec, burst float64) *TokenBucket {
    return &TokenBucket{
        capacity:   burst,
        tokens:     burst,      // start full: a cold start that rejects
        refillRate: ratePerSec, // the first request is unfair
        last:       time.Now(),
        now:        time.Now,
    }
}

// Allow consumes one token. refill MUST be capped at capacity: without
// the cap, an hour of idleness produces a bucket of 3,600 tokens and the
// next request triggers a minute-long thundering herd.
func (b *TokenBucket) Allow() (bool, time.Duration) {
    b.mu.Lock()
    defer b.mu.Unlock()
    now := b.now()
    elapsed := now.Sub(b.last).Seconds()
    b.last = now
    b.tokens += elapsed * b.refillRate
    if b.tokens > b.capacity {
        b.tokens = b.capacity      // cap: this is the anti-herd property
    }
    if b.tokens >= 1 {
        b.tokens--
        return true, 0
    }
    deficit := 1 - b.tokens
    return false, time.Duration(deficit / b.refillRate * float64(time.Second))
}

// The refill rate is the SUSTAINED limit; the capacity is the BURST
// limit. They are independent, and conflating them is the whole design
// mistake:
//   1000 req/min sustained, burst 100:
//     long window: 1000 per minute, enforced by a second, longer bucket
//     short window: 100 instant, so a legitimate client may batch
//   1000 req/min with burst 1000:
//     a client may send 1000 requests in ONE MILLISECOND, which will
//     break any downstream with a per-second ceiling.

// Sliding window with logarithmic buckets: a TRUE sliding window in
// O(log n) memory, rather than the fixed window that permits 2N across a
// boundary. The fixed-window problem, made concrete:
//
//   Fixed window, limit 100/minute, window boundary at 12:00:00
//   11:59:59  ->  100 requests admitted   (window 11:59)
//   12:00:00  ->  100 requests admitted   (window 12:00)
//   Total in 2 seconds: 200. The limit was 100/minute.
//   A client can trivially double its rate by aligning to the boundary,
//   and the boundary is publicly discoverable.
//
// Logarithmic sliding window: 10 sub-buckets per window, each covering a
// geometrically shrinking slice. The current slice is estimated, older
// slices are exact. Boundary straddling is approximated to within
// 1/10th of the window, which is more than accurate enough and removes
// the exploit entirely.
type SlidingWindowLimiter struct {
    mu       sync.Mutex
    limit    int
    window   time.Duration
    sub      int              // 10
    buckets  []int            // counts, oldest first
    subWidth time.Duration
    start    time.Time
    now      func() time.Time
}

func (l *SlidingWindowLimiter) Allow() bool {
    l.mu.Lock()
    defer l.mu.Unlock()
    now := l.now()
    l.rotate(now)
    if l.total() >= l.limit {
        return false
    }
    l.buckets[len(l.buckets)-1]++
    return true
}

func (l *SlidingWindowLimiter) total() int {
    sum := 0
    for _, b := range l.buckets {
        sum += b
    }
    return sum
}

// Weights, applied on read: a bucket that only partly overlaps the
// current window contributes proportionally. This is what makes it
// SLIDING rather than fixed.
var weight = []float64{0.001, 0.002, 0.004, 0.008, 0.016,
                       0.032, 0.064, 0.128, 0.256, 0.489}

# Distributed rate limiting. The hard part is not the algorithm; it is that
# a per-pod in-memory limiter permits N x pods of total rate, which is
# wrong by exactly the replica count and fails on every scale event.

# ---- Option A: per-pod limiters with a CONSISTENT HASH (recommended) ----
# Divide the global limit by the replica count and hash the identity to
# a fixed shard. Deterministic: the same key always lands on the same
# shard, and the total is the configured global limit regardless of
# scale. The cost: uneven hash distribution gives +/-10% error per key,
# and a scale event redistributes every key, briefly allowing a burst.
global_limit = 10_000
replicas     = 10
per_shard    = global_limit / replicas
# In the limiter, shard = fnv1a(identity) % replicas, and use per_shard
# as the refill rate. Total across the fleet is 10,000 by construction,
# not by luck.

# ---- Option B: Redis-backed (correct global, adds a hop) ----
# The token bucket state lives in Redis with a Lua script so the
# read-modify-write is atomic. One extra RTT per check, and Redis becomes
# a hard dependency on the rate limiter itself.
EVAL "local rate limit" 1 tenant:acme 100 10
-- KEYS[1]  = bucket key
-- ARGV[1]  = capacity (burst)
-- ARGV[2]  = refill rate, tokens per second
local key       = KEYS[1]
local capacity  = tonumber(ARGV[1])
local rate      = tonumber(ARGV[2])
local data      = redis.call('HMGET', key, 'tokens', 'ts')
local tokens    = tonumber(data[1]) or capacity
local ts        = tonumber(data[2]) or 0
local now       = tonumber(ARGV[3])
-- Monotonic via Redis TIME, not the caller's clock: the caller's clock
-- is irrelevant to a distributed limiter, and using it makes the
-- limiter's correctness depend on N host clocks being in agreement.
now = tonumber(redis.call('TIME')[1])
tokens = math.min(capacity, tokens + (now - ts) * rate)
local allowed = 0
if tokens >= 1 then tokens = tokens - 1; allowed = 1 end
redis.call('HMSET', key, 'tokens', tokens, 'ts', now)
redis.call('EXPIRE', key, math.ceil(capacity / rate) + 60)
return { allowed, math.floor(tokens) }

# Redis 8.x Cluster: the EVAL must declare the key hash-tag so the
# script and its single key land on the same slot:
#   EVAL "... KEYS[1] = 'rl:{acme}' ..." -- KEYS[1] must contain {acme}
# Cross-slot failure here is silent until the key distribution changes.

# ---- Envoy 1.34 global rate limiting: the correct place to do it ----
# A descriptor-based global limit with Redis as the descriptor backend.
# The edge sheds before the request consumes an application thread, which
# is the resource that is actually scarce.
- type: ratelimit
  config:
    domain: order-api
    rate_limit_service:
      grpc_service:
        envoy_grpc:
          cluster_name: ratelimit
      timeout: 0.05s        # 50 ms: the rate limiter itself is a
                            # dependency and it MUST have a short
                            # timeout, or a rate limiter outage becomes
                            # an availability outage
    failure_mode_deny: false   # FAIL OPEN on limiter error. Denying all
                               # traffic because the limiter is down
                               # converts a small problem into a total one.
AlgorithmBurst behaviourSmoothnessMemory per keyCorrect for
Token bucket✓ Up to capacity, then steadyBursty by designTwo numbers✓ Default for service protection
Leaky bucket✗ None; queue or drop✓ Perfectly smooth outputQueueDownstream with a hard per-second ceiling
Fixed window✗ 2N across a boundarySteppedOne counter✗ Exploitable; never for a hard limit
Sliding window, log✓ Bounded by the windowSmoothO(log n)✓ Contractual "N per hour" quotas
Sliding window, exact✓ BoundedSmoothO(n) per requestLow-rate, high-precision quotas only
GCRA✓ Configurable toleranceSmoothOne timestampAPI gateways; smooths at low memory
Concurrent request limit✗ Bounds in-flight, not rateAdaptiveOne number✓ Protecting a saturating dependency

The rate limiter itself must be a dependency you have thought about, because it is on the critical path of every request and its failure mode is usually wrong. A rate limiter that fails closed converts its own outage into a total availability outage; a rate limiter that fails open exposes the downstream to the very traffic it exists to prevent. The correct default is fail open for the service’s own protection — the alternative turns a 5% availability loss into a 100% one — and fail closed for anything that is a billing or security control, where the correct trade is refusing service. Two further details are routinely missed and both cause incidents: the limiter’s own timeout must be very short, of the order of 10–50 ms, because a rate limiter with a 500 ms timeout adds 500 ms to every request when it is slow; and the response must always carry the rate-limit headers, including on successful responses, so a well-behaved client can pace itself before it is throttled rather than after.

13.5 Graceful degradation and fallback ladders

Specifies degradation as a designed ladder of reduced-capability modes with business triggers, and distinguishes the fallback techniques that work from those that fail silently.

Graceful degradation is the practice of defining, in advance, an ordered set of reduced-capability modes and the business conditions that trigger each one. The defining property is that it is designed: every mode is specified, tested and observable before it is needed, and the trigger is a signal a human can reason about. A system that degrades because an engineer added a fallback during an incident is not degrading gracefully; it is failing with extra steps and an untested code path, and it will frequently fail worse than the controlled outage it was meant to prevent. The ladder must be written down with a named owner per rung, because the decisions about which capability to drop are business decisions and the person making them at 03:00 needs to know which revenue they are trading for which.

The fallback techniques divide into those that are visible and those that are not, and the invisible ones are more dangerous. Cached fallback — serving a slightly stale cached value — is usually acceptable and must be surfaced to the user, because a search result from four hours ago presented as current is a correctness problem that surfaces as a support ticket rather than as a design decision. Partial response — omitting an expensive field from a response while returning the rest — is the highest-value technique for a page-composition API and is the one most often left unimplemented because it requires every field to be independently degradable, which is a design constraint that must be present from the start. Default or synthetic value is legitimate only when the field is genuinely optional to the decision the user is making, and it is a lie in every other case. Approximation — a cheaper algorithm giving a close answer — is the strongest technique available and the least used: a precomputed recommendation list instead of a real-time one, a sampled count instead of an exact one, a region-level rather than a store-level stock figure.

Two failure modes deserve specific warning. The first is the fallback that is slower than the thing it replaces: a cache lookup that performs a database miss, a default that is computed by an external call, a "simplified" path that turns out to require the same dependency. The fallback must be genuinely cheaper and genuinely more available, and the only way to know is to measure it under the same conditions. The second is the stale cache without a bound: a fallback that serves data from an unbounded-age cache will, during a long outage, serve data from before the last deployment, which may be actively wrong. Every fallback must have a maximum age past which it returns an error rather than a stale answer, and that maximum must be a business decision, not a default.

# The degradation ladder, as configuration. Every rung has a trigger, a
# consequence, and an owner. This file is reviewed by product, not only
# by engineering: rung 3 trades revenue for availability and that is a
# business decision.
degradation:
  - level: 0
    name: full
    trigger: { load: 0.00 }
    actions: []
    owner: none
  - level: 1
    name: drop-analytics
    trigger: { load: 0.40, window: 2m }
    actions:
      - reject: { class: analytics, status: 503, retry_after: 60 }
      - disable: { feature: event_emission }
    revenue_impact: none
    user_impact: none
    owner: sre-oncall
  - level: 2
    name: serve-stale-recommendations
    trigger: { signal: model_unavailable, window: 1m }
    actions:
      - fallback: { recommendations: { source: cache, max_age: 6h } }
      - fallback: { search_suggest: { source: static_index, max_age: 24h } }
    revenue_impact: -3% conversion, estimated
    user_impact: "Recommendations may be up to 6 hours old"
    # The UI MUST show this. An invisible stale fallback is a bug.
    ui_banner: "Showing saved recommendations"
    owner: product-owns
  - level: 3
    name: partial-order-api
    trigger: { signal: inventory_unavailable, window: 30s }
    actions:
      - degrade_field: { orders.detail: omit }        # partial response
      - degrade_field: { orders.availability: approximate }
      - fallback: { availability: { source: region_aggregate, max_age: 15m } }
    revenue_impact: -0.4% conversion, plus a support load increase
    user_impact: "Availability shown is region-level, not store-level"
    owner: product-owns
  - level: 4
    name: checkout-degraded
    trigger: { signal: payment_provider_degraded, window: 10s }
    actions:
      - queue: { payments: { capacity: 5000, max_age: 5m } }
        # NOT a rejection: a payment is worth holding briefly. The
        # queue is bounded by AGE, so it cannot become a stale-data
        # problem after 5 minutes.
      - fallback: { payment_methods: { source: static_list } }
      - reject: { class: analytics, recommendations }
    revenue_impact: +0.2% (queueing preserves more orders than rejecting)
    user_impact: "Payment confirmation may take up to 60 seconds"
    owner: product-owns
  - level: 5
    name: maintenance
    trigger: { signal: corruption_risk, window: 0 }
    actions:
      - reject_all: { status: 503, retry_after: 900, maintenance_page: true }
    revenue_impact: total, for the duration
    user_impact: "Service unavailable"
    # The last rung, and the one that must be written down explicitly,
    # because a system with no designed full outage invents one during
    # an incident, badly, under time pressure.
    owner: vp-engineering

// Fallbacks with a BOUNDED maximum age. The bound is the whole point:
// a fallback that serves arbitrarily old data will, in a long outage,
// serve data from before the last deployment, which is not a degraded
// answer but a wrong one. (Go 1.25.)
type Fallback[T any] struct {
    maxAge  time.Duration
    load    func(ctx context.Context) (T, error)
    metrics prometheus.Counter
}

func (f *Fallback[T]) Get(ctx context.Context) (T, error) {
    v, age, ok := f.cache.Peek()
    if ok && age <= f.maxAge {
        metrics.FallbackHits.Inc()          // within bound: a real fallback
        return v, nil
    }
    if ok && age > f.maxAge {
        // STALE BEYOND BOUND: refuse rather than lie. The caller gets
        // an honest 503, which is degraded but not incorrect.
        metrics.FallbackExpired.Inc()       // this counter is the alert:
                                           // a rising value means the
                                           // outage is now damaging users
        var zero T
        return zero, ErrFallbackExpired
    }
    return f.load(ctx)                      // no cached value at all
}

// The measurement that distinguishes a real fallback from a lie: run the
// degradation path under load and compare against the primary.
func measureFallbackCost() {
    // NOT: primary 45 ms, fallback 40 ms  -> "the fallback is free"
    // ACTUALLY, with the cache cold and the primary degraded:
    //     primary: 8,000 ms (timing out, then retrying)
    //     fallback: 6 ms from cache
    // The fallback is 1,300x faster in the condition where it is used,
    // and roughly equal in the condition where it is not. A fallback
    // that is only evaluated under normal conditions has not been
    // evaluated at all.
}

// Three fallback properties to verify before shipping, each of which has
// been a real incident:
//   1. It is CHEAPER than the primary. Measure both, under the failure
//      condition, not under normal operation.
//   2. It has a MAXIMUM AGE, and returns an error past it.
//   3. It does not depend on the thing that failed. A fallback that
//      reads the same database is not a fallback; it is a slower query.

# Partial responses: the highest-value degradation technique and the one
# most often skipped, because it must be designed in from the start.
# Every field in a composite response must be independently degradable,
# which means every field must declare: (a) can this be omitted, (b)
# what is the cheapest acceptable substitute, (c) how old may the
# substitute be.
apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: HTTPRoute
metadata: { name: order-page, namespace: prod }
spec:
  rules:
    - matches: [{ path: { type: PathPrefix, value: /v3/orders/page } }]
      backendRefs: [{ name: order-page, port: 8080 }]
---
# Server side: field-level degradation with an explicit signal to the UI.
# (Go 1.25.)
func OrderPage(ctx context.Context, req Request) (Response, error) {
    resp := Response{Order: coreOrder(req.OrderID)}  // always present

    // Each optional field is resolved with its own deadline and its own
    // fallback. A slow dependency degrades ONE field, not the page.
    type field struct {
        name string
        fn   func(context.Context) (any, error)
        fb   func() (any, error)
        crit bool   // critical fields do not have a fallback and DO
                    // fail the whole response: correctness over liveness
    }
    fields := []field{
        {"customer", fetchCustomer, func() (any, error) { return nil, nil }, true},
        {"lines",    fetchLines,    func() (any, error) { return nil, nil }, true},
        {"availability", fetchStoreStock,
         func() (any, error) { return regionApprox(), nil },   false},
        {"recommendations", fetchRecs,
         func() (any, error) { return cachedRecs(6 * time.Hour), nil }, false},
        {"delivery_estimate", fetchCarrierRates,
         func() (any, error) { return staticRates(), nil },     false},
    }

    var wg sync.WaitGroup
    for i := range fields {
        wg.Add(1)
        go func(f *field, i int) {
            defer wg.Done()
            // Per-field deadline, deliberately SHORTER than the request
            // budget: a field that would consume the whole budget is
            // better omitted than slow.
            fctx, cancel := context.WithTimeout(ctx, 120*time.Millisecond)
            defer cancel()
            v, err := f.fn(fctx)
            if err != nil {
                if f.crit {
                    resp.CriticalFailure = f.name   // fail the response
                    return
                }
                v, err = f.fb()                    // degrade this field only
                resp.Degraded = append(resp.Degraded, f.name)
            }
            resp.setField(f.name, v)
        }(&fields[i], i)
    }
    wg.Wait()

    if resp.CriticalFailure != "" {
        return resp, fmt.Errorf("critical field %s unavailable",
                                 resp.CriticalFailure)
    }
    // The UI MUST consume this. A degraded response that the client
    // cannot distinguish from a complete one is a silent correctness bug
    // that surfaces as user reports weeks later.
    if len(resp.Degraded) > 0 {
        resp.Headers = append(resp.Headers,
            "X-Degraded-Fields: "+strings.Join(resp.Degraded, ","),
            "Warning: 199 acme.com \"Degraded response\"")
    }
    return resp, nil
}
Fallback techniqueFreshness boundUser-visible?RiskCorrect for
Cached valueMust be explicit; 5 min–6 h✓ Must be shownStale answer presented as currentTolerable staleness: catalogue, promos
Partial responsen/a, field simply absent✓ Layout must adaptClient assumes presence✓ Page composition, detail views
ApproximationBound by the aggregate’s periodUsually not, but should beUnderstates or overstates scarcityStock, ranking, demand forecasts
Precomputed valueHours; bounded explicitly✓ Should be shownPopular content never refreshesRecommendations, trending
Default or syntheticn/a✗ Invisible✗ Fabrication; a correctness lieOnly if genuinely optional to the decision
Queue and waitHard max age, 1–5 min✓ Latency is visibleBecomes a slow outage if unboundedShort bursts; high-value operations
Reject with 503n/a✓ ExplicitClient may not honour Retry-After✓ Honest refusal; lets clients back off

The fallback that is slower than the primary is the most common form of graceful degradation that makes an outage worse. It happens when the "simplified path" quietly acquires the dependency it was meant to avoid — a cached lookup that misses and reads the database, a default that is computed by an external call, a "lightweight" renderer that still loads the full customer graph. The service then serves a 4-second response where it would have served a 40 ms error, which is catastrophic because every thread and connection is still consumed. The verification is mechanical and must be done before shipping: measure the fallback path’s latency and its dependency set under the failure condition, not under normal operation, and assert in a test that the fallback does not reference the failing dependency at all. The second failure mode is the unbounded-staleness fallback, which in a long outage serves data from before the last deploy — at which point the system is not degrading, it is confidently wrong, and the correct behaviour is to refuse.

13.6 Chaos engineering and multi-window multi-burn-rate alerting

Specifies chaos practice with named tools, and defines the alerting scheme that is mathematically compatible with an SLO rather than with a symptom threshold.

Chaos engineering is the controlled injection of faults to verify that the system’s designed defences actually engage. The discipline, as practised by the teams that do it well, is not "break things and see" but hypothesis-driven: state a specific expectation about the system’s behaviour under a specific fault, encode it as an assertion, inject the fault, and treat a violated assertion as a defect with the same priority as a failing test. The steady-state hypothesis is the safety net — a continuously running measurement of a normal behaviour, such as p99 latency or order completion rate, which is compared against steady state throughout the experiment and aborts it automatically if the system leaves the expected envelope. Without a steady-state definition, a chaos experiment is an outage that someone chose to cause, and the practice becomes a source of risk rather than of knowledge.

The tooling has converged. Chaos Mesh is a Kubernetes-native platform with a large fault catalogue — pod kill, pod failure, network delay, packet loss, DNS, CPU stress, disk pressure, and time skew — and declarative Chaos resources that can be scoped precisely and rate-limited. LitmusChaos is also Kubernetes-native and is the tool of choice for chaos that spans multiple clusters or that must be driven by an external approval workflow, which is often a requirement in regulated environments. Gremlin is a commercial platform with a polished UI and strong host-level and network-level capabilities, which is what makes it usable by teams without Kubernetes depth. toxiproxy is the lightweight option for network conditions specifically and is the right choice when the requirement is "simulate 200 ms of latency and 5% packet loss" and not a platform. The selection criterion is not the fault catalogue; it is whether the tool can scope an experiment to one pod in a 400-pod production cluster and abort automatically on a steady-state violation.

The alerting scheme is where most SLO programmes fail, and the reason is specific: a threshold-based alert on a symptom fires constantly during normal operation and is muted within a month, and a threshold-based alert on an average never fires at all. Multi-window, multi-burn-rate alerting resolves both. The idea is to compute the burn rate — the rate at which the error budget is being consumed relative to the SLO — over two windows, a short one and a long one, and to page only when both exceed a threshold. A fast burn over a long window is a real, sustained problem; a fast burn over a short window is a spike that is either already over or about to be. The short window provides detection speed and the long window provides confidence, and requiring both eliminates the false positives that cause alert fatigue without adding detection latency. The window pairs and thresholds follow a published table, and the derivation is a short calculation from the desired error budget consumed.

# Multi-window, multi-burn-rate alerting, in full. The two windows per
# alert are the mechanism: a fast burn confirmed over a long window.
#
#   burn_rate = (1 - SLI) / (1 - SLO)
#   burn_rate 1.0 exhausts the budget exactly at the end of the window.
#
# Window pair derivation for a 30-day, 99.9% SLO:
#   A short window of 1 h at 14.4x consumes 2% of the monthly budget.
#   The long window must be long enough that 2% of it is significant:
#   at 14.4x, 14.4 * 6h/720h = 12% ... so use 14.4x for 1 h AND 6 h.
