#   The pair means: a real outage is caught in about 5 minutes (the
#   short window's evaluation period), but a transient that recovers
#   within 1 hour does not page anyone.
groups:
  - name: order-api-slo-burn
    interval: 30s
    rules:
      - record: sli:order_api:burn1h
        expr: (1 - avg_over_time(sli:order_api:good:ratio_rate5m[1h])) / 0.001
      - record: sli:order_api:burn6h
        expr: (1 - avg_over_time(sli:order_api:good:ratio_rate5m[6h])) / 0.001
      - record: sli:order_api:burn3d
        expr: (1 - avg_over_time(sli:order_api:good:ratio_rate5m[3d])) / 0.001

      - alert: OrderApiBudgetBurnCritical
        expr: |
          sli:order_api:burn1h > 14.4
            and
          sli:order_api:burn6h > 14.4
        for: 2m
        labels: { severity: page, slo: order-api-availability }
        annotations:
          summary: "SLO budget burning 14.4x; 2% of the 30-day budget per hour"
          runbook_url: https://runbooks/order-api-budget-burn
          # The ACTION belongs in the alert, because the alert is read
          # at minute 1 and the runbook at minute 4.
          immediate_action: |
            1. Check order_api_good_ratio for a code-class breakdown.
            2. If a deploy is in progress, roll back NOW; do not investigate.
            3. If a dependency is failing, engage the circuit breaker
               for it and confirm the breaker is OPEN in the metrics.
            4. If neither, page the on-call SRE and freeze releases.
          budget_consumed: "2% per hour of a 43.2 minute monthly budget"

      - alert: OrderApiBudgetBurnFast
        expr: |
          sli:order_api:burn30m > 6
            and
          sli:order_api:burn2h > 6
        for: 5m
        labels: { severity: page, slo: order-api-availability }
        annotations:
          summary: "SLO budget burning 6x; 5% consumed in 6 hours"

      - alert: OrderApiBudgetBurnSlow
        expr: |
          sli:order_api:burn6h > 3
            and
          sli:order_api:burn1d > 3
        for: 30m
        labels: { severity: ticket, slo: order-api-availability }
        # TICKET, not page: 3x sustained for a day is a real problem and
        # not an emergency. Paging on it trains the team to ignore pages.
        annotations:
          summary: "SLO budget burning 3x for over a day; reliability work required"
          policy: "Under 25% budget consumed: normal. Over 75%: freeze."

      - alert: OrderApiBudgetExhausted
        expr: |
          sli:order_api:burn3d > 1
            and
          sli:order_api:burn5d > 1
        for: 2h
        labels: { severity: ticket, slo: order-api-availability }
        annotations:
          summary: "Error budget exhausted; post-mortem required before next release"

# Chaos Mesh: a hypothesis-driven experiment with automatic abort.
# The steadyState block is what makes this an experiment rather than an
# outage someone chose to cause.
apiVersion: chaos-mesh.org/v1alpha1
kind: PodChaos
metadata:
  name: inventory-latency-injection
  namespace: prod
spec:
  action: pod-failure
  mode: one                     # ONE pod, not all: a fault that kills
  duration: 5m                  # the whole fleet is an outage, not a test
  selector:
    namespaces: [prod]
    labelSelectors: { app: inventory, tier: backend }
  steadyState:
    # THE SAFETY NET. If this leaves the envelope, the experiment ABORTS
    # automatically. Without it, chaos is just induced risk.
    - name: checkout-success-rate
      rules:
        - name: order-completion-rate
          assert:
            message: "checkout success rate fell below 99% - ABORTING"
            condition:
              jsonpath:
                - $.data["value"]
                operator: ">"
                value: 0.99
    - name: order-api-p99
      rules:
        - name: p99-under-400ms
          assert:
            message: "order-api p99 exceeded 400 ms - ABORTING"
            condition:
              jsonpath:
                - $.data["value"]
                operator: "<"
                value: 0.4
---
# Network fault: the one that actually tests the circuit breaker and the
# timeout budget, because a killed pod is handled by the Service and a
# delayed pod is not.
apiVersion: chaos-mesh.org/v1alpha1
kind: NetworkChaos
metadata:
  name: inventory-network-delay
  namespace: prod
spec:
  action: delay
  mode: all
  value: "400ms"                # 4x the p50: enough to blow the
  jitter: "100ms"               # 300 ms timeout budget and trip the
  duration: 3m                  # breaker, not enough to look like loss
  selector:
    labelSelectors: { app: inventory }
  direction: to                 # INBOUND to inventory only: the clients
                                # are not slowed, so the fault is isolated
  target:
    selector:
      labelSelectors: { app: order-api }
---
# Time skew: the experiment almost nobody runs, and the one that finds
# the most severe bugs. A 5-minute backward jump breaks every
# certificate refresh, every token expiry, and every timestamp-based
# deduplication window in the system.
apiVersion: chaos-mesh.org/v1alpha1
kind: TimeChaos
metadata:
  name: leap-forward
  namespace: prod
spec:
  action: offset
  mode: all
  offset: "-5m"
  timeOffset: "0s"
  duration: 2m
  selector:
    labelSelectors: { app: order-api }

# The experiment catalogue, ordered by what it finds. Run these against
# PRODUCTION, scoped to one pod, with steady state asserted. The
# experiments at the top find real availability bugs; the ones at the
# bottom find real operational ones.
#
# HYPOTHESIS:  "If inventory is slow, order-api sheds it and checkout
#               keeps its SLO."
# ASSERTION:    checkout success rate >= 99%, order-api p99 < 400 ms,
#               circuit_breaker_state{dependency="inventory"} == "open"
# FAILS IF:     the breaker never opens, or opens but checkout still fails
#
#  1. pod kill, single            -> restart, readiness, PDB effectiveness
#  2. network delay 400 ms        -> timeout budget, circuit breaker
#  3. packet loss 5%              -> retry storms, idempotency
#  4. DNS failure                 -> does the client cache? stale routing?
#  5. CPU stress on ONE pod       -> does the load balancer notice?
#  6. disk pressure / slow disk   -> fsync latency, etcd commit latency
#  7. node drain, one at a time   -> topology spread, PDB, replica count
#  8. database failover           -> reconnection pool storms, cold caches
#  9. region network partition    -> multi-region routing, stale reads
# 10. clock skew, backward        -> token expiry, certs, dedupe windows
# 11. dependency 100% slow        -> does the shed actually trigger?
# 12. partial dependency failure  -> outlier detection, per-instance
# 13. expired credential          -> is there a rotation alarm?
# 14. queue consumer killed       -> lag alert, rebalance time, idempotency
# 15. storage latency 100 ms      -> everything above, under pressure
#
# The four experiments above that are almost always skipped, and each
# one has found a P1 in a mature system:
#   5  CPU stress: services that pass every kill test still fall over
#      when one pod is merely SLOW, because the load balancer only
#      notices unhealthiness, not slowness.
#   8  database failover: connection pools reconnect simultaneously
#      across every pod at once, and the thundering herd re-creates the
#      overload that caused the failover.
#  10  clock skew: a 5-minute backward jump silently invalidates every
#      signed URL, every JWT, every "expires in 15 minutes" check, and
#      every timestamp-ordered deduplication window.
#  14  queue consumer killed: a killed consumer is fine; a SLOW consumer
#      that never acknowledges causes unbounded redelivery and a
#      duplicate-processing incident instead of a visible lag alert.

# Start here: the three experiments, in order, for a first programme.
#   Week 1: pod kill on a stateless service. Proves the harness works.
#   Week 3: network delay on a real dependency. Proves the circuit
#           breaker and the timeout budget.
#   Week 6: database failover in a non-production replica of prod.
#           Proves the connection pool behaviour you have never seen.

Before automating fault injection, prove you have anything to inject against. An experiment against a system with no timeouts, no circuit breaker, no bulkhead and no shedding policy does not produce information; it produces an outage with extra steps, and the first such incident ends the programme permanently. The precondition is a written list of the defences you believe you have, and a way to observe each one: the breaker state must be exported as a metric, the shed counter must increment visibly, the bulkhead rejection must be distinguishable from a timeout, and the degradation level must be readable in real time. If those four metrics do not exist, the first three months of chaos work should be spent adding them, and the work will pay for itself in ordinary incidents before a single fault is injected. This is the single most common sequencing mistake in chaos programmes, and the reason most of them end quietly.

Exercises 13

  1. Define SLIs, SLOs and SLAs for one real service, including a latency SLI with an explicit threshold and a freshness SLI where the service feeds a dashboard. Deliverable: the three definitions, the PromQL for each, and a dashboard panel showing the current value against the objective.
  2. Implement a multi-window multi-burn-rate alert set with the four published window pairs and verify each fires correctly by injecting a synthetic SLI violation. Deliverable: the alert rules, the firing evidence for each pair, and a measured detection-to-page latency.
  3. Build a circuit breaker with derived parameters and prove the derivation: sweep the failure rate and show the false-positive and false-negative behaviour of your chosen threshold. Deliverable: the parameter derivation, the sweep results, and the parameter you would ship.
  4. Implement adaptive concurrency limiting in front of a real dependency and compare it against a fixed semaphore under a saturation test. Deliverable: the limit trajectory for both, the recovery time after the dependency degrades, and the throughput loss during the transient.
  5. Write and run a chaos experiment in production, scoped to one pod, with a steady-state assertion that aborts automatically. Deliverable: the hypothesis, the assertion, the observed result, and either the defect you found or the written argument that the defence engaged correctly.
